# Krsna, Israel, and the Druze An Interreligious Odyssey

## Foreword

For several years before I met the Hare Krsna devotees in 1988 I had been actively interested in the idea of the Druze’s Vedic and Oriental roots, When I first saw A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami’s transtations of the Vedic scriptures, I sensed that in these books I would discover the origins of Druze thought and culture.

The Druze, as Dhira Govinda dasa explains in *Krsna, Israel, and the Druze,* are actually the *Muwahidoon,* a word that refers to “the eternal religion.” This non-sectarian understanding of the process of linking with the Divine is akin to the Vaisnava conception of *sanatana-dharma,* the eternal occupation of the living entity. Fascinating parallels between these two great spititual traditions continuously unfold throughout this volume, revealing astounding philosophical, historical, and cultural connections.

Kamal Jumbalat, the great Druze spiritual and political leader, traveled to India and awrote extensively about parallels between Indian and Druze practices and principles. He was a vegetarian and, in the latter part of his life, considered himself to be a *vanaprastha*. Jambalat extolled Vedic literature, such as the *Bhagavad-gita* and *Ramayana,* I consider Kamal Jumbalat my mentor and inspiration. Following in his footsteps, I traveled to India three years ago to pursue the historical and spiritual research he had conducted. I plan to return to India to learn further about Vaisnava culture and to develop relations with Vaisnava practitioners.

The correlations between these spiritual heritages are, I believe, of monumental importance for the student of religious history, providing compelling evidence for East-West interrelations dating to antiquity. As a result of the determined efforts more than a decade ago, of the Hare Krsna devotees described in this book, a contingent of leading Druze sheikhs recently visited Vaisnava holy places in India. Thise holy men, many of whom are my close friends, were profoundly inspired by this visit and by the Vaisnavas with whom they associated in India. Their inspiration will, I am confident, lead to continued discoveries of the principles and precepts shared by the Druze and the Hare Krsna movement, and will stimulate collaborative interreligious ventures.

While provoking deep thought about the souces of religious faith and religions themselves, the stories herein capture the heart and mind with their personal effect, presented against the backdrop of multicultural Israel at the end of the millennium. I have relished these tales of Sri Caitanya’s *sankirtana* devotees serendipitously encountering the Druze people and presenting Krsna consciousness to them, primarily through the books of A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami. I feel great honor to have been a part of these stories, and I invite you to join in the excitement of *Krsna, Israel, and the Druze: An Interreligious Odyssey.*

Dr. Salman Falah

Director-General of Druze Education in Israel

## Introduction

According to the ancient Vedic scriptures, Krsna, who appeared in this world about five thousand years ago, is the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Having forgotten Him, we have come into the material world. Being compassionate to the suffering living entities in material existence, Krsna presents various systems of spiritual advancement to different peoples according to time and circumstance. All of these processes are designed to disentangle us from mundane conditioning and to bring us closer to the state of pure devotional service to the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

Five hundred years ago, Krsna appeared as Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu. Although Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu is Krsna, He played the role of a perfect devotee of Krsna to demonstrate how one can love the Lord in pure devotion. In the line of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada brought Krsna consciousness to the West in 1965.

Srila Prabhupada taught Lord Caitanya’s method of performing *sankirtana,* complete glorification of the Lord and the most auspicious practice for self-realization. To expand the *sankirtana* movement Srila Prabhupada established the International Society for Krishna Consciousness (ISKCON), an organization dedicated to the propagation of Lord Caitanya’s teachings. Currently, there are hundreds of ISKCON centers (*yatras*) around the world.

I first contacted the Krsna consciousness movement while studying at Pennsylvania State University in 1980. During the summer of 1983, I traveled around Europe and had further association with Krsna’s devotees. In the spring of 1984, I moved to the ISKCON temple near Tel Aviv. Since then, I’ve dedicated myself to developing Krsna consciousness and sharing it with others. I practiced Krsna consciousness in Israel from 1983–1990, and the Druze-Arab adventures cover the time period from the end of 1988 till the end of 1990, with a few interludes describing some events in the mid-1980s.

This book describes some wonderful happenings in the *sankirtana* movement in the Middle East. The events I relate describe the distribution of Krsna conscious literature, the spreading of Krsna’s name and fame, and the story of people contacting Krsna connsciousness and advancing closer to their natural spiritual position as unalloyed servitors of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Therefore, these stories are both valuable and worth hearing. The author, however, is spiritually and materially unfit to present these transcendental adventures. In this spirit, I would therefore like to submit the following verses from divinely revealed scripture to invoke auspiciousness:

*om ajnana-timirandhasya, jnananjana-salakaya*  
*caksur unmilitam yena, tasmai sri-gurave nama*

“I was born in the darkest ignorance, and my spiritual master opened my eyes with the torch of knowledge. I offer my respectful obeisances unto him.”

*vande ‘ham sri-guroh sri-yuta-pada-kamalam sri-gurun vaisnavams ca*  
*sri-rupam sagrajatam saha-gana-raghunathanvitam tam sa jivam*  
*sadvaitam savadhutam parijana-sahitam krishna-caitanya-devam*  
*sri-radha-krishna-padan saha-gana-lalita-sri-visakhanvitams ca*

“I offer my respectful obeisances unto the lotus feet of my spiritual master and unto the feet of all Vaisnavas. I offer my respectful obeisances unto the lotus feet of Srila Rupa Gosvami along with his elder brother Sanatana Gosvami, as well as Raghunatha dasa and Raghunatha Bhatta, and Srila Jiva Gosvami. I offer my respectful obeisances to Lord Krsna Caitanya and Lord Nityananda along with Advaita Acarya, Gadadhara, Srivasa, and other associates. I offer my respectful obeisances to Srimati Radharani and Sri Krsna along with Their associates Sri Lalita and Visakha.”

*vancha-kalpatarubhyas ca, krpa-sindhubhya eva ca*  
*patitanam pavanebhyo, vaisnavebhyo namo nama*

“I offer my respectful obeisances unto all the Vaisnava devotees of the Lord. They can fulfill the desires of everyone, just like desire trees, and they are full of compassion for the fallen souls.”

*tad-vag-nisargo janatagha-viplavo,*  
*yasmin prati-slokam abaddhavaty api*  
*namany anantasya yaso ’nkitani yat,*  
*srnvanti gayanti grnanti sadhava*

“On the other hand, that literature which is full of descriptions of the transcendental glories of the name, fame, forms, pastimes, etc., of the unlimited Supreme Lord is a different creation, full of transcendental words directed toward bringing about a revolution in the impious lives of this world’s misdirected civilization. Such transcendental literatures, even though imperfectly composed, are heard, sung and accepted by purified men who are thoroughly honest.”

*sri-krsna-caitanya prabhu-nityananda,*  
*sri-advaita gadadhara, srivasadi-gaura-bhakta-vrnda*  
*hare krsna hare krsna, krsna krsna hare hare*  
*hare rama hare rama, rama rama hare hare*

In former ages, perfectly realized souls transmitted Vedic knowledge to students who were able to remember both concepts and information throughout their lives after hearing them only once. Due to the influence of the present degraded age, however, the individual’s ability to retain knowledge has decreased. Thus, about five thousand years ago, the great sage Srila Vyasadeva, a literary incarnation of Lord Krsna, compiled the Vedic literature. As divinely revealed instruction for the betterment of human civilization, the *Vedas* present fully all aspects of material and spiritual knowledge. Then, to prevent people from becoming distracted by prospects for material advancement, Srila Vyasadeva composed the *Srimad-Bhagavatam,* the essential conclusion of Vedic literature.

The *Srimad-Bhagavatam* describes the pastimes of the Lord and His devotees. According to the *Vedas,* hearing and chanting about these pastimes is the recommended process if we wish to purify our materially contaminated minds. Therefore, Srila Prabhupada painstakingly translated *Srimad-Bhagavatam, Bhagavad-gita,* and other Vedic literatures into English, He stressed that the expansion of the *sankirtana* movement is based on distribution of such literature. Therefore, his ISKCON centers made book distribution their main activity.

To record the history of these *yatras* is important for many reasons. Succeeding generations of Vaisnavas will receive immense inspiration and enlightenment from the experiences of their predecessors. As extensive narrations of the histories of almost every locale in the mundane sphere instill pride in future descendants, accounts of the struggles and successes of Vaisnava preachers will foster transcendental pride and self-respect in *Krsna-bhaktas* for generations to come. Such histories, aside from being source material for scholars, can also serve as stimulating educational texts for children attending Krsna-conscious schools.

Over the years, several devotees have encouraged me to write about our experiences preaching in Israel. I realized that to do such work properly would require that I conduct in-depth interviews with the devotees who participated in the Israeli *yatra* at that time. Taking advantage of a visit to the United States during the summer of 1989, I called Locanananda dasa, an early leader of the Israeli *yatra*. Upon hearing about my proposed book, he said, “I could write a book about each day I spent in Israel!” From my personal, six-year and very intense experience preaching Krsna consciousness in Israel, I know he was right. Therefore, I decided that I didn’t really have time to do the story justice; I wouldn’t write a book, but a brief chronology of events in the Israeli *yatra*. That chronology has been included at the end of this book.

Then I reconsidered. I very much wanted to share the story of the devotees sharing Krsna consciousness and distributing books through the period from November 1988 to November 1990. This was the period when we were cultivating relationships with Israel’s Druze and Muslim populations.

But still I was not prepared to execute the task in its entirety. How would I conduct the interviews? Members of those *sankirtana* teams had by now scattered around the world.

In the end, I decided to simply present some of the events that were recorded in notes, articles, and letters, and some of the stories that Maha-Laksmi dasi, my wife, and I remembered.

Israel’s position as the home of what are now called “Western” religions adds to its mystique for Western Vaisnava preachers. Raised with stories about Jesus of Nazareth walking across the Sea of Galilee, the tumbling of the walls of Jericho, and Jerusalem, the holy city of such ancient kings as David and Solomon, a devotee naturally imagines performing *sankirtana* on the streets of such antiquity.

Modern Israel is a relatively new country. After struggling for millennia, the Jews gained a nation in their Biblical homeland in 1948. Now they are surrounded by inimical Arab countries, whose populations far outnumber them. Since the birth of modern Israel, the nation has fought internally and externally with the Arabs. Although Arabs and Jews share a common ancestry, differences of political and religious conviction have caused persistent conflict between them. Thus due to their heritage and the serious and constant threat to their country’s survival, Israelis tend to be both patriotic and wary of any outside influence that may undermine the tough fiber of Israeli nationalism. On the otherhand, Israeli Jews pride themselves on being a tolerant and democratic people. It is for this reason that *Krsna-bhaktas* have been allowed to exist in Israel. (The devotees have, however, had to be mindful not to offend the Jews’ nationalistic and religious sentiments.)

Aside from preaching to the Jews, it is difficult to preach in the Muslim world at all. Srila Prabhupada acknowledged that difficulty when he responded to reports of book distibution in Islamic countries. “Whoever preaches in the Muslim countries,” he said, “I take the dust of his feet on my head.” Thus, that the Arabs in Israel gave the devotees a favorable reception is a significant breakthrough.

But this book especially describes the extraordinary response of the Druze community to Krsna consciousness. Although neither Arabs nor Muslims by many definitions, the Druze play an important part in the Arab world, particularly in Lebanon, Syria, and Israel. Scholars will surely find the link between Druze (*Muwahhidoon*) civilization and the original Vedic culture interesting, especially considering that there is abundant evidence of a primordial Vedic culture in fields such as linguistics and archaeology. Although secular academicians may find this work excessive in its religious seal, we believe that the inference of a strong Druze-Vedic historical and philosophical connection should inspire further research among the academic community. Scholars have long been mystified about the origins of the Druze people and religion. This research may shed light on the subject. subject.

When writing, defining one’s audience is of prime importance. This posed a difficult task, as I hoped to reach diverse groups—including scholars, sheikhs, and *sankirtana* devotees—with this work. In the end, I decided that this project should primarily glorify and chronicle the *sankirtana* movement as it was carried out through the distribution of Srila Prabhupada’s books. Srila Prabhupada’s guru, Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati, said that a newspaper could be published about each moment in the spiritual world. There is no limit to the volumes that can be produced to describe the spiritual activities of Krsna’s devotees in this world. A reader’s ability to appreciate the events described in this book, therefore, will be to some extent dependent on his or her realization of the profound value of Sri Krsna *sankirtana* and the distribution of transcendental literature.

Before closing, I would like to give very brief biographical descriptions of most of the members of the Israeli *yatra* mentioned in this book:

**Bhakta Yigal Enoshi**—A practicing Israeli lawyer, he adopted the practices of Krsna consciousness in the early 1980s.

**Devahuti dasi**—From a kibbutz in northern Israel. Devahuti adopted Krsna consciousness in the late 1970s.

**Dhira Govinda dasa**—Born in Philadelphia. Introduced to Krsna consciousness while studying at Pennsylvania State University in 1980. Dhira Govinda joined the ISKCON temple outside Tel Aviv in 1984.

**Dolayatra dasi**—Born in the United States to Israeli parents, who returned to Israel shortly after her birth. After university studies in the United States, Dolayatra returned to Israel and joined the Hare Krsna movement while serving in the Israeli military.

**Guna-avatara dasa**—Born in Israel. He joined ISKCON while in Europe in the mid-1970s.

**Isvara Krsna dasa**—Born to Israeli doctors. Isvara Krsna dasa became interested in Krsna consciousness while in the army, and joined the center outside Haifa in the early 1980s, shortly after his military service.

**Maha-Laksmi dasi**—Born in Tel Aviv. She began practicing Krsna consciousness while completing high school in 1979.

**Nagapatni dasi**—From a kibbutz in northern Israel. Nagapatni joined ISKCON in 1986 after having been hired by the devotees as a cleaner.

**Nitya-lila dasa**—Born in Mexico to a Jewish family. He was introduced to Krsna consciousness while residing on a kibbutz, and joined the Israel ISKCON temple shortly thereafter.

**Parama-guru dasa**—Born in Romania. Came to Israel with his parents at an early age. After becoming a leader of the vegetarian movement in Israel, he joined the ISKCON Haifa center in 1987.

**Radha-Madhava dasi**—Born in Israel. Joined the Israel ISKCON temple after completing military service.

**Saguna dasi**—From a *moshav* in central Israel. She joined the ISKCON Tel Aviv temple after completing high school in 1987.

**Santasya dasi**—Born in Eilat. She adopted Krsna consciousness in the early 1980s.

**Suddha-sattva dasa**—An Israeli kibbutznik and father of three. He was introcuced to Krsna consciousness while traveling in India. He joined the ISKCON temple near Tel Aviv in the mid-1980s.

My heartfelt thanks go to all the Vaisnavas and friends mentioned in this work. In addition, many persons gave encouragement and advice, for which I am grateful. I would also like to mention the following well-wishers for their kind support: Bhadranga dasa, Dave and Gert Grabel, Hrdayananda Gosvami, Kalakantha dasa, Kavicandra Maharaja, Madhava dasa, Pranada dasi, Puskara dasa, Urmila dasi, Jules Wolf, Yamaraja dasa, and Nurit Zaidman-Dvir.

## Historical Context

**Historical Context**  
In 1948, the modern nation of Israel was established in the Middle East, on the eastern Mediterranean Sea, on land that was the homeland of the ancient Hebrews. Israel’s population is primarily Jewish, although there is a sizable Arab minority. Since 1948, Israel has fought several wars with neighboring Arab countries. Since 1967, as a result of success in the Six-Day War, Israel has occupied various territories, including the Gaza Strip, the Golan Heights, Jerusalem, and the West Bank, none of which were included in its original 1948 boundaries. Arab residents of these occupied territories have become increasingly hostile towards Israeli rule. In December 1987, this hostility culminated in the *intifada,* a fierce uprising wherein the Arabs demanded political independence. Despite various attempts at peacemaking, tension between Israel and the people of the occupied territories continues today.

Within Israeli territory that is not disputed, there are several types of Arabs, including Muslims, Christians, and Druze. Most Muslims do not serve in the Israeli army, as they are considered potentially inimical (some Muslims, such as the peoples of some Bedouin tribes, do serve in the army). Among Arab Christians there is a continuum of perspectives towards Israel, with some siding with the Palestinian uprising and others markedly favorable towards Israel, and even serving in the Israeli army. Israeli Druze in the Golan Heights, however, tend not to be predisposed towards Israel and do not serve in the army, as they identify themselves as Syrians.

The Druze are considered by academicians and the world as an unorthodox sect of Islam. This book demonstrates a close relationship between the Druze and Vaisnava culture and beliefs, and indicates evidence for Vedic roots of the Druze. This book includes an appendix describing a visit some leading Druze sheikhs took to Vaisnava holy places in India and their appreciation of Deity worship. Muslim religion tends to be strongly averse towards worship of form. In this and many other ways, as described in the book you are about to read, an affinity between Druzism and Vaisnavism is prominent.

## 1: Druze Discovery

*Sunday Evening, November 6, 1988*

Sitting in the *sankirtana* house in the Vadi Nisnas district of Haifa’s Hadar section, I reflected on the events of the past few weeks. We had come to Haifa in October in a concerted effort to increase the distribution of Srila Prabhupada’s books in Israel. As had become the custom over the years, we had gone to various cities and villages with different items—silk paintings from India depicting scenes of *Krsna-lila* and candles produced by devotees in Israel. If it looked promising, we presented the books. As we had specifically been concentrating on book distribution these past few weeks, there had been a slight increase in the number of books sold.

Until today, each devotee on the party except me had given out at least one five-volume Grand Classics of India set. Just an hour ago I had been fortunate enough to meet a Hungarian Jewish woman, a worker in a Haifa beauty shop, who had purchased the set. Her Hebrew would not be strong enough to read the books herself, but she said her husband would be interested in such material.

The first devotees who had come to Israel had had only English books to distribute. Gradually, the *yatra* printed some small books in Hebrew—*Perfect Questions, Perfect Answers; Easy Journey to Other Planets;* and *Chant and Be Happy.* In 1985, devotees printed Prabhupada’s *Bhagavad-gita As It Is* in Hebrew. Then, in 1986, in a herculean effort, the Israeli devotees translated and raised funds to publish four of Prabhupada’s major works—namely, *Krsna, The Supreme Personality of Godhead;* the First Canto of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam;* and the Adi-lila of *Sri Caitanya-caritamrta* \[in two parts\]. Devotees then experimented with various ways to sell the books. Still, except for a few sets per month sold directly, most books were distributed singly, often as an incentive or award for donations given in exchange for other items.

At the start of 1987, we were not producing candles, and the *yatra* had to depend economically on direct book sales. Suddha-sattva dasa and myself went door to door, office to office, and shop to shop in Tel Aviv, occasionally selling sets of Srila Prabhupada’s books. Our most encouraging results, however, came as a result of contacting persons, such as yoga teachers and those interested in New Age lifestyles and philosophies, already interested in what we had to offer, and presenting them the books at pre-arranged meetings. In this way, over a period of six weeks, we distributed nineteen sets, a breakthrough with regards to book and set distribution in the history of the Israeli yatra. Then, coincidentally, interested contacts began to wane and candle production simultaneously resumed. For the next one and a half years, books and sets were distributed as they were during the 1985–86 period.

*Monday Morning, November 7, 1988*

Parama-guru dasa, Nagapatni dasi, Maha-Laksmi dasi, and myself drove north in the *sankirtana* Suzuki minivan, deliberating on where to distribute that day. Nagapatni suggested an Arab village she had once visited, named Cfar \[village\] Yasif. We were skeptical, but she described the village as a small, “safe” Arab village, primarily Christian. Until this time, almost all *sankirtana* in Israel had been performed in predominantly Jewish cities, towns, and small villages \[*moshavs*\]. The larger urban centers, such as Haifa, Jerusalem, Acho, and Tel Aviv, had significant Arab populations, and the devotees would also distribute books to them. Until this time devotees had distributed books to Arabs only in predominantly Jewish cities, because small Arab towns and villages were considered unsafe.

Nazareth was the only Arabic city where devotees would regularly perform *sankirtana*. In fact, the residents of Nazareth were more receptive to the devotees than in any other place in Israel. Both the Christians and Muslims, whose inbred piety contrasted the mood of the slick, Westernized sophistication that prevailed among the secular Jewish population, appreciated the Hare Krsna devotees as genuine workers for God. They would easily give donations in direct exchange for spiritual literature. Nazareth was a treat for the devotees, because they could dress in devotional clothing and present Srila Prabhupada’s books to people who sincerely respected their activities. Due to the population of Arabic Nazareth being only about fifty thousand and because it was far from our city base in Tel Aviv, however, we would limit our trips there to a handful per year. Often, we would make these trips on special occasions, such as Yom Kippur, when Jewish Israel stops all its activities.

Maha-Laksmi: *We had just printed the five-book set, and we were distributing in Nazareth. We parked the car in the Muslim section. I was the driver, and tried to rest before going out. All the kids started banging on the car. It’s always like that in Arab sections. It was Saturday. Almost no one was in the street. I just took the set, all five books, in my arms and climbed out of the car. A car passed in the street and the driver looked at me. He asked,“What are these books?” I showed him pictures. I showed him the picture of Aghasura \[a huge snakelike demon in Krsna’s pastimes\]. I said that these books are not only beautiful, but they explain that the most important thing in life is He completed my sentence. “To love God,” he said. “How much for the books?” And he gave 150 shekels \[ɱ100$\]. When I returned to the car, everyone was amazed that I had sold a set like that, on the street.*

While visiting Nazareth, devotees would often stay at church hostels operated by nuns of different orders, such as the Italian sisters. During the early and mid-1980s, nuns would participate in *harinama kirtana* when devotees chanted on Nazareth’s streets. The hostels were immaculately clean, and the nuns were very friendly. At 4:30 a.m. we would conduct morning *arati* in the church, sitting in pews, then chant *japa* in the courtyard. Sometimes we took *japa* walks to a nearby famous church, such as the Church of the Annunciation, where Mary received divine revelation that she would give birth to Jesus.

Although devotees considered Arab villages dangerous, when we heard that Yasif was, like Nazareth, mostly Christian and that many intellectuals resided there, we decided to make the venture.

We arrived and parked the car. The sights and sounds were strange. I had no way to know that in a short time they would become familiar and welcoming. We divided the town into three. Parama-guru walked off to one sector with his book bag, Nagapatni to another, and Maha-Laksmi and me to the third. Maha-Laksmi and I were the last to leave. While we were still preparing our books, Nagapatni returned. She had just sold a full set to the bank manager, a Druze from a nearby village, who had given her one hundred dollars for the five books. We congratulated her enthusiastically and went on our way. Visiting the various shops, we found the people friendly and hospitable, just as they were in Nazareth. We sold a few single copies of *Bhagavad-gita* and *Krsna* and several smaller books. When we returned to the car, we met Parama-guru, who had fared similarly. Then Nagapatni returned. She had distributed two sets and fourteen Maha \[extra large\] books—an Israeli record for one day’s distribution in Israel! We couldn’t imagine that, soon, all the devotees would be regularly achieving such results.

While we were having lunch, two teenage Druze boys pulled up in an old car. In a raucous mood, they claimed they were interested in our candles. They said they wanted several, but they had no money. They gave us their names and invited us to their village, Yarka, that evening.

Yarka is a slow, ten-minute drive up a steep hill from Yasif. Yarka’s population of about nine thousand is completely Druze. The village is said to have existed from the beginning of the Druze faith. We zigzagged our way through its narrow streets, finally reaching what appeared to be the central marketplace. We then asked a shop owner where to find the home of the teenaged boys we had met in Yasif. Following directions and finding the house, Maha-Laksmi and I knocked on the door. Rarely had I visited an Arab home.

A young, dignified man opened the door and, without hesitation, invited us in. This in itself was unusual. Devotees, especially when dressed as such, were not accustomed to cordial treatment at unfamiliar homes. The house was clean and the carpets and furnishings were, in contrast to the plain cement exterior, surprisingly luxurious.

Standing in the hall, we asked by name about the teenagers who had referred us there. The young Druze replied that he was the one about whom we were inquiring, but it was obvious to all parties that we had never met in Yasif. We pressed a little further, but the whole episode had apparently been the teenagers’ joke. The young Druze didn’t feel comfortable having us stand in his hall, so he invited us into the salon, which was orderly and spotless. He offered us a seat and implored us to accept different varieties of food. Feeling somewhat embarrassed, we accepted water. In an adjacent room we heard someone working in the kitchen. He explained that his wife was preparing a meal but that she would not come out because there was an unfamiliar man present. We briefly explained that we were devotees of Krsna and he briefly explained that he was a Druze. Thanking him for his hospitality, we departed.

As there was still about an hour of daylight left, and because we were exhilarated from the day’s uncommon events, we decided to do more *sankirtana*. Returning to the marketplace, we parked the car. Maha-Laksmi and Nagapatni went in one direction with a shopping cart full of books. Parama-guru and I walked down a road leading away from the village square. Shop owners eyed us curiously. Village children thought we were the most hilarious sight they had ever seen, and many followed us down the street. The dark street and clustered housing with no consistent pattern made it difficult to discern one home from another, especially because the villagers seemed to be constantly in the process of building extensions to their homes to facilitate their ever-expanding extended families. We chose a door and knocked. I forget who opened the door, but we were invited in and witnessed a bustle of Druze family evening activity.

Children of various ages scurried here and there, chattering about the amusing sight of two white-complexioned men clad in funny robes and dragging a shopping cart. In a back room, we could see the grandparents with some of their peers sitting on thin mattresses on the floor and speaking in Arabic while sipping coffee. The main room had a wood-burning pot belly stove in the center, with a pot of coffee brewing on the stove’s ledge. Yosef Habis smiled and invited us to come and sit on the sofa next to the stove. No one seemed alarmed by or resistant to our entrance. Strange we appeared, but they felt it their duty to make sure we didn’t feel like strangers. He offered us coffee. We explained that we didn’t drink coffee.

This was odd, and Yosef wanted to know why. Others in the room became attentive as we explained the principle of practicing purity, such as refraining from intoxicants of all sorts, in order to make spiritual advancement in the service of God. This was obviously an interesting topic for Yosef and his family. They served us water and a bowl of fruit. Parama-guru continued the conversation while I offered the foodstuffs to Krsna in my mind.

Then we took out the set of Prabhupada’s books and placed it on the table. First we showed Yosef the *Bhagavad-gita*. When he saw the “changing bodies” picture and heard our explanation of the soul’s transmigration, his eyes lit up and his curiosity overflowed. He told us about the Druze belief in reincarnation, and was excited to hear descriptions of the yoga system and the different pastimes from *Krsna*, *Srimad-Bhagavatam,* and *Caitanya-caritamrta*. He then purchased the set without arguing about the price. Walking back to the car on the dimly lit village streets, we bubbled with excitement. Rarely had a set been distributed so easily, and this was the first and only door on which we had knocked! Was it a fluke? Was such an experience waiting behind each door in this village? The *matajis* had sold a *Bhagavad-gita* at a small bank and were similarly excited with the Druze potential. The morning and evening totals combined to be the most Maha-books distributed in one day in the history of the Israeli *yatra*.

*Tuesday Morning, November 8, 1988 Rama*

Studying the map of Northern Israel, we tried to decide where to go for the day. None of us had much detailed knowledge of the constituencies of the Arab villages, but the *matajis* mentioned that they had once visited the town of Shefaram and had heard it contained some Druze. Parama-guru said that he had heard the village of Rama was also partly Druze.

Driving through downtown Haifa’s port district, past the smokestacks of Israel’s major oil refineries, we headed north and arrived at Shefaram, Israel’s second largest Arabic city. Leaving the *matajis* with their carts of books there, we headed further north toward Rama. Making a right turn on Acko-Sefat Road, we drove through the Muslim village of Majd El Kurum. Then on the right we passed the modern Jewish city of Karmiel, across from the much older Arab villages Bina and Dier El Assad, whose small buildings dot the mountainside.

A few miles down the road, we were gazing upon the steep, towering mountain that marks the entrance to the Upper Galil. Near its foot, nestled in an ellipse-shaped indentation, Rama village seemed custom-built to fit its mountain dwelling. Passing the villages of Nahf and Sagur on the left, we turned into Rama.

Though I didn’t remember at the time, I had driven through Rama once before. In the early autumn of 1984, we had arranged to do a program at a *mitzbe*, a small kibbutz, in the Galil. To arrive there we had driven up the steep mountain road that winds through Rama and leads to the far north. By the time we arrived, it was late evening and already dark. Children were playing noisily on the road. My thoughts, as well as those of others in the car, had been on the fact that if we were to break down there, we would probably be attacked. The village seemed hostile and unfriendly, the villagers eager to cause us harm if the opportunity should arise.

Now here we were, more than four years later, again following a main road into the village. We parked on a side street and got out of the car. I wheeled my shopping cart full of books into an area with a few shops. Surely I was a sight for the shopkeepers and other residents. I entered an electrical supply store and showed the books to the owner. He said he wasn’t interested, but since his brother would be he quickly purchased the entire set. I was stunned at the ease by which the transaction had transpired. The owner then told me, “You should visit Salach.” My mind was not agile enough to do anything but follow his advice.

*Salach’s Dream*

“Where does he live?” I asked. He gave me directions. Tracing my steps back toward the car, I then proceeded up a sharply inclined road. I’d never seen a road so steep, and found it hard to imagine how cars could drive on it. Dragging my cart of books behind me, my body leaned almost parallel to the road. By the time I neared the top, I was gasping for breath and my heart was pounding. I looked up. A young Druze man, about thirty years old, was standing in his doorway at the top of several steps. Smiling and welcoming me with an animated wave of his arm, he seemed overjoyed to see me dressed in my Indian-style robes. He invited me into his house and into his living room.

Before I could gather my wits, Salach pressed the “play” button on the tape recorder. I could only smile dumbly as strains of Srila Prabhupada singing “sad-gosvamy-astaka” filled the room. As if this were not sufficiently surreal, Salach Abas then related to me a dream he had had recently about Srila Prabhupada. In the dream, Srila Prabhupada was sitting in an abandoned house in Rama, and the residents were coming and worshiping him with flowers.

Salach’s table featured a picture of Kamal Jumbalat, the Lebanese Druze political and spiritual leader who had been assassinated a few years previously, posed with his Indian guru. Salach explained that Jumbalat had greatly admired the Vedic culture and scriptures. He had visited India several times, and was a strict vegetarian. His writings extolled Krsna and the *Bhagavad-gita.* During the latter part of his life, he had lived with his wife as a celibate *vanaprastha*. Jumbalat, who had mastered fourteen languages, is renowned as one of the greatest scholars the Arab world has produced. The *sankirtana* devotees would come to know Kamal Jumbalat very well, as his picture is standard material on the walls of Druze households.

Salach eagerly purchased the books, then told me of his interest in yoga. A few years previously, he had met a friend of ISKCON at the factory where both were employed. Through him, Salach had begun practicing *hatha-yoga* and was familiar with Srila Prabhupada and the Hare Krsna *maha-mantra*. After accepting Salach’s hospitality and association for more than an hour, I left his home euphoric. I felt that we had discovered a special phenomenon with the Druze.

The *matajis* had also a special experience at the Shefaram municipality, where they had distributed a set of books to a young Druze administrator. He was so excited that he arranged to have a picture taken of himself with the books and the *sankirtana* devotees. Six sets had been distributed in two days—another first for the Israeli *yatra.*

*Wednesday, November 9, 1988—Pekiin*

Behind the mountain looming over Rama is the village of Pekiin, whose population of a few thousand residents consists mostly of Druze and Christians. Although there had been a few Jewish families up until recently, only one Jewish woman remained. The Jews had moved and formed a small community, a *moshav* called Pekiin Hadashah \[New Pekiin\], on a nearby hill. After driving more than an hour from Vadi Nisnas, we descended the mountain into scenic Pekiin. Winding our way into the village square, we parked the car near a fountain pool in the center of the square where children were playing.

Dragging a cart of books about a kilometer to a corner of the village, I found myself standing in front of the village municipality, a somewhat modern, two-story brick building. Entering, I walked up the stairs to the offices of the village officials. After checking a few offices, I entered one where the workers didn’t seem too busy.

Behind each of two desks were middle-aged religious Druze, who, due to their identical traditional dress and strikingly similar large builds, appeared to be twins. The only way I could relate to their hand-knitted head coverings was to liken them to large, multi-colored *kepah* \[yarmulke\]. Their heads and faces were clean-shaven, except for full, well-groomed mustaches. The bottom halves of their bodies were clothed in the traditional black *sharwahl*. *Sharwahl* can be roughly described as loosely fitting pants with an extremely baggy mid-section. Their shirts were Western button-downs. Their attire revealed that these Druze were initiated into their religion as sheikhs, although they did not don the full black robe and tall, white, pillbox-shaped hat. Their dress, as well as their swarthy, olive-hued skins, blue eyes, and healthy, robust appearance, was typically Druze.

Mentioning books about Eastern culture and philosophy aroused their interest. I placed the set on a desk as workers from other offices gathered to hear. Turning directly to the “changing bodies” picture in the *Bhagavad-gita*, I explained the difference between the soul and the body, quoting the Sanskrit, then the Hebrew translation, for *Bhagavad-gita* 2.13. 2.13. Then I explained in more detail how the soul transmigrates from one body to another, quoting *Bhagavad-gita* 8.6. 8.6.

The midweek office routine was suspended for the town executives as they all became rapt in the philosophy of the transcendental soul. They knew all this from their upbringings to be true; such knowledge, they felt, was essential for a contented life. Transfixed, they wondered how this young Westerner with the shaved head, strange white robes, and thick, almost comical American Hebrew, could speak so fluently about the secrets of the soul. After all, this was confidential knowledge. Technically, only the sheikhs were permitted to know the philosophical truths of the Druze religion, although almost all Druze were familiar with the basic tenets of transmigration. Most shocking was that I was standing in the middle of their office speaking as if desperately wanting to convince them about the logic of the soul’s journey from one body to another. What could they say? They felt they were a lot more knowledgeable about the subject than I was, so they weren’t about to accept me as an instructor on spiritual truth, but if they were to say, “We know that,” then they would be divulging knowledge they weren’t allowed to reveal. Confusion resulted.

The worker behind the desk who had first invited me into the office adopted a playful mood. “We don’t believe in that,” he said gravely. I knew they did, and they weren’t sure if I knew they knew.

“What do you believe?”

“When we die, we die. Finished!” He indicated finality with a quick motion of his hand. Some in the crowd couldn’t contain their smirks. The sheikh was testing me.

Although I was accustomed to explaining the rational basis for the soul’s eternality to secular Tel Avivians, socialist kibbutzniks, and a slew of other members of Israel’s populace, the uniqueness of the current situation enlivened and challenged my intelligence. I reiterated Krsna’s explanation from *Bhagavad-gita* 2.13, then offered the analogy from *Bhagavad-gita* 2.22, 2.22, comparing changing bodies to changing clothes. I wasn’t aware at the time, but the Druze scriptures and sages use the same analogy to explain the phenomenon of transmigration. Therefore I didn’t understand the responsive gasps I received when I said that.

The sheikh continued to interrogate me, and the discussion ranged from the importance of sense control to the soul’s position as *abed Allah,* a servant of God. Both sheikhs, who were clearly the most respected persons in the room on spiritual and philosophical matters, wanted the set of books. After some perfunctory haggling about the price, we came to an agreement.

I only had one set. Never had two people wanted a full set simultaneously. I asked their permission to leave some belongings in the office while I ran to the car to get another set. Returning as quickly as possible so as not to deflate the sublime atmosphere, I loaded two full sets and some extra *Bhagavad-gitas* into my cart. Jogging back with the fifteen kilos of books in my cart, I easily caught the attention of other villagers. I finished the transaction, thanked the sheikhs, who smiled and expressed their eagerness to read the books, and left the office.

Exiting from the building I stood on the main road and wondered what to do next. Across the road on a high platform that served as a patio of an old house was an Arab woman under an awning before a fire, spinning huge chapati-like objects in her hand like pizza pies. Down the sloping mountain road on the right side came about a dozen teenagers, disembarking from a bus. Their loose dress and speech clearly revealed that they were not from Pekiin, Rama, or even Tel Aviv or Haifa, but from some faraway, more exotic location. Actually, they were two Jewish youth groups touring Pekiin, one from Fresno and the other from Northeast Philadelphia.

Then I remembered. I’d been to Pekiin, and I knew this chapati-spinning woman. In fact, I had purchased her product. Five years previously, while studying with the WUJS \[World Union of Jewish Students\] Institute, based in the northern Negev city of Arad, we had visited Pekiin on a field trip to the Galilee. There was an ancient synagogue in Pekiin, and several renowned kabbalists lived and were buried in the area. I remembered our group of Western Jewish students climbing the steps from the synagogue and congregating at this Muslim woman’s shop, many of us buying her Arab-style bread.

Crossing the street, I introduced myself to the woman and showed her the *Bhagavad-gita* in Hebrew. Her darker skin and features distinguished her as a Muslim rather than a Druze or Christian. Feeling a rush of spiritual excitement from the two sets just distributed, I began describing the *Bhagavad-gita* in simple terms, although ordinarily I would not have thought it worthwhile to present the books to such a person. “*Allah akbar*.” I told her that the book explained that God is great and that we are His servants. The Muslims, except for some mystical sects, do not accept the principle of transmigration, so I stuck to the principle of God’s greatness. She reached into her shirt and pulled out forty shekels for the book. I thanked her, encouraged her to read the book, although I doubted she could do so because I didn’t think she read Hebrew, and left her property amazed by the potency of Srila Prabhupada’s books and their message.

While walking through the village toward the car, a Druze, about twenty years old and accompanied by two friends, called out to me. He was curious about my dress and the contents of my cart. We parted fifteen minutes later, he with a full set and me with a lighter load. This was ecstasy! Three sets and a *Bhagavad-gita* distributed in about an hour.

I then knocked on a few residential doors, but nobody was home. Then I met a Druze doctor. He invited me into his home and treated me with the hospitality to which I was becoming accustomed in these villages. The doctor was intrigued by the philosophy and culture of Krsna consciousness and purchased a *Bhagavad-gita*.

Upon returning to the village square, I met Maha-Laksmi and Nagapatni. They had found only a few persons at home, but had distributed a few small books. We had discovered the Druze villages at the beginning of olive season, when families work in the fields until dark to harvest olives from the thousands of trees blanketing the mountains of northern Israel. Also, 1988 was a particularly strong crop, and everyone was busy. We drove out of the village and honored lunch *prasadam*. Then we decided to cross the mountain to do *sankirtana* in Rama for about an hour before returning to Haifa.

We arrived in Rama at dusk. After climbing a hill near Salach’s house, a Druze university student, who was socializing with several village friends, invited me into his home. He and his friends were eager to know who I was and what I was doing. I showed them the books and explained reincarnation and yoga. They were intrigued. The host student then purchased a set.

I discovered when I returned to the car that the *matajis* had also had a pleasant experience and had distributed a set of books to a friendly Druze family.

*Wednesday, November 16, 1988—Beit Jann*

Five of us—Parama-guru, Maha-Laksmi, Santasya, Nagapatni, and myself—headed down from the Hadar on our way to a village called Beit Jann, which we heard was one of the largest Druze villages in Israel. We found it on the map, located between Rama and Pekiin, a little further east on the mountain range. Santasya had now joined the newly formed Druze distribution group for the first time. She had been doing *sankirtana* locally, in Haifa, depending on city bus transportation, but after several days of hearing a continuous stream of nectarean stories about unparalleled set distribution, she couldn’t resist coming with us to the villages.

Around another rough and rocky curve we found ourselves entering Beit Jann. We all had a feeling that could be described as ethereal. It seemed impossible that a village could exist here. It was as if it had been dropped from above. Appreciating the picturesque scene as we slowly entered the village, I found it difficult to accommodate how some houses on peripheral mountain ledges didn’t slide into the valley. Desperately trying to avoid the unpredictable rush of children darting in and out of the village street, which they were convinced was an extension of their play area, I finally pulled into a small parking lot more than one kilometer into the village. Verbally partitioning the village to the best of our ability, considering that we didn’t understand how it was organized, we went on our separate ways. We had decided to concentrate on shops and offices, as well as other places where we could expect to find people during the day—schools and government buildings, for example.

I found an elementary school, but the manager at the gate said I couldn’t enter. A little further along, I entered a sweet shop where a customer introduced himself as Radhe. Gladdened at hearing his name, I gave a description of the books. He showed interest, but had no money or checks with him. We made an appointment for another time. I wandered somewhat aimlessly. The area was a maze of thin streets lined with a myriad of houses and an occasional grocery store. The village children decided that I was a worthy object of derision. Following the cart, they would kick, spit, and sometimes throw rocks. This went on for a few hours. Around 3:00 p.m., after having distributing only two books, I straggled down a steep hill to a dirt road and knocked on the door of an opulent, Western-style house. The Druze couple invited me into their home. They weren’t interested in books but liked the decorative candles. As candles had been our main source of income for many years, we continued to carry them almost out of habit. Soon we would give up this habit, as it became clearer that our full physical and mental energies were required to carry as many books as possible for distribution to the receptive Druze. This family wanted a particular type of candle that I had in the van.

When I arrived at the van, Maha-Laksmi asked if I had any books left, explaining that we didn’t bring enough. I was shocked, since we had packed more than twice as many books as the *yatra* had ever managed to distribute in one day. I had been thinking that this was not a good village for distribution since I had distributed only two small books all day. It was only afternoon and there were only two sets left in the van—and these were already reserved for interested customers. I gladly gave the books in my cart to the other devotees to distribute.

After the candle interaction, at about 3:45 p.m., I returned to the van. The books were gone. Twelve full sets, three *Bhagavad-gitas*, three *Krsna* books, and several small books had been distributed. If we had brought more, we felt we could have done the same quantity during the late afternoon and evening. Stories rocketed back and forth in the van as devotees shared their feelings of transcendental ecstasy from their special day of distributing Srila Prabhupada’s books.

*Letters*

November 16, 1988—Dhira Govinda dasa to Danavir dasa:

...Some highlights of last week. The biggest week in Israel-yatra book distribution history —more than 260 book points. 12 full sets sets distributed, doubling the previous weekly set record. Nagapatni gave 14 maha books in one day, a new record. Your humble servant gave six sets in one week—a new record. Actually, this was done in only four days of *sankirtana,* as there were two festivals in Tel Aviv last week. But the point is that all of this has been forgotten by now. Yesterday, I gave four full sets, but today both Santasya dasi and Nagapatni defeated me by giving 4 sets each. Added to Maha-Laksmi’s three sets, it makes a total of twelve sets and sixty-six maha books in one day. After two days of *sankirtana*, we’re at about 100 maha big books distributed. Yesterday, I gave twenty-two, but today Nagapatni gave 24 maha-big books. The books are going like hotcakes. It’s really amazing. I’m afraid we’ll run out of stock while the BBT devotees are in Mayapur. Today, we were sitting in the car to take *prasadam* and a policeman disturbed us and took a set. Today we did $2,000 and hardly any candles were given; just with books. We don’t even think about *laksmi*, but everyone is doing much bigger than when they concentrated on candles. We still have only one car for the *sankirtana*

Everyone is astounded at the mercy Lord Caitanya is bestowing. We’re going to small and mid-sized Arab villages, mostly Druze and Christian, in Israeli territory where there is no political agitation. These Arabs serve in the Israeli army and all the devotees agree that these villages are safe. I can foresee going on in these areas for at least a year, if not more. I think it’s a whole field opening up.

Last week, I had a meeting in one village with a friend of David Harzion named Salach. I walked in his house and was greeted by a tape of Srila Prabhupada singing *bhajana.* Salach chants *japa* regularly, sometimes sixteen rounds, is vegetarian, and very intelligent. He is interested in translating Srila Prabhupada’s books into Arabic. He said he had a dream that Prabhupada was sitting in his village, which is named Rama, and that the residents were coming and worshipping him.

Many Arab university students are taking sets. A large percentage of these villages do not read Hebrew, so don’t take books, although they would if we had books in Arabic. It’s hard for me to conceive of how many Arabic books we could give in this area. Whereas the Arabs in their homelands are especially proud and intolerant, the Israeli Arabs, at least those in unoccupied territories, are thirsty for knowledge of different cultures and philosophies.

We’ve encountered not the slightest resistance to “Hare Krsna.” They have no preconceived notions about us. I go in with a shaved head, *dhoti, kurta,* and *tilaka,* and we give sets based on direct preaching. It’s really ecstatic.

So, I would like to relate this to any other devotees who are interested in preaching in Arab lands. Every day, Isvara Krsna is stunned by the results. Tomorrow, he’ll be coming here for class with a car filled with four hundred books. The devotees are really excited about what’s going on. Just wanted to let you know what’s happening. *Haribol!!!*

P.S.—Today, November 19, we did about 110 book points, making about 350 350 book points in three days this week, including twenty-three full sets.

Today Maha-Laksmi and myself gave a set to a very nice Druze family. At the end they said they thought the books would help them become better Druze. They had two guests from Lebanon at their house, both of whom were extremely favorable. If devotees could somehow or other get to the millions of Druze in Lebanon and Syria, I think there is huge potential for book distribution. I don’t think it’s possible for devotees in Jewish bodies though. We’re not neglecting the Jews; we’re still going to *moshavs,* etc., sometimes, but we’ve struck a vein in these Druze/ Christian villages.

November 20, 1988—Maha-Laksmi dasi to Danavir dasa:

I didn’t find time to reply to you earlier because we were having a spontaneous marathon. When I say marathon I mean that we were so much in ecstasy that we couldn’t stop. I think Dhira Govinda Prabhu has already informed you of the scores.

It started like this. Since we were finding a nice response from people living in Nazareth, we thought that there must be more people with the same mentality living in Israel. We thought to make an experiment with some Arab villages.

In the beginning, we were a little hesitant, but then we discovered that there are three types of “Israeli Arabs”: Muslims, Christians, and Druze. With Muslims we still don’t have much experience. Christians are nice, and Druze are special as they believe in reincarnation! We feel completely safe going to their villages, because they serve in the Israeli army and many of them are policemen in the Israeli forces.

So we had some very nice success by the special mercy of the Lord, and it will be difficult to describe all the nectar, but I will do my best.

We tried one place after the other and the “avalanche” of mercy grew bigger and bigger until last week—which was the biggest explosion of book and set distribution done in Israeli history!

We gave twenty-six sets, 161 maha big books, thirteen Prabhupada books, and 133 small, which equals 419 BBT points!

I wish I could tell you about all the nectar, but I don’t think it is possible, so I will tell you about one ecstatic *sankirtana* day we had on Tuesday. We went to a Druze village called “Beit Jann” (*“bhajan”*). It looks like a “primitive” place; Arab women wearing white veils covering half of their faces and men with big mustaches and shaved heads covered by traditional cloth.

Right after we parked, two *matajis* jumped out of the car. It took me longer because I was the driver (what an excuse!). By the time I was preparing my paraphernalia, Santasya Prabhu had already given her first set! I rushed to the first place where they took a set plus about two candles, and I was very happy with myself. But when I came to the car, I was informed that Nagapatni Prabhu had already given three sets in the school of the village! So I filled my cart and rushed out again.

There were not so many shops, but I was just running here and there—to a house, talking to a person on the street, stopping a car—and since it was already noontime, there was not so many people around. I sat down on a stone to chant *gayatri.* Santasya Prabhu came. She had already given three sets by now. As we were talking, some kids were passing by, looking at us curiously and saying, “Hind, hind.” One of them approached me and asked me about the books. I showed them to her very seriously. Santasya was laughing at me, but then I saw that it was not a girl but a dwarf woman. While we were talking, a few cars passed and the drivers were saying “I took, so you should take, you should take.”

After a brief conversation, the dwarf woman pulled the money from her pocket and took the set, leaving me with an empty cart and a happy heart. I ran back to the car to fill the cart again and to continue giving sets. I managed to make an appointment for the afternoon, but when I came back to the car, the devotees were giving a set there! Someone was disturbing them while they were trying to take *prasadam*—a school boy who brought his father to buy him a set. Then the people who lived where we parked the car knocked on the window asking about “the books.” They ended up taking a set too. Nagapatni Prabhu came back to the car, her eyes shining, and we knew she had given another set! So there was only one full set left in the car and I was hiding it carefully for my meeting, which was successful, and the set was delivered

This was just amazing. We came back to the car, our hearts filled with ecstasy. It was 5:30 p.m. and we had no more sets or books: the car was empty. Sixty-six books and twelve sets had been distributed. We were excited, talking loudly, telling how this one took and that one took.

In my last letter I wrote you that Lord Caitanya is smiling at us, but on that day I think He was laughing like a madman and crying and dancing in ecstasy. His tears will enter my heart and overflood it and extinguish the blazing fire of material existence, which is burning there.

I feel that I am put in this situation by the causeless mercy of the Lord, because I have no qualifications. So because the devotees here are pure and enthusiastic, we get also some encouragement to render some service .

There are a few nice anecdotes which I wanted to tell you.

• One of the villages where we gave many sets is called Rama. We told one man who was taking a set from us that if he will chant the name of his village all the time, his life would be perfect. He could appreciate this very much and said that the word “Rama” in his language means “the best thing that exists.”

• Russian books can also be distributed there. One Arab communist student had studied in Moscow for six years and didn’t read Hebrew. When she saw the Russian books, she was convinced to take. It was interesting, because she was an atheist, but she couldn’t refuse books in Russian. On the other hand, she was attracted to the subject of reincarnation, which is close to her basic culture.

• One last story: Dhira Govinda Prabhu and myself had an appointment with a Druze couple. It was a long mantra and finally they took. Of course, they received us nicely in their hospitable way, and when we were leaving the man said, “So I hope that after reading these books, we can become better Druze.”*Sankirtana yajna ki jaya!*

*Shabbat, November 19, 1988*

Although Shabbat is traditionally a day of rest for Israel and the Israeli *sankirtana* devotees, Maha-Laksmi had made an appointment with a woman named Wahida Canaan from Yarka. We drove to Yarka to meet Wahida and her family.

*Druze Women*

Wahida is considered special within the Druze community. Keeping strictly to the religious life expected of Druze women, including wearing the traditional dress and head covering, she is simultaneously earning an advanced degree from Haifa University. Her combination of extensive material education and chaste, faithful adherence to family and Druze tradition is rare.

Nisim Dana, in her book, *The Druze—A Religious Community,* explains the duties of the traditional Druze woman:

She is admonished not to become accustomed to a life of luxury and an amount of jewelry which is beyond the standards of good taste. She is expected to reduce the number of her trinkets to acceptable limits or fewer still. This holds true even for a woman of a well-to-do family, out of concern that she will become so used to these things that she will be unable to withstand the vagaries of time. In like fashion, she must listen to her parents’ advice regarding her manners and the women she befriends, so as to maintain her religion, her honor and her integrity. After marriage, she is required to live in her husband’s house, on condition that it meets the requirements of religious law; she must obey him and accompany him to another city unless there is a good reason preventing her from doing so. She is likewise obligated to support her children should their father be unable to do so, and to behave properly and harmoniously with her husband and his family, being respectful of the elders among them and acting with tenderness and tact towards the youngsters: easy-going, indulgent, pleasant, refined, intelligent and patient. If her husband encounters financial difficulty, she must live within his means, refraining from needless expenses and luxury items, while restricting herself to essentials and managing the house properly. She is also required to refrain from unduly criticizing her husband and his family, on account of their limited financial means, but rather to display a joyful and satisfied countenance so that the house is filled with good neighborliness and contentment. For there are many wealthy people whose house contains power, respect and male issue, and yet, anger, instability, misfortune and wretchedness reign supreme.

After giving birth to her children, the woman is obligated to nurse them for two full years, to raise and educate them with loyalty and devotion, to instill in them religious principles, training them to behave honestly and decently, so that they are reared on firm foundations. Together with her husband, she must take pains to see that her children are given a good education, be they boys or girls. She is not to accustom them to a life of luxury and pleasure, nor to one of excess and needless expenditure, since these lead to future sloth which will take root and become so ingrained in the personality that they will be unable to undo such harmful habits when, as adults, they take their place in the struggles of life.

If she is more perspicacious than her husband, she must act with tact and delicacy in order to bring him to the correct conclusions and convince him of the correctness of her approach. She must never act toward him rudely or in any manner she deems fit, since that would result in controversy, quarrels and a life of misery. She must likewise recognize the fact that the circle of males close to her is very limited in number and that she is not permitted to be found in strange company nor to shake hands with a man or begin a conversation with him, unless he belongs to the circle of permissible male acquaintances. To behave otherwise is considered lewd and adamantly prohibited by religious practice. In addition, she must lower her gaze, be modest and timid, make an attempt to cover her body and behave in a polite and serious manner. If she is bright, intelligent and understanding, as well as inclined to think that she is capable of improving the lot of the society in which she lives, especially with regard to matters of religion and tradition, she must not defer from those good deeds which it is in her power to perform she is obligated to bear her own suffering in silence, in the case of mishap or sickness, to refrain from worry and anxiety, and to frequently mention the name of Allah, praising Him in times of happiness as well as in times of distress distress

A long black or dark blue dress, covered with a white apron reaching almost to the feet, is the basic attire of the religious Druze woman. White cloth covers their heads, attached to which is a long, white, diaphanous veil used to cover the mouth. They wear no earrings or rings on their hands. Women who have not been admitted into the ranks of the initiated dress like Arab Christian women, with conservative Western-style dresses. We’ve never seen a Druze woman smoke.

Compared to Muslim women, Druze women have many more rights in social and economic spheres. In fact, their rights equal those of women in Eastern Christian societies.

Spiritually, there are many female saints in Druze history, and currently there are women in some villages who have leading roles in the religious community. Proportionately, more women than men join the ranks of religious Druze. In many ways, it is easier for them to practice a strict religious life because their sphere of activities does not permit exposure to other ways. Conservative Druze would not allow an unescorted woman to travel more than one kilometer from her village. In Israel, the Druze have induced the government to waive the requirement for Druze women’s photographs on official documents, such as identity cards. Such a photograph is considered shaming to a woman’s honor, as is attending a movie or congregating in places such as shopping malls. Increasingly, though, Druze women in Israel go shopping together or even to see a film or hear a lecture. Still, our experience is that Druze women are characterized by sobriety, gravity, and decorum. Almost all Druze women marry before the age of twenty. Men generally marry before the age of twenty-five.

*Taquiyya and the Muwahhidoon*  
Wahida’s husband, Mohanna, serves as an officer in the Israeli army. Because of their dedicated and competent service in the military and police forces, the Druze are well-respected in Israel. There is an extensive political analysis surrounding this phenomenon, with different sectors of the Druze population in various Mid-East countries exerting various amounts of influence. For this narration it suffices to say that the Druze ascribe to a philosophy of social interaction known as *taquiyya.*

Taquiyya means performing one’s duties according to the national milieu while internally remembering one’s identity as a Druze, or, more precisely, a member of the Muwahhidoon, the eternal religion. This philosophy has enabled the Druze to exist despite their lack of a geographically defined nation. Also, this has resulted in unfortunate situations in which Druze from one nation fight against relatives from another. Druze have had to adopt the policy of *taquiyya* to avoid persecution, especially from Muslims.

The people that are currently known as the Druze originate from an Egyptian Muslim king known as Al Hakim Bi-Amr Allah, the sixth Fatimid Caliph, who ruled during the late Tenth and early Eleventh Centuries. According to the Druze, Al Hakim is a manifestation of the Godhead, whose body and activities are completely spiritual. Those who could understand and accept his Godhood became initiated into the order of the Muwahhidoon. *Muahhi* comes from the root *eka,* or “one.”*Doon* translates roughly as “religion,” or *dharma*. Thus in Vaisnava terminology, *Muwahhidoon* can be understood as *sanatana-dharma,* the common eternal religion of all living entities.

Interestingly, the Druze explain the Muwahhidoon in ways similar to how a Vaisnava would describe *sanatana-dharma.* The Muwahhidoon exists eternally, manifesting differently according to time and place. Druze history, unlike that of conventional historical accounts of religious traditions originating in the Middle East, dates back hundreds of millions of years, with God in human form appearing at regular intervals.

Kamal Jumbalat, in *I Speak for Lebanon* (pp. 33–34), writes,

But one should remember that, according to the tradition and established belief, the Druzes, which is to say the adepts of Unity—the Vedantists in a way—exist under different names in all the great religions of the world and thus represent more than one quarter of humankind. A new cycle of initiation will begin around the year 2000 with a message from God, the appearance of a great sage. Then the road will be opened again. People throughout the world will be able to set out along it, towards the true goal of all human life, knowledge of the Truth and its realization.

Strictly speaking, I am not a religious leader, but I have studied Druzism sufficiently to be able to offer some analyses. I really came to understand it through the teachings of the Indian *vedanta advaita* and the philosophy of the Greeks, which enabled me to discover the key to the Druze mysteries, for our gnosticism is the same as that one finds throughout the world, most notably amongst the great sages of India and classical antiquity. My knowledge gives me a certain spiritual, but not religious, authority. The sheikhs and the initiates come to see me; we pray together and sometimes we meditate. I read them old texts of Hindu *advaita* thought, very strictly elaborated in India over the ages, and explain to them the essential ideas of this historical and human spiritual endeavour. The local initiates are inspired by these readings. I myself was taught in India by a great sage who is my Pythagoras and Socrates. The Druzes hold that the first manifestation (*tegeli*) of the Most High (*el-Ali*) took place in the (imaginary?) town of Jim Matchin in India a million years ago. Westerners count in thousands of years—we count in millions and tens of millions of years.

The *Vedanta,* the hermetic key, opens up all the ancient books of wisdom of this part of the Near East, be it Egyptian antiquity, Greek antiquity or Sufism. The light of Advaita illuminates the teachings of Plato, Socrates, Plotinus and the other masters of antiquity, including Heraclitus.

Al Hakim, although appearing in the Muslim tradition, inherently has little connection with the historical phenomenon of Islam, as Sri Krsna, or Lord Ramachandra, would not be considered “Hindu” by a practitioner of *sanatana-dharma,* even though. They are both worshipped in what is now called the Hindu tradition.

After less than two generations following the disappearance of Al Hakim, the doors of initiation were closed and the Muwahhidoon teachings became confidential. One of Al Hakim’s leading disciples, El Drazi, preached the eternal philosophy anyway, and the sect ex-communicated him. Mainstream Muslims, who persecuted the Muwahhidoon adherents severely because of their alien beliefs, began to refer mockingly to this upstart sect as the Druze, after their fallen leader, El Drazi. This is similar to the Muslim’s coining of the term “Hindu,” referring to those on the other side of the Sindhu River. One result of fleeing from the Muslims was that the Druze settled high in the mountains. Also, to minimize conflict with Muslims, the Druze kept their philosophy secret, while carrying on with necessary social interactions. Thus *taquiyya* developed.

In particular, modern Israel presents a unique situation for the Druze, because far from having to acquiesce to Muslim dictates, they are serving in an army whose main opposition, both inter- and intra-nationally, are Muslim, Arabic forces. Among Israeli Druze there is a wide spectrum of opinion concerning how to relate to the Jewish Israeli nation and how the Druze should relate with the Arabic world. On one side are those who, owing to their long-standing affiliation with Muslim culture, and their external adoption of many Arabic behaviors and rituals, identify themselves as Arabs and are wary of the Jewish state. They may agree that ultimately they are Muwahhidoon but*,* in the present historical context, they see themselves as Arabs and should act as such. Best representing this portion of the Druze population are the vast majority of those living in the four Druze villages of the Golan Heights, who, nationally speaking, consider themselves Syrian, not Israeli, and of course who do not serve in the Israeli army. On the other side are those who, although remaining proud of their Druze heritage, feel it is advantageous for the Israeli Druze to cooperate, even if only for political reasons, with the Jewish government. As a result, most Druze serve in the army. Israeli society appreciates them for this. Of course, outside social and cultural influences are a concern to Israeli Druze society. One display of this influence is that Druze are increasingly giving Israeli names to their children.

The Canaan house was classically Druze. We were sitting on the floor on thin, covered mattresses spread along the wall. Three portraits common to most Druze households decorated the room, unmistakably identifying the religious and cultural tradition of the Canaans. Most prominent was the picture of Sheikh Tarif Amin, the most revered and venerated Druze leader. Sheikh Amin, well into his nineties, has been guiding the Israeli Druze for decades. Because of his expertise in adjusting political and social influences with eternal spiritual truths, he has become prominent as the foremost leader in the Druze world. Among Druze sheikhs, there are those who are strictly brahminical, dealing solely with scripture, religion, and philosophy, and those, exemplified by Sheikh Amin, who combine *ksatriya* functions with their priestly roles. Although as a natural result of political involvement Sheikh Amin has acquired detractors among the Druze, the Druze community as a whole has immense affection and admiration for him—to the extent that his portrait hangs in most homes. No personality inspires greater love and dedication for the Druze people than Sheikh Amin. Indeed, he personifies the Druze spirit.

Once, while trying to arrange a flower garland for Sheikh Amin before a personal meeting with him in his home in the small village of Julis, Isvara Krsna dasa and I knocked on a resident’s door, hoping to use flowers from his garden. A blue-eyed Druze in officer fatigues answered the door. We explained that we wanted to make a garland for Sheikh Amin for our meeting with him in the next few minutes. The man melted. “I’d give my life for the Sheikh,” he said. It was obvious that he could conceive of no better use for his flowers—or *any* of his possessions—than to offer them directly to his beloved sheikh. He provided needle, thread, and the flowers from his garden, as well as some gorgeous blooms from the government building across from his house.

Druze sheikhs attempt to renounce as many material amenities as possible. They are forbidden to drink alcohol or to smoke, to engage in any sort of gambling, including card playing, to speak obscenities, to wear jewelry, silk, or fancy clothes, to attend nonreligious celebrations or functions generally meant for entertainment, and to speak to women who are not members of their immediate families. They strive for abstemiousness in eating. Television is forbidden for sheikhs, and strict adherents don’t listen to the radio. Union with one’s wife is permissible only for procreation. We have heard that for serious sheikhs, such union is allowed only once every three to five years.

To the right of the Sheikh’s picture was a large picture of Kamal Jumbalat, dressed in a Western suit. Jumbalat, due to his courageous yet liberal leadership of the Lebanese Druze through endless political turmoil, as well as his deep sense of fairness and spiritual affinity, represents another paragon of virtue for the Druze.

To the Sheik’s left was Sultan Atrash, wearing a turn-of-the-century sports coat and an Arabic turban, standing next to his horse. Atrash was a great warrior Druze who freed Syria from French occupation. Descriptions of his legendary prowess on the battlefield border on the mystical. Also, Sultan Atrash was a strict Druze sheikh who staunchly followed the principles of a religious life. His picture was vibrant with the courage and vigor for which the Druze are famous.

We can see that these heroes share a wealth of moral and spiritual qualities, reflecting Druze values. As the effects of Kali-yuga operate through their innocence, however, the Druze have shown a tendency to accept well-known personalities from their heritage to glorify, even if these individuals are not spiritually praiseworthy. Thus, descendants of Jumbalat and Atrash, who have gained notoriety in the political and entertainment fields respectively, have found places on Druze walls next to, and sometimes in place of, their worthy ancestors. Also decorating this room were shots of Mohanna in full uniform with Israeli governmental and military dignitaries.

Children scurried around the house, and many visitors arrived, making their Saturday social rounds. The traffic made it difficult to sustain a conversation. As everyone was curious about us, we found ourselves again and again starting from the beginning, explaining about the Hare Krsna movement and Urila Prabhupada’s books. At first, Mohanna wanted to take one or two books as an introduction, but his wife was intent on the full set. Together we had all created a mood of intimacy in his home and, in the end, he agreed to buy all five books.

Spiritual business concluded, the Canaans invited us to the lunch table. Their hospitality was sincere, and we accepted their offering of pita bread, *labneh,* and olive oil. While Maha-Laksmi offered the foodstuffs to Krsna, I took the opportunity to ask Mohanna about Israeli Druze demography. Eager to oblige, he listed for me all Druze or partially Druze villages in the country.

Daliyat El Carmel, Julis, Beit Jann, Jatt \[Galil\], Yarka, Yanook, Ain El Assad, Hurfeish, and Kisra constitute the purely, or at least 95%, Druze villages, and Osafia, Shefaram, Yasif, Abu Snan, Maghar, Rama, Pekiin, and Samiah are villages with Druze as well as Christians and/or Muslims. Also, Magd El Champs, Masade, Buqatha, and Ain Kunya are four completely Druze villages in Ramat Ha Golan. Generally, Israeli Druze don’t reside in villages that are predominantly Muslim. Mohanna also told me the approximate populations of the villages.

Returning to the Hadar, we picked up the other devotees and drove to the Saturday feast in Tel Aviv.
