Sunday morning is the time we traditionally announce the weekly *sankirtana* results and share stories of book distribution in Israel. This week, there was more to tell than ever, and the Tel Aviv devotees were anxious to hear about the newly discovered *sankirtana* field.

*Letters*

November 21, 1988—Dhira Govinda dasa to Danavir dasa:

Hare Krsna. So the week of Nov. 13–19, the Israel yatra distributed 420.80 BBT points, and earned 65.51 *laksmi* points, including twenty-six full sets ...

November 22, 1988—Radha-Madhava dasi to Danavir dasa:

The *sankirtana* is really nice. The book distribution is above all expectations. One day the devotees distributed twelve sets, and my mind was so disturbed because I couldn’t relish the nectar of book distribution that I decided to become next life a *brahmacari* participating in traveling *sankirtana*

With Mohanna’s list, an able van, and plenty of enthusiasm, the devotees invaded the Galil with a full arsenal of potent, transcendental literature specifically designed to dispel all the illusions of this fallen age. First, we decided to try the large village of Maghar. Guttural consonants are different in Arabic than in Hebrew, and none of them bear a significant resemblance to anything English, although the prayer books of some synagogues in the diaspora approximate sounds like the Hebrew *chet* with the English “ch”. Anyway, I couldn’t exactly reproduce what Mohanna said when he pronounced Maghar, but I could understand there was Ma-something from deep in the throat, and “ar”. On the map we located “Maker,” not far from Yarka, and concluded that that must be it.

We found the village and drove around the streets. Sensing a distinctly different atmosphere than Rama, Beit Jann, or Yarka, we stopped and asked. Our suspicions were confirmed when we learned that Maker was a Muslim village, whereas Maghar was about half an hour away in a different part of the Galil. At the time, we still considered Muslim villages a likely springboard to the next body. Carefree and confident no more, I pressed the gas and maneuvered the van out of the village.

Arriving at about 1:00 p.m. in Maghar, I took *prasadam,* then wandered somewhat aimlessly for about two kilometers, my cart following faithfully behind me. When I sat down to rest, some children came over to bother me. Not in a mood for their fun and games after our unintentional visit to Maker and my struggle through the cold in Maghar, I asked them if they knew anyone who would want these books. “No!” they replied, laughing at my accent.

“What about teachers? Give me the names of some of your teachers in school.”

Taken slightly aback by my request, they gathered their wits and rattled off the names of several teachers. My pen was busy copying down what I could understand. Choosing a name at random from the list, I asked, “Where does Yosef Dagesh \[Daresh? Dahesh? Dachesh?\] live?” They all pointed in different directions, each trying to explain how to get there and wanting me to believe them. I chose one who looked a little sincere about my welfare, and followed his directions.

Walking down a few streets and up some tortuous hills, I knocked on a door. Not bad—Yosef Dagesh’s uncle invited me inside. We talked. He took a set. As I was leaving, Yosef arrived to visit his uncle. It all sounded interesting to him, and he offered to drive me to his house, located in a distant corner of Maghar.

Yosef was an English teacher, still unmarried, and his house was what might be called the Druze equivalent of a bachelor pad, although his lifestyle would surely seem tame by Western standards. It was obvious that Yosef loved books; he had dedicated an entire room to Arabic and English literature. He didn’t like to read in Hebrew, but Hebrew-language books were all I had, so he purchased the set.

As it was early evening, I returned to the car, having distributed two sets. The other devotees had distributed seven sets between them, and each was full of interesting stories about their adventures. Parama-guru had given a set to a friendly sheikh named Aboowoomie, who worked as an engineer. The *matajis* visited schools and distributed sets to the managers and teachers. All of us had lists of names of village intellectuals who had been recommended as prospects for Srila Prabhupada’s books.

Despite our fixed routine, our days were filled with adventure. Rising before 4:00 a.m., we were all showered and ready for *mangala-arati* by 4:30. From 5:00–7:00 we chanted *japa* and studied privately. At 7:00 we held *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class, with one or two devotees hearing from the kitchen speaker as they prepared a simple breakfast and a lunch for the *sankirtana* party. Most of the villages were too far away for us to be able to return for the midday meal. After class, we held *guru-puja,* followed by breakfast *prasadam.*

Loading the van became a sizeable task, requiring at least one devotee to rush out from breakfast and load the party’s books and carts. Our party now consisted of seven, as Dolayatra dasi was on a sort of vacation from her army service and Saguna dasi had joined the party from the Hebrew BBT. To ensure that each distributor had a full stock of books for the whole day, we loaded twenty full sets, as well as extra *Bhagavad-gitas* and *Krsna* books, along with hundreds of small books, five or six *sankirtana* carts, and lunch *prasadam* for a pack of hungry, mountain-climbing devotees.

The van seemed comfortably packed *before* the devotees got in. It had seats for five, and one or two could fit in the storage space if it was empty. But it was the opposite of empty. Still, somehow or other all the devotees squeezed in and we closed the hatch, rolled into Haifa, and headed for the Galil, map in hand. Although we could easily appreciate the brilliant space efficiency of the van, its riding comfort left quite a bit to be desired, especially considering our loading arrangement and the rough mountain terrain. Soon we adopted the policy of leaving one or two devotees, on a rotating basis, in the Vadi to do *sankirtana* in the Haifa area. That allowed us a more reasonable situation in the van, and allowed them to take care of such things as the shopping.

Upon arriving at a village, each devotee was assigned an area. We found that the most efficient way to perform *sankirtana* would be to visit the schools, government buildings, and shops in the mornings, trying to distribute as many books as possible and collecting names of people to visit in their homes in the afternoons and evenings. Some villages were so small—as few as five hundred residents—that they had almost no shops or administrative buildings. In such cases, we would leave some devotees in one village and have the rest go to another, agreeing on a pick-up time and place at midday. Lunch *prasadam* was honored in our van, and each devotee could return for a meal at any point in the day, some staying out all day and honoring *prasadam* at 6:30 p.m. on the return drive to our *sankirtana* house. After returning to the Vadi, devotees would study or discuss the day for about an hour before resting.

*Vadi Nisnas*

Haifa is a small peninsula jutting into the Mediterranean Sea, bordered on the north by Haifa Bay. The city sprawls along and around the Carmel mountain range, and is divided into three sections. The top portion is the Carmel, named after the Biblical mountain on which it is situated. This is also the location of most of Haifa’s affluent housing and apartment complexes. At the top of the Carmel is Haifa University, one of the two tallest structures in Israel, rising thirty stories above the mountaintop. This monolithic building is visible for great distances in all directions and is Haifa’s most prominent landmark. A few kilometers down the mountain road is Haifa’s other university, the Technion, perched on a ledge overlooking the Krayote, a group of towns on the bay coast that developed around Haifa’s oil refining industry.

The middle of the mountain peninsula is the Hadar, which means “majestic” or “luxurious”. The Hadar consists of several sections or neighborhoods as well as many crowded shopping streets. In the upper Hadar is the beautiful Baha’i temple, whose facade of classical columns and golden dome greets sea vessels as they sail into Haifa Bay.

The Vadi Nisnas is like an Arab village in the southern Hadar, bordering downtown Haifa at its southern edge and the Street of the Prophets in the north. Our house was in the northern section, two blocks from the Shekem \[army supported, all-purpose store\] indoor shopping mall and three blocks from Haifa’s government complex, including the City Hall, the Ministry of the Interior, and the courthouse.

Often, we would take pre-dawn *japa* walks in the park in front of City Hall, overlooking Haifa’s bay and the Krayote coastline. Gradually, as the sky lightened, subtle gradations of color unveiled the horizon, and we could distinguish the Mediterranean Sea from Israel’s northwest coastal land. Refinery smokestacks greeted us with vigorous outpourings from the valley, northern winds conveying their odor to the densely populated mountain face. As the sun rose on a clear day we could see all the way to Kibbutz Rosh Ha Nikra at the Lebanese border. Further east were rolling hills dotted with Druze and Arab villages, where, in a few hours, we would be rolling in with our *sankirtana* van.

The Vadi’s population is mixed Muslim and Christian. Our downstairs neighbor, Raja, was a friendly although sometimes volatile Muslim factory worker who lived with his wife, mother, and three children. He also had a few chickens, which he kept in a cage outside. Two Christian families and seven Hare Krsna devotees occupied the first floor of the old building, and the second floor housed another two Christian families. We rented our apartment from an elderly Jewish woman living elsewhere in Haifa.

The apartment, with ceilings about three meters high, consisted of two large rooms, together occupying more than fifty meters, a spacious kitchen with an open patio, one shower, and a separated washroom. Parama-guru and I resided in the smaller of the two rooms, closer to the entrance door, which became the temple room at 4:30 a.m. In one corner were tall metal shelves, fully stocked with Srila Prabhupada’s books in Hebrew. In another corner was an altar. Our altar consisted of a picture of the Pancatattva, below which were pictures of Srila Prabhupada and the *parampara*. On either side of the spiritual masters were the six books comprising the Hebrew set. When devotees rested in the room, we closed the altar with a curtain. Five *matajis* crowded into the second room, whose door led to the kitchen. Each room overlooked Rehov (street) Yavneh, with an un-obstructed view of a huge dumpster that local children enjoyed setting on fire. The street was usually noisy with children playing football or otherwise creating a racket. When we would pass through, they would half-mockingly chant Hare Krsna to the tune often heard emanating from our window.

About two months passed with devotees exploring each Druze village, distributing books and sets in schools, shops, homes, and while walking on the street. As devotees became more familiar with the Druze mentality, and more confident in presenting the books, distribution scores increased to more than forty sets per week—more sets than the *yatra* would formerly give in half a year. For several weeks, each day brought forth new stories, as devotees contacted and befriended Druze from all walks of life. *Sankirtana* devotee and villager became amazed as more and more similarities between the Vaisnava and Muwahhidoon cultures surfaced. Practically all the Druze were fascinated with their new friends carrying these most amazing books, although a large percentage couldn’t read the books in Hebrew.

The *sankirtana* devotees often conjectured bittersweetly how many books they could distribute if they were in Arabic. Because of the language barrier, we tended to concentrate our efforts among the Druze who were the learned sector of their community.

A typical *sankirtana* day would begin by entering a school, primary or secondary, in the morning. When the school children saw devotees for the first or second time entering their school yard, they laughed uproariously and sometimes jeered. After they became familiar with us, however, and understood that we were respected visitors of the principal, they became friendly and offered their assistance.

When we went to a school, we would begin our preaching by speaking to the principal. He would often purchase a set for himself and/or the school library. Then he would give us permission to show the books to the teachers, which we did during their break. Sitting in the teachers’ lounge, the books arrayed on tables, we greeted the instructors as they entered. A crowd would gather as we answered questions and talked about the books. Generally, one or two teachers would purchase a set. Also, we would obtain a list of teachers and other intellectuals in the village, whom we would visit in their homes later in the day. We had time to visit two or three schools in a morning. During the afternoon and early evening, devotees would visit persons on their referral lists.

Except for the large villages Shefaram and Daliyat El Carmel, where streets had numbers, streets were unnamed and unnumbered. If we wanted to find someone’s house, we would simply ask a villager, who would invariably be eager to help. However, there would often be confusion because we would slightly mispronounce a name or perhaps have an incomplete description of who we were looking for.

For instance, Salaach Saad may live on one side of the village, whereas Saalach Saad lives on the other side. Also, several persons of the same name often resided in the same village, distinguished only by their father’s name. Thus, if a devotee asked for Salim Reyad, not specifying Ben \[son of\] Jamal, villagers may have directed him to a different Mr. Reyad. This resulted in devotees crisscrossing the mountain villages, determinedly seeking their referral. Often, though, mistakes produced gratifying experiences.

“Oh, you’re not the teacher Kamaal Farchad?”

“No, I’m Kaamal Farchad. I own a grocery store. But don’t stand out there, please come in. What are you carrying in that cart? I’ve seen you and some others walking in the village today and I’m curious.”

An hour later, we would have become close friends with the grocer Kaamal and his family and would have to leave to reload the cart.

Also, Druze would often stop us on the street to see the books their friends had described. This often led to lively street scenes wherein several villagers would walk away with a Grand Classics set.

One day in Maghar, when schools were closed, I decided to work from a list of referrals, mostly teachers, I had collected. Loading two sets in the *sankirtana* cart, I located and visited each individual, beginning from the top of the list.

Adnon Tarboosh is an actor who had spent some time in America. He was familiar with Hare Krsna and was pleased to receive a devotee in his home. We discussed the condition of the world and the need for spirituality. Adnon was more worldly than almost any Druze I had met. He appreciated the work of the Krsna consciousness movement and placed a set in his home. At the time, we were selling the five-book set for the shekel equivalent of about one hundred dollars.

Then Nagib Sarkan, principal of a Maghar high school, invited me into his home, although he was ill. His curiosity aroused by hearing a description of the philosophy of *Bhagavad-gita* and Vedic culture, he shared with me many intricacies of Druze culture, including their custom of renouncing business and family involvement at age fifty, as in Vedic culture.

From 11:00 a.m. to 7:00 p.m., I would have visited about six people, each inviting me into their home, discussing for about an hour, and taking a full set. The remaining time was spent hurrying back to the car to load more sets.

*Letters*

November 28, 1988—Danavir dasa to Dhira Govinda dasa:

I am in due receipt of your letters dated Nov. 5, and Nov. 15, 1988, and have enjoyed the contents. The later letter was so ecstatic with the report of “set fever” in Israel that I read it in front of all the devotees at the San Diego temple and I’ve sent a copy to the IWR for publication. Everyone is amazed.

You are correct when you say that Israel as a whole is advancing in Krsna consciousness. If the *sadhu-sanga* principle works individually, why not collectively? The more devotees, the more books, the more *prasadam,* the more *harinama,* naturally the heart will self-cleanse. Books are the basis, and that means they are the main paraphernalia surely.

It’s nice that everyone gets together for the feast on Shabbat to enliven all.

Your *sankirtana* results are very pleasing to me, and they help to convince devotees here about straightforward set distribution. I pray for your total success in this most exalted service to Lord Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

P.S.—Keep trying to increase the book sales.

December 1, 1988—Param Guru dasa (Haifa) to Danavir dasa:

As far as my service I’m very satisfied and happy to engage in book distribution. We give the books to the Druze, and they take them in fives!! ... Talking to them they say that there are 100,000 Druze Arabs in Lebanon, a little more than a million in Syria and a couple of villages in Jordan. Even in India they are found by the name of Mou’ahidin, namely those that believe in one God. In Iraq they’re Hamzawiya. It is very impressive to find sparks of Vedic knowledge in their beliefs and tradition. A wonderful field for Arab books!!! The program set-up is very nice. Enough time for S.K. Enough time for reading and rest. I feel very fortunate to take part in this party.

Although adults and teenagers almost invariably treated the devotees with heartwarming hospitality, children aged twelve and under were often hostile. Indeed, such children were the main obstacle the devotees faced in the initial stages of preaching in Druze villages. Sometimes they would swarm an unsuspecting devotee. “What do you have? Give me a book! Where are you going? What’s the mark on your face?” Although their demeanor often started out friendly, anything the devotee said, whether congenial or tough, would result in the children becoming increasingly excited about their new plaything, the *sankirtana* devotee. Thus they would interfere with the devotees attempt to distribute books by making it difficult for the devotee to approach people or even to move around. Sometimes the children would be overcome by a mob mentality, as one child would inspire the others to trip the cart as it was rolling, spit, or even throw rocks at the devotee. Thus the devotees’ tolerance and perseverance were greatly tested. Sitting in the car to read, rest, or take *prasadam* was often impossible, as village children would crowd around, pressing their faces to every square inch of window, jeering and screaming.

Once, after a particularly wonderful day of set distribution in the village of Hurfeish, children pelted the Suzuki with stones as we drove away in the dark of night, causing fear and a broken rear headlight. Of course, if we met these children alone or with their parents, they behaved amicably and often expressed sincere friendship. When parents would witness cruel behavior by their off-spring toward the devotees, they would reprimand them on the spot, sometimes physically, but this did not deter the children from their mischievous behavior.

Only after more than a year, after devotees had visited their village several times and had established themselves as part of community life, did the children relent. Especially after we developed positive relations with Sheikh Amin and other influential members of the Druze community, and the Druze community thought of the devotees as members of the Muwahhidoon*,* the village children began to respect and admire the *sankirtana* devotees.

I recall that by 1990, whenever the children of Beit Jann would see the blue Suzuki *sankirtana* van roll through their village, they would spontaneously burst into a chorus of the Hare Krsna *maha-mantra* and would earnestly help us to distribute as many books as possible by encouraging their parents and others to purchase.

By December we had visited all the Druze villages except the two closest to Haifa, namely Osafia and Daliyat El Carmel. There was a conception among the devotees that these villages, being so close to a large Jewish city, were “Israelized” and would not provide the authentic Druze response we had come to cherish in the outlying communities. Fortunately, we decided that this was probably our mental concoction, and we visited Daliyat and Osafia. The residents proved to be as favorable and receptive as the rest of Druze Israel.

*Previous Druze Encounters*

I remembered having visited Osafia a few years previously on a Yom Kippur evening in 1986. Guna-avatara dasa had arranged a meeting with Jaber Aboo-rooken, manager of Osafia’s library. We drove together on the empty streets (empty because the residents were observing a solemn holiday) from our temple in Raanana, about half an hour north of Tel Aviv. We visited Jaber in his home, and he took all the books we had at the time for the library. Then he referred us to a few others in the village, one of whom also took books.

Driving on the highway back to Raanana, a rock thrown from a bridge smashed the van’s windshield, as some religious Jews objected to our traveling on the holiday, not understanding that Krsna’s devotees were fulfilling the aim of all scriptural regulations by distributing Srila Prabhupada’s books and thus giving love and knowledge of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

I remembered a few other encounters with Druze in previous years. Once, while doing *sankirtana* with candles in a *moshav* in the Galil in 1985, a Druze businessman was passing on the street as I exited a house. He was friendly and initiated conversation. I showed him the candles, but he showed no interest in them. He seemed philosophical, so I spoke about the difference between the soul and the body. He became quite interested. I pulled out the *Gita,* the only Maha-big book we had at the time. Very excited by the reincarnation picture, he purchased the book at the full price. At the time it was rare to sell a *Bhagavad-gita* directly, so I felt a special thrill.

Then I remembered an experience on traveling *sankirtana* in late 1986 in Kiryat Shmone, in Israel’s far north, at the border of the Golan Heights. I was in an office, and many workers were buying candles. They purchased enough so that I gave the employees a *Bhagavad-gita.* They thanked me politely. One man, however, who was visiting the office, became interested. We discussed *Gita* philosophy and I mentioned that we had three more books in the set. He wanted to see them, so I ran back to the car. When I returned, he purchased the set. I asked which *moshav* he was from, and he said he was from a village in the Golan called Magd El Champs.

Another memory was from the time I was marketing sets, mostly to contacts, as the Tel Aviv temple president in early 1987. Once, while going shop-to-shop across the street from the municipality, I stopped at a video arcade. Although not hopeful about distributing books in such a place, I showed the books to the manager. As expected, he was uninterested, and even made a comment about my hairstyle. Close by, however, an older teenager playing Ms. Pacman said he wanted the whole set. I thought he was joking, but I had been trained to take every possibility seriously and had seen that Krsna could work in very surprising ways. He emphasized that he really wanted the books, but he didn’t have money or checks at the moment. We exchanged numbers.

When I called him a few days later, he invited me to his apartment where he took the whole set. I remember he told me he was something called a Druze, and that his culture was very similar to that described in the books. I invited him to our evening program, and he visited our center on Gordon Street a few times.

Santasya arranged a meeting with Salman Falach, the Minister of Education for the Israeli Druze. Salman, originally from the village of Samia, where the Falachs are a prominent family, lived and worked in Haifa. His office was on Rehov Ha Bankim, downtown in a heavy commercial district. Santasya was glowing as she returned from the meeting. She said that Salman wanted a *Srimad-Bhagavatam* set in English for his personal library and fifty Hebrew sets for the school system. I remember responding “J*aya! Haribol!*” but not actually giving the report much credence. The next week, however, Salman Falach purchased a thirty-volume *Srimad-Bhagavatam* set and some other books in English. He said that the check for ten thousand shekels for the fifty sets could not be issued for about three weeks due to holidays and administrative procedure. To avoid becoming too hopeful, I put the incident out of my mind.

Toward the middle of December, having visited each Druze village at least once, we began to interact more with Christian Arabs both in villages mixed with Druze and in a few small, completely Christian villages—Miilia and Fassoutta, for example. The Christians were pious, as we had already experienced from our trips to Nazareth, and supported our mission by taking books and sometimes sets. Santasya and Saguna, distributing together as a team on our first visit to Miilia, distributed six sets in one day. On the same day, I met Kahil, a young man proud of being the most fervent Christian preacher in Miilia. We sat in his home and talked with his mother and other family members. Kahil narrated the story of his rebirth as a Christian, and described his passionate desire to share the mercy of Lord Jesus Christ with others. With his mother’s consent he gave a donation for a *Bhagavad-gita.*

One December day, Isvara Krsna dasa came from Tel Aviv to participate in the adventure. We went with Parama-guru to Rama. It was an exceptionally cold day, and at one point snow fell. For me and some of the native Israeli devotees, it was the first time we had ever seen snow in Israel. After visiting Salach Abas, who asked if we thought he should take *sannyasa*, *sannyasa*, I went with Isvara Krsna to distribute in the village. We visited a few Christian doctors living in the middle of the village, but they were not available or not particularly interested. Then we located the home of Georgette Awokian, manager of one of Rama’s elementary schools. Georgette, of Armenian Christian ancestry, lived with her two sisters. One was a teacher and the other an education inspector. We discussed with the three of them about spiritually centered education, after which the Awokians purchased a set. We continued visiting people, giving a few books to respectable members of Rama’s Christian community.

The dramatic increase in book distribution caught the *yatra* by surprise. We were rapidly depleting our stock of books, especially *Bhagavad-gitas,* which hadn’t been printed since 1985. As the BBT devotees were about to leave for India, they quickly arranged for another printing of the Hebrew *Bhagavad-gita* to satisfy the needs of the book distributors.

*Letters*

December 9, 1988—Nagapatni dasi to Danavir dasa:

This month something wonderful has happened. The distribution of books has increased and especially sets are being distributed at an amazing rate. We have found real purchasers of Srila Prabhupada’s books. It is an Arab cult whose people accept the principle of reincarnation. It’s unbelievable, immediately after seeing the books they take them. So after so many battles with the Jewish *karmis* in the big cities such as Tel Aviv and Haifa (where we constantly have to argue with the people if we are a cult or not a cult, if we are idol worshippers, etc.), suddenly we find people who live in the mountains, aside from the materialistic environment. People who think they are the only ones in the universe who believe in reincarnation most of them don’t even speak Hebrew. They have never heard of “Hare Krsna” and neither have they seen the books.

They say that their leader from Lebanon, Kamal Jumbalat, used to go to India and that he translated some of the Vedic literature into Arabic (It’s hard to know if the information is correct or not). Since they are being persecuted by the Muslims they keep their religion a secret. But we have managed to get some pieces of information. Many of them tell stories about their past lives. I met a fifteen-year-old boy who told me that in his previous birth, when he was forty-five, he got bitten by a snake and that’s how he left his body. He remembers his previous wife and children and goes to visit them in the nearby village. Another story of a five-year-old girl, who told the story in Arabic and her parents translated it into Hebrew so that I could understand: in her previous life she was a Lebanese woman who got murdered together with her four-year-old son. She remembers it as if it just happened today.

The Druze educate their people that the eternal soul passes into another Druze body at death. Slightly different than the description in the *Bg.* But when they see our books they snatch them from our hands. They don’t want just one or two books, they want sets, sets, and more sets. The devotees are fired up and they distribute many sets. If book distribution is going to continue like this, there will be no more books in the storage and what will we do then??

December 15, 1988—Santasya dasi to Danavir dasa:

It seems like devotees of Krsna, in the name Druze, are there in small villages in the mountains of Israel and amazingly enough, they have many realizations by which they are eager for our books. Their faith has been kept secretly, and only their religious persons can read their scriptures. general feeling is that many could make more advancement by further cultivation. Saguna Prabhu joined our party enthusiastically and eager for the new field of set distribution. I was complacent in my mentality, thinking that to give sets we only have to go to these Druze villages where they believe in reincarnation. However, Krsna mercifully withheld the results, as we—Saguna and myself—presented the books in one school and received no favorable responses. We then increased the chanting of the holy name and I realized that we still need to be dependent on Krsna’s will. On our way to the next school we entered a shop. Most of the shops were already done in our previous visit to this village, Beit Jann. (See Maha-Laksmi’s letter with detailed ecstatic stories from there.) Somehow or other they hadn’t seen the books before and the manager immediately took a set out of curiosity about reincarnation. As he was writing the check, a person entered asking what the books were about. He then right away on the spot asked if he can order a set also, and upon being answered that he can have them now, he responded, “Then I’m fortunate!”

We went back to the car to load sets and proceeded to the school. On the way we took a bus, because the school was located on the top of a mountain. The bus driver found the set interesting, and upon entering the school he showed us the principal’s room. A meeting was going on there, but somehow or other, they invited us and were really captivated by “the books.” Then the principal took two sets, one for himself and another for the school. His secretary took another, then the bus driver entered and saw everyone signing checks, so he asked for one set for himself. Somehow or other, by Krsna’s mercy, we gave that morning six sets and were obliged to return early out of not calculating enough sets. The total that day the devotees gave eleven sets. So Lord Caitanya is overly merciful towards these special souls, and for sure He is reciprocating your strong desire and prayers for success.

All the devotees here are hard workers. Dola Yatra prabhu with a nice spirit of preaching and determination to distribute the books. Nagapatni Prabhu keeping super-excellent *sadhana,* and Maha-Laksmi Prabhu, who is our loving mother, providing us always a warm atmosphere and shelter. ...Dhira Govinda Prabhu for his tolerance and purity in his dealings with all the devotee. He is always there in front of the commando, inspiring and exemplary. The glories of these devotees can be narrated more and more, but it will increase the volume of this letter.

In a letter Srila Prabhupada wrote (74-10-19) “Book distribution is also chanting. Anyone who reads the books, that is also chanting, and why distinguish between chanting and book distribution? These books I have recorded and chanted and they are transcribed. It is spoken *kirtana*.” By this the understanding of spreading the Holy Name can be more clear to me.

December 21, 1988—Danavir dasa to Maha Laksmi dasi:

Yes, the “spontaneous marathon” has become famous already. It is a *sankirtana* devotee’s dream come true. But it didn’t happen by accident. All of you have been so intensely meditating on and actually distributing sets of books that the Lord wanted to satisfy His beloved servants more. Krsna personally arranges everything for His sincere devotees just as a loving father and mother personally raise their tiny babies.

December 23, 1988—Maha Laksmi dasi to Danavir dasa:

Dear Danavir Prabhu,

Please accept my humble obeisances. All glories to Srila Prabhupada. All glories to set distribution.

In one of your letters to Dhira Govinda Prabhu you mentioned about the “set fever,” but now it is more like the “set explosion.”

This morning we went to Salman Falach, the Minister of Education in charge of the Druze people in Israel. He is the top man for all schools and libraries and all other educational institutions.

As usual he received us very nicely. Actually we had to make an appointment with him two weeks ahead.

We brought a Hebrew set and also a thirty (30)-volume English set. After a brief explanation he ordered that a check for 750 shekels ($500 U.S.) be issued to us and purchased both sets.

Actually three cousins of his all have sets. Two of them are school headmasters and one has a book shop.

One of the headmasters, Jamal Falach—we had a very nice conversation with him, explaining to him the basic principles of Krsna consciousness. He was very impressed and wanted that all the teachers and students in his school should study the books and become conscious of the knowledge presented there. He further wished us a success in our “sublime mission.”

Salman Falach told us that he had correspondence with the late Kamal Jumbalat, the famous Druze leader who knew Sanskrit and translated books into Arabic. He was vegetarian and one of his books was about Krsna.

We explained that *Srimad—Bhagavatam* is the ripened fruit of all Vedic knowledge. He said that he is developing a thesis that shows that actually Druze religion and knowledge have all come from Indian scriptures and since *S.B.* is the essence of them, then he said, “I think after reading these books I will discover that our religion is coming from them.”

Then he went on and ordered from us fifty (50) Hebrew sets to distribute to all of his institutions. (It is not 100% sure, but if it gets through it will be ecstatic.)

We also previously met one teacher, Abdallah Salama, who is a vegetarian and is practicing TM. After a brief talk, he agreed that TM is all nonsense because the aim is to become one with God and how can a person with intelligence say that he is God? So he acknowledged the superiority of Krsna consciousness and purchased two sets. We gave him also *japa—mala* and taught him how to chant. He said that all his life he was looking after yoga, and here we are at his door, giving him yoga. He thought himself to be very fortunate.

Many of them actually remember their previous births. We had a talk .

January 3, 1989—Dhira Govinda dasa to Danavir dasa:

We have so many stories we’d like to share. Maybe you’ve received some letters from the other devotees. Last week I was sitting in the lobby of the municipality of a village named Daliyat El Carmel. Many passersby were looking at the books without too much interest. Then the mayor came down the steps, dressed in religious robes. He’s both a political and a religious leader, and from his demeanor it was obvious that he was used to being held in high esteem. He kindly asked me who I was and what I was doing. I responded that we have books here that explain about the spirit soul. He asked what is my name. I said “Dhira Govinda dasa. It means I’m a servant of God.” He looked at the crowd and said in a profound way, “He’s a good man. These are very good people.” On his way out the door he turned around once more and said to me, “You are doing a very great work.”

*Salman Falach Buys Fifty Sets*

Santasya again visited Salman Falach during the first week of January, this time with Maha—Laksmi. Mr. Falach, receiving Krsna’s special mercy from within his heart, insisted on taking fifty sets. We set a date for delivery. In addition, another fifty books and 1,500 shekels were added to the transaction, as the BBT had just published the Second Canto of the *Srimad—Bhagavatam* in Hebrew. The set now had six books, and Salman wanted all six.

Many Druze regarded Salman Falach as a man sincerely concerned about the welfare of the Israeli Druze and possessing the intellect and political acumen to effect progressive change. Salman, along with his staff of inspectors and supervisors, adamantly regarded the Druze as a separate cultural and political entity, and objected when their people were lumped in as “Arabs.” In fact, Salman had written extensively about the Druze and wished to demonstrate academically that they were not Arabs. Association with Krsna devotees and Srila Prabhupada’s books, combined with the connection between India and the Muwahhidoon, Muwahhidoon, seemed to have sparked something within Mr. Falach.

Other Druze criticized Salman, claiming that he compromised the purity and authenticity of Druze culture with unprincipled Western standards and thus assisted in diminishing the quality of spiritual and cultural education for their children. In later months, on *sankirtana*, when we would present a preaching kit including pictures of ourselves with Salman and his staff, reactions would vary greatly. We learned that before presenting such pictures, it was advisable to assess a Druze’s political stance. In contrast, our pictures with Sheikh Amin invariably evoked affectionate feelings within Druze hearts, to the extent that they would sometimes repeatedly kiss the picture in expressions of spontaneous emotion.

Inching along in heavy Haifa morning traffic, we navigated the few blocks from Rehov Yavneh to Rehov Ha Bankim. Our party consisted of Isvara Krsna, Santasya, Maha—Laksmi and me. We circled around the block until we found a parking space in a lot. More than three hundred books filled the van. Santasya and Maha—Laksmi walked to the office of the Druze Education Ministry, while Isvara Krsna and I waited in the car. They carried their side bags and two apple streudels —enough to feed the entire staff of Salman’s office. At the previous meeting, the *matajis* reported, *prasadam* sweets were a huge success and contributed toward the establishment of a warm working relationship.

Maha—Laksmi returned after about twenty minutes and told us to pull in front of the building and unload. Parking on Rehov Ha Bankim, almost at the corner of Haifa’s main commerce street, Rehov Ha Atzmaout (Street of Independence), Isvara Krsna and I each took a couple of packages and walked up the steps to the Druze Education offices. The secretary, a conservative-looking Jewish woman in her fifties, wore an intimidating frown. She showed us where to place the books. Then Santasya introduced us to Salman Falach, who was enjoying the streudel *prasadam*. Formalities concluded, we continued with our ecstatic although strenuous task of transferring more than three hundred kilos of books from the van to the building, up a large flight of steps and into Mr. Falach’s office.

We were in bliss, especially seeing the surprised faces of the hundreds of business-minded pedestrians on Rehov Ha Bankim. Seeing us in full Vaisnava dress, *dhotis* pulled up a bit to facilitate lifting, many couldn’t help asking, “What are you doing?” Matter-of-factly we explained that the Department of Education was purchasing our books for schools and libraries.

Finally, the van was emptied and the office of the secretary, whose demeanor had significantly changed after a few pieces of streudel and from having been part of such a transcendental event, was filled with packages of Srila Prabhupada’s books, leaving little space to maneuver.

In January 1989, Salim Zedan, an English teacher and well-wisher of our movement, arranged for us to do a program for his teenage students. We prepared *prasadam* and brought instruments and books. All the devotees on the *sankirtana* party attended. Salim introduced us and requested that we speak in English for his students’ edification. After a talk about the *Vedas,* yoga, service to God, and reincarnation, the students asked intelligent questions. Then we held a *kirtana* and served the *prasadam*.

*Letters*

January 21, 1989—Maha Laksmi dasi to *World Sankirtana Newsletter*:

The following is some history of the development of *sankirtana* in Israel, culminating in the ecstatic and overwhelming “set explosion” which occurred during this late winter when the number of sets of Srila Prabhupada’s books distributed doubled, tripled, quadrupled, until it increased x8.

Since I joined the movement in 1980, there have been many significant changes and much development in the preaching and *sankirtana* techniques. It is said in the *Bhagavad-gita* 10.11: “Out of compassion for them, I, dwelling in their hearts, destroy with the shining lamp of knowledge the darkness born of ignorance.”

If a devotee is sincere Krsna will guide him through revelation to so many improved ways of distributing His causeless mercy .

When I first began to go out on *sankirtana* it was with stickers and cassettes on the streets, asking for donations. After a few years we added some small books and began more and more to relish the nectar of sowing the *bhakti-lata-bija* in people’s hearts. Of course this was all in Western clothes, because *sankirtana* couldn’t be done with *sari* because it will create fear and rejection in the minds of the people. ...We introduced Indian silk paintings What really helped the preaching is going dressed in devotee clothes, because then they can see who we are and that we are not ordinary salesmen. People appreciate when someone is honest and straightforward with them. Many times people would tell us, “I am against your movement, but still I will buy this because of you or because you are honest or because you are trying so hard.”

But the latest and most amazing discovery is the Arab villages. I cannot even properly describe it in words. First we didn’t go there because of the tense political situation in Israel, but we were encouraged to try because whenever we would meet an Arab on *sankirtana*, he would always be very friendly and receptive.

We don’t really have a sophisticated technique to distribute the books to them. Sometimes we just park the car in their village and they come and buy the sets from the car. Sometimes they run after us in the streets and buy a set (we ask $120 for a set of six books).

Of course we go to all the schools, libraries, municipalities, all the doctors, lawyers, dentists. All the respectable people in those villages now have Srila Prabhupada’s books .

This is really amazing because previously we thought that “books cannot be distributed in Israel.” But Krsna is unlimited and so is His mercy.

January 23, 1989—Dhira Govinda dasa to Danavir dasa:

About the fifty sets—the Israel Ministry of Education, Druze Division, purchased fifty sets (300 books) of Srila Prabhupada’s books for 10,000 shekels (about $7,000 U.S.) for use in educational institutions. Maha-Laksmi and Santasya Prabhus did the mantra. Isvara Krsna Prabhu and I came for the final agreement and to deliver the books (we carried the books from the car to the office). It was an amazing scene. Our Suzuki full of books was parked in front of the Ministry of Education. A crowd gathered, as two Hare Krsnas (in full attire) began unloading box after box of Hare Krsna books into the Education Ministry. while we were delivering the books there was in progress a meeting of all of the leaders of Druze education, many of whom are our friends and had purchased sets in their villages. I am wondering how far we can go in this field until we meet resistance. The political, cultural, and even religious leaders are welcoming us with open arms. Last week I gave a set to a Druze University student. I was in the dormitories and he was involved in all sorts of nonsense activities. When he saw the books, he dropped everything and became very serious. He said he has to have them and any other books we may print. He has relatives in New York whom he says would surely take all of our English books. He had no money, but arranged for a friend to lend him 200 shekels on the spot. (The serious religious Druze follow the four regulative principles. They can have sex once in five years with the wife for a child, and many are vegetarian. Among the mass, there are not many vegetarians, however. It is their tradition at age fifty to renounce worldly affairs and dedicate one’s energies spiritually. Also, the religious Druze shave their heads.) He says from his research it is clear that the original Druze literatures were in Sanskrit. He repeated several times, “These books are the source. These books are the source.” Also, last week I spent an hour one evening with Abdullah Salami, a twenty-eight-year-old Druze who took a set a few weeks ago. He’s a high school teacher, vegetarian, and he claims he’s been celibate his whole life. You mentioned during your last visit to Israel how cultural training is an important factor for the youth to learn restraint. So in these villages they maintain the social code of no illicit relations before marriage. I showed him how to chant *japa.* He’s intelligent and inquired philosophically. His religious father was in and out of the room, making sure we were comfortable (bringing fruit, etc.). Yesterday, the Ministry of Education said they want to arrange for us to do programs at the Druze villages. I think these people could become devotees while living in their villages. The next step as I see it would be to do these public programs with *kirtana,* lecture, and *prasadam,* along with *nama-hatta* type visits to individuals. And if, as soon as possible, Arabic books could be distributed to the mass of people, I think that would finish their material life. Maybe I’ll arrange to do some public programs while the devotees are in India

February 3, 1989—Maha-Laksmi dasi to Danavir dasa:

We had another meeting with Mr. Falach and he has purchased a set of *Caitanya-caritamrta.* Whenever we show Srila Prabhupada’s books to the man his eyes become wide open and he immediately asks, “How much?” and then purchases. So now he has fifty-one full Hebrew sets, a thirty-volume *Srimad-Bhagavatam* set, and one *Caitanya-caritamrta* set.

The next step is an organized tour of programs in Druze villages around the country backed by Mr. Falach and the first program will be in his office this Sunday. We plan to bring a whole feast, have *kirtana*, and give a nice lecture. Present will be Mr. Falach and his school inspectors.

I don’t remember hearing even one Druze saying that these books are expensive. They are always so surprised how come it is so inexpensive. The point is that their scriptures are kept secret and only religious men can read them. They are handwritten and can rarely be bought for about $500 each book. So when we approach these nonreligious intellectuals, teachers, lawyers, insurance agents, mayors, doctors, etc., they are very eager to receive holy books that talk about reincarnation and have their origin in India, because many of them say that their religion has originally come from India. But because of severe persecution from the Muslims, they have adopted some Muslim habits as a disguise.

Also some very religious men bought our books. Their religion doesn’t make them closed-minded and fanatic.

*Hare Krsna—Purity Is the Force*

Midnight—Vadi Nisnas:

Rudely awakened by the phone’s loud ringing, the seven members of the *sankirtana* party wondered both who could be calling at such an hour and which one of us would spring up to answer the phone. As the last time this happened it was my father calling from the United States to say hello, not realizing the Israeli time, I staggered to the kitchen phone.

“This is Sergeant Kessler from precinct Ha Rakevet Tel Aviv. Shalom Rosen \[Suddha Bhakti dasa\] and Noam Levy \[Krsna Lila dasa\] are in jail.”

“What?” I responded cleverly. He repeated the message and put down the receiver. I assumed this was a joke, although obviously in bad taste. Bomb threats, countless varieties of phone pranks, and many other types of harassment were familiar to Israeli devotee preachers. Anyway, to be on the safe side, I telephoned Bhakta Yigal, our devotee lawyer, at his home in Tel Aviv.

Next day—late morning:

“It’s the most terrible thing I could imagine!” blared the dramatic Bhakta Yigal into the telephone. He proceeded to relate the events of the past few hours. Suddha Bhakti and Krsna Lila were in jail, and Bhakta Yigal appeared in court for their preliminary hearing, not knowing the exact charge but confident that it was an innocuous misdemeanor stemming from a petty complaint from an antagonist. “Just let them go,” he urged the judge, hoping to persuade the court that chanting Hare Krsna *bhajana* or selling candles on the wrong street, or whatever the charge, was not reasonable cause for detaining decent people. Bhakta Yigal explained that the judge and legal opponent looked at him as if he were a lunatic.

“Are you crazy? Do you know what the charge is?” They handed him the paperwork and he turned pale. He turned to the sullen Suddha Bhakti and Krsna Lila. No one had anything to say. They were charged with possessing and intending to sell the largest quantity of heroin to enter Israel in a decade. To complicate matters, the national newspaper *Hadashot* had printed an article declaring that the Hare Krsna’s were caught with heroin. They reported it as conclusive, not using the word “suspected.”

We knew this couldn’t be true but feared the worst—that opponents of Lord Caitanya’s movement had framed our Tel Aviv center so well that our existence in Israel, as well as the futures of Suddha Bhakti and Krsna Lila, would suffer a severe setback. Yigal gathered himself and told the judge that the charges didn’t make sense. Suddha Bhakti and Krsna Lila had perfectly clean records. With no history of criminal activity, how could they suddenly be catapulted to becoming the top narcotic dealers in the country?

After contacting Danavir dasa, Israel’s Governing Body Commissioner, in San Diego, Parama-guru and I headed for Tel Aviv. Isvara Krsna made plans to return from Europe instead of traveling on to India as he had planned. The Tel Aviv center was a shambles from the police search. Dressed in Western clothes, I met with Bhakta Yigal at a designated spot in Tel Aviv and discussed a course of action. We were accustomed to having bugged phones in our city centers. As our Rehov Frishman center was surely under visual and telephone surveillance, we decided not to base there.

Fortunately, after a few nerve-wracking days, the police laboratory analyzed the confiscated substance and found it to be an innocuous cleaning agent. Bhakta Yigal reported that the judge told him, “Unfortunately, the substance turned out not to be heroin.” Krsna Lila and Suddha Bhakti were released, although this procedure was also fraught with inane and unjust bureaucracy.

Returning to Tel Aviv from Haifa, where our *sankirtana* house was under thinly disguised surveillance, Isvara Krsna and I went to the *Hadashot* offices. Both the writer and the editorial offices realized their blunder. The next day they printed a retraction, in which they wrote that the “heroin” was actually laundry soap, and depicted the police as buffoons. Thinking this unsettling adventure terminated, we rested somewhat peacefully that night.

About 5:15 the next morning, however, as we left for our customary *japa* walk along Tel Aviv beach, we noticed several posters on our door and in the small lobby of the building. The posters featured a huge blow-up of the scandalous *Hadashot* article. Surrounding the article were pictures of several members of the Israeli Hare Krsna movement. Under the article were some Hebrew quotes from Srila Prabhupada’s books, which our foes obviously considered implicating in some devious way. Finally, the poster included handwritten commentary about the wretched influence of the Hare Krsnas, and described that we consider the cow to be more elevated than the Jew.

There was no mystery regarding the origin of the propaganda. Yad Lachim, the most prominent anti-cult organization in Israel, had detailed files on each member of our movement, and these posters were their style. Desirous of proceeding with our *japa,* we tore down the posters. We had established good relations with our neighbors, and we were glad to spot the venomous propaganda before anyone else saw it and possibly became disturbed.

Walking down Frishman toward Dizengoff, we crossed Reinetz Avenue and stopped, stunned, in our tracks. The posters were on every tree in sight. We walked to Dizengoff, Israel’s bastion of sense gratification. Trees, poles, signboards and bus stops on both sides of the street in both directions, as far as the eye could see, were studded with the pernicious placards. As if this were not enough, another poster appeared, depicting line drawings of several dogs jumping for many bones. On the body of each dog was written in Hebrew the name of one of the devotees, and on each bone was written “cocaine,” “heroin,” “LSD,” etc.

We ripped down posters as quickly as possible. Still, by 9:00 a.m., when crowds filled the streets, the city remained filled with hundreds of posters. Actually, the event polarized public opinion. For those who already thought unfavorably about Krsna’s devotees, the article and posters confirmed their suspicions. Others increased their resentment toward extremist religious Jews, whom they perceived as intolerant and unfairly persecuting others, as the Jewish people had been persecuted many times in the past.

There is considerable strain between Israel’s secular Jewish population and the religious Jewish community. Although religious Jews constitute a small percentage of Israel’s population, they exert a large political influence, as the major parties often support their proposals to get votes. Some groups of religious Jews, extremists on the political spectrum, consider that the present state of Israel, due to its atheistic government, is actually delaying the appearance of the messiah and should be disbanded.

Fortunately, since 1987, devotees had been cultivating favorable relations with most Israeli newspapers, and many favorable articles about Krsna consciousness had appeared over the past two years. Thus the press did not take advantage of the situation and join in the denigration of our movement.

After discussing the situation with Danavir dasa, we launched a public relations campaign entitled, “Hare Krsna—Purity is the Force.” We printed a few thousand flyers explaining the regulative principles of Krsna devotees and emphasizing the restriction about intoxication. With large signs and loud voices we went to Dizengoff Square to perform *kirtana.*

As we were handing out our “Purity is the Force” flyers, some Yad Lachim members, dressed in the traditional black of their religious sect, handed out the same posters they had plastered on the trees. Isvara Krsna tried to speak rationally with them, but to no avail. He pointed out that even the newspaper whose article they were distributing had retracted their statements. Despite this, our side-burned friends were not fazed in their determination to distribute their fallacious literature.

Parents of devotees strongly supported the Hare Krsna movement in this episode, and many appeared at the Dizengoff Square rally. Although devotees’ parents generally had reservations about their children’s complete commitment to Krsna consciousness, years of cultivation had produced a healthy respect for the principles and character of the *bhaktas.* Now they were ready to publicly proclaim this appreciation, as they did not tolerate abusive and erroneous comments about the devotees.

Simultaneously, Isvara Krsna and I researched avenues of legal retribution for the nefarious incidents. From the neglected due processes of law during arrest and detainment to the scandalous newspaper article to the felony of covering city trees and poster boards with deceitful propaganda to the mysterious events surrounding the initial police break-in and search, we thought some wrongdoers could be exposed by judicial processes. We even had photographs of the people handing out the antagonistic posters on Dizengoff Mall. After discussing with Bhakta Yigal, twice visiting a friendly police chief, and talking to a private investigator, we realized that unless we were willing to invest time, money, and energy—none of which we had—we would not be successful in prosecuting any of the guilty parties.

The solution lay in preaching boldly about Krsna and His *sankirtana* movement, and this attack provided a great opportunity to do that. For more than a year after these incidents we distributed and preached from the “Purity is the Force” flyers, which we later also printed in Arabic. As a result, the Israeli public became more aware of the pure principles of Krsna consciousness. We were reminded how Srila Prabhupada, in the 1976 New York “brainwashing” case, turned a seemingly devastating attack into a glorious preaching opportunity.

Meanwhile, I was aware that the check from the Ministry of Education for the fifty sets had not yet cleared the bank. Salman and the Druze inspectors had seen the original article but not the retraction. We sent them the retraction, with an explanation of the incident. They weren’t surprised that such intrigues and sensationalistic reporting occurred in their country. Their doubts cleared and our credibility restored, we resumed our fruitful and satisfying relationship with the Druze community. Fortunately, *Hadashot* was not well-read in Druze villages. To counter occasional confrontations, however, *sankirtana* devotees were prepared with a copy of the retraction article along with “Purity is the Force” flyers.

*Ramat Ha Golan*

Except the four Ramat Ha Golan villages, which we had not yet visited because of their distance, most of the intellectual, Hebrew-speaking Israeli Druze population had seen the books at least once. Almost all Druze schools and libraries had full sets of Srila Prabhupada’s books.

Visiting the Golan in one day was not practical. To go there we would have to have a base in the Upper Galil. As it was mid-winter, camping outside was impractical. Scouting the potential, Parama-guru and I drove two-and-a-half hours from Haifa to Kiryat Shmoneh, at the Golan border. After reserving a room in a youth hostel at a historic site commemorating the activities of the Zionist soldier Joseph Zabotinski, we drove into the Golan. In the early evening light we saw signs warning travelers not to walk in the area, explaining that there were still land mines from when the Syrians had retreated during the 1967 war. Any thoughts of a pleasure walk we may have entertained were now erased. We crossed the Banyas and climbed the mountainous Golan terrain, which is characterized by black lava rock. Passing beneath the castle of Nimrod, the Biblical hero, snowy Hermon, Israel’s tallest peak, loomed in the distance at the Syrian border. The first village we encountered was Ain Kunya, lying about three kilometers below and to the left of the main road. Bypassing the turnoff, we continued up to Masade.

We regarded the Golan as a potentially risky expedition, as much or more so than the Israeli Muslim villages. It was “occupied territory,” although violent incidents were not nearly as frequent there as in the West Bank, Gaza, or Jerusalem.

February 24, 1989—Danavir dasa to Dhira Govinda dasa:

...Krsna is really empowering all of you in preaching to the Druze. IWR is printing an article, and the new “World Sankirtana Newsletter” will broadcast it more. All your activities there are splendid. I was only somewhat concerned about your venturing into the occupied territories. Lord Caitanya protects His devotees. Still, couldn’t we solicit some friends from the Druze unoccupied areas to preach and sell books on our behalf? Since many friends have cautioned against it, I think we should heed their concerns.

The seven-thousand-dollar books/sets sale may rival the Russian government’s order this year. This is nice transcendental inspiration. All of you are cooperating for spreading the glories of the Lord, and this proves your love for Srila Prabhupada.

...Unloading those boxes of books from the full Suzuki must have been an exhilaration. Congratulations to all who assisted in that transaction.

It seems that there are so many similarities between the Druze culture and the Vedic culture.

Because the Golan residents were Druze, we decided to make the venture anyway. The Golan Druze consider themselves Syrians and do not serve in the army. Some are bitter toward the Jewish government, although most Golan Druze accept their situation placidly.

Whenever there are peace talks with Israel, Syria demands that Israel return the Golan to them. Thus we knew that we may not have too many opportunities to distribute this sublime literature to the residents of Ramat Ha Golan. Actually, all of Northern Israel could be closed off at any time due to the concentration of Arab population. Therefore we felt it urgent to give as many books out as possible in these towns and villages.

Arriving in Masade at dusk, we parked on the main street in front of some shops. As we sat in the van, our hands and feet froze. I hadn’t experienced such low temperatures—below ten degrees Farenheit—since living in State College, Pennsylvania in the winter. Finally convincing ourselves to get out of the car, we entered a grocery store and asked where we could show our books. The proprietor was friendly and classically Druze in his dress and hospitable expressions. His features, like those of other Golan Druze, appeared more Asian than the Druze of Israel proper. He gave names of a few teachers, and we went on our way.

The Golan Druze responded as favorably as the rest of Druze Israel, although not as many of them, even among the intellectuals, spoke or read Hebrew. Carefully navigating the unlit mountain road at night, we arrived at the youth hostel. Telephoning the *matajis* in the Vadi, we informed them that they could join our Golan party as soon as possible to distribute books to the 15,000 Druze who hadn’t yet seen a *sankirtana* devotee.

The *matajis* arrived by 10:00 a.m. Tuesday morning. Parama-guru and I headed for Masade and Ain Kunya, and the five *matajis,* in a devotee-owned Romanian station wagon from a company called Delta, whose design looked state-of-the-art 1962, headed further up the slopes to Buqatha and Magd El Champs.

Ain Kunya is a tiny, picturesque village with one elementary school and a small local administration building. I went to the school and gave a set to a teacher, and Parama-guru did similarly with the town’s mayor. Then we headed for Masade, a village with a few thousand residents as well as the high school for all the Golan Druze. While waiting in the teachers’ room of the school, I met a married couple from Beirut. They said they often visited a yoga center there, and the wife especially showed interest in the books. Unfortunately, they spoke no Hebrew, and I had no books in either Arabic or English.

By the end of the day, the *sankirtana* party had distributed ten full sets and more than sixty maha-big books. The next day we all went to Magd El Champs, the largest Golan village and the northernmost village in the Golan Heights, situated at the foot of Mount Hermon. On the previous day the *matajis* had had great success distributing books in Magd El Champs.

We parked the car in the main square next to a large statue of Sultan Atrash. Parama-guru wanted to be the first one out of the car and into the streets, but he wasn’t successful. Standing behind the car loading his cart, he was swarmed by villagers shooting questions and thrusting shekels at him. Within ten minutes, he had sold two sets and a number of other books. I met a few students in the village who were visiting home from Russia, where the communist party was financing their education. We happened to have one Russian *Gita* in the van, which a student purchased. The two-and-a-half day Golan excursion had been a great success.

*Letters*

February 3, 1989—Maha-Laksmi dasi to Danavir dasa:

Altogether there are eighteen Druze villages in Israel, fourteen are in the Galil and we have visited them many times. There are four situated in Ramat Ha Golan, which is occupied territory from the Six Days War. It is right on the border of Syria and Lebanon, and the inhabitants are not even considered Israeli citizens. A few people told us better we don’t go there, and when Dhira Govinda Prabhu said that he is planning to go with Param-guru Prabhu, we became alarmed. Indeed we couldn’t fall asleep at night from anxiety. So we issued a protest paper signed by the *matajis* claiming our rights to also preach in every town and village, as Lord Caitanya said. However, pleas and tears didn’t help, and the men took a car to the frozen mountain area of the north. But Lord Nrsimhadeva heard our prayers and the next day, after the place was found to be nice and safe, we were allowed to join.

Krsna says in *Bhagavad-gita* 10.36 “*vyavasnye ’smi*”“I am adventure,” and I was thinking about this verse as we saw Mount Hermon full with snow. I have never experienced snow in Israel before. Of course, we are not after adventure, we just want to please Krsna, but we can see that spiritual life is full of tastes and experiences.

On the two sides of the roads that led to the small villages there were signs, “beware shoe bombs.” Dola Yatra Prabhu explained that before withdrawing from the land the Syrian army filled the ground with bombs that explode when one steps on them. (So we make sure not to walk there!)

We arrived at Buk ata, the first village. Our friends appeared, dressed in their traditional way, but they looked more Arabic and the houses were built in a more Oriental way and painted in different colours.

We were a little hesitant in the beginning and didn’t know what to expect.

We felt better when a very friendly villager tried his best to explain to us, in broken Hebrew, how to get to the school. But all our suspicions and fears, if we had any, disappeared when we entered the school and were warmly received by the manager. He didn’t speak Hebrew so well (we found that most of them don’t speak Hebrew), and his accent was very Arabic, but still he gave us 50 shekels more than we asked for a set as a “donation,” then gathered all the teachers and we gave them a short explanation of our movement and the books. They were very impressed and surprised and one of them purchased a set and brought us to the mayor of the village, who didn’t know a word in Hebrew, but took a set “to help us” and out of appreciation for the beauty of the books and the nice ideas of our movement.

Many of them said that if we had the books in Arabic they would definitely take.

We also met a man named “Ganga” who took a book from us. When we asked him the meaning of his name he said, “Don’t you know the holy river in India?”

So it is amazing how the *sankirtana* movement of Lord Caitanya is spreading in every town and village. We were told that in this area there is a Muslim sect called “Aalowi” who also believe in reincarnation. So we see that this field is very big.

Our hearts fill with joy as the pile of books in our room is shrinking more and more. The Arab population is very big and variegated. Santasya Prabhu looked in the phone book for Arab lawyers and contacted many of them and they took sets. Only after visiting them she saw that they were Muslims and Christians.

Yesterday we went to Nazareth and forgot to take candles, so we were forced to use the books only and three sets and four books were given. So now, at least when we go to an Arab place, we take only books.

I was reflecting on the last verse of *Bg.* that wherever there is Krsna and Arjuna there will be opulence, victory, extraordinary power, and morality

February 3, 1989—Nagapatni dasi to Danavir dasa:

There was a great excitement here that Salman Falach took fifty sets. Half an hour ago the devotees came back from meeting him and he also took the English *Caitanya-caritamrta* set. Santasya brought a camera and took photos of the event to eternalize it.

For myself, I was having a few meetings, very successful, with Druze students in the university. A few saw the books when we visited their villages and then they were escaping from buying them. However, this time Krsna did not allow them to escape and four of them took sets.

Especially I had one exciting meeting with one of them as I was walking down the university path. One of the Druze students approached me and asked, “Tell me, please, aren’t you from the movement of Abhay Prabhupada? Isn’t it that you have the *Vedas?*” I was a little bewildered and asked him, “From where do you have this knowledge?” He was relating that he borrowed the book *Prabhupada* and started to read it with great enthusiasm. He remembered many details (things that I forgot long ago).

I talked to him and realized that he is thirsty for spiritual knowledge and just wants Krsna. I wrote him the *maha-mantra* on a big paper and promised to bring him a *japa.*

I saw him reading them as I was leaving. This event increased in me the faith that without Krsna consciousness no one can be happy. There are four more untouched Druze villages in Ramat-Ha Golan. It is an occupied territory. Therefore it is a bit dangerous there, but many people told us to go there since there are many intellectuals there.

So on Sunday, January 21, Dhira Govinda Prabhu and Param-guru Prabhu will be going to check out that area. Surely we will distribute many sets there. I’m anxious to go there already.

*Sankirtana*is an eternal service full of bliss and it gives endless happiness to my soul.

February 11, 1989—Dhira Govinda dasa to Danavir dasa:

It was quite an anxiety-filled week, surely also for you. By Krsna’s mercy the danger has passed; and now we’ll launch a public relations campaign to make the whole affair a forum for preaching Krsna consciousness.

Three weeks ago we had a meeting with Salman Falach. He took an English set of *Caitanya-caritamrtas* and arranged a meeting for us to make a two-hour presentation of Hare Krsna to the top level Druze education inspectors. The meeting was supposed to be last week, but when we arrived Salman was very sorry to inform us that he and the inspectors have some unexpected meetings and there’s no time to hear our presentation. But he appreciated that we prepared some food and allowed us to serve *prasadam.* Then the whole thing turned into a party of *prasadam* and Prabhupada’s books. The inspectors were relishing *samosas* and *bharatas* while intently looking through *Bhagavad-gita, Srimad-Bhagavatam,* etc. Although they had claimed they had an urgent meeting and no time to spare, after an hour I still wasn’t able to leave with the devotees as the inspectors were so enchanted with the books. They wanted us to stay and preach. I guess they got a higher taste. So the presentation meeting is scheduled for Wednesday (February 15, 1989). If the devotees present things nicely I think this could lead to regular programs all year round at all the Druze villages. Cultural clubs as well as schools. I’ve enclosed some pictures from the aforementioned meeting with the Education ministers. Last week book distribution reached new heights. Please excuse the pun. The devotees went to the Golan Heights to give transcendental literature to the conditioned souls there. This is “occupied territory,” and the Druze there do not serve in the Israeli army. Very few are able to read Hebrew. But results were very nice as the devotees distributed 390 book points, including twenty-four sets. Highlights of the week included two mayors of villages purchasing full sets, and one mayor taking a *Bhagavad-gita*....This past week the *matajis* performed very admirable on *sankirtana* considering the unusual happenings. I was traveling back and forth form Haifa to Tel Aviv almost every day and had Param-guru accompany me. Although you had instructed to make my headquarters in Tel Aviv, Frishman Street was for three days a very unsafe place. Simultaneously, Yavneh 15 in Haifa was under constant “undercover” surveillance. The phones of all our places are probably still tapped. The sequence of events was bizarre and wild. About the Druze—we’re always having more revealed to us about their secret religion. We’ve recently found out that both Krsna and Buddha appear in their scriptures.

P.S.—We’re going to write and print a flyer entitled “Hare Krsna—Purity is the Force.”

*Meyer Cohen, Israeli Lawyer*

Some *sankirtana* devotees, myself included, came to Tel Aviv to run the preaching programs and oversee general management while Isvara Krsna and the BBT devotees pilgrimaged to India. A few *matajis* remained in the Hadar. Parama-guru and I resumed the type of contact *sankirtana* that was done in Tel Aviv in early 1987. We contacted many yoga teachers, theosophists, and New Agers of various persuasions, some for the first time and some who were old friends of the movement. They took several sets, and many, who had taken a set of four or five books in 1987, completed their series.

Meyer Cohen, a renowned Tel Aviv lawyer, was a particularly noteworthy contact. Meyer was an observant Orthodox Jew in both behavior and dress. We had been given his name in 1987 by Rifka Doron, a middle-aged woman with a passion for esotericism. She had told us that Meyer Cohen was an open-minded man and an avid reader and book collector. For weeks I had tried contacting Meyer, but he was always busy. Finally he agreed to give us fifteen minutes to present the books.

As driving to the Allenby section of Tel Aviv was more troublesome than taking a bus, Suddha-sattva and I visited the lawyer by public transportation. Allenby is a passionate and noisy district, crowded with offices, shops, and people. We located his office building, climbed several flights of steps, and entered the waiting room. We could hear Meyer speaking with a client. His speech was abrupt and decisive. One might regard him as the archetypical Israeli lawyer. About twenty minutes later, he invited us into his office.

I had rehearsed a presentation, considering carefully how to present the Hare Krsna movement and Srila Prabhupada’s books in such a way as to appeal to a religious Jewish lawyer. But it turned out that I was no match for Meyer. I said something about yoga. “Yoga shmoga,” he retorted.

Meyer was, however, fascinated by the quality of the binding, artwork, and printing of the Grand Classics of India Encyclopedia, which consisted of the five maha big books that were available in Hebrew at the time. Books filled his high-ceilinged office, which was decorated on all sides with custom-made floor-to-ceiling wooden shelves. Then he showed us another large room, shelves over-flowing with literature. There were thousands of books in the office in several languages. The room was replete with classics, histories, religious literature, and varieties of other literary works. I had never seen such a large personal collection. Meyer explained that this was only a fraction of his volumes, the bulk of which were at home.

Despite his coarse exterior, we could perceive that Meyer had become attached to the beautiful Grand Classics and that he knew their contents were something more sublime than ordinary yoga mush pseudo-philosophy. After making a few disparaging remarks, which we took as affectionate expressions, he came to the question of price. My thoughts were organized enough to mention a price higher than usual, expecting an excruciating bargaining session.

“You crazy?!” shot back the seasoned lawyer. Then he spun off some quotes from book shops and a few more disparaging remarks. I stammered something about a donation for our projects. Meyer reached into his wallet, pulled out a wad of shekels, handed them to me, and said “*Lech* \[go\]!” The donation was sufficient for the books, and we obeyed Meyer’s imperative.

Now two years later, I was again thinking of Meyer Cohen. Would he be interested to have a set in English? Again, after much difficulty, I made an appointment. As I couldn’t carry all the English books on the bus, I decided to drive, and parked the car near his office. He had done some reading and was impressed. Meyer mentioned that Krsna says in *Bhagavad-gita* that He is the letter “A.” In Judaism “,” the first Hebrew letter, denotes God.

“What do you have today?” Meyer asked, sitting below a picture of himself standing with his rabbi. Also hanging in his office were pictures of Meyer’s ancestors in religious attire. I showed him a few volumes of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* set, Singapore edition. The cover pictures were beautiful, and the scholarly textual presentation, with the Sanskrit, word meanings, and transliterations, was like the Hebrew editions. Meyer wanted the books. He heard the price and knew it was fair, but he still said I was crazy. We came to an agreement on the set of thirty books. As I was leaving I mentioned another set of seventeen books.

“Why didn’t you bring them?!”

“They’re in the car, not far away.”

“*Nu* \[so\]? Don’t just stand there.” Lugging the *Caitanya-caritamrta* set up the steps with some other books that Meyer didn’t yet possess—*The* *Nectar of Devotion* and *The Science of Self-Realization*—I placed the volumes on his desk. He was fascinated by the pictures of Lord Caitanya’s pastimes. In his mind the books were already a part of his library, and soon the transaction was complete.

Meyer is a rarity among religious Jews, but he is not the only rarity. In 1986, devotees had also had favorable contact with Rabbi Lippel, a Hasidic Jew of the Bressler sect and founder-director of the Jerusalem Institute for Interreligious Affairs. Devotees taped a discussion between Rabbi Lippel and Danavir dasa. There are also stories of devotees in Israel receiving private donations—no one was allowed to witness—from rabbis who appreciated their activities.
