In Israel, rabbi means Orthodox or Hasidic. The Reform and Conservative movements are not very developed or much accepted by the Orthodox mainstream. Therefore, the Jewish population is, generally speaking, either religious—following as many *mitzvot* \[rules\] of the Torah as possible—or secular. Gradations are not as prominent as in the diaspora.

More recently, a Hasidic rabbi, Moshe Hirsch, a student of Rabbi Schneerson, today the most influential rabbi in the world, has resided at ISKCON centers for more than six months, following the Vaisnava rules and regulations and the schedule of temple programs, as well as daily chanting at least sixteen rounds of the Hare Krsna *maha-mantra*. Simultaneously, of course, Rabbi Moshe continues to observe Shabbat and other Halachic injunctions. During my visit to Israel in 1991, Rabbi Moshe, who had relocated to Jerusalem, chanted *japa* with me one morning on Tel Aviv beach. The rabbi said that Srila Prabhupada is a Lahmed Vahvnik, one of the thirty-six perfected souls who regularly appear in this world to help conditioned living entities revive their God consciousness.\*

Coming from the Jewish tradition, Rabbi Moshe would often refer to the following quote from the famous Jewish writer Martin Buber to support the personal conception of God: “The description of God as a Person is indispensable for everyone who like myself means by ‘God’ not a principle and like myself means by ‘God’ not an idea, but who rather means by ‘God’, as I do, Him who—whatever else He may be—enters into a direct relation with us men in creative, revealing, and redeeming acts, and thus makes it possible for us to enter into a direct relationship with Him. This ground and meaning of our existence constitutes a mutuality, arising again and again, such as can subsist only between persons. The concept of personal being is indeed completely incapable of declaring what God’s essential being is, but it is both permitted and necessary to say that God is also a Person.”

Generally, since the introduction of Krsna consciousness in Israel, effective strategy has necessitated that we avoid the Orthodox Jewish population. Once, in the early 1980s, when devotees attempted to do *harinama sankirtana* in Jerusalem, the religious Jews began to beat them. Fortunately the police intervened and helped the devotees. Persons such as Rabbi Moshe, Rabbi Lippel, and Meyer Cohen encourage us that open dialogue and friendly relations are a realistic possibility between the established religious majority in Israel and the practitioners of Krsna consciousness.

Krsna gives the world various religious systems according to the mentality of a particular population and time and circumstance. The essence of all bona fide scriptures is to help people advance to the stage of pure love of God. Therefore, although details may vary due to time and circumstance, all authoritative spiritual traditions have a common platform and universal goal.

*Bhakta Mahmood*

A frequent visitor to our Tel Aviv center, Bhakta Mahmood Masser, a Palestinian resident of Gaza, had been an important member of the Tel Aviv devotional community since early 1987. At that time, all the members of the Tel Aviv Gordon Street temple would go out in the early evening six days a week, distributing flyers and inviting people to the evening program. Each evening, devotees performed *bhajana* on Dizengoff Square, Israel’s hippest spot. After more than a year of daily chanting on Rehov Dizengoff, regulars on the Square would eagerly look forward to these evening appearances of the devotees.

One day I presented a flyer about vegetarianism and an invitation to our center to a mustached Arab gentleman, sitting and holding his bicycle. “Do you remember me?” he asked. Surprised, I strained my memory but I couldn’t place his face. He reminded me that we had met a few weeks previously when I had visited the house of our accountant in Ramat Aviv. Mahmood was cleaning the house, and he recognized my white robe and shaved head.

That night, Mahmood came to the evening program with his friend, Yosi, who worked at a Tel Aviv hotel. After a video, *bhajana,* and *Bhagavad-gita* class, we held *kirtana*. Mahmood and Yosi were the undisputed stars of the *kirtana,* bouncing from wall to wall with reckless abandon while clanging the newly discovered instrument, the *karatalas.*

Mahmood and Yosi began to attend our evening programs regularly, three or four evenings a week. A single copy of Ravanari dasa’s translation of the Arabic *Bhagavad-gita*, featuring a large hardback red cover with beautiful golden Arabic lettering, rested in the temple’s display case. Mahmood would remove the book and try to follow the *Gita* lecture, although to this day we’re not sure he could read. (I’m sure he was purified by trying.)

After some time, both of our Palestinian friends confessed that they saw no conflict between Krsna consciousness and their Islamic faith. They said they were constantly chanting Hare Krsna, Hare Krsna, Krsna Krsna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare at home, on the street and while working. In their Gaza village, though, they had to be careful, as the Muslim Brotherhood, a fundamentalist group, was active and influential. Mahmood and Yosi’s devotion manifested itself especially during *kirtana*, when their ecstatic dancing caused everyone present to transcend bodily designations.

Of course, we were concerned about having two residents of Gaza as regular visitors and emerging members, and we considered the effect on other guests. Each day, however, we saw the people of this troubled land come to the Hare Krsna center to forget the anxieties and political tribulations of a misdirected society based on illusory conceptions of the self and God. A typical evening might include young kibbutzniks from the Galil visiting Tel Aviv, a handful of American and Scandinavian teenagers staying at a nearby youth hostel, a couple of Palestinians, an Israeli Muslim or two, a middle-aged Jewish couple from Rehovot who had followed the *bhajana* party back to the temple from Dizengoff Square, and a few long-time, regular members of the Israeli *yatra,* such as Murari Caitanya dasa, Bhakta Meyer Artsi, Bhakta Adrian, and Mathura-mandala dasi. Everyone became spiritually surcharged by chanting Krsna’s names, hearing and discussing *Bhagavad-gita*, and enjoying *Krsna-prasadam* together. Everyone understood that political differences belonged on the other side of the door.

Mahmood continued to attend regularly, whereas Yosi, due to a change in his work schedule, could no longer visit the temple often. Sometimes Mahmood was restricted to Gaza for weeks at a time due to social unrest and a regional military confinement order. Upon returning, he usually would appear downtrodden and often angry or bitter. He related stories of the attacks and defenses of the Israeli military and the Palestinian resistance. After a few days of *kirtana* and *prasadam,* however, Mahmood would return to his blissful self, leaving vengeful thoughts aside.

Often Mahmood would bring homemade Arab sweets, such as *baklawah* and *basboosah,* prepared by his wife, to offer to Krsna. I don’t remember how many children he had, but the number seemed to increase by one a year. I recall his goal was to have a dozen.

In 1988, Mahmood was planning to attend the Mayapur-Vrndavana festival with the Tel Aviv devotees. He had the money, cleared the time, and was arranging for his travel papers when the Intifada uprising began in the final months of 1987. By December 1987, Palestinians found it difficult to travel, especially to politically controversial destinations such as India. Thus Mahmood couldn’t accompany us to the holy *dhamas.*

After a particularly serious and lengthy uprising and consequential detainment in Gaza during the summer of 1988, Mahmood returned angry and dejected. Some of his close family members had been critically injured, and soldiers had insulted his wife.

On the bright side, however, he related an enlivening story: He was about to be beaten by a few soldiers. Then he told them, “I’m a Hare Krsna!” They were astonished. He looked like a Palestinian. Then he explained to them that he couldn’t wear the distinctive Hare Krsna beads around his neck because that would cause the fundamentalists in Gaza to separate his head from his neck. “But in my home,” he dramatically explained, “I always chant Hare Krsna!” Then he delivered his best rendition of the *maha-mantra* for the squadron. Convinced that he was not a subversive Palestinian but merely an amusing Hare Krsna, they released him.

*Gargamuni in Tel Aviv*

Once, at the start of an evening program in Tel Aviv a few weeks after we returned from the 1988 Mayapur-Vrndavana festival, Gargamuni dasa entered the preaching center with his sister, her son, and his mother, Mrs. Scharf. They were all in Israel for the Bar-mitzvah of Gargamuni’s nephew.

On our flight to Italy on the way to India about two months previously, a pleasant woman, noticing our devotional dress, had asked if we were Hare Krsna devotees. She was surprised to hear that we had a center in Israel. “I’m Brahmananda’s sister,” she said proudly. We couldn’t believe it. This was Brahmananda’s sister? She lived in Rehovot, a large city south of Tel Aviv, and was married to an airline pilot. During the flight she told us about her association with Srila Prabhupada’s movement. She told us that her son was soon to be a Bar-mitzvah and that her brothers were planning to come to Israel for the occasion.

We were all honored to host one of Srila Prabhupada’s first disciples. During the bhajana, Mrs. Scharf, sitting in a chair, raised her hands like Lord Caitanya and rhythmically swayed back and forth, as Srila Prabhupada had taught. She shared with us feelingly her association with the “Swami.” After the evening’s festivities, Gargamuni stayed with us for several hours and shared intimately about ISKCON’s early days. Before leaving Israel, he attended two more evening programs in Tel Aviv, one of which was conducted by Jayadvaita Swami. The devotees relished the fortuitous meeting between the godbrothers.

## 2: Into the Mouth of Aghasura

*Sunday Evening, November 6, 1988*

In Haifa, the *matajis* continued to visit Druze villages and began to do contact *sankirtana* with white-collar workers, especially Muslim lawyers. The results were encouraging, as most took sets and were favorable toward Krsna consciousness. Many lawyers provided references, several of whom were available only in their villages. Thus a team of *brahmacarinis* headed by Mother Santasya began to follow up the leads in the Muslim villages. Their reports were reminiscent of the first days of Druze *sankirtana*, and the results were almost as high. Like the Druze, the Muslims were hospitable. There were, of course, differences. A Muslim would not generally accept the idea of transmigration or of a personal manifestation of the Supreme, and neither would he or she be inclined toward vegetarianism. Still, most Muslim intellectuals could appreciate Srila Prabhupada’s books as an authentic and scholarly presentation of a culture deserving to be studied. They also responded positively to the commonality of basic ideas, such as that God, the Supreme Controller, is great, all beings are spiritually equal, etc. This point of equality was especially relevant for Israeli Muslims, as they often felt like second-class citizens.

Aside from the differences between the Druze and the Muslims themselves, however, we realized that both our method of presenting the books and our style of village *sankirtana* needed to be adjusted. For example, walking aimlessly in the streets or sitting in a car in a village to rest or take *prasadam* was tantamount to asking for trouble. Many of these villages were politically agitated. To avoid or transcend politically sensitive issues with the common villagers, whom we encountered while distributing books or simply while walking from destination to destination in the village, was much more difficult than in Druze villages. Each *sankirtana* devotee had their own learning experiences in this regard. Still, somehow or other, Lord Caitanya’s workers in Israel were venturing into areas where Jewish bodies usually feared to tread. Sakhnin, Arabe, Taibe, Tira—these villages, although within Israel’s borders, were considered more or less off-limits to the Jewish population.

We heard about the Muslim phenomenon while we were in Tel Aviv. We brought out the map. Muslim villages far outnumbered Druze villages, and several of them were larger than even the most populated Druze villages. They seemed countless, and stretched as far south as the Tel Aviv suburbs. This didn’t include the West Bank or Negev villages. The first village we visited in central Israel was Baka (El Garbiyya), not far from Netanya.

Rolling into the village, we were immediately struck by the artistic Eastern architecture of two mosques, towering majestically over opposite ends of the village. Druze villages do not have mosques, because the Druze pray in humble structures called *khilwas.* Dividing up about a dozen names of lawyers, school principals, and teachers, Parama-guru and I went our separate ways.

After receiving directions to one of my referrals, I began walking. Although alcohol is strictly forbidden in Muslim culture, I saw a drunken man on the street, both disturbed and creating a disturbance. He noticed me and made a few antagonistic comments, but I paid no attention. My contact lived on the far side of the village. After walking for about ten minutes, a young, friendly Muslim offered me a ride in his van. Thanking him, I climbed into the back seat with my cart. My smile vanished when I saw that the drunk was sitting in the passenger seat. The driver then asked me what I was doing. I answered carefully, hoping to avoid any possible disturbance. He seemed satisfied, but then the passenger said something about Zionism, bottle still in hand. His voice increased in volume and his bearded face turned toward me. I remembered a verse from the Second Canto of *Srimad-Bhagavatam* (2.1.6): *janma-labha para pumsam, ante narayana-smrti.* “The highest perfection of human life is to remember the Personality of Godhead at the end of life.” Fortunately, the driver, sensing that I was apolitical, told him to leave me alone. Then he let me out of the van.

A bit shaken by my first experience in a Muslim village, I tried to find my contact. He wasn’t home. I searched for a few other referrals with no success. Walking back to the main street, someone directed me to the principal of a grade school. A genial man, the principal showed interest in the books for the school library. He couldn’t pay on the spot because the check had to be issued from the school during business hours. We made an arrangement for mail payment, and I left the set with him, after which he directed me to another school manager. The second manager invited me into his home and also purchased the books. Returning to the car, I met Parama-guru, who had also distributed two sets.

The next day, accompanied by Nitya-lila dasa, veteran Israeli devotee from Mexico, we found a village called Kalensawa. In the morning I went on *sankirtana* with Nitya-lila and we distributed three books to a school manager named Natoor, who had studied in the United States for a few years. In the afternoon, Nitya-lila and Parama-guru, visiting shops, distributed a few books, although it wasn’t easy. We all noticed posters for political rallies in the village. A few days later we heard from our Palestinian devotee friend Mahmood that Kalensawa was in occupied territory, meaning that it was politically agitated and potentially volatile, and that it was risky for us to have gone there.

Deciding to try villages closer to Tel Aviv, the next day we visited Tira. Driving to the village center, we began to discuss our plan. A few dazed teenagers soon approached us, one of whom mentioned something about hashish. Nitya-lila and I felt we should leave the village immediately. Parama-guru turned the ignition key and we headed for the nearby and larger village of Taibe, on the border of occupied territory. Still enthusiastic, we parked near the village entrance and went our separate ways.

A few steps from the car, a friendly young man approached me and expressed interest in the books. Standing on the sidewalk, I began to show him the *Bhagavad-gita*. Someone else came and listened. Then several others gathered. A young man from Janine, in the occupied territories, began distributing magazines. Being naive, I was shocked to see the cover and contents, full of bloodied Palestinian children. They had been injured by Israeli soldiers. It was a militant magazine designed to elicit sympathy from Israeli Muslims for the Palestinian cause. No one seemed particularly disturbed by me, but neither was anyone interested in discussing our philosophy. Nitya-lila had a similar experience. However, all three of us also had pleasant encounters with village leaders, including the mayor, and managed to sell them some books.

*Letters*

March 24, 1989—Maha-Laksmi dasi to Danavir dasa:

we met many nice, respectable and open-minded gentlemen, heads of schools, teachers, writers, lawyers, etc., who appreciate our philosophy and our movement and who say, “We wish that everyone were like you.”

We also got to preach to some Palestinians. We met one doctor who didn’t speak Hebrew and talked to him about the basics of our philosophy. He told us that it is all very nice but that they are a subjugated country and cannot appreciate it now. We told him the story of the first meeting between Srila Prabhupada and Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati. How Srila Prabhupada argued that “who will listen to your message. India first must become independent.” Bhaktisiddhanta replied that this message is so important that it cannot wait. It is beyond any temporary material situation.

Once Nagapatni and I met an Arab in Jerusalem University. He was saying so many bad things about Israeli government and how he wants to be considered Syrian. We preached to him that these are all nonsense bodily designations and that this political situation or that political situation will never bring anyone real happiness or peace of mind. So long as the heart is full of lust, anger, and greed, there is no question of satisfaction. He became somewhat ashamed and took a book.

Somehow or other, by the magic power of Lord Caitanya, these agitated and disturbed people are taking books.

But I feel that the political situation is coming to a boiling point. Nagapatni went to visit her kibbutz up in the north, and on the same day some terrorists were caught and killed in the next village. Two days ago an Arab worker from the West Bank got wild and started stabbing people in the street (this happened two streets away from the temple); one man was killed and a few injured.

April 15, 1989—Maha-Laksmi dasi (Haifa) to Danavir dasa:

\[A large portion of this letter appeared in the June 1989 “World Sankirtan Newsletter” as an article entitled “Entering Aghasura’s Mouth.”\]

I feel a great urge to tell you about a very ecstatic phenomena which occurred lately in the preaching field of Israel *yatra.*

It seems that Krsna is gradually removing “the curtains” and revealing to us newer and newer ways to preach and spread the mission of Lord Gauranga, showing us that actually *sankirtana* is unlimited.

A few days ago we had a program in a Muslim village called Kabul. I think it was an amazing program. It took place in the school of the village and was arranged through some doctor who had purchased a set from Santasya.

About fifty people attended, most of them respectable men in the village. Since it is Ramadan and all the Muslims are fasting till the evening for one month, they all came after breaking the fast and after attending their evening prayer in the mosque.

They asked many intelligent and challenging questions and got nice answers, heard a long *kirtana,* and took *prasadam* (these are very religious-minded Muslims). At the end of the lecture they came to shake our hands and gave us flowers.

The prediction of Lord Caitanya that the holy name of Krsna will spread to every town and village is coming true.

There is one Muslim town in Galilee called Uhm El Fah’m. After the recent local elections the religious party has become very prominent in this place, and actually the mayor is a very religious sheikh.

I remembered the story of the cowherd boys entering into Aghasura’s mouth as we drove inside this place. We had a name of some manager of a bank who received us in a very friendly way, although he was not interested in the books. But after spending some time with him, we begged him that he takes at least *Bhagavad-gita,* so that we can say that one person from Uhm El Fah’m took a book. He didn’t want to disappoint us.

I think the timing was perfect. They are all on a month-long fast, so they are less able to resist the devotees’ preaching. I also think that we benefitted from their shock. I don’t think Israeli Jewish feet step on this land, plus these Indian-looking strange Jews!

So next we were referred to some offices. We met a very nice man, who said that he would definitely take the books if they were in English (many of these Arabs go to study in foreign countries). He had the *Bhagavad-gita* at home and said that in his opinion, the Vedas are superior to the Koran! Next person was a dark-skinned man who was interested in *Mahabharata!*

We were a little disappointed because we thought that we are the “brave pioneers” to spread Krsna’s name in this remote place, but Krsna is already famous there! As they saw us people were saying, “Krsna, Krsna.”

We were referred to the manager of the high school, Mr. Jamil Mahajna (“Mahajana”). We went to his house and were invited in by his wife. He was resting, being weak from the fast. As we entered his living room we saw that he had a very big library with books about Judaism and Darwinism, etc., included. Finally he came in in his pajamas and started ceremoniously welcoming us in a British-Arabic funny accent. He was very excited to meet us, and we began a long conversation, or more precisely, he preached to us very strongly about Islam and how any religion besides Islam, Judaism, and Christianity are against God and some more nonsense. We didn’t argue with him but stressed the importance of unity between religions and their common goal, which is love of God. He was in ecstasy when he heard about the four regulative principles (except the one about meat). Then he became so excited he started going here and there, climbing on the chair, talking loudly, saying something, making himself laugh, then laughing uncontrollably. He completely forgot the weakness of the fast. It was such a scene!

He was completely confused because on one hand he blasphemed everything about us and on the other hand he blessed us many times and said he was attracted to our personalities. We gave him many compliments and explained the meaning of the word “*mahajana*.” Then we humbly asked him to give 200 shekels and take the books. He objected, saying that “I am against these books.” We said “But you also have Darwin on your shelf. He was a big ‘*kufr*’ (heathen).”

So he agreed, saying that he must take these books because “You special people came to my house.” Then his wife protested, “Haram (Abominable)!! We will be boycotted by everyone. It’s against the Koran. What are you doing, you crazy?!”

Fortunately, women’s lib movement is not prominent among religious Muslims, and he gave us the money and even more than we asked (to prove who’s boss). Then he showed us a volume of an encyclopedia he has, called “The Supernatural/ Knowledge from Afar.” It was a book about all sorts of Eastern religions and prac-tices, and to our great surprise, the first practice described and also exhibited on the front page was ISKCON, the International Society for Krishna Consciousness, showing pictures of devotees on *harinama* and Ratha-yatra and pictures of Srila Prabhupada. *Param vijayate sri krsna sankirtana!* And the *maha-mantra* was written there, and we all started having *kirtana,* chanting Hare Krsna and laughing like madmen. So this is very enlivening to the soul.

April 18, 1989—Dhira Govinda dasa to Danavir dasa:

So the field of Muslim preaching is really opening up. Practically every village we visit takes a set for their libraries and schools and many of the intellectuals also take sets. Last week we had a wonderful program at a small village called Kabul. About fifty intellectuals from the Muslim community attended and very much enjoyed and appreciated the philosophy, *prasadam,* and Vaisnava culture.

Our discovery of *sankirtana* in Muslim villages during the early months of 1989 corresponded to the month of Ramadan, when the Muslims fast during the day to increase their religious determination. Attendance at mosques and personal prayer sessions are increased during the month-long holiday.

Also, during this time, Salman Rushdie released *The Satanic Verses,* which caused serious agitation in the Muslim world. Various Muslim groups had reportedly vowed to kill Mr. Rushdie. As Rushdie was of Indian origin, there was a natural suspicion of Indian culture. When villagers saw us in their villages, they would often inquire “Rushdie?! Rushdie?!” wondering whether we were selling his book. Although they had heard abundant rumors about the book and its blasphemy, only a very few intellectuals had read or even seen it. One of these brahmins was Monsur Lutfi, principal of a primary school in Taibe. He took Parama-guru and I to his home one day, purchased a set, and showed us his copy of *The Satanic Verses*. “It’s really not that bad,” he told us in private, and went on to analyze the text in greater detail.

*Sankirtana Is Transcendental to All Material Influences*

Thus Israel and American Jews entered the Muslim villages to present traditional Indian literature during the peak of the Intifada Revolution, in the midst of the religiously fervent Ramadan, at a time when opposition to Western influence was increasing, and exactly at the outbreak of a reactionary response to Indian culture! Despite the odds, the Muslims purchased sets enthusiastically from the *sankirtana* devotees, proving the transcendental nature of Lord Caitanya’s *sankirtana* movement.

During this time, devotees often found themselves on the receiving end of inspired attempts to convert them to Islam. Once, in the village of Jaljulya, Parama-guru and I were speaking with a school manager and his friend. The friend became enlivened by the idea that I could become a very good Muslim. From our conversation, he concluded that I was already almost a Muslim; it was only a matter of incorporating a few rituals into my life. For more than an hour he explained about various Islamic practices, which I found interesting. The school manager, although reluctant at first, naturally took a set after such a discussion. Usually, after a brief interaction, our Muslim friends would be impressed by the essential similarity of Krsna consciousness to their religion, as well as by the sincerity of the devotees in their work for Allah. Also, we were continually impressed and ingratiated by the warm friendliness and receptivity of our Muslim friends.

Before long we felt comfortable with the nuances of Muslim village life, especially the *moazin* calling out the greatness of God from atop the minaret five times a day—*Allah akbar. Allah akbar. Allah akbar. Allah akbar. Ein rashad Mohammeder rasalallah. La illa ilala Allah akbar. Allah akbar,* his sonorous voice ringing in amplified echoes through the village and surrounding valleys to supplicate the mercy of the Supreme. We often reflected on the purifying effect this sound had on the village. No matter what people were doing, at least five times a day their thoughts were drawn toward Allah.

After distributing a few sets in a village, especially to highly respected persons such as the mayor or high school principal, it became easier to distribute to the rest of the village, because we were able to show how their leaders had accepted the book. Before entering a village, devotees would often be familiar with dozens of persons in the town who had taken books. Reciting their names and describing our experiences with these people would often become the real incentive for others to purchase sets of books.

Once I was struggling up a steep hill with my book cart in the Muslim village of Tamra. A police car was patroling the village, and the officers stopped and looked at me in disbelief. Both officers were Jewish. They asked for my ID, assured themselves that everything was all right, then asked what I was doing there. I showed them the books and explained my mission. They told me to leave. It wasn’t an authoritative command, however, because I wasn’t breaking any laws, but they were worried that I would be killed if I stayed. I appreciated their concern, but I explained that I routinely came to these villages to distribute books. They were truly shocked, wished me good luck, and drove away.

Toward evening of the same day, I was on one of Tamra’s small side streets trying to locate a contact. Just then, a man in his thirties holding a huge, bloody knife walked toward me. He was a butcher, as was evident from his bloodied white smock. He wasn’t too friendly and asked me what I was doing in the village. For a moment I became aware that I was standing alone in the village of Tamra, two inches away from a suspicious Muslim butcher holding a sharp knife dripping with blood. Although this sort of thing had practically become an everyday affair, I realized that one wrong word, expression, or tone of voice could make me headlines in tomorrow’s paper. Carefully, I gave the name of the school teacher I was trying to locate and the butcher gave me directions.

April 20, 1989—Danavir dasa to Santasya dasi:

Maha-Laksmi Prabhu told me how you have been selling sets of Srila Prabhupada’s books to the Muslim businessmen in their offices. This is delightful news. It reminds me of how much Ghanasyama dasa pleased Srila Prabhupada by distributing full sets behind the Iron Curtain with the library party. Prabhupada confessed that he never expected these people to take to our books so eagerly and thus continued to praise his disciples’ expertise in presenting the books.

Yes, the drug accusation episode turned out in our favor. Competent devotees are like skilled stock market traders who make a profit when the market prices are high by selling their shares. Devotees similarly glorify Krsna’s mission in popular or unpopular circumstances.

*Bedouins*

By May 1989, devotees had distributed more than two hundred sets in Muslim villages and most Muslim schools and libraries had full sets. Sometimes distribution in Muslim villages was as big as the best days in Druze villages, with devotees distributing more than sixty Maha-big books. Visiting Arab villages, devotees traveled from the Negev to the Galil, becoming acquainted with various Muslim groups—Sufis, a sect of Asian origin called Charcese, and the Bedouins.

Bedouin Muslims usually serve in the Israeli army. Like the Druze, they are considered invaluable to Israeli survival. Bedouins are especially known for tracking along borders, enabling the army to detect terrorist and inimical military activity. Bedouin means “nomadic tribe,” and Muslims respect these people for their austere, desert lifestyle. Mohammed the prophet was a Bedouin.

In Israel, Bedouins have gradually adjusted their lifestyle from nomadic to settled villages. The government has encouraged this. Villages such as Zarzir in the north and Rahat in the Negev fall into this category. Of course, as soon as they settle, they can no longer technically be called Bedouins, but they are still proud of their Bedouin heritage. Fewer Muslims than Druze are fluent in Hebrew, and among the Muslims, the Bedouins tend to be less academically developed than others since then have only recently been urbanized. Notwithstanding, the devotees still managed to find a few educated persons when visiting their villages, and these persons would purchase books and sets. All the Bedouins, whether able to take books or not, were hospitable.

In May 1989, Isvara Krsna, Parama-guru, and I went on traveling *sankirtana* in the Negev and visited Bedouin villages there. Rahat is the largest village in that area, with about 20,000 people. Also, we visited other “villages,” some of which were no more than the huts and tents of a few scattered families who had settled for the time being in the same general area of the desert. We visited several families in their dwellings, requiring the Delta (Romanian-model car) to drive away from anything that could be called a road, or even a path, into the expanse of rough, sandy terrain that is Northern Negev. Each Bedouin family was surprised to receive such visitors, but they all graciously welcomed us into their homes.

Our Negev outing reminded me of the days I had spent in Arad. Springing up in the middle of a practically barren wasteland, Arad is a rough, forty-five-minute drive from Beersheva in the Judean desert. An exemplary model of the Israeli “development town,” the small city is a monument to the nation’s engineering ingenuity. Machzor WUJS (The Institute of the World Union of Jewish Students) has its headquarters in Arad. While studying there in 1983–84, I would often take *japa* walks in the desert that surrounds every inch of the town. To my back stood paved streets, multi-level apartment buildings, Western-style villas, and basketball courts. Before me were hills of sand, lines of camels trekking across the dunes.

On one such walk I had met Hassan, a sixteen-year-old Bedouin boy and camel herder. He had asked what I was doing. We had showed each other the beads on which we each chanted the Lord’s names, he in Arabic and me in Sanskrit. I taught Hassan the Hare Krsna Maha Mantra and he developed an affinity for chanting Hare Krsna on beads and singing the mantra loudly. He would visit me in our small but comfortable dormitory. This had caused quite a stir among my roommates and the other Institute students. Hassan had seemed only an uncultivated savage in comparison to the Western Jewish students, but actually, he had been the only one interested in chanting the holy names of God.

Mount Tabor, its smooth rounded peak rising gracefully over the hills of the eastern Lower Galilee, is encircled at its foot by Bedouin and Muslim villages like Shiblie and Deboria. Across from the mountain’s northeastern face is the Jewish settlement Cfar Tabor, where *sankirtana* devotees have been successful distributing books over the years. Mount Tabor, eight kilometers from Nazareth, is the site of Jesus’s transfiguration, and a beautiful church on the mountaintop commemorates this sacred event.

Deboria, a village with more than five thousand residents, including many intellectuals, is the most notable among the Muslim villages on Mount Tabor. In 1989, we visited Deboria on several occasions, distributing more than twenty-five sets in the village. Especially memorable was the Masalchah family. Machmood Masalchah was the high school principal. I visited him at his home on a day when school was closed. He and his family treated me graciously. After more than an hour of stimulating philosophical discussion, he purchased the Hebrew set for the high school.

Isvara Krsna and I visited Achmed Masalchah, an affluent and influential Israeli lawyer. We heard he was about to be offered the position of judge, although he wasn’t sure he would accept it. Many educated and successful Muslims in Israel are in a delicate position. While they are appreciative of the Israeli nation and the facilities it provides them for education and economic advancement, they are simultaneously sympathetic to the plight of many of their Arab kinsmen living in the same land. Thus they have to be careful not to put themselves in compromising or dangerous positions.

We found Achmed to be perceptive, erudite, and humorous. He described himself as a faithful Muslim, and denounced the extreme elements within his religion and those outside who perpetrated the Muslim stereotype as “violent, ignorant terrorists.” Intrigued by the wisdom of the East, Achmed gave a generous donation for a Hebrew *Bhagavad-gita.*

On another occasion, Isvara Krsna and I visited a Muslim karate teacher in his home in Abu Snan. Before taking Srila Prabhupada’s books he criticized the Israeli school system. “They’re not going to destroy our culture with guns. They’re going to undermine us by turning our daughters into prostitutes.” He went on to describe how his pre-teen daughter was required to mix with boys and learn modern dance in school.

Favorable persons, both Druze and Muslim, began to request us to give lectures in their villages. Abdallah Saalami, a young shop teacher and contact of Maha-Laksmi, invited us to speak to a gathering of friends in his home in the Druze village of Hurfeish.

We drove to Hurfeish, where we met Abdallah and four friends. After a brief *kirtana,* we explained the basics of the philosophy and history of Krsna consciousness, after which we answered questions. Abdallah and a friend named Tourki were especially interested. Abdallah had dabbled for years in the meditation process of a famous impersonalist guru.

We explained the relationship between personalism and impersonalism, with special emphasis on the fallacy that the minute individual living entity is, or can become, equal in all respects to the Supreme Lord. As the idea that he was actually God starkly contradicted his Druze upbringing,, Abdallah was uncomfortable even to defend such a position.

This reminded me of an experience I had had recently in the Muslim village of Bina. In the teachers’ room of the high school I was talking to an older student who had been influenced by communist propaganda. Proud of his radical stance, he espoused atheistic philosophy, even managing to include terms like “proletariat.” Just then, a group of mostly women teachers entered. I continued to challenge his philosophy, asking him to explain how he could account for such a structured creation without reference to a higher intelligence. Suddenly, he could only smile bashfully, turning red in front of so many maternal figures. He would not dare have them hear him saying that Lenin and Marx were greater than Allah.

The Krsna conscious understanding of our position as eternal servants of the one Supreme God was easy for Druze, Muslims, and Christians to accommodate. Abdallah especially appreciated when we pointed out that his name meant “servant of God.” Tourki was a well-read intellectual with a cynically comic personality. He asked many questions. The authenticity of Krsna consciousness impressed him. Before leaving, we taught Abdallah and Tourki how to chant *japa.*

*Kabul*

Maha-Laksmi dasi: *I went with Santasya on the bus to Kabul, a Muslim village in the Galil. They couldn’t believe that two Israeli women were on this bus filled with Arabs going to Kabul. Achmed, the only vegetarian in Kabul, and maybe in that part of the country, happened to be on the bus. We had great discussions about God and vegetarianism and air pollution (while passing the huge smokestacks). When we arrived in the village, Achmed personally took us from house to house, practically forcing each* *family to take at least one book. At* *the* *end of the night, the final family—he made them take a full set.*

*There were no more buses back to the Hadar. Achmed and his brother drove us all the way back. We served them* prasadam *and taught them to chant* japa.

Santasya and Maha-Laksmi had also contacted a favorable doctor in Kabul, who arranged for the devotees to lecture in his village. All seven *sankirtana* devotees drove to the secluded village, located northeast of Shefaram. Village leaders reserved a large room in the high school for the program. After some delay, the doctor arrived and opened the classroom doors for us. We set up a book table, a *prasadam* table, and a table for the devotees to perform *kirtana* and speak (only male devotees would be on stage out of respect for Arab culture).

Guests filed in. Soon the perimeter of the room was filled with the village’s leading spiritual and intellectual personalities, including many teachers and a few sheikhs (with their distinctive headdresses). After a brief introductory talk about the importance of chanting the names of God and explaining the *maha-mantra,* we began *kirtana*. This captured their attention, although the children peering in from outside and making noise created a disturbance. The Muslims have their string of *masbachah* (chanting beads), on which they chant ninety-nine names of God in the Arabic language. Thus they appreciated the sublime importance of vibrating the name of the Supreme.

As the lecture began, translated from English to Hebrew, the audience managed to stop the children from disrupting the meeting and listened carefully. We spoke about the history of the *Vedas* and the *sankirtana* movement, yoga, regulative principles, and the central importance of God in all aspects of life. Then they asked several intelligent questions in a respectful though challenging mood. One man had heard about mystic powers available through the yoga system and he inquired about them. We stressed that, although it is possible to develop such powers, the point of life is to develop our love for God. Without this love, mystic feats have no significance and actually cause a disturbance. After about twenty minutes, when the audience’s questions were satisfied, the devotees served *prasadam* and mingled with the guests. Everyone enjoyed the evening.

Next, Maghar arranged a program for the devotees. Wahib Saad, a contact of Parama-guru and an insurance agent, teacher, and head of Maghar’s chapter of the Rotary Club, organized the program. Two other events, a Scouts meeting and the funeral of a prominent village member, were scheduled simultaneously. Therefore only about twenty people attended. However, those who attended, mostly school teachers who had already accepted books, were sincere and attentive. We followed the same schedule as the previous programs—*kirtana,* lecture and discussion, more *kirtana, prasadam.* The guests, many of whom were chanting softly on their *masbachah,* were appreciative that we had come to their village to offer genuine spirituality. After the program, Wahib arranged a formal presentation in which he presented the devotees a banner of Maghar’s Rotary Club.

One evening in Sakhnin after dark, a devoutly religious Muslim school teacher invited me to his home. Dressed in a long, grey robe, he listened while I explained the philosophy of *Bhagavad-gita.* Then he gave an extensive explanation about the Koran’s superiority to other scriptures, from the social, philosophical, and practical points of view. Specifically, he elaborated that other religions allow either too much or too little sense indulgence, causing the practitioner to become either spoiled or frustrated. The Koran, however, offered the perfect balance. To support the idea of balancing sense gratification and austerity, I quoted *Bhagavad-gita* 6.17 6.17 and gave the example that a horse needs to be controlled by reins, but they can’t be pulled too tight.

The teacher’s aim was not pedantic intellectualizing; he wanted to convince me to practice Islam. I offered that the meaning of “Muslim” is to be completely honest by submitting oneself to Allah. I had read that in one of Srila Prabhupada’s conversations. conversations. The process of Krsna consciousness, I said, was teaching me how to do that. The teacher was enlivened by the conversation.Then the time for evening prayer arrived, and he requested me to stay and observe. Fascinated, I watched as he performed the rituals of worship on his prayer rug. Afterward, I explained that I had to leave. To my surprise, he gave a generous donation for a Hebrew *Bhagavad-gita*.

Some people within the Muslim community began to adopt Krsna conscious practices. One day, while on *sankirtana* in the Vadi Ara, Parama-guru and I drove to a scenic wooded area for lunch.There we met Yosef, a resident of the nearby Muslim village of Arara. Yosef was bright. He spoke English and was interested in drama and spiritual life. We spoke with him for more than an hour and gave him some books. The next week he contacted us and wanted to visit our center. Soon after, he came to the Frishman Street center in Tel Aviv and spent an afternoon chanting Hare Krsna, discussing philosophy, and taking *prasadam*. Yosef contacted us after that and said he was chanting eight rounds a day and reading Srila Prabhupada’s books regularly.

Maha-Laksmi: *We were doing* sankirtana *in a primarily Arabic industrial section of Haifa. I entered the garage of a Muslim car mechanic. He was pious and attracted to the godly nature of the books, but then he saw the pictures and objected. “We do not worship form.” I noticed on a wall were all sorts of forms of women from magazines. I said, “You are worshipping so many forms,” and pointed to the wall. He became embarassed and bought some books.*

*Sheikh Amin*

On a visit to the small Druze village of Julis, I met Ali Amin, school teacher and grandson and personal secretary of Sheikh Tarif Amin. Ali was friendly and suggested that we might meet the Sheikh. A few weeks and a few phone calls later, we had an appointment with the esteemed leader of the Druze. Isvara Krsna, Parama-guru, and I drove to Julis. We arranged for a flower garland through a local villager, who was more than eager to help. The Sheikh’s house was in an alcove off a main village street, about halfway up a large hill rising in the direction of Yarka. The construction of Sheikh Amin’s home was similar to other village residences, although slightly larger than the average home. Unlike most other homes, however, Sheikh Amin had a small parking lot next to his house to facilitate guests. The elderly women sweeping the front porch with whisk brooms (one of the main activities of Druze women) directed us to the back. There, Ali led us through a small hallway and into a clean and spacious waiting room.

Druze symbols covered the walls. Red, white, blue, yellow, and green are the Druze colors, and these five colors appear together abundantly in various mediums in Druze households. Most common is the five-pointed star, each point a different color. There is also the popular picture of five differently colored riderless horses, posed at a vigorous gallop. We soon learned that these horses had profound significance for the Druze, representing among other things their five main prophets, headed by Schweib, the Biblical personality Jethro.

Several pictures of religious and political dignitaries, Israeli as well as international, posing with Sheikh Amin, also adorned the walls. To win favor with the Druze, politicians from Jimmy Carter to Menachem Begin visited Sheikh Tarif Amin.

Ali brought us a huge bowl filled with a variety of fruits and nuts. We had also prepared a few *prasadam* sweets for the occasion. After discussing briefly with Ali and his brother Sheikh Mwafak, Ali received a signal and invited us to meet Sheikh Amin.

Sheikh Amin appeared regal in his freshly pressed robes, tall, spherical white cap, and full white beard. A few others, either relatives or close friends, were present in the large sitting room. Isvara Krsna made a motion to garland the Sheikh, but it was not practical due to the large turban. Ali took the garland and hung it on the window handle behind the Sheikh’s couch. Isvara Krsna and I sat on chairs opposite the Sheikh, while Parama-guru stood in back with a camera.

Ali introduced us to the Sheikh. Sheikh Amin’s visual and auditory faculties were obviously impaired, though his mind seemed sharp. After exchanging greetings, we discussed briefly the Druze relationship with the Hare Krsna movement. The Sheikh was pleased to hear about our book distribution in the Druze villages. As none of us had a specific agenda, we ended the introductory meeting after about twenty minutes. Before leaving, we posed with the Sheikh for a few photographs. Isvara Krsna tried to sit at the Sheikh’s feet, but he would not allow it. Sitting on either side of him, we took photos. Before leaving his home, Ali told us that Sheikh Amin had enjoyed the meeting and appreciated receiving the *Bhagavad-gita,* although he couldn’t read Hebrew.

Santasya had established contact with Dr. Achmad Foad Aslan, Egypt’s cultural attachī to Israel. He took a *Bhagavad-gita,* and she arranged another meeting with him at the Egyptian embassy in Tel Aviv.

Isvara Krsna, Santasya, and I entered the embassy with a plate of *prasadam* sweets to meet Dr. Aslan. He was a doctor of philosophy and discussed *Bhagavad-gita* with us keenly for more than an hour. We also spoke about the possibilities of presenting Krsna consciousness in Egypt.

*Jerusalem*

Since the early 1980s, when religious Jews attacked devotees who were doing *harinama sankirtana* in Jerusalem, preaching attempts in the holy city remained undercover. Now, with the development of Arab *sankirtana,* devotees could go to many sections of Jerusalem with books and devotional clothes. Indeed, in all Arab areas, devotional dress was the only sensible choice; other garb would identify devotees as Jewish, Israeli, or American, poassibly inviting attack.

Jerusalem’s Old City is comprised of the Jewish, Muslim, Christian, and Armenian quarters. On one visit to the Old City, Isvara Krsna and I spent most of our day in the Armenian quarter. We had recently obtained some Armenian books (*Sri Isopanishad* and *Coming Back*). We found the Armenians friendly, and almost all of those whom we approached gave us a donation for a book. In one office, a headquarters for Armenian nationality, the director said proudly that the Armenians were the first nation to adopt Christianity. He thought this was spiritually beneficial, of course, but from a nationalistic point of view, he thought their cause had been damaged because before Christianity had become popular and other countries had dissident views, other nations had fought and weakened the Armenian nation.

I also spent time in the Christian quarter. As in other Israeli villages and cities, the Christians were pious and amiable. In Jerusalem, however, their economic condition seemed much worse. They explained that most of their funds were, by choice or otherwise, channeled into the revolutionary cause.

For me it had been more than five years since I had spent an extended period in the Old City. During the winter of 1983–84, just after I had adopted the principles of Krsna consciousness, I had studied and resided for a few weeks at Esh Ha Torah, a yeshiva situated across from Ha Kotel (the Wailing Wall). Memories of wandering the Jewish Quarter’s narrow streets while chanting *japa* crossed my mind as I rolled the book cart through Jaffa Gate.

*Rama Preaching Program*

Before leaving for a visit to the United States at the end of May, I arranged for a program in Rama. Salach, with Kamil Schadeh and Maher Faraj, two friends interested in yoga and Krsna consciousness, arranged for a hall in Rama’s high school on a Saturday evening. The program coincided with a visit from Jayadvaita Swami to Israel.

*Story by Santasya Dasi*

Taking advantage of His Holiness Jayadvaita Swami’s visit to Israel, the Israeli devotees arranged to do a program in the predominantly Druze village called Rama. Over the past year, the Druze of Israel have taken and read sets of Srila Prabhupada’s books with great enthusiasm. At the center of the Druze religious philosophy is the concept of reincarnation. Also, their culture contains many evidences of Vedic influence, and one of their recent spiritual-political leaders, Kamal Jumbalat, did extensive research into Sanskrit literature, such as *Bhagavad-gita.*

The weather was sunny and hot as the devotees made their way to Rama village in the high hills of northern Israel. As the cars arrived in front of the school where the program was to be held, curious and excited village children swarmed the devotees. The audience consisted of about twenty men, most of whom had already received a set of Srila Prabhupada’s books (the Hebrew set consists of *Bhagavad-gita*, the First and Second Cantos of *Srimad-Bhagavatam, Krsna* book, and two parts of the *Caitanya-caritamrta’s Adi-lila*). The village sheikh, Farhoud Kasem was the guest of honor. He was a respected Druze religious leader. Shiekh Farhoud Kasem opened the program by speaking words of appreciation for Krsna consciousness and referring to His Holiness Jayadvaita Swami as “a holy man.” Jayadvaita Swami’s lecture was then translated into Arabic, especially for the Sheikh.

After receiving the Sheikh’s blessings and permission, the devotees performed *kirtana*—to the Druze’s delight. Following are some excerpts from Jayadvaita Swami’s lecture and some of the questions that arose:

**Jayadvaita Swami:** The body has a beginning and an end, but the consciousness within the body is eternally existing. When I leave this body I go on to the next body according to my work in this lifetime....?So many students are in school; some, according to their work, will go on to the university and some will get ordinary work

Everything is taking place by the Supreme Lord’s sanction. Without His sanction, we cannot lift even a finger.

**Question:** Is it possible to explain life without referring to consciousness?

**Jayadvaita Swami:** Suppose we brought a dead body here and asked him to prove that he was conscious. He wouldn’t be able to offer any argument, despite the fact that all the material elements are present. Until you can generate symptoms of life in a dead body, your claim is useless. I may say, “I am a very rich man,” but if I can’t show you even one shekel, what good is my claim? Until life can be produced from dead matter, we can accept that the body is growing on the basis of consciousness.

**Question:** Why did Bhisma fight with Krsna if Krsna is his worshipable Lord?

**Jayadvaita Swami:** Oh, they’re so advanced in reading the books. Well, this is one of the *rasas,* loving relationships, that a devotee can have with the Lord. In a chivalrous relationship, Krsna fights with some of his devotees in the material world.

After the question and answer session, the audience was invited to honor *prasadam* and talk with devotees on an individual basis. Many Druze purchased more books. Shortly after the program, the devotees received a letter from a young man in Rama, which reads as follows:

Hare Krsna, Hare Krsna, Krsna Krsna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. To the dear people of the Hare Krsna movement, I was so much touched and impressed by your program in our village. It was a sweet and beautiful experience that awakened in me a consciousness of the Supreme Lord in his full personal presence. I beg you to please send me a *masbachah* (*japa* beads)...? Hope this finds you in good health. With love, Kamil.

The letter was received with a donation.

## 3: Return to the Galil

*June–December, 1989*

While visiting the United States in June 1989, we discovered fifteen copies of the Arabic *Bhagavad-gita As It Is,* translated by Ravanari dasa in the early 1970s, in ISKCON’s New York temple. We purchased the books and took them to Israel.

The Druze were wide-eyed to see the *Bhagavad-gita* in Arabic. This opened the doors to a large segment of the population to which we had previously had little access. Up until that time, devotees had distributed books and sets to families who didn’t read Hebrew on the basis that their children would probably learn Hebrew in the future and that the Grand Classics Encyclopedia would be a great spiritual and cultural asset for their home. Still, most of the Druze and Muslim families did not have the opportunity to read Srila Prabhupada’s books until this time.

One hot August day, while walking near the top of Osafia, close to where we would be living in less than a year, I saw an old, simple-looking Druze sheikh sitting in his *sharwahl* in front of his house, feeding his goat. We smiled at one another and exchanged greetings. He had seen devotees in his village a few times, and heard that they distributed literature about the Muwahhidoon. Because he neither spoke nor understood Hebrew, there was no opportunity for him to converse with the devotees. I approached the sheikh and handed him an Arabic *Bhagavad-gita*. He was immediately impressed by the large red cover with its beautiful, gold Arabic lettering. I couldn’t understand the script and he couldn’t understand me, so I guided him to turn to the “changing bodies” picture. He read the caption, then paged through the book.

“*Ka desh ha oh?*” (How much does this cost?) This was one of the few Arabic phrases I had learned over the year.

“*Tabaro,*” (“donation”) I replied. He pulled out a fifty shekel bill and wanted to know if it was sufficient. Indicating that it was, I expressed my gratitude and strongly encouraged the smiling sheikh to read the book. He began before I had even left his patio.

A few houses beyond the sheikh’s I knocked on the door of Salim Zedan, an English teacher who had already organized a program, held earlier in the year in Daliyat, for his students to hear about Krsna consciousness. Salim had met Parama-guru and appreciated the bold preaching mood of the Hare Krsna devotees, as he himself was a prominent activist in the Druze community. Salim invited me into his home. Although we didn’t know it at the time, devotees would soon be residing in the downstairs of Salim’s house, which now consisted only of air and a few cement support poles. Situated atop the Carmel Mountain at the edge of Osafia, the location provided a gorgeous view of the surroundings. About three kilometers to the southeast, across the rolling hills of the mountain range, Daliyat El Carmel, Israel’s largest Druze village, spread along the countryside. After some discussion about arranging a program in Osafia, Salim donated fifty shekels for an Arabic *Gita.*

In this way, we quickly sold the few Arabic *Gitas.* We had been lamenting for months that we lacked Arabic literature to distribute, and now that we had a taste of such distribution, we didn’t want to stop. A few faxes here and there and a quick call from Tel Aviv to Berkeley, California, where Harivilasa dasa, the Arabic BBT trustee, resided, and we had permission to reprint the Arabic *Bhagavad-gita*. Within a short time, the BBT printed 2,000 copies of the unabridged *Gita* in a more compact edition, with Krsna and Arjuna on a pop-band cover and sixteen color pictures.

In October 1989, Maha-Laksmi and I relocated to the Rehov Yavneh apartment in Haifa, intending to distribute the newly printed *Gitas.* We resumed the *sankirtana* schedule of going to a village in the morning and returning by early evening. All villages, Druze, Muslim, and Christian, were fresh to receive the Arabic *Gita*. Although we would mostly distribute the Arabic book, we would regularly find people interested in the Hebrew set too. Now we were going deeper into the Druze villages than before. With Arabic literature we could approach everyone, not just the village intellectuals. Mostly we stayed on the western side of the country, visiting closer villages such as Daliyat, Osafia, Julis, Yarka, and Abu Snan, to minimize travel time.

During this period we became increasingly intimate with the Druze, especially with several sheikhs, who gradually revealed to us more secrets of the Muwahhidoon.

*Letters*

November 3, 1989—Dhira Govinda dasa (Haifa) to Danavir dasa and Kirtiraja dasa:

I’m so much absorbed in *maya,* but after about half an hour of *sankirtana* in a Druze village my faith in Krsna and enthusiasm for preaching is sparked. Every day is an amazing adventure, meeting at least several incredible people who are worth telling their story.

Today was a “typical” day, and since I’m writing a letter anyway, I thought to share with you some of the highlights.

The Israeli authorities took away my driving license for the time being, so Maha-Laksmi does all the driving. But today she could not go out because of health reasons, so I took a bus to the closest Druze village, called Osafia. After wandering to a few houses and finding no adult at home, I saw a man at the top of a long flight of steps. He came down the steps and invited me in (for coffee, which I didn’t take of course). He was a simple Druze, named Wahib Sandid, who works at the Haifa port. With only Hebrew books, we would not be able to approach such a person, except for maybe a small symbolic donation. But he was very enthusiastic to see the Arabic Bg. When I talk about the ancient wisdom of the *Gita,* he’s interested. When I show the picture of reincarnation, he’s a bit shocked. (How did I learn the secret?)

I casually talk about how the ancient Greeks, such as Socrates and Aristotle, were familiar with the ancient *Vedas* (I say “casually,” because I know that Plato, Socrates, and Aristotle are considered by the Druze to be God-sent prophets). He’s now completely absorbed. His wife’s interest has also been piqued. She is a traditional Druze woman, almost no trace of Westernization in her dress or behavior. Speaks almost no Hebrew. I talk a little about yoga, spiritual equality, and then they become completely devastated upon hearing that Kamal Jumbalat read the *Gita* and said it’s the most important book in the world. To support this statement I pull out a page from an Arabic book by Jumbalat wherein he directly glorifies the *Bhagavad-gita.* This leads to the presentation of the preaching kit, with me and Sheikh Amin, Salman Falach, etc. The wife excitedly asks if it’s true that there are Druze in India. They both agree to buy the book (I simultaneously feel bad I don’t have an Arabic set of twenty books. They are nicely situated financially and could easily donate a few hundred dollars for books).

Then I face a recurring problem in Druze villages: it’s time to go on to distribute more books, but I’m attracted to staying and talking with the nice person who is interested to find out more about Krsna consciousness. I don’t resist the temptation. I stay. Wahib tells me that as a Druze, he doesn’t smoke or drink, and it’s forbidden to look at another woman other than his wife. I explain our regulative principles. Although Wahib has not adopted the traditional robes of a sheikh, he lives practically like a Druze sheikh. He points out my dress (*dhoti, kurta,* and shaved head) and says, “This is good.” He goes on to describe how the degraded people of today are going naked. But he suggests that I should grow a mustache. I give him and his wife some *prasadam* sweets to taste. I leave amid profuse thanks and sincere offers that I should come to their home whenever I’m in the village and they’ll read the book and if I have more books in Arabic, please bring, they will buy. I’m not exaggerating when I say this is a “typical” meeting. It’s impossible not to become enlivened by these people.

I wander around the village a little more. A teenage boy lets me in the house. He’s there with his mother. I explain about the *Bg*. I show the preaching kit. Socrates, Jumbalat, Sheikh Amin. The effect is there. The boy shyly asks, “Are you a Druze?”

“Depends how you define Druze,” I answer. They have no money. The mother asks me to please return at 4:00 when her husband, the lawyer, returns from Haifa.

Then I meet Sharoof Yioone, a Druze policeman (this is their favorite profession). He takes the Arab *Bg*. without a second thought. Then I sit down with three somewhat Westernized Druze in their mid-twenties. They’re all impressed and admit it’s interesting. They take some small books in Hebrew.

I climb up and down the mountain village for a while. An intellectual-looking man invites me in. He has an impressive library. He heard about the books and is impressed, as all Druze are. He says he’s really not interested in any more books, though, but out of kindness, he’ll give forty shekel for the *Gita.*

At the next house I meet a sheikh and his two sons, all dressed in Druze religious dress. After doing the basic presentation, he has all appreciation but is simultaneously feeling a bit threatened (he admits it). It’s like it was an ordinary day for him, then out of the mystic blue comes something incomprehensible and perfect. He can feel how it all fits together perfectly—reincarnation, Sheik Amin approving, Jumbalat, etc.—but he just wasn’t prepared for such a thing. Then just for fun I pull out the five horses—in the *Bg*. there’s a painting of the five horses representing the five senses. Somehow Krsna arranged that the Druze have a deeply hidden secret connected with five horses of exactly the same varied colors depicted in the *Bg.*—each color representing a different prophet.

I don’t directly let on that I know these things, but I just turn to the page and describe how there are five horses of different colors, etc. Anyhow, this was too much for the simple sheikh. He became nervous and starts to sweat. (How did the five horses get into this book that this youngster with the American accent is showing him?) I discussed for some time with the sheikh different philosophical issues. He gave a small donation; I gave a small book in return. He admitted that he’s afraid of what he might find in the big book. Later in the day, I run into him on the street; he is warm and friendly toward me.

Then I met family Rooken Labib Aboorooken. He works in insurance in Haifa. His wife is traditional, with a sharp intelligence. All the kids gather around. Everyone likes the *prasadam* sweets. The Arab *Bg.* is a sure thing. He wants the Hebrew set also, but finances are a group decision. They take *Krsna* and *SB* First Canto in Hebrew, and the Arab *Bg.* Again I can’t resist staying. The wife looks at me intensely and says, “It’s so much like our Druze religion.” They bring out an Arabic book about different religions with Mahatma Gandhi on the cover. In the book, the *Bhagavad-gita* and *Vedanta-sutra* are mentioned. She says she’s wanted to read these books for a long time. As the husband is the only one in the family who reads Hebrew, they didn’t take the whole set. But anything we have in Arabic, please come. He expresses gratitude and desires further contact, promising to read the books.

I call Maha-Laksmi and tell her to come, as I’m almost out of books. It’s about 3:30. ML arrives around 4:05. We go to the lawyer whose family I visited during the day. He’s hosting many people. It’s the middle of a meal, but we’re invited in. They gradually come out of dining. There’s an old, robust sheikh, a Muslim chanting Masbachah (*japa* beads), a Druze in army uniform, and several women and children. It’s a party atmosphere. We bring out the sweets and the books in Arabic, Hebrew, and also the *SB* set in English, which we keep in the car. ML has nice conversation with a woman high school teacher. The lawyer asks me briefly about the books, then after some time gives me twenty shekels as a donation, saying he doesn’t want any books. I give him *Prabhupada* and *Isopanisad* in Hebrew, which he graciously accepts. He couldn’t devote his full attention at this time, but he’s very favorable.

It’s already dark. We take the car to a market square. Several Druze come out of the office of the headquarters of a political party. They crowd around. Standing in back of the car, I explain about the books to a crowd—reincarnation, yoga, Jumbalat, etc. Many are interested and could take, but no one quite has the courage in the crowd to break the ice this time. There’s a big sheikh (in status and body) standing there. He’s keeping his cool, not showing much impression. But when I delivered the bit about the five horses, ML said he almost fell down. He grabbed the book and started looking through it like a child.

A woman storekeeper gives five shekels for *Coming Back* in Hebrew. A store owner to whom I gave a Hebrew set last year passes by in his car. He’s excited to see me. I show him the Arabic book. He says he wants it but he has no money on the spot, but definitely next time I’m in Osafia. He tells me he’s read all six books in the set. I’m so happy.

These people are reading. The books are not collecting dust. When I’m with Bhakta Salach, I begin to quote verses (in Sanskrit) and he gives me the translation before I finish the verse. So many have expressed great appreciation after reading the books. I feel bad we’re not set up to take them to the next level. I thought to start an Arabic newsletter to unite these people. We could also establish a life membership program, with occasional fund-raising dinners for projects like Arabic book printing. I’m confident these people would respond really well if we organized something. You wrote in a previous letter how Lord Caitanya seems to have plans for converting a whole religion to Krsna consciousness. I can see it happening. Their tendency, it seems, is that if something is great, they decide it must be Druze. We’ve even heard about the Buddha being a Druze (not confirmed yet).

Gradually, with mass book distribution, I think Srila Prabhupada will be seen as a great Druze messiah of that Supreme Original Druze, Sri Krsna. (We’ve already heard from a few Druze sources that the original literature appeared 7000 years ago in the Sanskrit language—but mum’s the word.) Bhakta Salach says that Druze history goes back many millions of years—and includes descriptions of incarnations of the Personality of Godhead. (But don’t make pictures, or else the Muslims will smash your head.) I’m sure that the books that are already out are causing quite a stir in their communities. But as of yet, no significant negative reaction. I think if anyone’s interested in Arab preaching, they should know what’s going on here. Hare Krsna.

Maha-Laksmi dasi to Danavir dasa:

As we go on *sankirtana* Krsna opens the doors more and more. We just came back from *sankirtana* and I have some nice stories to tell you. We went today to “Judeida,” which is a mixed Christian and Muslim village and is considered to be somewhat politically agitated. But one thing we found is that these politically minded people love the devotees and read our books. We met Farid Aquawi, who sat in jail for five years because of his Palestinian point of view (and we don’t know, maybe some actual misbehavior). Now he is a student of philosophy, working on his second degree. He had purchased a six-book set from Santasya about six months ago and had read the *Bg.* He said that he was reading about the battle of Kuruksetra between the Kauravas and Pandavas. (It is amazing; we have so much experience that many of the intellectual Arabs who purchase our books actually read and appreciate them.) Here is a man whom the Israeli police consider a threat to society, a most dangerous man, and we were having a long philosophical discussion with him. He happily purchased the Third Canto and *Bhagavad-gita* in Arabic, and he invited us to come whenever we had more books

Then in the morning we went to the village’s high school and showed the books in the teachers’ room. One of the teachers was observing the *Gita* in Arabic very thoroughly. Then he said, “I have something against this book.” We thought, “Oh, no, now he’s gonna say it’s against the Koran, etc.” But his only objection was that the Arabic book didn’t have the Sanskrit, like in the Hebrew books which he had purchased a few months ago; he was already reading and trying to learn the Sanskrit language

Then we met a teacher of biology who told us that he firmly accepted Darwin’s theory. He didn’t know who he’s dealing with Dhira Govinda Prabhu gave an excellent speech, which smashed Darwin’s theory into a “primordial soup” The man complimented him and took a *Gita.* He said that he had spoken with religious people who were against this theory, but they didn’t have any actual knowledge about how to defeat it. This was the first time he had met someone religious who could defeat it scientifically with great logic and arguments

There is a very favorable article printed in a national Arabic newspaper about our movement with a picture of our wedding. They are listing the four regulative principles and saying that we are “good people and lovers of peace.”

October 28, 1989—Maha-Laksmi dasi to Danavir dasa:

P. S.—There is a very nice story which I would like to tell you. A few days ago we met a Druze who told us that in his previous life, he remembers being a yogi in the Himalayas and that he feels love for Krsna deep inside him. He said, “As much as you love Krsna, I love Him deep inside me more than you.”

Of course, we didn’t accept every story of past-life remembrance that we heard from the Druze. In fact, we suspect that most were unknowingly fabricated, usually from early childhood, with the support of the social, family, and religious structure. There were, however, many cases that seemed undeniable. Also, empirical studies dealing with reincarnation have studied the Druze and discovered verifiable cases of past-life remembrance. From a Vedic viewpoint, it is reasonable that someone attached to a particular race, as the Druze tend to be to theirs, would take birth in the same race again. Also, it can be argued that in a group where reincarnation and remembrance of one’s previous life is highly valued, a relatively greater percentage of those who take rebirth in that group would remember their past lives, as the concept of remembrance may have been part of their consciousness while departing from the body. As Sri Krsna describes in the *Bhagavad-gita* (8.6):

*yam yam vapi smaran bhavam*  
*tyajaty ante kalevaram*  
*tam tam evaiti kaunteya*  
*sada tad-bhava-bhavita*

“Whatever state of being one remembers when he quits his body, O son of Kunti, that state he will attain without fail.”

In our discussions with Druze, we found that those who died suddenly, such as in a car accident or from a bullet, were more inclined to remember their past lives.

*Al-Hakim Bi-Amr Allah and Druze Theology*

Some sheikhs spoke about Al-Hakim Bi-Amr Allah. He’s described as having possessed a splendid countenance and a commanding voice. He always worked to establish a universal religion. Sheikhs warned of malicious accounts of his character and activities. After reigning as a king for several years, he renounced all external opulence, although maintaining his sovereignty. He ate sparingly and ordered that no one should bow before him or kiss his hand. One day, he didn’t return from a desert trip. No evidence of his body was found. Academicians say political enemies killed him. Many sheikhs say he went to the border of India to meditate. In any case, the Muwahhidoon are sure that Al-Hakim, the manifestation of the Universal Intelligence, was not killed. He disappeared according to His will. After his disappearance, his royal successor, Ali-al-Zahir, persecuted and killed many of his followers, although he promised Al-Hakim that the Muwahhidoon would be treated charitably.

Al-Hakim’s four predecessor kings, who were also his blood ancestors, were also manifestations of the Deity. Five is a very important number for the Druze. Their Panca-tattva consists of the Universal Intelligence, the Universal Soul, the Divine Word, the Archetype Antecedent, and the Archetype Follower. According to the Druze sheikhs, although the Deity cannot be perceived by human senses, He appears to us in human form so that we can approach Him. Though adopting human form, He remains infinite, incomprehensible, and inaccessible to mundane senses.

Druze theology and ontology is complicated. They describe elaborate systems of prophets, sub-prophets, and divine manifestations, often referring to Aristotle, Pythagoras, Hermes, Jesus, Buddha, Mohammed, Elijah, and Nabi Schweib. In Israel there are many shrines for Druze prophets, the most prominent being Nabi Schweib’s tomb near Tiberias.

Meanwhile, in Rama, Salach was advancing quickly. He had begun chanting sixteen rounds a day and following the four regulative principles. As he was already vegetarian, the only activity he had to endeavor to restrict was his coffee drinking, at which he was becoming successful. In addition, he expressed interest in selling books in Rama. We left him a package of twelve Arabic *Bhagavad-gitas,* which he sold at forty shekels each, keeping none of the money for himself. Also, he arranged another program for us in Rama.

Salach scheduled this program in a big hall next to the *khalwa,* the Druze place of worship. Afterward, we heard that some of Rama’s sheikhs who were not yet familiar with Krsna consciousness had objected to our lecturing in the *khalwa* building. Maha-Laksmi and I picked up Bhakta David Harzion, who originally introduced Salach, Kamil, and Maher to Krsna consciousness, at his apartment in Karmiel. By 6:00 p.m., more than fifty Druze, including many intellectuals and sheikhs, filled the hall. After about fifteen minutes of *kirtana,* I spoke from the *Bhagavad-gita,* describing the relationship between personalism and impersonalism and the importance of accepting a spiritual teacher. Maha-Laksmi translated the lecture into Hebrew, and Kamil translated it into Arabic.

After this program I resolved to lecture in Hebrew, difficult as it was for me, to avoid the double translation. After serving *prasadam* to all the guests, we held a *japa* session, with more than a dozen Druze *bhaktas* participating.

Then Salach invited us to his home (his parents’ home, actually). Whenever we visited Salach, his elderly mother was faultlessly hospitable despite her failing health. His father, who spoke some English due to having served in the British army when the British controlled Palestine, appreciated and welcomed devotees whenever they visited. Salach had many brothers, all of whom treated the devotees kindly and sometimes inquired about Krsna consciousness.

We talked with Salach for more than an hour, explaining various practical and philosophical aspects of Vaisnava life. He was beginning to seriously grasp the import of Krsna consciousness, and expressed an appreciation for Vaisnavas from foreign lands who had recently visited his village of Rama, namely Jayadvaita Swami and Kirtiraja dasa.

*Letter:* Sankirtana *Story—“*Krsna Appears in Rama,*” Nov. 1, 1989*

About one year ago, in November of 1988, Parama-guru dasa Brahmacari and I drove to an Arab village called Rama. This was the second day we were making the experiment of doing *sankirtana* in these Arab villages. We weren’t sure what to expect. After timidly approaching a few shop owners, one young man suggested to me, “Go to my brother, Salach. He’ll be interested in these things.” I trudged up toward the top of the mountain village, dragging a cart full of books. Salach was at home, and his face revealed emotions of surprise and delight upon seeing me, as I was dressed in *dhoti* and *kurta.* (This is the safest dress in Israel, as it doesn’t identify the devotees with any political faction.)
