We exchanged greetings and sat in his home. He put a cassette in a tape recorder and pushed the “play” button. I was surprised to hear the sound of Srila Prabhupada chanting *Gosvamy-astaka*. Salach had had some contact with a friend of ours in a nearby town and had received some small books and tapes. He purchased the Hebrew set of six Maha-big books, and went on to relate to me a dream he had. Salach dreamed that Srila Prabhupada was sitting in an abandoned house in the village of Rama, and the residents of the village were coming and worshiping him.

Now, one year later, Bhakta Salach is chanting a minimum of sixteen rounds a day, following the four regulative principles, and successfully distributing the newly printed edition of the Arabic *Bhagavad-gita As It Is* in his village. All glories to Sri Krsna *sankirtana!*

*Yada Yada Hi Dharmasya*

Kababir is at the foot of the affluent Carmel section of Haifa. Like the Vadi Nisnas*,* it is an Arabic village within Haifa. Contrasting the plurality of Arabic sects and disunified demography of the Vadi, however, more than ninety percent of Kababir’s population is Achmadiyya Muslim. The Achmadiyyas believe that after the prophet Mohammed, another prophet appeared to fulfill the Koran’s prophesies. This was Achmed, who appeared about one hundred years ago. The Achmadiyyas, like the Druze, have been severely persecuted within the Muslim world for their highly unorthodox beliefs. In Israel they have developed favorable relations with the government, which has given permission and funding for the Achmadiyyas to construct a beautiful mosque with twin minarets. That mosque now towers majestically above their community. The minarets overlook the Mediterranean Sea from near the top of the Carmel mountain. Driving north on Tel Aviv–Haifa Road, the Achmadiyyas’ architectural salute to the Supreme Being is the dominant edifice as one approaches Haifa. We’ve heard that this has caused serious consternation among some sectors of the dominant religious community in Israel. Unlike *moazins* in other villages, however, the Achmadiyya cantors are restricted by Haifa ordinances as to how loudly they are permitted to broadcast the greatness of God five times a day.

After more than half a year of planning to visit Kababir, we finally arranged a day to do so. Turning off from Herzl Street, the Carmel’s main thoroughfare, we drove through a small Jewish neighborhood of apartments, arriving in Kababir fifteen minutes after departing from Rehov Yavneh. Although this was not a work day, we found few people at home during the morning. We heard that many were in the mosque, which was obviously the pride of the village. Located near the village’s edge on the side of the Carmel cliffs, the mosque was in the final stages of construction, and fine touches and additions were being made to the base and surrounding area. After about an hour of fairly uneventful *sankirtana,* I meandered into the mosque. Removing my shoes and leaving the cart at the entrance, I entered with a side bag and offered obeisances. Although I tried to be unassuming, curious stares sped my way. I decided to remain at least for a few minutes, listening to an impassioned lecture in Arabic (which I couldn’t understand, of course).

About half an hour later, as the crowd was dispersing, I walked up the path leading to the front door. Twenty meters away was the young sheikh who had captivated the audience with his discourse. He looked at me in my *dhoti* and *kurta,* and I looked at him in his flowing black silken robes and elegant turban. He spoke melodiously:

*yada yada hi dharmasya*  
*glanir bhavati bharata*  
*abhyutthanam adharmasya*  
*tadatmanam srjamy aham*

This is verse 4.7 from the *Bhagavad-gita:* “Whenever and wherever there is a decline in religious practice, O descendent of Bharata, and a predominant rise of irreligion—at that time I descend Myself.”

Stunned, I smiled. He invited me to his home. Maha-Laksmi also came, and we talked for more than an hour.

Sheikh Muhamad Hamid had been sent from India, the land where Achmed appeared and home of the Achmadiyyas, to conduct a mission in Haifa. On his bookshelf were many Indian philosophical and Sanskrit books, including an original Arabic printing, from 1972, of Srila Prabhupada’s *Bhagavad-gita As It Is*. He described that the Achmadiyyas accept Krsna as a prophet, but that His teachings are applicable especially for Indians, as indicated by the line *glanir bhavati bharata.* Bharata, explained the sheikh, refers to India, not Arjuna.

Then we discussed transmigration of the soul, which the Achmadiyyas do not accept. I referred to *Bhagavad-gita* 2.13. 2.13. Again, however, the sheikh’s interpretations were wildly speculative. Whenever we would refer to Krsna’s words in *Bhagavad-gita*, he would say that our version of the *Gita* had been changed from the original, or offer a conclusion unacceptable to any accepted *parampara,* Vaisnava or impersonal. Seeing that the discussion on philosophy was futile, we switched to lighter topics. He gave us some literature, and we departed amicably. Later, we found in the Achmadiyya literature many Indian influences, such as the comparison of eating cow’s flesh to ingesting disease.

We also met Adel, an old friend of the devotees, in Kababir. Adel had worked in the “Maharaja” restaurant which the devotees had operated in the early 1980s on Herzl Street in the Carmel. He had become attracted to Krsna consciousness and had traveled to France with the devotees to attend a festival. I had heard about him many times over the years. Devotees said that his wife had become upset because, as his attraction for *prasadam* and serving devotees increased, his interest in material life was seriously on the wane. He was overjoyed to see Krsna devotees at his door, especially Maha-Laksmi, with whom he had worked in the restaurant’s kitchen nearly a decade before.

*Manda*

On the way to Nazareth is the Muslim village of Manda. One day, Maha-Laksmi and I went there for *sankirtana.* We went separately to visit schools in the morning. Before purchasing a set of Srila Prabhupada’s books in Hebrew for the school library, the high school principal delivered a heavy dose of Islamic preaching. At such times, I doubted the wisdom of distributing the fifth book in the set, *Caitanya-caritamrta, Adi-lila,* Part 2, describing the pastime of Lord Caitanya’s talks with the Muslim Chand Kazi. I talked with the principal for most of the morning. He liked the idea of constructing a place of worship in his village together with the Hare Krsna devotees. He also induced me to learn the *Azan,* or Call to Prayer, sung by the *moazin* before each of the five prayers of the day: “Allah is great. Allah is great. Allah is great. Allah is great. I bear witness that save Allah, there is no God. I bear witness that save Allah, there is no God. I bear witness that Muhammad is the Prophet of God. I bear witness that Muhammad is the Prophet of God. Come to the prayers. Come to the prayers. Come to the Salvation. Come to the Salvation. Allah is great. Allah is great. Save Allah, there is no God.”

After an exciting morning of *sankirtana* in Cfar Manda, we had lunch, then decided to visit contacts in the evening. After a few pleasant visits with school teachers who took books, we visited Sheikh Adel Abed al Hamid. The sheikh was gentle, and graciously offered us fruit and drink after introducing us to his family. When he saw the Arabic *Gita,* he exclaimed that he already had the *Gita* in English. He went to another room and found his copy of the abridged, paperback version of Srila Prabhupada’s *Gita*. The sheikh explained that he used to associate frequently with devotees when they had lived in Kiryat Haroshet, a suburb of Haifa. In the early 1980s, when the devotees operated a *prasadam* restaurant in Haifa, they lived in a rented farmhouse about twenty minutes from Haifa. Adel Abed al Hamid, a Sufi sheikh and a school teacher, was interested in Indian mysticism and had become close to the *Krsna-bhaktas.* He explained to us a little about Sufism and the Darwishes, who are famous for their whirling dance. Hassan described the few small Sufi communities in Israel, as well as a community of Darwishes in Baka.

*Conversation with Sheikh Amin*

As our relationship with the Druze community deepened, we more fully appreciated the importance of Sheikh Amin. Thus we arranged another meeting. Ali was happy about this, and indicated that the Sheik also wanted to meet with the “Krsna Muwahhidoon” representatives again. This time we had many points to discuss, and the meting lasted over an hour. Maha-Laksmi and I attended. We recorded the discussion which has been transcribed below:

November 12, 1989, Conversation with Sheikh Tarif Amin Julius, Israel. 12:15 p.m.–1:15 p.m. Dhira Govinda dasa and Maha-Laksmi dasi speak in Hebrew; Sheikh Amin speaks in Arabic. Ali Amin translates between them.

Sheikh Tarif Amin (SA) motions for Dhira Govinda (DG) to sit on cushioned seat next to him. Others (Ali Amin, Maha-Laksmi \[ML\], Marfak Amin, another young sheikh, and two teenagers) sit on chairs across from Sheikh Tarif Amin.

**DG:** Is it possible to tape the words of the Sheikh?

**Ali** \[After introducing us to the Sheikh in Arabic\]: Sure.

**DG:** I want to thank you for allowing us to meet you again Here are some pictures from our last meeting. \[DG shows pictures to the Sheikh.\]

**SA:** Where do you live?

**DG:** We live in Haifa now.

**Ali:** But sometimes you go to India?

**DG:** Sometimes we visit India.

**Ali:** The religion is from India?

**DG:** Yes, the spiritual roots are from India \[Ali translates.\] There are Druze in India. I wanted to present to the Sheikh the most important book from India, now translated in Arabic. The book is titled *Bhagavad-gita.* \[DG presents book. Ali talks in Arabic for about two minutes about the *Gita.*\] Kamal Jumbalat read the *Bhagavad-gita* in the Sanskrit language and glorifies the *Bhagavad-gita* very much. He wrote that this is the most important book in the world. \[DG shows a copy of a page in Arabic, written by Jumbalat, glorifying the *Gita.*\] This book was written 5,000 years ago in the Sanskrit language. All of the wise men throughout history have read this book, including the great Greek philosophers Socrates and Plato, Kamal Jumbalat—all philosophers. This is the source and authority on Indian philosophy and yoga. \[Note: The Druze accept the aforementioned Greek philosophers as divinely inspired prophets.\]

**SA:** Who wrote this book?

**DG:** 5,000 years ago, the great sage from ancient India, Vyasadeva, wrote down the *Bhagavad-gita.* About twenty years ago, Srila Prabhupada translated this book into English. Now someone has translated it into Arabic.

**SA:** What happens to a person after death?

**DG:** *Bhagavad-gita* scientifically explains the difference between spirit and matter, between the soul and the body. One of the qualities of the soul is that it’s eternal, whereas the body is temporary. We can even experience now in this body that the body changes every second.

**SA:** Who is responsible for the soul’s passing from one body to the other?

**DG:** Ultimately, God is the Supreme Controller.

**SA:** Who is the founder of your sect?

**DG:** The original source is Krsna, or God. Krsna is a name for God. There is one God, but He has many names. Someone calls Him “Allah,” someone calls him “Krsna” or “Adonai.” There is no limit to the qualities of God. And every name of God is a holy name. To chant the names of God purifies the heart. That’s why it is recommended that the most practical way to achieve love of God is to chant the names of God. That’s why we have a *masbachah* \[Arabic for “chanting beads”.\] We chant Hare Krsna, Hare Krsna, Krsna Krsna, Hare Hare / Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. \[DG shows *japa* beads—gives beads to the Sheikh.\]

**SA:** *Shukran!* \[Thank you!\] \[The Sheikh is excited to hold the beads.\]

**DG:** I am happy to give the beads to the Sheikh.

**SA:** Is there a particular symbolism to the beads?

**DG:** Well, they help us to chant the names of God.

**Ali:** Ah. Is there any meaning to the number, or

**DG:** There are 108 beads.

**Guest:** Why 108?

**DG:** It’s an auspicious number according to the Vedic culture, the culture from ancient India. Why? There are a few reasons.

**SA:** How many people believe in this sect?

**DG:** Actually, there are millions in the world. In India almost everyone believes in Krsna. Now we have centers all over the world, even in Russia, China, Israel. *Bhagavad-gita* explains...\[ML finds the *maha-mantra* in the Arabic *Gita* and hands it to DG.\] Maybe I can show you something. \[Shows the *maha-mantra* in Arabic to the Sheikh.\] This is the *mantra,* the names of God we chant.

**Ali** \[Translates into Arabic and reads words to the Sheikh\]: Hare Krsna, Hare Krsna, Krsna Krsna, Hare Hare / Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare.

**SA:** This is the prayer? Or generally, when do you say this?

**DG:** All that is possible, because the ultimate goal is to remember God at every moment. The goal is to serve God with love and a pure heart. To chant the names of God, like “Hare Krsna,” cleanses the heart of all material desires and allows us to achieve love of God.

**SA:** How many people in India believe in this?

**DG:** About half a billion. About half a billion accept the *Bhagavad-gita* as a holy book, so we are very happy to present the book to the Sheikh.

**SA:** Thank you. What will be the results or conclusions of one who reads and follows this book?

**DG:** What are the results? The results will be that one will acquire spiritual knowledge, knowledge of what is the self—that I’m not the body but the soul, and detachment from material desires, such as sex desire. *Bhagavad-gita* explains that one who is advanced in spiritual understanding does not see according to the body, so he doesn’t see, “This is an Arab, this is an American, this is a Jew.” But his vision is that everyone is the spirit soul within the body, and the nature of each soul is equal; the nature of all souls is to serve God with love.

**SA:** What about marriage?

**DG** \[Pointing to ML\]: This is my wife. \[The Sheikh can’t see so well.\] We live according to principles of purity and chastity. So it is forbidden to look at another woman. Also, we’re vegetarian.

**SA:** Why are they vegetarian? Why it’s not allowed?

**DG:** Well, this helps to develop finer qualities. If we don’t kill the animals, this helps us to develop spiritual qualities. Good qualities. \[The Sheikh shows acceptance and appreciation for the answers.\] Also, we do not use intoxicants—even cigarettes, alcohol, coffee.

**SA:** Why is coffee forbidden? \[An integral part of Druze culture is to serve coffee to guests, although we have heard from some sheikhs that this custom was adopted through the generations by association with Muslim societies.\]

**DG:** It’s not the most important thing; there’s a little bit of an intoxicating effect, but it’s not the most horrible thing. Also, a husband and wife are together only to make a child.

**Ali:** Just one child? Or more?

**DG:** Just to make a child, but the responsibility of the parents is to raise the child to be a pure devotee of God.

**Ali:** But just one child? Or more?

**DG:** It’s possible to have more, but the parents must be capable to educate them. Also, to perform austerities to attain God. *Bhagavad-gita* explains something interesting. \[DG turns to pictures of *Bhagavad-gita*.\] Here’s an analogy with a chariot with five horses.

**Ali:** Why five horses? \[Five horses in five colors is an important Druze symbol.\]

**DG:** I’ll explain. \[All the Druze in the room are a bit bewildered at this point, as they realize that we are aware of many of their “secrets”. Ali translates for the Sheikh, stressing the point about five horses.\] Each horse represents a sense. The chariot is the body and we are the passenger in the chariot. If we, the passenger, cannot control the horses, the situation is precarious. \[Ali is extremely excited in his translation.\] In a similar way, we are the passenger inside the chariot of the body. If we cannot control our mind and senses, there’s no possibility of being happy or peaceful. \[Everyone in the room, especially a middle-aged Druze businessman who came in after we had started, shows approval for the preaching.\] Therefore, the first step on the path of love of God is to learn how to control our senses. That’s why we follow principles of purity. If we don’t control our senses, then our senses will control us, and we will be a slave of our senses. But our natural position is not to be a slave of our senses, but to be “Abed Allah.” \[Arab word meaning “slave of God.”\]

**SA:** Please take something to eat.

**DG:** Oh, we ate. The juice is nice, thank you. We very much appreciate that the Druze people appreciate this wisdom.

**SA:** Are there similarities maybe between Krsna and Druze? Points of sharing?

**DG:** What does the Sheikh think, based on what he’s heard so far? \[Laughter in the room.\]

**SA:** I have heard, but maybe you have a better idea. \[Laughter.\]

**DG:** It seems to me that the roots are the same.

**SA:** When the soul leaves the body, it returns?

**DG:** The soul passes to another body, like the body

**SA:** In the body of the man, where is the soul found?

**DG:** In the area of the heart. \[All nod in appreciation.\] The consciousness of the soul is distributed throughout the whole body. The symptom of the soul is consciousness, and the consciousness is spread to the whole body. But the soul itself like the sun is in one place, but the heat and light of the sun is distributed throughout the whole universe \[Break in tape.\]

**SA:** Among Islam, Judaism, Christianity, and Druze, which do you find the closest to Krsna consciousness?

**DG:** The Druze without a doubt. But the goal of all bona fide religions is the same: to achieve love of God. But we have found that within the philosophy of the Druze there is more developed knowledge, but the goal is the same.

**SA:** How did you find out all this information about the Druze? Who told you about the philosophy of the Druze?

**DG:** Well, we’ve spoken to many Druze during the last year. Actually, they spoke to us. They have read our books and found, “Wow, there are so many similarities.” We’ve even met someone who said that maybe the original Sanskrit writings

**Ali:** The soul passes from body to body—can a Druze pass to a Muslim or Christian?

**DG:** The principle here is that we are not Jews or Americans or Arabs or Russians; that’s just the body. If I’m driving a Toyota, that doesn’t mean Japanese. Maybe tomorrow I’ll be driving a Mercedes. That won’t mean I’m German. This is just my body. These designations—American, Jew—they do not apply to the soul, just to the temporary body. That’s the principle.

**Ali:** So you mean there is a possibility to pass?

**DG:** The soul can pass to all kinds of bodies, but it is illusion for the soul to identify itself with any material body. Actually, all the problems of the world emanate from this illusion. People are thinking, “I’m an Arab, you’re a Jew.” But everyone is a soul, a servant of God. We are not this body. The qualities of the soul

**SA:** Who transfers the soul?

**DG:** The great controller is, of course, God, and He sees our desires. According to the consciousness that we have developed in this body, that determines the next body.

**Ali:** Just from man to man, or for example, to animals?

**DG:** This is explained in *Bhagavad-gita.* According to one’s consciousness. Now, for example, there are many souls in human bodies who are living like animals.

**Ali:** In a human body?

**DG:** Yes. The privilege of a human body is that we can understand spiritual matters, about God. But if someone wastes this opportunity and uses this body only for sense gratification, like the animals, then we can say that he’s already an animal. \[Ali nods his head, but doesn’t translate. Pause.\]

**SA:** What about the end of the world?

**DG:** That’s explained in the *Bhagavad-gita* too—that the history of the world is not a matter of thousands of years but of billions and billions of years. And as we have cycles of births, so does the universe.

**Guest:** How did God create the world?

**DG:** Well, to explain all the details would be complicated, but it’s explained in the *Vedas* that matter comes from spirit, and the gross elements emanate from the more subtle elements.

**Guest:** For instance, the Jews say that God created in six days and rested on Shabbat. The Christians say he created and rested on Sunday. According to you, he worked five days, six days?

**DG** (laughs): Actually, there is always creation going on.

**Guest:** But at the beginning?

**DG:** In the beginning, some details are given in the scriptures, but it’s complicated. It’s explained that God appears in a specific incarnation called Visnu for the purpose of creating the material world, but it’s a question of great spans of time. \[Up until this time, I had never encountered the Druze version of creation. Maha-Laksmi had, however, and informed me after the meeting with the Sheikh that the Druze believe that creation doesn’t require days or eons. They believe that God created in one instant. So, a good answer, which I would use in future meetings with sheikhs and other Druze is, “God created the world in one glance.” According to the *Vedas,* Visnu impregnated the material energy with one glance.\] So, thank you. I’m happy to present the book and the beads to the Sheikh.

**SA:** What can I give to you to properly close our meeting?

**DG:** Well, if the Sheikh can give his blessings that we will have continued nice relations with the Druze people.

**SA:** Thank you. \[Gives nod of appreciation.\]

**DG:** *Shukran.*

**SA:** In regards to relations between man and woman, if one meets the other, can they shake hands?

**DG:** It is recommended to minimize relations between men and women. To separate. Education separate, everything separate.

**SA:** For example, from a religious point of view, if a man meets a woman, he can say hello and shake her hand? Allowed?

**DG:** If a man is really serious to advance in spiritual life, to achieve love of God, then it is recommended not to associate with women, because this desire to be with women is the desire that keeps us in the material world. We’re not fanatical about this, but the principle is to minimize. In Western countries the people are very free in this regard, and they are thinking this is advancement, but actually this degrades the people. The results of these Western countries are that, like we explained, everyone has become a servant of their senses and has forgotten about God.

**Ali:** Eat something?

**DG** \[Takes a raisin\]: What does the Sheikh think about the Hare Krsna movement?

**SA:** There is closeness.

**DG:** We heard that the Sheikh recently met with some people from Tibet.

**Ali:** Yes.

**DG:** The people from there, there’s also a lot of influence from the Vedic literature.

**SA:** You are close to the Tibetans?

**DG:** Actually, the original culture of Tibet is this Vedic culture. Thousands of years ago in India. As years passed, things were changed.

**Ali:** What are these symbols, the hair style and everything?

**DG:** These are symbols that I am a servant of Krsna. It symbolizes that the proper use of the body is to serve God.

**Ali** **and Sheikh:** Eat, eat.

**DG:** Before we eat, we offer the food to God. We cook for Krsna.

**Ali:** Before you eat something, you say something?

**DG:** Yes, we offer prayers. \[Pause.\] Has the Sheikh heard about Krsna?

**Ali:** Yes, of course.

**SA:** Are you allowed to go with someone who is not your wife?

**DG:** No. He agrees? Principles of purity. Purity is the force.

**SA:** We’ve invited the Dalai Lama to visit here.

**DG:** He’s invited the Dalai Lama to come to Israel? There may be great devotees of Krsna to visit Israel, and we’ll be happy for them to meet the Sheikh.

**SA:** If it’s allowed \[unclear\] to sit next to someone’s wife \[unclear\]

**DG:** Well, the principle is to be

**SA:** If someone gets divorced, he can take his wife back?

**DG:** Actually, according to Vedic culture, it’s not permitted to divorce. Actually, if a husband and wife serve God together, why will there be problems?

**SA:** So divorce is not allowed?

**DG:** No.

**ML:** It’s possible to separate, but not to marry another. They can separate from each other.

**Ali:** Why it’s forbidden to divorce?

**DG:** It’s not good for society. The principle is to put Krsna, God, in the center of family life; then there won’t be problems. If God is not in the center, there will be problems. Not just in family life but in the center of the state, of one’s profession. That’s the goal, that’s the science, how to put God in the center of all aspects of life. \[Pause.\] We’d like to thank the Sheikh

**SA:** You said your leader is coming here?

**DG:** Right.

**SA:** So, when he comes we can arrange for some discussions to further research the closeness between us.

**DG:** When we meet, we’ll do research. That will be good. *Shukran.*

**SA:** I thank you, and for God I want that we will work together as one race. If you’re a member of the sect, you’re allowed to marry with someone else? \[One may wonder why Sheikh Amin is asking all these questions about marriage and relations between men and women. After having learned something substantial about our movement, he is seriously considering the possibility that we are Muwahhidoon. If so, then Druze could intermarry with Krsna devotees.This is a crucial point for them. They very strictly prohibit marriage to outsiders. A Druze must marry a Druze, otherwise he risks being ostracized from the community. As their numbers are relatively small, this intermarriage has caused various problems, as is common in other small populaces that intermarry over many generations. After hearing about the tens of millions of Krsna devotees and their similar philosophies and customs, Sheikh Amin is examining the possibility of allowing his people to intermarry with us.\]

**DG:** Hmm. It’s my choice whom I want to marry but, obviously, if I’m a devotee of Krsna, I won’t want to marry with someone who is not a devotee of Krsna. If I’m following principles of purity, I won’t want \[Pause.\] How do you want to go about organizing a forum to do research?

**SA:** When we’re together, we’ll check.

**DG:** How to check?

**Ali:** Many ways, what is similar

**DG:** It seems there are many similarities.

**Ali:** Yes, yes.

**DG:** And we’ll be very happy if the sheikh will read the *Bhagavad-gita.*

**SA:** *Shukran.* I’ll be happy to read it. I hope, again, that we can be very close and work together. Thank you.

**DG:** Also, we want. We very much appreciate the opportunity to associate with the Sheikh *Shukran.*

**SA:** *Shukran.* \[DG, ML, and Ali exit.\]

\[In lobby\]

**DG:** Our behavior was okay?

**Ali:** Really great. The Sheikh was happy. We talked about many things. Very interesting. The Sheikh thinks there’s a specific similarity. Very serious. Maybe there are some differences. But there’s definitely a great similarity.

**DG:** We’re serious

**Ali:** Our religions believe that not just the Druze believe like this. For instance, in Iran, there are the Hamsaween, but not

**ML:** But they have a slightly different belief.

**Ali:** There may be a difference here and there. I haven’t read exactly. But all over the world there’s

**DG:** But not called Druze. But they are

**Ali:** There are.

**DG:** Also in India. It’s called religions

**Ali:** Also in India. Kamal Jumbalat wrote many books about all kinds of religions.

**DG:** Hmm. What are the differences that you see?

**Ali:** In what respect?

**DG:** From the topics we discussed?

**Ali:** Differences, for instance, in regards to divorce. For us it’s allowed to divorce. For example, other things, like in matters if a man meets a woman, it’s allowed to say hello, shake hands, \[indistinct\] within limitations.

**DG:** Yes, these are details, social.

**Ali:** Yes, details. We have similar thoughts about God.

**DG:** God, reincarnation.

**Ali:** Reincarnation, I can’t say that, I’m not really permitted. He won’t say because it’s a secret.

**DG:** Oh, yeah, a secret. \[Ali laughs.\]

**Ali:** But yeah, there are many things just the same in belief.

**DG:** And the five horses.

**Ali:** Also the five horses.

**ML:** We don’t know what, but

**Ali:** You say five, there is something

**ML:** There’s the picture, five horses in different colors.

**Ali** \[Indistinct\]: Five horses exactly. There are surely many similarities. There seems to be a definite relation.

**DG:** So maybe in another few months we’ll see you again. The Sheikh will look at the book?

**Ali:** Of course. For sure. Even the next time you meet he’ll ask you questions. Give you a test. \[Laughter.\]

**DG:** Great. *Shukran.* \[Indistinct.\]

*Haifa University*

Another field that developed during this period was the Haifa University dormitories. A few months earlier, Nagapatni had visited the Technion dormitories, found Druze students, and successfully distributed books to them. As both the Technion and Haifa University were closer to the Vadi than any village, we wanted to check out the possibilities.

One day I drove to Haifa University and visited Faiz Ajami, a Druze professor and one of Salman Falach’s staff members. He was happy to receive me in his office, but he already had Prabhupada’s books through the Ministry of Education. Then, while sitting in the lobby of the Haifa University skyscraper, a Christian student from Nazareth approached me. He was curious about my dress, and ultimately purchased the Arabic *Gita* as well as the First Canto of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* and a *Krsna* book in Hebrew.

At about 4:00 p.m., I walked down to the dormitory buildings. I was anything but inconspicuous in my devotional dress, wheeling a cart full of books and carrying a bulky side bag. An American student saw me outside the dormitory entrance and made a rude comment, but another American student told him not to bother me. The other student asked to see the books. He saw Srila Prabhupada’s picture and said, “He looks very pure.”

Entering the building, I came face to face with the student guard, whose main function was to prevent solicitation in the building. After responding to a few stern questions, I asked him where he was from.

“Beit Jann,” he said. I rattled off a few names, some of whom were his cousins and uncles from the village. Then I pulled out a photo album, which now served as a preaching kit, and showed pictures of ourselves with Sheikh Amin, Salman Falach with the set of books, and Salach Abas in his home with a set of the Grand Classics. Almost in tears, the guard wanted to see the books. Following some discussion about reincarnation, Kamal Jumbalat, and the confidential Muwah-hidoon, he supplied me a list of all the Druze, Muslims, and Christians, along with their room numbers, in the Haifa University dormitories.

Whenever I found students at home, they invited me into their rooms to hear what I had to say. Four or five students, often from the same village, occupied each dorm room. The university provided an atmosphere where the students could relate in ways that were taboo in their village. Mainly, this refers to the mixing of men and women. In most rooms, at least one student bought an Arabic *Gita.*

Once, I met a Druze from Samiah named Sammy. He and a few friends were hosting a party in their dormitory room with several American and Israeli female students. They were also drinking. Sammy, whose behavior showed no traces of his religious upbringing, smilingly invited me into his room. The others, although amused at my appearance, saw that ultimately I would detract from their party. Sammy, however, was captivated by Srila Prabhupada’s books. “I must have all these books!” he exclaimed, before even asking the price. His Druze heritage manifest itself, as did his fascination with Kamal Jumbalat’s research into the connection between the *Vedas* and Muwahhidoon origins. “This is our original language,” he said, pointing to the Sanskrit. Excitedly, he purchased all the books and asked that I bring him more when I got them.

After the purchase of Hebrew sets for all Druze schools and libraries, Salman Falach met with us several times, sometimes alone and sometimes with his ministers. We would always bring *prasadam,* and he would take any new publication, such as the Arabic *Gita.* During discussions he revealed his fascination with the Vedic literature, and expressed a desire to show academically the Vedic origins of the Druze. Salman commented that he was happy that articles about the Druze appeared in the ISKCON publications *Back to Godhead* and *ISKCON World Review,* but that he was displeased that the articles sometimes referred to the Druze as Arabs.

Maha-Laksmi’s father, David Oren, worked for the Israeli government from 1957 until 1960, setting up medical care, sanitation, water systems, and administrative facilities in the Arab villages from the Negev to the Golan. Thus his name was well-known and appreciated in the villages. Mr. Oren relates the following: “After the army, I moved to Nazareth. The women—they were carrying big buckets of water on their heads long, long distances. I asked, ‘What if we could bring water right to your house?’ ‘Oh! That would be a miracle.’ Within one month, we had running water there. We went to all the villages. Set up schools, medical, electric. They had no electric. When one village would get it, every village would quickly hear about it and would want electricity.” Often just by referring to her father in villages like Maghar and Gush Halav (Jish), Maha-Laksmi would be able to sell sets of books to the village leaders.

On one visit to the Vadi Ara, while visiting the Muslim village Kara, I met a dentist named Akel Akel. He had studied in Romania, as had many Israeli Muslim doctors. Akel was impressed with the depth of the Krsna conscious philosophy. He asked to see the Hebrew set, which he decided to take. Then I told him about a bigger set in English, which I happened to have in the car. Eager to see it, he told me to get it. I ran across the village to where the car was parked, then drove to Akel’s office on the outskirts. He was wonderstruck by the beautiful books and gave a donation for the entire set, as well as an Arabic *Gita.*

During the same visit to Kara, I encountered a Palestinian woman, Fatima, who described the situation in the West Bank. She was visiting her brother, another Romanian-educated dentist in Kara, from her West Bank village. Fatima described the occupied territories as “very tense,” especially during afternoon and evening hours. Because she appreciated the importance of the knowledge in Srila Prabhupada’s books, she wanted to advise me how, where, and when to present the books in the territories. She told me to go during the morning hours; violent outbreaks tended to occur later in the day. She also told me to approach professors and other intellectuals at the university. Several towns in the West Bank had university branches, which were formally closed due to the uprising. Also, Fatima cautioned against speaking any Hebrew or driving a car with an Israeli license plate. Other than East Jerusalem, cars from occupied territories displayed distinctive license plates, and an Israeli car was subject to attack in the Shomrone. Her advice was to park in Jerusalem and take a taxi to the West Bank, being sure to depart by mid-afternoon. Fatima was confident that the intellectual community would enthusiastically receive the Vedic literature in English, but especially in Arabic.

Ramallah is the site of the largest West Bank university. I had a pleasant experience in Ramallah in 1986, before Intifada, when devotees would go to West Bank Jewish settlements to perform *sankirtana.* On our return trip from a week of *sankirtana* in Jerusalem, Guna-avatara and I stopped in Ramallah, where we had an Arabic grocer friend who sold *samn* (ghee). It was difficult to find ghee elsewhere in Israel. On this visit, he sold us some ghee, purchased an English *Bhagavad-gita,* and recommended that we visit a nearby apartment complex inhabited by Arab Catholics. We set out with our boxes of candles and bags of books. Direct book distribution was uncommon in the *yatra* at the time, so we were excited when almost every family invited us in, treated us as honored guests, and showed great interest in the *Bhagavad-gita,* the only English book we had. After about an hour, we returned to the van to reload, sharing our stories about the friendly people we had just met. By dark we had sold all our English books and returned to Ranaana to tell the other devotees about our book distribution adventure.

We were regularly amazed at how deeply the Druze and Arab communities were reading Srila Prabhupada’s books. While visiting Salach, we discussed the process of offering foodstuffs to Krsna. I quoted the Sanskrit to *Bhagavad-gita* 9.26. Before I could finish, however, Salach began to recite the Hebrew translation. Later, when we would give weekly lectures in Rama, many Druze displayed extensive knowledge from their reading of *Bhagavad-gita* and other books in the set.

On another occasion, while going door to door in the village of Yasif with the Arabic *Gita* and the Hebrew set, I met a woman who was a student at a Christian university. She showed me the full set on her bookshelf, explaining that she had read all the books and was composing a term report based on Srila Prabhupada’s writings.

After a particularly ecstatic day of book distribution and preaching in Osafia, I attended a program with a teenage Girl Scout troop in Denia, Haifa’s most affluent neighborhood. The program had been organized by Isvara Krsna dasa. By coincidence or otherwise, that day I happened to meet a group of Druze teenagers in Osafia, all of whom took several books after inquiring seriously about the mission of human life, the commonality of spiritual paths, and the soul’s trans-migration in the material world. The Denia program was pleasant, although the discussion was on a different level. An initial question from the teenagers was, “Isn’t \[Hare Krsna\] brainwashing?” After being satisfied by our answers, everyone enjoyed *prasadam.*

Salach took a bus from Rama to attend a Saturday evening program at the Hadar *sankirtana* house. In Israel, buses generally stop running during the weekly Shabbat holiday—from Friday sunset to Saturday sunset. After *prasadam,* we discussed the possibility of translating the “Purity is the Force” flyer into Arabic.

The Arabic and Druze populations didn’t share Jewish Israel’s misconception about the Hare Krsna movement that we were a new cult that “brainwashed” its members and engaged in immoral activities. During ISKCON Israel’s early stages, there were even rumors that the devotees kidnapped children and drank their blood. Such rumors are similar to those circulated about Jews in previous ages.

To the contrary, the Druze and Arabs regarded Krsna’s devotees positively—as sincere practitioners of a godly, spiritual path. Although many may have considered this path inferior to their own religious system, we almost never encountered seriously antagonistic people in their villages. Still, we considered that the Arabs and Druze would be inspired to know about the strict principles of *Krsna-bhaktas.*

Salach returned to Rama and, with the help of Kamil and Maher, a teacher of Arabic literature, translated the flyer into Arabic. I took it to a printer in downtown Haifa to typeset and print. When I picked up the flyers I noticed, although I wasn’t familiar with the Arabic alphabet, that the pattern of words in what was supposed to be the *maha-mantra* did not correspond to the actual sixteen word *maha-mantra*. I sent a copy to Rama, and the Druze *bhaktas* detected several other mistakes. After the flyers were finally printed correctly, we distributed them whenever we met someone who spoke Arabic, on *sankirtana* or otherwise.

*Our Next Move*

As the contract at Rehov Yavneh would soon be up, we considered whether or not we should relocate and to where. “Why not one of the villages?” we thought. A year ago we were reluctant to set foot in the Druze and Arab villages; now we felt more at home there than anywhere else. Almost without exception, Israeli Jews did not live in Arab villages. Even the devotees were surprised about the possibility, despite the successes of the past year.

Dec. 30, 1989—Danavir dasa (Dallas) to Maha-Laksmi dasi (Rama):

You mentioned that you were planning to move your residence up to the Druze village of “Rama,” where your friend Bhakta Salach lives. Is this plausible? It sounds unimaginable, but if Rama wants something, nothing can stop Him.

One day, Maha-Laksmi and I were doing *sankirtana* in Ilaboon, a small, friendly Christian village located a few miles from Maghar. Ilaboon impressed us as especially clean and quiet. During the day I distributed a set to Alifi Sror, the high school principal, who was enthusiastic about the idea that we would live in Ilaboon. In the evening, we went together to the house of the Vice Mayor, Noahf Sheebahnli, a member of the developing Bedouin community on the outskirts of the village. He purchased the Arabic *Bhagavad-gita* and offered to help us arrange a place in Ilaboon. While returning to the van, we decided to knock on one more door. Rashid Zoreek, a Christian travel and tour agent, welcomed us in. His appearance, as well as the decor of his home, was as Western as one could find in an Arab village. He was a tall man with well-defined features. He had a full mustache and styled, frizzy brown hair. Rashid spoke fluent English, a necessity for guiding tourists through the historical holy sites of Jerusalem, Nazareth, and Bethlehem. Seeing the set as a cultural addition to his household, he purchased the books, along with a copy of the Arabic *Gita.* When he heard that we were considering relocating, he became inspired by the idea that we live in Ilaboon. “I’m waiting for a little more cash to come in, and then I want to finish building my downstairs. I wouldn’t want to rent to just anyone. I’d really like to have you as neighbors, and I could give you a good price for the apartment. I think you’d be a valuable cultural addition for Ilaboon.” He showed us the ground beneath his house, which, like many Israeli Arab homes, was in the middle of construction. Most families were continually in the process of extending their homes for future generations. It didn’t seem to bother them that their property always looked unfinished. Very few villagers rented space, as they reserved everything for family, but Rashid liked the idea of renting to the Hare Krsna devotees. We discussed possible rental terms. He would need some money in advance to complete the house.

We liked Ilaboon. It was serene and the people were friendly, although they mostly kept to themselves. We thought we could execute our Krsna conscious duties there without disturbance. Also, the village was situated about a seven-minute drive from Maghar and a twenty-minute drive from Rama, two villages where we had many favorable contacts. Ilaboon was centrally located in the northeastern Galil, with convenient access to the Druze villages in the upper Galil, as well as to Christian and Muslim towns such as Nazareth and Deboria.

When Salach heard we were thinking of moving from Haifa to a village, he became excited about the idea of having devotees in Rama. He said he would have given us a place, but he still lived with his parents and there was no room in their house. He combed Rama for possible vacancies. One evening we received a call. A middle-aged Druze named Amin Kasem had a ground-floor room. The next evening we went to Rama to look at the place. Kasem was friendly and happy that we appreciated Druze culture, but primarily he wanted to do business. Whereas Rashid’s floor plan involved three rooms, a kitchen, and a bath, Kasem’s ground floor was a 6.5 x 4 meter hall with an attached kitchen. Adjacent to the kitchen was a shower. Amin’s house was located in the center of Rama, at the bottom of the Druze section and near the Christian section. He had once rented the facility for business purposes. We imagined that such a room would be used by some family to stock their olive oil or other non-perishable produce, but not for living. Also, Kasem was asking a high price. Salach was hoping we would move to Rama, but he admitted the price was high for the room.

As the Rehov Yavneh contract was expiring in less than three weeks, we considered the advantages and disadvantages of Rama, Ilaboon, and staying in the Hadar. The novelty of moving to an Arab village appealed to us, as we thought this would assist our preaching. Ilaboon seemed like a pleasant place to live, but it was somewhat distant from the Druze villages and Rashid required a down payment for constructing the home. Such construction usually involved delays. Also, for Salach, Kamil, and Maher to visit us from Rama would be difficult. We decided there was no better place to base our preaching activity than Rama. Telephoning Amin Kasem, we asked for a significant price reduction. Surprisingly, he agreed. Within a few days we signed the contract on his room.

## 4: Rama

*Sunday, December 10, 1989*

Suddha-sattva and Isvara Krsna helped us to make the move from the Vadi to Rama. We loaded the Suzuki and the Delta, along with an attached open trailer. Still, we were unable to take everything and had to return to the Hadar for the rest of our things. Night was approaching as we entered Rama. Although I’d been through the village often, somehow I directed the party down the wrong streets in an attempt to find Kasem Amin’s house. We hit a dead end. We couldn’t back out, so the devotees had to unhitch, then rehitch, the Delta trailer after we had turned the van around. We finally found our way to our new home and went in for the night, leaving the heavy unloading work for the morning.

The next morning we got the vehicles unloaded, and Suddha-sattva did the electrical wiring and divided the space with a curtain. The others went to Tel Aviv while Maha-Laksmi and I sat, surrounded by our belongings, in the cold room. Looking around, we saw that the middle of the north wall had an indentation wide enough to house the narrow door that led to one of Amin’s storage rooms. A couple of crates covered with a *cadar* fit perfectly into the alcove, and we set up a small altar with pictures of the Panca-tattva and the *parampara.*

By the end of our first day in Rama, visitors had begun to arrive. Maher visited, bringing enough *baklawah* for everyone, and we offered it on our altar. We then invited everyone to attend a Friday program, including *kirtana, prasadam,* and a *Bhagavad-gita* discussion.

As we had no refrigerator, we developed the habit of purchasing only enough foodstuffs for one day. In that way we avoided wasting anything. Fortunately, winter in Rama was cold enough to keep items fresh for a considerable time.

*Friday, December 15, 1989*

Salach, Kamil, and Maher came for our Friday program. They brought with them a tall Christian in his late teens, two young Druze, both about fourteen years old, and Rezek Zedan. As I began to speak, I asked the newcomers what they knew about Krsna consciousness. Rezek, a simple construction worker living at the top of the village, said matter-of-factly that he read Srila Prabhupada’s books every day, followed all the principles, and chanted eight to sixteen rounds of *japa* daily. I then remembered having sold Rezek a set of Prabhupada’s books the previous winter. I hadn’t contacted him since, but through the books and other devotees in his village, he had begun to practice *bhakti-yoga.* I began to imagine how many others there were like Rezek in Druze villages. I explained to the guests that we were establishing their temple. The ISKCON center was for the spiritual benefit of Rama’s citizens, and Maha-Laksmi and I could be considered caretakers of the spiritual center.

Salach was excited that Krsna devotees had come to reside in his village. He had been practicing yoga for several years and had recently become attracted to *bhakti-yoga*. Now followers of Srila Prabhupada, the foremost proponent of *bhakti-yoga* in the modern age, were living and interacting with his friends and family.

Kamil Schadey had a strong attachment to the impersonal path, but also had high regard for Krsna’s devotees. *Yatha mata tatha patha*—all paths lead to the same goal, he believed. *Bhakti-yoga* was definitely one of them. Therefore, he was happy that sincere practitioners of a bona fide yoga system had come to his village to give light to the ordinary course of activities. Although he maintained his impersonalist’s stance, he appreciated the devotees’ detachment from material activities and qualities. He had read many of Srila Prabhupada’s books, and it was Kamil who asked the question about Bhismadeva that had impressed Jayadvaita Swami. Kamil admitted that he had only heard or read about impersonalists who had achieved exalted states of *samadhi;* any he knew were still addicted to at least a few material habits.

Maher Faraj was an English teacher in nearby Beit Jann and a political activist and the leader of a youth theatre group. Previously his political activities leaned toward communism. More recently, however, he had realized the need for a politic possessing a meaningful spiritual aspect and had become attracted to the writings of Kamal Jumbalat. Maher enjoyed our *kirtana* and *Gita* sessions. Although he was attached to smoking and other unwanted habits, and he wasn’t seriously considering changing his lifestyle, hearing the *maha-mantra* and the *Bhagavad-gita* philosophy soothed his mind and provided him relief from life’s stresses. Maher didn’t understand Krsna consciousness, but he knew that it had a positive effect on the people of Rama.

*Wednesday, December 20, 1989*

A loud crash against our metallic door shattered the quiet of late evening. For the second time in the week, local teenagers were throwing rocks at our door. After the first incident, Amin Kasem, our landlord and upstairs neighbor, became irate. He described the various types of injury he would inflict on the perpetrators.

Adib Amin, Kasem’s cousin and close neighbor, promised us protection. Adib was a robust man both in physique and demeanor. I had visited him a few days before, and he had purchased an Arabic *Gita*. Adib spoke fairly good English and was proud to have relatives in the United States. He was intrigued by the devotees and the *Bhagavad-gita’s* philosophy, and he promised to read the book in earnest. Now I was sitting with him in his living room as he enjoyed a plate of *prasadam.* Adib worked at Rama’s municipality and said he would investigate the rock-throwing episodes. He already had suspects and planned to contact their parents. Simultaneously, Salach, Kamil, and other friends were upset that citizens of the village were trying to disturb us. They talked to the suspects and warned them that we were doing God’s work and that there would be a severe reaction for their mistreatment of us. Our friends assured us that these particular teenagers caused trouble for everyone and that they meant nothing personal toward us. Within a short time, the harassment ceased.

*Mangala-arati* was at 5:00 a.m. in Rama, and *Srimad-Bhagavatam* class began at 7:30 a.m., followed by *prasadam*. Salach often attended *arati,* morning *japa,* and class. Woosam Serkan, a fourteen-year-old school teacher’s son, also frequently attended the class and evening programs. He was an enthusiastic *japa* chanter and had become vegetarian. His age concerned us, as we feared his parents might become upset. We discovered, however, that his father owned a full set of Prabhupada’s books and was encouraging his son to learn about spiritual life from us.

After morning *prasadam* I would leave in the Suzuki for a nearby village, usually Druze, but sometimes Muslim or Christian, to distribute books. Beit Jann, Pekiin, Maghar, Kisra, Samiah, Sagur, and Ain El Assad—all Druze villages—were each within twenty minutes of Rama. While I was distributing books, Maha-Laksmi would interact with the residents of Rama. She became close with many members of the village. Naaieem Amin, Kasem’s wife, spoke no English or Hebrew, but somehow Maha-Laksmi established a friendship with her, exchanging knowledge about vegetarian cooking as well as Vedic and Druze culture.

On *sankirtana,* people would at first be surprised when I told them I lived in Rama. It was common for them to first ask, “*Min when inti?* (Where are you from?)” After they understood that I was living in Rama, they would want to know, “But where were you born?” As the most nectarean aspect of Druze preaching was that the more we spoke direct philosophy, the more favorably the Druze responded, I would usually answer this apparently commonplace question, “This body is from America, but I am eternal. The soul inside the body is never born and never dies. The body may be called American, Jewish, Arab, Druze, tall, short, etc., but these material designations have nothing to do with our eternal nature, which is to serve God with a pure heart.” Moved by this conversational icebreaker, they would usually ask to see the books. Handing them the Arabic *Gita,* I would ask them to turn to page 113. Sometimes I would find the page for them, as I had learned to recognize the Arabic number 113. Upon finding the page, I would ask them to read the verse and purport to *Bhagavad-gita* 2.22, which compares the soul’s change of bodies to our change of garments, an analogy already familiar to the Druze. Captivated by the verse translation and the first line of the purport—“As a person puts on new garments, giving up old ones, the soul similarly accepts new material bodies, giving up the old and useless ones.” Purport: “Change of body by the atomic individual soul is an accepted fact. ”they dutifully read the entire purport, sometimes aloud. Then I would ask them to turn to a few other verses, such as 2.13, and to read similarly relevant purports.

Holding a *Gita* myself, I would show the pictures in the middle of the book, usually stopping at the “changing bodies” illustration and discussing further about the nature of the soul. By now, they would be impressed by the *Gita’s* knowledge. I would continue leafing through the pictures, stopping and explaining, according to the nature of the person, about Srila Prabhupada, the background of the Battle of Kuruksetra, Lord Caitanya’s *sankirtana* movement, the ten incarnations of Godhead, the sages’ vision of spiritual equality, the yoga system, love of God, different types of pious and impious persons, and what determines a person’s next body.

Transmigration as it is described and illustrated in *Bhagavad-gita* is a sensitive issue when speaking to Druze, because they believe that a soul in a human body cannot degrade to an animal body. However, I’ve never heard them satisfactorily explain the difference between the life force in an animal and that of a human being. When describing the illustration of a woman becoming a tree and a man becoming a pig, I explained that most persons in society live like animals, because they use their time only to fulfill the animal propensities of eating, sleeping, mating, and defending. Such people do not fulfill the real mission of human life—to understand their spiritual nature and develop love for God. Druze, Muslims, and Christians all accepted this explanation.

While showing the pictures, I would move past the painting of the five horses slowly. If the person was Druze, he would stop me and ask, “What’s that?! What’s that?!” He would then examine the picture from all angles. I would describe the horses as red, white, blue, yellow, and green, then explain the analogy of the chariot of the body and the importance of learning to control our senses. For the Druze, this would invariably add to the mystic attraction of Krsna’s book.

*Letters*

December 18, 1989—Dhira Govinda dasa (Rama) to Danavir dasa:

Maha-Laksmi and I have moved to Rama, the purpose of the move being to situate ourselves closer to where we usually distribute books. But we’ve been here only a few days and, from what I can understand, Krsna is giving some really amazing indications. Last year at this time the seeds were planted in the form of sets of Srila Prabhupada’s books. Now the Druze community is practically demanding a Hare Krsna center(s), and it seems to me that they’re willing to financially support the temple. We’ve been here for about a week and the community is responding to us exceptionally favorably—from the sheikhs to the political leaders to the simple people. The core of our support is a crew of *bhaktas:*

Bhakta David Harzion, with whom you are familiar, is very happy to have a center just a ten-minute bus ride from his town of Karmiel. He sells books and has offered to help support us financially also.

Bhakta Salach Abas—chanting sixteen rounds and following the principles. He’s anxious to advance in Krsna consciousness—has also offered financial assistance to relieve Maha-Laksmi and I so we can hold more public programs with *kirtana, prasadam*, and Vaisnava philosophy.

Bhakta Kamil—vegetarian, chanting, is gradually coming from an impersonal hodge-podge philosophy to Vaisnavism.

Bhakta Maher—an Arabic literature teacher who is writing a book. He’s not currently following the principles or chanting regularly, but he can’t stay away from the association of devotees and is enthusiastic to support a center.

Many, if not most, of the political and cultural leaders already have our books, are favorable, and have offered their assistance. It’s very exciting preaching.

Enclosed is a favorable article printed in the biggest Arabic Israeli newspaper, *El Ajami.* Also enclosed is a copy of an Arabic flyer we printed explaining about the regulative principles of the devotees and advertising the *Bhagavad-gita* in Arabic. Of course, I’ll be happy if you relate the happenings in Israel to any devotee interested in Arabic preaching.

Dec. 25, 1989—Dhira-Govinda (Rama) to Kirtiraja dasa (Sweden):

Maha-Laksmi and I have moved to Rama for the purpose of being closer to the villages we usually visit. Also, we’re paying much less rent here. But since we’ve come, the place has practically been turned into a full center by the demand of the residents. The book distribution is continuing to go nicely. In addition, we’re holding a public program on Friday evenings, and the residents are helping to support the center with donations of *bhoga* and *laksmi.* We present ourselves not as a Hare Krsna center but just as a married couple practicing Krsna consciousness. But the community is greatly interested. We’ve encountered no opposition whatsoever from any sector of the village, only encouragement.

Bhakta Salach is coming on strong. He’s chanting sixteen rounds per day and following the four regulative principles. Also, he comes to morning *SB* class two or three times per week. He’s a *krsna-bhakta* who is simultaneously accepted in the village as a nice Druze boy. He speaks in terms of dedicating his life to the path of *bhakti.*

We have a few other young men who regularly visit and chant *japa* and are vegetarian. Bhakta Rezek Zedan works in construction and tries his best to do sixteen rounds a day, but always manages to do at least five or ten. Bhakta Salach has been unemployed for a few months and has had ample time to develop his Krsna consciousness. He accompanied me on *sankirtana* yesterday. He’s a good preacher. I was thinking he could support himself by distributing Srila Prabhupada’s books. If we could get some small books in Arabic, it would be a great boon to the preaching here.

Also I think we’ve found a great way to get books to Lebanon. The most holy place for the Druze is the tomb of the prophet Jethro, and it’s situated here in Israel. In April, there is a week-long holiday, and thousands of Druze from Lebanon come to visit. I want to base myself there for that week.

Bhakta Salach also speaks of buying land and building a temple. It may sound a little dreamy, but I don’t think we’re too far away. We have many wealthy Druze friends who could donate for such a project.

Back to the books—I’ve heard there are three small books ready for printing. If we could have the material, we could print them here. Also, one boy has approached me and has specifically requested to work as a translator of Prabhupada’s books to Arabic.

Sheikh Tarif Amin—it will be really great if someone can come to Israel as a world leader of Hare Krsna and meet with the Sheikh Our last meeting with Sheikh Amin has had a tremendously favorable impact on the Druze community. The Sheikh requested to meet with our world leader. I’d really like to arrange this before he leaves his body...

People would often ask to see Prabhupada’s books in Hebrew and we would distribute one or two sets a week. When showing the Hebrew books, we would base our descriptions of them on the pictures. For example, if we were showing the *Bhagavatam,* we would tell the story of Bhismadeva lying on a bed of arrows and explain about mystic powers and pure devotion. If we were showing *Caitanya-caritamrta,* we would describe the pastime of the Chand Kazi and explain how only God consciousness (and not any concocted political philosophy) can unite all factions and bring real peace to the world. From *Krsna* we would describe many of the depicted pastimes and discuss God’s mission when He descends to this world. When someone donated for a full set, we would often include an abridged version of the *Prabhupada-lilamrta* in Hebrew and/or some small Hebrew books. Occasionally, we met an intellectual who would take the thirty-volume *Srimad-Bhagavatam* set in English.

In Samiah, Maha-Laksmi and I went to visit Jamal Falach, a high school principal and Salman Falach’s cousin. Maha-Laksmi had sold Jamal a Hebrew set the previous year. When Jamal heard that an English set was available and that Salman already owned it, he was eager to see the books. We strolled into the school’s courtyard, despite the jeers and otherwise curious stares of the students, and into Jamal’s office, where we spread the thirty books out on his desk. He was astounded when he saw them and ordered his assistant to write a school check.

Going daily to Druze and Arab villages, I became a familiar sight in nearby towns. Meanwhile, Maha-Laksmi made many friends in Rama. Our Friday feast programs continued. Only men appeared at the public programs, as Druze culture prohibited women from participating in most public functions as a means of protecting them.

About one-quarter of Rama’s six thousand residents are Druze. Most of the population is Christian, with a few hundred Muslims living at the foot of the village. During our stay in Rama, the Muslims established a mosque with a *moazin.* If, as sometimes happened, our alarm clock did not succeed to interrupt our pre-dawn slumber, the call of the *moazin* reminded us that it was *brahma-muhurta.*

Rezek’s older brother, Farid, lived near us in the center of Rama. I went to visit him one evening. He and his family treated me graciously. He knew that Rezek practiced Krsna consciousness, and he respected the devotees and Srila Prabhupada’s books, although he himself was not a literary person. He told me that he didn’t have money and that he didn’t think he would read the books anyway but, in the end, he agreed to take an Arabic *Gita* in exchange for vegetables. Almost every morning he rode through Rama’s streets selling his vegetables. Arriving at our place, he would give some tomatoes or whatever he had. This went on for a while. No one kept track to see whether the vegetables equaled the price of the book or not.

Kamil, Salach, and Maher arrived at 6 p.m. for the special program. Amin Kasem came down from upstairs to join the festivities. I sat on a cushion on one side of the altar, with Amin sitting on a chair on the other side. The Druze *bhaktas* sat cross-legged on thin cushions in the room. Maha-Laksmi was completing the feast preparations in the kitchen. After a few minutes of singing *bhajanas,* everyone listened as I struggled to read in Hebrew the glories of Lord Nityananda and the *sankirtana* movement from chapter five of *Caitanya-caritamrta’s Adi-lila.*

During one Friday program in late December, Wahib Saad visited with a few friends from Maghar, where he lived. He also brought lights and a video camera, and he filmed the entire evening program. On another occasion, Bhakta David Harzion came to an evening program with a reporter friend from a Jewish newspaper in the Galil, *Tsafone. Tsafone* printed a full-page article entitled, “When Hare Meets Rama,” featuring a large photo of the Druze *bhaktas.* Salach commented that he liked being identified as a “Druze Krsna.”

*Meeting Between Jews, African-Americans, and Hare Krsnas*

In January 1990, Isvara Krsna, Parama-guru, and I traveled to the Negev to visit the Hebrew Israelites in Dimona. This group operates an ice cream store called “Eternity” in Tel Aviv, and we had become friendly with several of their members. As a result, they had invited us to visit their community.

In Dimona, the Hebrew Israelites occupy a sizable section of town. We drove into their community at dusk. We noticed that a vigorous game of basketball was being played in the parking lot. Then our hosts arrived and took us to one of their homes. There through discussion we discovered a great affinity between Krsna consciousness and the Hebrew Israelites.

Also known as the Black Hebrews, the Hebrew Israelites believe that they are descendants of an original Jewish tribe. Most members come from the black communities of American cities like Chicago. Their leader is Ben Ami (Son of the People). They are strict vegans—refraining from meat, fish, eggs, and milk products. They don’t gamble or take intoxication of any kind. Sexual indulgence is allowed only for procreation. Some men are polygamous. In Israel, despite heavy government persecution, this group has established successful communities. Dimona is their headquarters.

In 1983. when I lived in the Negev town of Arad, I had heard that there was a small society of Hebrew Israelites. The directors of the Institute where I was studying often warned us, sometimes during official programs, about the Black Hebrews and their subversively dangerous political and historical views.

After broad philosophical and sociological discussion, we performed *bhajana* together. Our hosts were fascinated by the Indian instruments and spell-bound by the sound of the *kirtana*—such an unfamiliar and exotic sound in the middle of the Negev desert. Although they appreciated the principle of chanting the names of God, they did not have a medium like *kirtana* in which to do so. Each of our hosts was an accomplished musician (ironically, the *bhajana* performers were not), and they delighted in this way of glorifying God. After *kirtana,* they served a well-prepared vegetarian meal.

Early the next morning they gave us a tour of their community, culminating in the morning assembly of their school children. The school seemed well organized, and the children opened the day with beautiful, well-rehearsed songs glorifying the Supreme Lord. They performed a touching song of welcome for us, sung to the tune of “Mrs. Robinson.” Before departing, we gave them books in Hebrew, which they appreciated. We all desired further association with one another. Recently, in 1992, His Holiness Bhakti-tirtha Maharaja visited the Dimona community with several devotees. We’ve heard that he was very well received.

February 5, 1990—Dhira Govinda dasa (Rama, Israel) to Hebrew Israelite community (Dimona, Israel):

Dear Saints \[this is how the Hebrew Israelites address one another\],

Hare Krsna. We wanted to express our heartfelt gratitude for your kind hospitality during our visit to Dimona. We were most enlivened by our philosophical discussions as well as the purified spiritual atmosphere that pervades your community. We are eager to continue our relationship with you, for the spiritualization of society. Hare Krsna.

At our center in Rama we were becoming part of the village family:

Maha-Laksmi: *Naeem and Amin always made me feel welcome. If I was alone downstairs, they would send one of their children to sit with me so I didn’t feel lonely. Naeem was always happy. Whenever she went to the mountains to pick herbs, she* *would* *ask me to accompany her. She was always cleaning, baking bread, or taking care of the garden.*

*Another interesting thing is that Druze homes usually have a plant outside their door. The plant has manjaris, like basil. It reminded me of Tulasi-devi.*

*There was some humorous confusion with names. One of the Kasem’s children was named Baha. When they called him, I thought they were calling me, Maha. One of their daughters was named Mira, which is my name from birth. When they would call her, I thought they were calling my husband, Dhira, to come to the telephone.*

*Grape leaves are expensive in the store, but they are abundant in Druze villages. Once, they picked a lot of grape leaves. I wanted to make Dwarak Diwali, and I asked them for some. They brought me a big bowl of leaves. I went upstairs to ask Amin how much to pay for them. He said, “Katsat booshah (have a little shame).” We were like family; they didn’t want money for grape leaves.*

*Sheikh Farhoud*

Salach was eager for us to meet Sheikh Farhoud, Rama’s leading sheikh and among the most respected sheikhs in Israel. Sheikh Farhoud had attended Jaya-dvaita Swami’s program in Rama and been impressed with what he could understand (he listened through a double translation) about Krsna consciousness. Although Sheikh Farhoud considered Sheikh Amin a father (Sheikh Farhoud was just a child—in his seventies) and had the greatest admiration and respect for him, he sometimes disagreed with him on certain managerial and political issues. His disagreements were due to a different interpretation of Druze scripture. Salach and most of the others in Rama had great affection for Sheikh Farhoud. Some thought him unsurpassed in knowledge of scripture and philosophy.

We walked to the Sheikh’s home near the top of the village. Removing our shoes, we entered his sitting room, which was adorned with pictures of Druze holy places and saints. We knew that each of those pictures contained a fascinating story. A popular picture in Druze homes depicts about a hundred leading sheikhs standing in concentric circles of different sizes. Sheikh Farhoud also had a photo of a Lebanese sheikh who was considered very pure and who had dedicated his life to caretaking the grounds of Job’s burial site. Job is a Biblical personality important to the Muwahhidoon. The Lebanese sheikh had recently been murdered, as he unknowingly became caught up in the unpredictable tides of Lebanese politics.

Most prominent on Sheikh Farhoud’s walls were artistic renderings of the Nabi Schweib memorial, located next to Mount Arbel, a short drive from Tiberias and the Sea of Galilee. Nabi Schweib is the prophet Jethro, considered by Jews and Christians to be Moses’ father-in-law and, other than God, the most important personality in Druze theology. Among five primary prophets, Schweib is topmost. The Druze do not consider him to be a blood relative of Moses; they claim he was unmarried. According to their Biblical hermeneutics, he was the “guardian” of Sarah, not her father. He owned land, and Sarah was under his protection, but he was never married and had no children. We heard from some persons in Rama that Nabi Schweib had once walked through their village during his nomadic life.

As “Jerusalem Art”, depicting the Holy City in countless varieties of forms, mediums and perspectives has become a standard on Jewish walls throughout the world, “Nabi Schweib Art” portraying the memorial built at the site of Jethro’s tomb, is practically required for anyone claiming to be Druze. Annually, on April 25, the biggest Druze festival of the year is held at Nabi Schweib’s memorial. Thousands of sheikhs, as well as other Druze from Lebanon and Israel, gather on the occasion. Smaller festivals are held throughout the year at the burial places of other major prophets, such as Nabi Sabalan, located above Hurfeish, and Nabi Al-Yafuri, in the Golan Heights.

Sheikh Farhoud explained that the Druze scriptures concur with Vedic descriptions of the modern age becoming continually more degraded. Druze cosmology describes universal cycles of 343,000,000 years. Sheikh Farhood also explained that they consider the “Jesus” crucified on the cross to be a “*maya*” Jesus, not the real one. This will remind a Gaudiya Vaisnava of the story of Ravana kidnapping Sita. The Sita taken by Ravana was actually an illusory representation of the real Sita. Also the Druze consider that Al-Hakim’s son was not actually his son. This was an illusory arrangement for the bewilderment of persons who could not realize the Godhood of Al-Hakim.

Unfailingly hospitable, Sheikh Farhoud offered fruits, nuts, and drink at each lull in the conversation. Although appearing as a typical elderly sheikh with his full white beard and sagacious wrinkles, his movements were sprightly, his mood jolly, spontaneous, and childlike. He narrated many stories of his life and often digressed into discussions of comparative religion, the degradation of modern society, and spiritually based political ideologies. I was fascinated by Sheikh Farhoud, as Salach translated between us. Points of agreement between intricacies of Vedic and Druze philosophy continually surfaced, amazing all three of us. Salach was especially keen to these points, as he wanted to be able to justify his adoption of Krsna conscious practices to his family and community. I presented the Sheikh with an Arabic *Bhagavad-gita* and took my leave.

Less than two weeks after my visit to the Sheikh, Salach approached me one morning and said that Sheikh Farhoud wanted to visit us at our home that afternoon. I stayed back from *sankirtana* and Maha-Laksmi prepared some special *prasadam*. Sheikh Farhoud arrived at about 1:00 p.m. We offered him a chair, but he preferred to sit cross-legged on the floor. We had arranged our apartment in typical Druze style, with thin cushions on the floor around the walls.

Our conversation with the Sheikh began where our previous conversation had left off. Sheikh Farhoud had been reading the *Gita* seriously and he had many points he wanted to discuss. He described the spiritual nature of Al-Hakim’s form, as well as many other facets of Druze philosophy. He stressed the importance of devotion, emphasizing that academic knowledge alone is not sufficient to realize God. Sheikh Farhoud shared more than any sheikh had so far, and he was eminently qualified as a leading pundit of the Muwahhidoon to do so.

Although points of agreement predominated, we did touch upon a few variances. The Sheikh pointed out that the Druze would not agree with the *Gita’s* description of souls in human bodies entering animal bodies. I had often discussed this point with Druze. Their argument is that animals don’t know they’re being punished, so it would be useless and unfair for God to punish someone in that way. He would therefore not enact this sort of chastisement. I countered that there are many examples of humans who are suffering but who are not clever enough to realize that they are being castigated, such as mentally retarded people or gross materialists who think they’re happy while sustaining the agonies of a godless life. Also, I requested him to describe the qualitative difference between a human soul and an animal’s life force. Few Druze would attempt to draw this distinction. Usually I would broach such a topic sparingly and only among friends, as this was a sensitive point for the Druze. Also, they weren’t even supposed to have revealed their belief in reincarnation, what to speak of arguing with outsiders about details.

The Druze also believe that, among human souls, there are rules governing transmigration, such as the belief that a soul in a Druze body will always remain in a Druze body. Also, they say that life begins at birth, not at conception.

Another issue I discussed with the Sheikh was the relationship between the personal and impersonal aspects of the Absolute Truth. Sheikh Farhoud understood from *Bhagavad-gita* that Krsna’s form and personality are the source of, and are therefore superior to, the all-pervading impersonal Brahman. He explained that although the body and personality of God’s manifestations in the material world are spiritual and eternal, they emanate from that which is beyond form and quality. Thus according to Vedic understanding, the Druze are Brahmavadis as opposed to Mayavadis. That is, they accept Brahman as the highest realization of the Absolute Truth, but they do not consider the form of God to be *maya,* a manifestation of the material energy.

Regarding confidentiality, Sheikh Farhoud said that theological and mystical truths should not be placed before the masses. Each person should be given the truth according to their individual capacity to understand it, so that he can utilize it for his advancement. We discussed further about how God and his representatives reveal knowledge according to time and circumstance.

Sheikh Farhoud stressed that we must be completely dependent on God. He said that God maintains the animals without them having to pray for it. Thus we shouldn’t pray to God for our daily bread but only to love Him purely.

Sheikh Farhoud revealed that he had not prayed at the *khilwa* for many years. He conducted his prayer sessions at home because he felt the other sheikhs in the village had abandoned the proper standards of spiritual life and ritualistic practice. Before leaving our humble abode, he said, “I’m happy to have found some real spiritual association.”

A few days after the meeting with Sheikh Farhoud, Salach recounted some Druze stories containing the message of pure *bhakti:*

A sheikh who was strict for forty years asked God in his prayers when he would make it to Gan Eden (heaven). God asked if his prayer and following of the rules and regulations had been for the purpose of achieving heaven. “Yes,” replied the sheikh. “So you’ve got a long way to go,” responded God.

A famous Druze woman storyteller prayed to God, “If I pray to You for money or to go to heaven or for anything material, please don’t give it to me. But if I pray out of pure love, please inspire me to continue in this way.”

Salach said he had read in an authorized Druze book that if a Druze is pious, then his next birth may be in “Seen (China).” But the word for China, “Seen,” can also be understood to mean “the moon.” In the *Vedas,* the moon is accepted as an upper planet, where pious people may go after their life on earth. earth.
