Also, Salach had read in the same book that beyond the Himalayas is a region which no one has entered.

January 11, 1990—Maha-Laksmi dasi to Danavir dasa:

Now we are living in the heart of the village Rama. Book distribution is going on nicely, especially to Druze. Many of the people we approach with the Arabic book and the Third Canto already have some of Srila Prabhupada’s books and have read them. During the first weeks we had many curious guests.

Bhakta Salach is doing nicely. He is chanting and reading the books and visiting the temple frequently. On Fridays we have an official program with nice lecture and feast, and there has already been a newspaper article about “the Hares in Rama” (in a Jewish newspaper).

A few days ago we had a very nice visitor—Sheikh Kasem Farhoud Fadood, who is one of the most well-known Druze sheikhs in the whole Middle East. He is younger than Sheikh Amin Tarif (Sheikh Farhoud is eighty years old) and is very energetic and also involved in national politics. After having read the *Gita,* which was given to him by Dhira Govinda Prabhu a week before, he became so excited that he came to our humble house and had a long philosophical conversation with Dhira Govinda Prabhu (about two hrs.). He said that ninety-five percent of what he read was exactly like their Druze religion, that he is happy to have us in the village, and that he finally found good association—someone he can talk to about his realizations and spiritual knowledge. He told us that previously a Christian priest came to talk with him, and in the middle of the conversation Sheikh Farhood became angry at him and walked away, because the priest didn’t have any philosophy. He said that for the past thirty years he didn’t visit the “*helwi*” (their place of worship), being displeased with the religious people in the village. He was also praising our *sari* and saying that the Druze ladies are becoming spoiled and giving up their traditional chaste outfit.

So everything is going on very nicely. D.G. is going to Egypt on the 19th of January for about one week to visit the devotees there as well as to participate in the big book fare with our friend Samih Natoor. I am staying in the meantime in the temple with Santasya and the other devotees, trying my best to assist the mission of Lord Caitanya.

P.P.S. Just now I received your letter of Dec. 30. Indeed we have moved to Rama. It is unimaginable from a social point of view, but not from the point of view of security, since our neighbors are very friendly and we have the full support of Sheikh Farhood.

Some people are a little surprised to see “Jewish” Krsnas in their Arab village, but no one has tried to attack us or anything except for a few naughty kids who threw stones at our door out of childishness.

I will send you the conversation with the Sheikh by separate mail ...

*Visit to Egypt*

Some time past, we received a message from Ananda-svarupa Swami indicating that he was planning to open a center in Egypt. One day, we got a confirmation that he had indeed arrived in Egypt and had brought two other devotees with him. I decided to visit. Maha-Laksmi and I went to Tel Aviv to get my visa. I was traveling on an American passport, but still there were complications because I was not carrying my release papers from the Israeli army. After resolving this problem, I was able to catch a bus to Egypt. Maha-Laksmi stayed behind, because she was in the early stages of pregnancy. After a lengthy delay at Rafah, the occupied town at the Sinai border, our bus crossed into Egypt. Unlike the fertile, rocky desert of Israel, the Sinai was more like one would expect a desert to be: sloping dunes made of fine sand for as far as the eye could see in all directions. Several hours later, we stopped for a break in a small desert town. All the passengers gathered in a coffee shop. Later, the bus was delayed for two hours at the Suez Canal. I remembered that Srila Prabhupada had set foot in northeastern Egypt at Port Said while sailing through the Suez Canal during his initial journey to America. Fourteen hours after leaving Tel Aviv, we arrived in Cairo at 9:00 p.m.

When the bus arrived at my destination, I took the address Ananda-svarupa Swami had sent me and found a cab. After much searching we finally found Hassan Assam Street #5. Gaura-bhakta dasa greeted me at the door. As I entered, I saw Ananda-svarupa Swami, whom I had met once before in India in 1988. He and Gaura-bhakta were wearing yogi pants. They were just ending an evening program, and most of the guests had left. Maharaja was talking to a young, American black man at the dining room table. They happened to be absorbed in the letter package I had sent from Israel two weeks earlier. It had only arrived that day. The package included a copy of an article from *El Ajami,* an Israeli-Arabic paper that had printed a favorable, half-page article about the Hare Krsnas, focusing especially on our recent involvement in the Arabic villages. The article portrayed us as sincere peace workers, and included philosophical explanations of how Krsna devotees regard everyone as spiritually equal. The American guest spoke fluent Arabic and was translating the article for Maharaja.

The six-room apartment, located in the Zamalek section of Cairo, was spacious and contained a few elegant furnishings. Zamalek was a good area, with plenty of foreigners. Despite the good location and fine facility, the cost for such a place was exceptionally low by Israeli or Western standards.

Maharaja described the preaching program in Cairo. Gaura-bhakta was a qualified *hatha-yoga* instructor, and several guests had come to learn yoga exercises. Afterwards, they would discuss philosophy and chant Hare Krsna. They had Arabic copies of *Coming Back* to distribute, and Gaura-bhakta would go out and sell a few each day. He told me one story in which he had been taken to the police station while distributing and, after some interrogation, the police chief had revealed that he was interested in yoga and had taken a book. Gaura-bhakta, raised as a Muslim in Algeria, chanted Muslim prayers once a day. This increased his credibility when distributing books in Egypt.

Egypt has two major population sectors, Muslims and Coptic Christians. Compared to other Arab countries, Egypt is pluralistic and democratic. Maharaja said that the Coptic Christians were especially showing interest in Krsna consciousness. Their tradition recommended fasting from meat six months out of the year.

On my second day in Cairo, I went with Gaura-bhakta to visit the pyramids and the Great Sphinx in Giza. We also visited a large museum of ancient Egyptian culture in Cairo.

Gaura-bhakta, who had for years been preaching undercover in the Muslim world in countries such as Algeria, was enlivened by reports of Arab preaching in Israel and said he would like to visit us. As I mentioned, he is Algerian by birth. Due to Algeria’s connection with PLO activities, Algerian was about the worst nationality to be if someone wanted to visit Israel. But through the Algerian embassy Gaura-bhakta arranged, with difficulty, his papers. Then, together, he and I went to the Israeli embassy. The embassy was located on the 18th floor of a building, a good security measure. There was a heavily guarded elevator that served only the embassy. Seeing Gaura-bhakta’s Algerian passport, the clerk behind the bulletproof glass was shocked. He turned to me and asked in Hebrew, “He wants to visit Israel?!” Obviously, it would not be an easy task for Gaura-bhakta to get permission. It would take time, and we would not be receiving permission on this initial visit.

In the meantime, Ananda-svarupa Maharaja and I discussed Druze preaching. I told him that *Coming Back,* a book explaining in simple terms and from many points of view the concept of reincarnation, would be ideal for the Druze. As there were not many Arabic copies remaining, Maharaja and I agreed to print some in Israel.

I also showed Maharaja the few copies of the new edition of the Arabic *Gita* I had brought with me from Israel. Maharaja was pleased to have them. And he was lucky, because while going through Egyptian customs, the books had aroused some suspicion. The customs officer had sent me to a special room to meet the supervisor. The supervisor had decided the books were innocuous, especially since I was only carrying a few, but he asked a few curious questions.

During the week I visited, there happened to be a huge book fair at the university in Cairo. I decided to visit it. Although I had heard that a Druze friend, Samich Natoor, from Daliyat El Carmel, was going to be selling books there, I could not locate him among the throngs of people and myriad buildings. Through Samich and other Druze book merchants, we hoped to distribute Srila Prabhupada’s books to the Arabic world. Falach Falach, another of Salman’s relatives, ran a book business in Samiah, with contacts in Lebanon. He had given the Arabic *Gita* to some of his Lebanese contacts to research the possibilities.

At the end of the week, I returned to Israel carrying a few items that were common in Egypt but difficult to obtain in Israel—pure ghee and real milk powder. We usually obtained these items from Arabic villages, often through our Palestinian friend Mahmood, but they were not always available. Curiously, although items such as these were abundant in Egypt, foodstuffs such as sugar and flour were rare, and the government tightly rationed available stocks.

A few days after my return, Israel was thunderstruck by news of a terrorist attack that killed several Israelies on an Israeli tour bus in Egypt.

“*Cooloh* \[All of them\] *Muwahhi”,* reflected Hilwah. Maha-Laksmi sat on the porch at the house of Kasem Amin’s mother, atop Rama village. Gazing across the mountainside, spotted with Druze, Muslim, and Jewish towns and villages, the old woman, approaching her nineties, commented on the common, eternal nature of all spirit souls.

The old woman’s two daughters lived in the house with her. One sister, Sheikhhah, had been married by her mother’s arrangement to an elderly man when she was fifteen years old. The man had died before she had borne children. Since then, she had been living peacefully with her mother, serving her needs.

Maha-Laksmi and I would discuss the contrast between Rama’s culture and that of the West. The other sister was almost fifty and had never been married. The house was like an *asrama* for widows and *brahmacarinis.* Maha-Laksmi would describe how these women, especially the old mother, insisted on taking care of her during pregnancy. Bringing her to their home, they made her lie down and eat. Then the mother would walk Maha-Laksmi down the mountain back to Kasem’s place, not minding the return uphill hike. When I had to visit Tel Aviv, the women were concerned that Maha-Laksmi had to stay alone, and they offered her accommodations. Each day, Maha-Laksmi said, they would gather with other Druze women in a small hall and spend hours cutting and preparing *hoobez* and other herbs, spices, and vegetables, for bread-making and the evening meal. We could imagine that the large room in which we lived was formerly used for the same purpose. From the village women Maha-Laksmi learned how to cook with many plants growing wild in Rama. They would also share tidbits of culture and tradition with one another. They told Maha-Laksmi that the custom for giving names to newborns, as in Vedic culture, is to visit a sheikh astrologer, who would suggest the most auspicious first syllable for the baby’s name.

Hussein and his extended family lived in a three-story stone structure across the narrow road from us. Along with Kasem and Adib, Hussein adopted the role of our paternal guardians in the village. Often, when I would return late from *sankirtana,* Sheikhhah, Hussein’s wife, insisted that Maha-Laksmi come to their place so that she wouldn’t be alone. Their home was usually bustling with children and grandchildren. One evening after *sankirtana,* they invited us over, and we showed them the set of books. Hussein gave a generous donation for an Arabic *Bhagavad-gita.*

One of their grandchildren, Salman, a five-year-old boy, seemed especially attracted to Krsna consciousness. He was constantly singing Hare Krsna to the popular tune he heard at our door. I distinctly remember one morning while chanting *japa* in our apartment at about 6:30 a.m. hearing a melodious child’s voice softly singing the *maha-mantra* in a sweet tone. Salman had been sitting on the veranda of his third-floor apartment for more than half an hour, legs hanging over the edge, absorbed in the transcendental sound vibration of Krsna’s name. Many children in the village, especially those in our vicinity, were naturally attracted to Krsna consciousness and to chanting Hare Krsna.

*Life in Rama—by Maha-Laksmi dasi*

\[This story was printed in ISKCON World Review, May–July 1990\]

Everyone—from devotees, friends, and family members, to the village people—was surprised when we decided to move to Rama. The village people were surprised because they were conservative and simple, and families lived in the same villages for generations. But soon everyone got used to the idea, and now we have nice relationships with everyone. Krsna is all-attractive, and the people always greet us in the street with “Hare Krsna,” especially the children.

One afternoon I went to pick flowers for the *arati.* When I returned, there was a knock on the door and about twenty kids were standing there with flowers in their hands as an offering to Krsna. I let them in and gave them *prasadam.* Then they all sat on the rug in a half circle, and one became the leader of the “musical band.” They chanted the holy name to an Arabic melody, clapping their hands. I was happy to see how the new generation of Druze are accepting the holy name and devotional service in such a spontaneous way.

About a week before, we had held a program in the school at Beit Jan, where about forty people (ages eight through forty) had attended. They had chanted in ecstasy, clapping their hands, devoured the *prasadam,* and asked many wonderful questions about the soul and reincarnation. In fact, the conversation had become so lively that the school guard had had to ring the bell many times to bring the meeting to a close. Even then, the people had walked us to the cars and continued talking and exchanging addresses.

I cannot describe all the wonderful questions that were asked, but I’ll give an example: a thirteen-year-old boy approached me with a football in his hands. He had two questions, which he had written down. We needed a translator, because he didn’t speak Hebrew. He asked if the soul carries some conceptions from one body to another. Then he asked that if God is a localized person, how can He simultaneously be present everywhere? This reminded me of the comment H.H. Jayadvaita Maharaja had made after a lecture he gave in Rama: “Such questions have not been asked since the time of the sages of Naimisaranya.” At Maharaja’s lecture, one Druze had raised his hand and asked, “If Bhismadeva was a pure devotee, how come he fought against Krsna?”

One day I went to the head of the Christian school of the village, Mrs. Georgette Awoquian, who had previously purchased two Hebrew sets from my husband, one for the school and one for herself. She is Armenian in origin and had just returned from Armenia, where she did service helping people after the earthquake. She was excited to see that I had two books in Armenian, *Coming Back* and *Isopanishad.* She took them both and invited me in. We had a nice conversation about how society is becoming godless and is bringing its own destruction.

It is amazing to see how even in this most degraded Kali-yuga, there are still pious souls hankering after Krsna’s mercy, and how Srila Prabhupada is supplying it by his unlimited compassion for them.

Om tat sat.

*Coming Back*

Parama-guru, who had been serving in the Tel Aviv temple, joined our party in Rama. The front part of the room became a *brahmacari-asrama,* at least at night, and we used the back for *grhasthas.* Our goal was to print five thousand copies of *Coming Back* in Arabic by April 25, in time for the Nabi Schweib festival, where we planned to sell books.

I began searching for printers. Samich Natoor referred me to a Palestinian printer with whom he had worked in East Jerusalem. Basing myself in Tel Aviv, I drove one morning to Jerusalem and found the print shop. We discussed cost and printing options.

I had with me an Arabic copy of *Coming Back,* based on the sixth English printing, but there were a few ideas I wanted to incorporate in the second printing of the Arabic version. First, I wanted to include a quotation by Kahil Gibran at the beginning of the book. Gibran, best known in the West for his book, *The Prophet,* is greatly respected throughout the Arab world for having made an impact on the international intellectual community. Born in a Lebanese Christian family, his writings are famous around the world for their introspective depth. Maher Faraj, who taught Arabic literature at a high school, was especially eager for me to include the Gibran quote. Maher researched Gibran’s books and found several statements about reincarnation.

Next, I planned to replace one of the pictures of famous persons who believed in reincarnation with a picture of Kamal Jumbalat. I also considered using a quote from Sheikh Amin, but Ali Amin said we could not print a direct quotation from the Sheikh about transmigration, because Druze knowledge is confidential. In any case, to assist the production of a Druze/Arab edition, I decided to remove a picture indicating a human transmigrating into a dog’s body and replace it with another. I made no changes in the text.

East Jerusalem was occupied territory, and susceptible to outbreaks of violence. The day I visited the printer happened to be a strike day, when most proprietors are closed as a protest against the government. I was lucky the print shop had been open. The streets were quiet. I visited the American Hotel, where I had heard that many Palestinian intellectuals congregated. There, I met a few professors who invited me to Ramallah, a large West Bank town with a university. The professors confirmed the recommendations of a Palestinian I had met in Cfar Kara, that is, that I arrive by bus or taxi early in the morning, visit interested members of the academic community, and leave by noon. They indicated that driving to the West Bank in a car with Israeli license plates would be risky (vehicles from the territories have special plates). Also, they said that conflicts between the soldiers and the Palestinians usually erupted in the latter half of the day.

Yosi Mizrachi owned a medium-sized printing business in downtown Tel Aviv and had done work for the Hebrew BBT. He made an offer to get our business on the Arabic *Coming Back*. Although his terms were not as good as those of the Jerusalem printer, we decided to work with him. We had had good experience working with him in the past, and his Tel Aviv location would make traveling to his office easier than going to Jerusalem. Our only concern was that he didn’t speak Arabic. He wouldn’t notice mistakes if they occurred. But Parama-guru and I later visited Yosi to finalize the printing and payment schedules, obliging us to travel from Rama to Tel Aviv every few weeks to check progress.

Parama-guru, who knew some Arabic, noticed during a visit to Yosi in the first week of March that many captions on the pictures were backwards—about to be printed in mirror image. Yosi’s son, who did the layout work, had an office in a different section of Tel Aviv, and we rushed to see him. Under Parama-guru’s direction, a worker made the necessary changes and the book was readied for printing.

On the first of April, exactly on schedule, we loaded the five thousand copies of *Coming Back* into the Suzuki and gave Yosi the final payment. Yosi complimented us on making our payments on time, which he said was a rarity in his work. “One thing about the Hare Krsnas,” he declared to his workers, “is that they keep their word.” Yosi was a philosopher at heart and a traditional Sephardic Jew. Every time we visited, he would keep us for at least an hour and discuss the principles of Krsna consciousness. We would also bring him *prasadam,* and his attachment for devotees grew steadily. We similarly complimented him on his prompt, quality work, and drove to the Frishman Street temple with the Suzuki loaded beyond capacity. After unloading some of the newly printed books in the ground-floor apartment, we returned to Rama.

A Jewish Israeli shot and killed several Palestinians at a bus stop in Rishon Letsione. This story shocked the Israeli nation. Maha-Laksmi was alone in Rama when the news hit. She related the following:

*“I was alone for two days. The first night, Hilwa invited me to stay at their* brahmacarini-asrama. *The next morning there was the news about the incident in Rishon Letsione. In the Christian section of Rama, they held demonstrations, shouting into megaphones. Everyone was very disturbed.*”

Many Israeli Arab villages, what to speak of those in occupied territory, were extremely agitated and causing disturbances, even to the point of rioting. Whereas most Israeli Arab villages did not openly identify with the Palestinian cause—because they wanted to maintain working relations with the Israeli government—all of them were now unanimously enraged, and many held demonstrations and vigils. Even in Druze villages, whose citizens served in the Israeli army, the people were publicly denouncing the tragedy and speaking sympathetically of the Palestinians.

Although the Druze, led by Sheikh Amin, cultivated their political position in Israel carefully, sometimes their Druze or Arab sentiments would override this consideration. For example, after Kamal Jumbalat was murdered in Lebanon, there was unabashed public mourning in the Israeli Druze community, although Jumbalat often spoke favorably of the Palestinian cause.

Returning to Rama, we had to pass through the Muslim town of Magd El Kurum, near Karmiel. We had heard on the radio that villagers were stopping cars and causing disturbances in Magd El Kurum. We hung a *masbachah* on the van’s front mirror. I had received the beads as a gift from a teenager in the town of Judeida. It was the custom among Muslims to hang chanting beads on their car’s front mirror. Our doing so identified our vehicle as one not to be tampered with in the Arab villages. From that time on, we kept those beads on the car mirror when driving through Arab villages, and our anxiety was relieved. At times such as these, we also appreciated the value of wearing devotional dress in Arab villages. Dressed as Krsna devotees, we could go anywhere and avoid identification with any political side. We were on Krsna’s side, and we had nothing to do with mundane politics. When drawn into a political discussion, devotees would carefully avoid partisanism. “The land doesn’t belong to the Arabs and it doesn’t belong to the Jews,” I would often explain. “The land belongs to God, and our responsibility is to use it in His service.” Everyone in the Arab or Druze community respected and agreed with this point of view.

We appreciated the warm familial relations of the Arab extended family. Once, a young Christian man living at the top of Pekiin, invited Maha-Laksmi and I to his home. Lying on his couch was an old man who was dying. We discussed Krsna consciousness with the men, and the younger man purchased an Arabic *Bhagavad-gita.* We were impressed with the way the family cared for this elderly man. They did not try to hide him away. Old age and death are accepted in the Arab community as inevitable parts of life. In such a culture, the ultimate importance of spiritual life is apparent.

Another aspect of Arab culture: Sometimes Salach would knock at our door. If I was still out on *sankirtana* and Maha-Laksmi was alone, he would take three steps back, state his business, and depart. He said that Kamal Jumbalat described this as proper etiquette.

Once, Maha-Laksmi visited the sweet shop in Rama. She was impressed with the *baklawah* and asked the shop owner for the recipe. He said his son does the cooking and he would have to show her how to prepare the sweet. But the owner was disinclined toward this, stating that it would not be respectable for a fifteen-year-old boy to be with a woman in the shop kitchen.

*Marvelous Mehenni*

Since my return from Egypt in February, we had worked to bring Gaura-bhakta dasa to Israel. He was also working on the problem from his side through the Egyptian branch of the Algerian and Israeli embassies. We continued to wade through Israeli bureaucracy on our other side of the border. Mother Santasya made strenuous efforts from Tel Aviv, phoning and faxing embassies and ministries in Tel Aviv, Jerusalem, and Egypt, and I did the same from Amin’s phone in Rama. We would often receive a confirmation, only to have it denied by a different agency. At one point, Gaura-bhakta’s entrance seemed arranged; he had all the necessary documentation. We informed Gaura-bhakta to pick up his visa at the Israeli embassy. When he arrived, however, they claimed to know nothing about it. We received another confirmation, and a high-ranking officer in Jerusalem personally promised to contact the Israeli embassy in Egypt to ensure successful processing of the papers, but again Gaura-bhakta received nothing but cold stares at the Israeli embassy. In the end, we did manage to cut through the bureaucracy and Gaura-bhakta received a one-month visa.

Suddha-sattva dasa had planned to visit Egypt, but had delayed his trip because of the recent terrorist attack on an Israeli tour bus in Egypt. Now, he thought it would be a good time to go. He traveled to Egypt and stayed with Gaura-bhakta dasa on Hassan Assam Street. They planned to return to Israel together. Suddha-sattva reported that the customs officials at the Israeli border were incredulous at the idea of allowing Gaura-bhakta, Mehenni, to enter the country. They didn’t care what papers he had, they had no intention of permitting an Algerian into Israel. Suddha-sattva made a scene—Israelis are adept at such things—on Gaura-bhakta’s behalf. Gaura-bhakta was a great yoga teacher—didn’t they know that? So many places in Israel had already arranged for him to teach. Who were they to stop him?! After delaying the bus for several hours and searching Gaura-bhakta and his luggage threadbare, the Israeli customs officers found nothing of particular interest and finally allowed Gaura-bhakta in.

March 6, 1990—Gaura-bhakta dasa (Cairo) to Dhira Govinda dasa (Rama):

This morning I went to Israel embassy to ask for a visa. The same lady read the Hebrew letter and told me: this is good, but why this letter is addressed to Paris? So she wants the same letter but addressed to Cairo. She tried her best to help me, but I think their Israeli authorities are strict. But she told me that she will try to give me the visa the same day when I will receive the same letter (permission) to Cairo. She wrote this name in English for me: Foyo Kozine 57 Frishman St. Tel Aviv. Also, she gave me number of Israel Embassy Consular Dept. in Cairo 726000 729329.

My plan is—to wish to come to Israel as soon as possible I mean when Maharaja will be back. End of March. The 23rd of April, ’90. I have to go to France. So if everything is OK, then I can come for two or three weeks. I am sorry to give you troubles for the visa. Hoping everything will be fine by Krsna’s mercy. The preaching is going nice. More people are coming—they like *bhajanas* and philosophy. All my *dandavats* to Israeli-yatra devotees.

March 31, 1990—Gaura-bhakta dasa (Cairo) to Dhira Govinda dasa (Rama):

I got your telegram on Thursday, 22–3–90. On Sunday 25–3–90 I went to Israeli Embassy. I showed her the telegram. She looked in the mail and could not find the letter. So she told me to come back in the weekend. On Thursday 29–3–90 I phoned the Israeli Embassy. There is no visa permission from Israel for me. So I do not know how many days letter take from Tel Aviv to Cairo. I think no more than one week. What did you send? A letter? A telex? I do not know what to do because the time is going and my departure day to France is coming close. I have to be in Cairo before 27 April. So if I can come and spend two or three weeks, that will be very nice. Please do the necessary. I am eager to come and preach to the Druze people. Maybe you should insist that they should do telex or telegram for you, because it’s coming late.

P.S. Ananda-svarupa Swami is coming on 10th April, and he told me I can go to Israel before his coming. So I am ready to come. I am just waiting for the permission.

Gaura-bhakta is an enthusiastic devotee dedicated to Arabic preaching. Israel was exciting for him, because it was both a new culture compared with the Arabic and Western countries he had visited and it provided an opportunity to present Krsna consciousness directly to an Arab population. Although his Algerian, Barboor Arabic was different from the dialect spoken in Israel, he knew enough classical Arabic to communicate well with Israeli Arabs and Druze.

After a brief stay in Tel Aviv, we drove Gaura-bhakta to Rama. On the way, we stopped in Osafia and visited Salim Zedan, a prominent Druze intellectual. Gaura-bhakta was impressed with his first Druze encounter. Salim spoke extensively about Druze culture and his appreciation for the *Bhagavad-gita* and the Hare Krsna movement’s preaching mood.

Rama residents went wild over Gaura-bhakta, whom they came to know as Mehenni. They had, of course, already developed a deep appreciation for Krsna consciousness and Vaisnavas. Still, because we spoke in a different language, we were foreigners, and some distance still existed in our relationships with the Ramabasis. But this was not the case with Gaura-bhakta. Krsna consciousness presented in Arabic by a native Arab—what could be more exciting? Amin Kasem responded immediately to Gaura-bhakta.

During Gaura-bhakta’s second evening in Rama, we hosted a Friday program. Our Druze *bhaktas* were enthralled to speak with Gaura-bhakta, who gave the lecture. As all the *bhaktas* were attracted to the physical yoga process, his expertise in that field added an additional attraction to his personality. Gaura-bhakta could not leave our one-room apartment without being mobbed, especially by the children under twelve. Trying to take a *japa* walk or go on *sankirtana,* he would instantly be swarmed by dozens of children shouting, “Mehenni! Mehenni!” He would entertain them in various ways, ultimately getting them to chant Hare Krsna in chorus. Never was Rama as Krsna conscious as during his visit. All our Rama friends came to see him, and his departure was hard for many of them to bear.

We did manage to go outside Rama for *sankirtana* excursions during Gaura-bhakta’s visit. He witnessed and participated in the distribution of many books and several sets to Druze, Christians, and Muslims in villages such as Abu Snan, Ain El Assad, and Nazareth. Also, during his one-week visit, Mother Santasya, traveling between Haifa (at Isvara Krsna’s new location) and Rama, distributed two thirty-book English *Srimad-Bhagavatam* sets to Muslims in surrounding villages. Such events enthused all the devotees, especially Gaura-bhakta, who had been preaching undercover in the Muslim world for years.

Maher arranged a program at the high school in Beit Jann. The program was specifically for the theatre group he directed, but was open for the whole school to attend. We prepared *prasadam* and drove up the mountain to Beit Jann, where Parama-guru and I had distributed one Hebrew set and fifteen Arabic *Gitas* earlier that day. While other villages were occasionally uneventful, Beit Jann unfailingly provided new book distribution adventures on each visit.

Six chairs were lined up in the front of the classroom for the panel of devotees, while a throng of high school students crowded a room that held about forty people. Maher introduced us in Arabic, praising the Hare Krsna movement for its fine work in helping humanity. Gaura-bhakta declined to give the lecture, reasoning that I knew more about the Druze mentality. I spoke in Hebrew, briefly explaining the history of the *sankirtana* movement from Lord Krsna through Srila Prabhupada, as well as the purpose of yoga and the principles of transmigration. The students were scrupulously attentive and were prepared with formidable questions. For the first time, the issue of the soul’s transmigration to animal species was discussed in a public forum among Druze. At times, the discussion became heated, as the issue sparked deep emotions among many. One girl walked out. Ultimately, the crowd respected the arguments and authority of the *Bhagavad-gita,* although for most it was too difficult to overcome the way they had been taught since birth.

One seventeen-year-old boy presented communist views for human improvement and questioned the relevance of God. We explained that communism is a perfect philosophy when God, and not an imperfect state with imperfect leaders, is at the center. Maher chimed in that Kamal Jumbalat postulated spiritual communism. During the discussion, many points became so technical that I had trouble following them; my Hebrew just wasn’t developed enough. At such times, Isvara Krsna and others carried the dialogue. Gaura-bhakta did not speak much Hebrew either, but he understood the essence of what was transpiring.

After the discussion, we served *prasadam* and mingled with the guests. They were surprised and delighted to meet a Hare Krsna who spoke Arabic as his mother tongue. The students asked him intelligent, probing questions for almost an hour, and many of them insisted that the devotees remain in close contact with them. Gaura-bhakta exchanged addresses with several teenagers. All the devotees were amazed at the quality and profundity of the young audience and left the school building with an ecstasy that comes from sharing Krsna consciousness with sincere and intelligent people.

*Festival at Nabi Schweib, April 21, 1990*

A few days before the festival at Nabi Schweib, we went with Gaura-bhakta to visit Sheikh Amin. Ali Amin was favorable to the idea of an Arabic-speaking Krsna devotee meeting the Sheikh. This was the final day it would be possible, because soon Druze Israel, and especially Sheikh Amin, would be flooded by Lebanese pilgrims coming for the holy celebration. Already there were many Lebanese sheikhs visiting Julis, and some were with Sheikh Amin as we entered his sitting room.

Gaura-bhakta kissed the Sheikh’s hand, as is the Druze custom. They talked in Arabic for about twenty minutes. Sheikh Amin’s hearing had become weak, and Ali had to repeat everything close to his ear to clarify Gaura-bhakta’s points. In attendance was a young Lebanese sheikh who knew something about yoga and who asked several questions. Then they questioned Gaura-bhakta about a tribe in Algeria they suspected were members of the Muwahhidoon.

Salach had taken Maha-Laksmi and I to visit the Nabi Schweib memorial soon after we moved to Rama. After navigating the road from Rama to Mount Arbel, the “Masada of the North,” we made a final downward turn into a small valley. Nabi Schweib’s tomb, an irregular conglomeration of a few rectangular-shaped buildings, appeared at the bottom of our vision. We parked the van in the large empty lot. Having interacted extensively and intimately with the Druze, we were exhilarated to see the holy monument. On the side opposite the memorial building were a few Druze souvenir stands selling varieties of Druze memorabilia. Most popular were portraits and photographs of Sheikh Amin, sometimes with the four other most esteemed sheikhs in the Druze world.

Five is an auspicious number for the Druze—five colors, five prophets, five horses, etc. Although they didn’t often discuss it, there was an underlying assumption—or hope—that Sheikh Amin was an incarnation of Nabi Schweib, and that the other four leading sheikhs were the other four major prophets. Many great spiritual personalities in history, such as Jesus Christ, are reputed by the Druze to be incarnations of one of the five prophets. On more than a few occasions, Druze who studied ISKCON’s books made comments of this nature about Srila Prabhupada. For a Vaisnava, of course, it is not inherently significant that a group called the Druze may consider an empowered *acarya* to be an incarnation of one of their prophets, but it is exciting that the group has developed such an admiration for a pure devotee of Krsna. Also, it is natural to conjecture hopefully about the effect that such respect and admiration can have on uniting religions under one theme, especially considering Sheikh Amin’s statement that he would like the Druze people to work together with the Hare Krsna movement “as one race.”

Other popular items at the souvenir stores included various depictions of the five horses and motifs of the Nabi Schweib memorial building. By modern standards, the memorial building could not be considered impressive in size, architecture or aesthetics. Connected to the main building were a few residential guesthouses, seemingly slapped on at different times according to need and financial availability. The buildings were constructed like other structures in Druze villages—simple and solid, with a timeless look about them.

The main building was the holiest place on earth for the Muwahiddoon. About three stories high, the dome on top of the roof signified the burial place of a great saint. Salach took us to the top-floor veranda. We removed our shoes and followed the directions of a sign instructing us not to step on the crosspiece at the doorway. Inside was an ornately decorated rectangular structure: Nabi Schweib’s tomb. Scores of colorful garments, given as gifts by pilgrims, covered the prophet’s burial site. At the far end of the room were Nabi Schweib’s footprints engraved in gold. We offered obeisances and took dust from the footprints on our heads, which had to be covered according to Druze etiquette when visiting a holy place. Salach and the few other pilgrims kissed their hands and touched the tomb. After this, Salach introduced us to an old sheikh from Beit Jann, who served as caretaker for Nabi Schweib.

After our visit to Nabi Schweib, we went door-to-door with Salach in the village of Kisra. Salach was helpful to our *sankirtana,* especially in dealing with the children. We hadn’t visited Kisra often, and we were still somewhat of an oddity there, but the children immediately respected Salach’s command not to disturb the *sankirtana*. Also, it was impressive for the villagers to meet a Druze *sankirtana* devotee. That day we distributed a few books to a primary school manager.

*April 23, 1990, Evening*

We had heard varying reports about when the festivities at Nabi Schweib would begin and when it would be worthwhile for us to arrive there, but everyone agreed that the most important day was April 25th. Parama-guru and I left Rama at about 5:00 p.m. on the 23rd with a van full of books. We picked up Gaura-bhakta at the Tiberias train station. He had been to Jerusalem with Suddha-sattva. The lot at the festival site was significantly more crowded than on my previous visit, although still not ten percent full. Several Druze families, many from Lebanon, were setting up cooking and overnight facilities for the festival, some in vans and some in the guesthouses. We parked our van and opened the back, but we didn’t attract much attention. Then all three of us took carts and bags of books and wandered around the site.

None of us succeeded in distributing any books, so as night fell, we set up a book table. The evening wore on and we were just about to pack up and leave when a Druze businessman from Osafia, trying to perform as much *punya* as possible during his three days at the celebration, donated forty shekels for an Arabic *Bhagavad-gita.* Then we returned to Rama.

*April 24, 1990*

Although we weren’t encouraged by the results of the previous evening’s book distribution, we resigned ourselves to devoting the next two days to the Nabi Schweib festival. On the morning of April 24 the crowd at the festival grounds was only a little larger than it had been the night before, although cars full of sheikhs began steadily to arrive. Like the evening before, we alternated between standing behind a book table and roaming around the grounds, again with little success. At one point, the three of us were on the veranda on the level below the prophet’s tomb. Many sheikhs had congregated and were inquiring about yoga and Krsna consciousness. Their questions were challenging, but they appreciated thoughtful, philosophical answers. The young, thin sheikh from Lebanon that we had met at Sheikh Amin’s was present in the gathering, and he took the opportunity to ask Gaura-bhakta to demonstrate some *hatha-yoga* postures. Gaura-bhakta obliged. Ali and Mwafak Amin were also there, and they expressed sincere gratitude that we had come to the festival. By the end of the day we had established and strengthened many relations among the Druze but sold few books. By late evening, the lot was more than half full, and carloads of sheikhs were still arriving when we returned to Rama.

*April 25, 1990*

Although we left at 8:00 a.m. the next morning to head to Nabi Schweib, we didn’t manage to avoid the traffic jam leading to the tomb. Hundreds of cars were trying to squeeze into the lot, which was already full. No one seemed to be arranging for people to park here or there, but there was an aisle reserved for parades and other parts of the festivities. And once in the lot, it was impossible to leave; too many incoming cars were blocking the entrance, and parked cars blocked any possibility of finding the road by another route. No one was going to get past the throng of people and their vehicles until at least 5:00 p.m.

We parked the Suzuki near the lot’s entrance, about halfway across the width of the asphalt field where there was still a clearing. After opening up the van’s back hatch, we set up a book table toward the van’s rear. Unlike the disinterest we had experienced over the previous two days, crowds of sheikhs approached the table and showed interest in everything we had. Many Druze booksellers also had tables selling religious literature. Parama-guru set prices and spoke to several people at once. It was a very hot day, almost 30 degrees C in the morning, and I was sitting in the van’s front seat drinking water. Actually, I didn’t have much confidence that this day’s results would be different from the previous days’. Within a few minutes, however, Parama-guru and Gaura-bhakta had sold several *Gitas* and *Coming Backs.*

Many sheikhs from Lebanon and Ramat Ha Golan, who were not familiar with the devotees, showed special interest and purchased books. Meanwhile, throughout the day, the festivities involving Druze leaders and dignitaries from the Israeli government were held on the main stage, just below Nabi Schweib’s shrine. One young, intellectual-looking Israeli sheikh, who had studied psychology for a year at the university, approached us and asked about the Vedic idea of transmigration from human to animal species. At one point, his words were almost derisive; he was confident that our idea was primitive and that it had no place among serious philosophers. But as we closely analyzed his arguments and supported the Vedic conclusion with reasoning he couldn’t refute, he became respectful and his aggression vanished. Others in the crowd appreciated the interaction and both inquired further and bought books.

Gaura-bhakta was helpful in communicating with the Lebanese sheikhs, who generally spoke Arabic and French, but not English or Hebrew. By the end of the day, we had distributed many *Gitas* and *Coming Backs* to the Lebanese. We hoped these books would find their way to other parts of the Arab world. Falach Falach, our book merchant friend from Samiah, told us that Lebanon is an intellectual center for the Arabic world. He said they might print the *Bhagavad-gita* there at an inexpensive price and distribute it throughout the Middle East.

By late afternoon we were running out of Arabic *Gitas.* The favorable response to the books had caught us by surprise. I maneuvered my way out of the lot while Parama-guru and Gaura-bhakta continued to preach and distribute from the table. At Rama I met Maha-Laksmi and told her the good news. Then I loaded a few more cases of *Gitas* into the van and back to Nabi Schweib and the sea of friendly sheikhs.

The following article appeared in the May 1990 issue of the “World Sankirtana Newsletter” under the title, “Druze News from Israel”:

Since last December, Maha-Laksmi and I have been living in the auspiciously named Israeli-Arab village, Rama. Our preaching there has been supported by book distribution to the Arabs in the area, mostly Druze. In 1988, the Israeli devotees were delighted to discover the Druze’s enthusiasm to take sets of Srila Prabhupada’s books in Hebrew, as they appreciated the great similarity between the Vedic teachings and their own. Since then, editions of *Bhagavad-gita As It Is* and *Coming Back* have been printed in their native Arabic and the Druze are even more eager to read these books.

We had been hearing for months about the annual Druze festival on April 25, held at the burial site of their greatest prophet, Schweib (known in English as Jethro, and considered in the Judeo-Christian tradition to be the father-in-law of Moses). The event marks the most signficiant day in the Druze year, and is attended by their most esteemed religious leaders (sheikhs).

On the morning of the festival I was joined by Parama-guru dasa from Isreal and Gaura-bhakta dasa, an Algerian-born devotee visiting Israel from Cairo. Thousands of Druze sheikhs from Israel, Lebanon, and the Golan Heights crowded the holy site, clothed from head to toe in their traditional religious attire. We set up a book table and they quickly gathered around the devotees. Just to see Hare Krsna devotees, a non-Druze group, attending their festival, was remarkable for the sheikhs to behold—what to speak of the fact that the group was selling religious and philosophical literature. Lively philosophical discussions ensued and continued throughout the day, including topics such as the nature of the soul’s transmigration and the descent of God to this planet. Parama-guru dasa sold several *Gitas* in the first few minutes. Far from feeling threatened or being dogmatic in their thinking, the sheikhs proved to be sincerely curious, open-minded, and appreciative of our presence at their sacred event. Gaura-bhakta dasa, an Arabic speaker, was of great value in communicating with the Lebanese Druze, who generally speak neither Hebrew nor English. By the end of the day, many *Bhagavad-gitas* and *Coming Backs* had entered the Druze religious community. Many of these books will cross the Israeli border and enter into one of the last great frontiers on the plaent yet to be flooded by the mercy of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s *sankirtana* movement—the Arab countries.

Sri Krsna Sankirtana *ki jaya*! Transcendental book distribution *ki jaya*!

Dhira Govinda dasa

Israel

Once, in the Muslim village of Tamra, I presented the Arabic *Gita* to the high school librarian. Pointing to the five-book Hebrew set already on the shelves, he said, “We’ve had these books for a year. No one reads them. I’m a little interested personally, but I don’t think it’s worth it for the library.” I tried to convince him that students would be more inclined to read the book if the library stocked the *Bhagavad-gita* in Arabic. He wasn’t swayed. I suggested that he take the book for himself, but he said he was satisfied reading the Hebrew version. After talking to him for more than half an hour, I could see my attempts to sell him the book was fruitless.

Suddenly, the buzzer sounded, ending the class period. Several students wandered into the library, among them a twelve-year-old Muslim girl who approached the “Religion” shelf, chose *Krsna*, sat down nearby, and began reading with concentration. The librarian had insisted that the students had no interest in our books. “She looks pretty interested,” I remarked. As the girl continued to turn pages, the librarian watched her and finally agreed to take an Arabic *Bhagavad-gita* for the library.

By now, practically every Arab and Druze library in Israel had at least one set of Srila Prabhupada’s books in Hebrew. Most Druze schools and libraries had two—one they had purchased for themselves and one that was sent to them by Salman Falach. The Israeli devotees were enlivened to read what Srila Prabhu-pada had once told the Library Party: placing his books in public libraries was a “great victory.”

One day, Kasem Amin told us some of his experiences in the Israeli army. He said the soldiers spoke scurrilously about women and especially about women soldiers. Kasem said he didn’t engage in such talks, because “these women are someone’s sisters, and I wouldn’t want someone talking about my sister in that way.” We went on to discuss how the Druze youth were becoming distanced from their culture by their association with non-Druze in the army and universities.

Magid Kasem, Amin Kasem’s younger cousin, was a journalist for *Diwali,* the newspaper for the Israeli Druze community. He expressed an interest in interviewing us; he wanted to write an article about our relationship with the Druze. I visited Magid several times at his home, which was at the corner of the road leading to Beit Jann and Rama’s main business route. Magid was fascinated by what he heard about the history and philosophy of the Krsna consciousness movement, especially when he heard about our meetings with Sheikh Amin. “There’ll never be another sheikh like Sheikh Amin,” he mused.

Somewhat of a visionary, Magid began speaking about the establishment of a geographical Druze nation. He became so excited by what he was saying that he proposed that the Druze and the Hare Krsnas create it together. A Hare Krsna nation of Druzes, or a Druze nation of Hare Krsnas—either would be nice, we agreed.

Magid then told me stories about Fahr El Din, a Druze Lebanese ruler from the seventeenth century. I had heard of Fahr El Din from Salach and others, and knew that Druze influence had peaked during his reign. Although Fahr El Din was Druze, he is also honored by Muslims and Christians, who consider him the father of Lebanese nationalism. (Many, however, consider this idea anachronistic: Fahr El Din ruled during the early seventeenth century, before nationalism had been conceptualized in the Middle East.)

Although the Druze enjoy reminiscing about Fahr El Din, there has not been in modern history a serious political attempt to create a Druze nation as there has been with the Jews and the Armenians.

Magid wrote the article and *Diwali* printed it on the front page, with a picture of Isvara Krsna and me sitting with Sheikh Amin at his home. Amin and Naaeem Kasem were awed. A simple Druze couple living humbly with their five children in Rama, they never imagined that their home could become the center of such attraction. The article described how we lived and practiced Krsna consciousness in their ground-floor room. For months, of course, they had been answering questions about us and explaining us to their friends, but now the entire Israeli Druze community was aware of and interested in their tenants.

We obtained copies of the *Diwali* and made it part of our preaching kit. For a few weeks, we distributed copies of the article to anyone who showed special interest in what we were doing. When we appeared at their doors, Druze families who had read the article greeted us as members of the Muwahhidoon and bought Srila Prabhupada’s books.

Adding to the impact of the Hare Krsna–Druze connection was an article printed next to the one about us. This article both pictured and described a Druze shrine connected with the activities of Al-Hakim in Cairo, Egypt. From the report of several Druze who had visited there, a group of Indian spiritualists thought to be a Muwahhidoon lineage were presiding over the place. Druze often asked us, when they suspected that we were related to India in some way, whether we knew of a race called the Bharatas. There was a rumor that in India a group called the Bharatas were actually Druze.

*Letters*

April 19, 1990—Ravindra-svarupa dasa \[Philadelphia\] to Dhira Govinda dasa \[Rama\]:

I am very encouraged to hear about your interesting preaching among the Druze. During our big protest march and Ratha-yatra in Washington, D.C. on April 1st, I met a Druze on the Capitol Mall who was very enlivened to see us. He said that he had heard that their leaders were very interested in and favorable toward Krsna consciousness. You can see that word is getting around. Keep up the good work.

I have had one long meeting with Nurit Zeidman, who has been accepted into the doctoral program in sociology at Temple University. She is amazed at the differences between Krishna consciousness in the U.S.A. and Israel. \[Note: Nurit Zeidman is an Israeli who lived with the devotees in Israel as part of her research for a master’s degree in Anthropology. Then, for her doctoral degree in Anthropology, she studied the Vaisnava community in Philadelphia with the assistance of Ravindra-svarupa Prabhu.\]

May 9, 1990—Parama-guru dasa \[Rama\] to Danavir dasa:

On 25th of April the place became crowded with hundreds of cars and thousands of sheikhs, religious Druze in traditional costumes. We met a few sheikhs from Lebanon. We gave many *Bhagavad-gitas* in Arabic through the early afternoon. We gained one important goal of being recognized as very close to the Druze belief that sparked great interest. Some of the books are in Lebanon by now. GB Prabhu had common language with them. They seemed to know a group of villages near his birthplace that are considered to be Druze villages. The most amazing thing is that they allowed us, as non-Druze, to sell religious books at their most religious place on their most religious day. That’s Krsna’s mercy. Krsna also facilitates my attachment to preach at festivals. I gave about fifteen big books and many small.

There is a fantastic story about the Nabi. He buried his body in his following incarnation. He left his body by stepping on a snake—there is the footprint in the rock and a crack as a sign that body waited standing until he incarnated again and buried it near the Sea of Galilee. This prophet is the central of their “Panca-tattva”five prophets symbolized by five horses in five colors. It has to do with the doctrine of the “Day of Judgment.”

One of the Lebanese leaders, Jumbalat, wrote in a book that according to Druze scripture, around the year 2000, the new cycle of initiation will open after being closed for the last one thousand years since the Druze religion had been established. I suggest it refers to Prabhupada. They think their real number to be one billion scattered all over the world. They are searching. One Israeli official from Beit Jan is going to meet the Dalai Lama passing through India. Dhira Govinda supplied him with a lot of centers and an introduction letter. I hope it will be of ...

The Druze community was developing a growing interest about the connection between the Muwahhidoon and India, so the Dalai Lama sent a representative to Israel to meet with Sheikh Amin and other prominent sheikhs to discuss questions of common origin. Newspapers, radio, and television all reported the story. Concurrently, the Israeli government presented Sheikh Amin with a yearly peace prize for being the Israeli who was most effective in promoting the cause of peace. Sheikh Amin’s prominence in the Israeli consciousness made the Dalai Lama affiliation more newsworthy. The Druze, pleased that their affiliation with Eastern culture was receiving favorable national publicity, became even more enthusiastic for the association of the devotees and Srila Prabhupada’s books.

When we received a bulk shipment of the October ’89 issue of *Back to God-head*, with a picture and article about Sheikh Amin, the Druze were excited. Ali Amin wanted several copies to show the Sheikh and others. They were happy about the international exposure. During *sankirtana,* we often showed that *BTG*. Unsophisticated Druze would become wide-eyed to see their sheikh in a slick magazine produced in a place they had only heard about called the United States.

I remember on one occasion when I presented the *BTG* to an old Druze woman, she confidently took the magazine, opened to a page at random, and attempted to read it. She didn’t know she was holding the publication upside down. I told her, in my broken Arabic, to turn to page twenty-three, then helped her locate the page. Disbelieving what she saw, she turned the publication right-side-up and kissed the picture of Sheikh Amin. She insisted on keeping the magazine, and gave a five-shekel donation for it.

*Assad Assad*

Before moving to Rama, Maha-Laksmi met Assad Assad while distributing books in Karmiel. She relates the story as follows:

*Santasya and I were visiting offices of Druze lawyers and accountants in Karmiel. In one office, the manager took a set. An employee asked, “Do you have a set for me?” I rushed to the van to get another set. As I was opening the back of the van, someone came running out to me. “You’re the one with the books! I want to buy a set. Do you have it here?” I took a set from the back of the van and gave it to him. Leaning against the car, the man wrote a check. Then he told me that his name was Assad Assad and that he worked in the military. After he left, I returned to the office and delivered the other set. Three sets in ten minutes. I told them about Assad. The office workers told me that Assad Assad was the top Druze in the military and that he used to work at the United Nations in New York.*

We heard that Israel was planning to send Assad Assad to India to visit the Dalai Lama, as a Druze envoy exchanging friendship and intercultural dialogue. As chance would have it, Assad happened to be a cousin of Naeemah Amin Kasem’s wife, who hailed from Beit Jann, Assad’s home village. Thus Assad would occasionally visit the Kasems, and we would speak with him during those visits. After performing *sankirtana* in Beit Jann, I would often visit Assad’s home with letters and pictures depicting the affiliation between Hare Krsnas and the Druze, including our talks with Sheikh Amin and Salman Falach. Assad was usually only home a few days a month, because he spent most of his time in Jerusalem, so I didn’t always find him in. Once, however, I found him at his home. He invited me in to talk, although he had only a few minutes before he was scheduled to give a seminar to a group of Israeli government inspectors. Assad was interested in the Hare Krsna movement and asked several questions based on what he had read in our books. Then the inspectors began to arrive. They were surprised to see Assad, whom they had traveled so far to hear, discussing philosophy with a Hare Krsna devotee. Assad introduced Maha-Laksmi and me and began his seminar.

After that day, I extended a formal invitation to Assad to visit us. He accepted the invitation and arrived one evening at our humble center in Rama. He brought with him an education inspector from Beit Jann—someone we had met in Salman Falach’s office. Maha-Laksmi cooked a feast for them. For more than two hours we discussed the Vedic role of the *ksatriyas* and the *Bhagavad-gita’s* explanation about the soul’s transmigration. Assad also accepted a copy of the *Back to Godhead* issue containing the article about the devotees and the Druze, as well as other books to complete his set. We showed him the address list and encouraged him to visit our centers during his visit to India.

During one of our visits to Assad’s house, I met Muadi, a young military career man who was like a protege of Assad. He lived with his family about one hundred meters from Assad on the slopes of Beit Jann. Muadi enjoyed discussing philosophy, history, and politics, and eagerly bought all of Srila Prabhupada’s books in Hebrew and Arabic. He stressed that these books would be a valuable asset for his children’s education and cultural development. Also, he explained many things about the Druze and the Arab world. He told me that in many Arab countries they teach in the schools that Druze are vicious people with long tails. Because of such prejudice, Druze are careful about revealing their identity in Muslim countries.

One evening I decided to distribute books in Rama. I was roaming near the top of the village when I knocked on a door. To my surprise, Kamil opened the door. He had been recently married and had moved out of his parents’ house. I was invited into the kind of informal gathering that forms the basis of village social life. In the living room sat Kamil’s younger brother, who was in his mid-twenties, Sheikh Farhoud, and a teenaged Druze boy. They happened to be discussing God’s nature. Specifically, they were contemplating the interrelationship between God’s omnipotence and the human being’s free will. This led to discus-sions about the nature of evil and the proper use of human intelligence. I enjoyed being with Sheikh Farhoud again, and found their discussion stimulating. Mostly, I was struck by the naturalness with which such discussions tended to transpire—in relaxed, unplanned gatherings of a few villagers, such topics were obviously common topics for discussion.

Habis was an elderly Christian gentleman who had retired from his work at the oil refineries. He was an intellectual and an avid reader. Maha-Laksmi had met him while shopping, and he was extremely curious about our lifestyle and beliefs. He became a regular visitor to our Rama temple. A devout Christian, he had read a few books about Jesus’ travels to India. He firmly believed in this and, wanting to know more about Indian philosophy and culture, purchased an Arabic *Bhagavad-gita* and read it scrutinizingly.

Once, while rolling my *sankirtana* cart along the narrow paths and alleys of Maghar, several children called me excitedly. They indicated that someone wanted to see me. Although the children were by now friendly, I still decided that they were joking and were trying to lead me on a wild goose chase around the village. Still, as I lacked a more concrete plan, I decided to follow them.

After climbing village roads and carrying the cart down rugged paths, the children and I arrived at a door. A woman in her late twenties or early thirties, wearing Western-style clothing, invited me in. It was her parents’ home. In her mother’s presence she explained that she had seen me walking in the village and had felt compelled to tell me how much her life had been influenced by the books we were distributing. She had received a set more than a year ago, either from her school or from a devotee, and had read all five books (*Bhagavad-gita,* Krsna book, the First Canto of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam,* and the *Caitanya-caritamrta’s Adi-lila* in two parts). Since completing the books, she had decided to follow all four regulative principles and had developed a desire to understand more about chanting the *maha-mantra.* She asked if we had cassettes of the *maha-mantra* she could hear. Her name was Samira, and she was a primary school teacher. At one point she had decided to lead the life of a renunciate, but she had recently had a dream that directed her to marry Maher Faraj, our friend in Rama.

Another interesting personality in Maghar was the pastor of the largest church. He gladly invited me into the church and eagerly wanted to hear about Krsna consciousness. After hearing, he instructed the church treasury to issue a generous donation in exchange for two copies of the Arabic *Bhagavad-gita.* Then, after perusing the Arabic “Purity is the Force” flyer, he complimented us on our strict principles and commented on the lack of morals in the modern age.

Palestinian merchants would regularly sell their wares, from fresh produce to used furniture, in the Israeli Muslim and Druze villages. Devotees often met these merchants and sold them books. During this visit to Maghar we met two Palestinian teenagers selling large plastic mats—the kind that are popular in Arabic households because they are inexpensive and easy to set, remove, and clean. We didn’t sell them a book but purchased one of their mats for our Rama apartment.

*Sankirtana* devotees now felt comfortable enough to live in an Arab and Druze village, but we realized that this was a matter of consciousness. As stated in the *Narada-Pancaratra:*

sarvopadhi-vinirmuktam, tat-paratvena nirmalam  
hrsikena hrsikesa-sevanam bhaktir ucyate

“One should be free from all material designations and cleansed of all material contamination. He should be restored to his pure identity, in which he engages his senses in the service of the proprietor of the senses. This is called devotional service.”

A devotee’s perception is free from material designations, such as Arab, Jew, American, or Israeli. The villagers understood that we saw them as spirit souls and that we related to them as the Lord’s servants. Respecting Krsna’s devotees as sincere servants of God, they responded to us similarly, not based on our bodily or political designation but on our common God consciousness. Otherwise, even entering these villages would have been dangerous.

*A Farce of Fate, by Dhira Govinda dasa*

The doctor was a genial, cultured man. He had just given a donation in exchange for some of Srila Prabhupada’s books. I was in Sakhnine, a small Muslim village nestled in the hills of northern Israel. As I was leaving his office, I noticed in the waiting room a large calendar displaying a photo of a teenage Palestinian boy gracefully posed like a little league pitcher about to deliver strike three. His hand didn’t clasp a baseball, however, but a sizable rock. And his target wasn’t the catcher’s glove of a playmate, but the vulnerable body of an Israeli soldier, who for him represented the enemy that had been oppressing him and his family for many years.

It’s the Middle East. As devotees of Krsna in this part of the world, we interact extensively with many peoples—Jew, Muslim, Christian, Israeli,and Palistinian. In each of these groups, we meet many good people, such as the doctor, who are sincerely concerned about the distressing situation in which they’re living. So why all the difficulty?

According to the philosophy of Krsna consciousness, the cause of the difficulty is the bodily conception of life. A person is the spiritual spark, or soul, within the body, and has no inherent connection with the body or its designations. The soul is eternally a servant of God, Krsna. From the *Bhagavad-gita* we learn that the body is a temporary covering of the soul.We are not Arab or Israeli, communist or capitalist; we are God’s eternal servants who are foolishly playing different roles in different dresses in this material world.

In the north of Israel live the Druze, a religious sect that accepts the concept of transmigration of the soul, or reincarnation. As stated in the *Bhagavad-gita,* “As the embodied soul continuously passes, in this body, from boyhood to youth to old age, the soul similarly passes into another body at death.” Many Druze claim to remember their previous lives. One of them might say, “Yes, my father died a few years back; now he’s a four-year-old boy in Yanook \[a nearby village\].” Some Druze even claim to remember that their previous death occured while fighting against the Israeli army in a country such as Syria. If this is true, just imagine the irony! A man who previously fought against the Israelis now proudly wears the uniform of the Israeli military forces.

According to the *Bhagavad-gita,* “Whatever state of being one remembers when he quits his body, that state he will attain without fail.” Thus, the rock-throwing Palestinian youth who is stopped by a bullet might take birth in suburban Tel Aviv, as his concentration at the time of death was on an Israeli soldier. And at the age of eighteen, his army commander will instruct him to guard his zone against “those vicious Palestinian boys.”

The situation is obviously farcical, but because we take these bodily designations so seriously, the results are tragic. We are pleading with the citizens of this part of the world to understand the nonsectarian, transcendental philosophy Krsna speaks in the *Bhagavad-gita* as the way to bring peace to the Middle East.

\[This article appeared in *Back to Godhead* magazine, October, 1989.\]

Gradually, I began presenting myself in Arabic: “*Ahalan, ismi Dhira, oo mah ee ktab* (Hello, my name is Dhira and I have some books).”

“*Min when Inti?* (Where are you from?)”

“*Ana fi Rama.* (I’m from Rama.)”

Maha-Laksmi’s Arabic developed much quicker than mine. She became so fluent that she could shop and even hold light conversations in Arabic. The Druze and Arabs had no difficulty with our spiritual names, which we used exclusively. “Maha,” an Arabic word for the eyes of a deer, is a common name for an Arab woman.

Rama had several churches, each representing a different denomination—Greek Orthodox, Rosicrucian, and Catholic. I used to visit the priests, and several took books for their libraries. On one visit, a Jesuit priest from Jordan said he had met Hare Krsna devotees during his travels to the West and had been impressed with their philosophy and austerity. Still, he insisted that Christianity was the most difficult religious discipline to follow, because a serious practitioner had to have deep faith that the wafer was actually the body of Christ.

From the time I returned from Egypt in February 1990, Isvara Krsna and I began to discuss plans to form a devotional community in the north of Israel. In one of our first attempts to research the possibility, I visited Salim Zedan, our intellectual English teacher friend in Osafia. Like Rashid from Ilaboon, Salim wanted to build the area beneath his house but had no money. He suggested we supply the funds for construction, then move in with a year’s worth of rent to our credit. Salim, a leftist on the Druze political spectrum, liked the idea of hosting emissaries from a different culture for Osafia’s enrichment. He discussed the possibility that we become involved in social work in his village. Julia, Salim’s wife, worked in social welfare in the nearby village of Daliyat El Carmel. Both Salim and Julia had degrees from Haifa University.

The Zedans believed strongly in the need for spiritual life. Julia wore the traditional dark dress of a religious Druze woman, and the couple often sent their son, Ihab, to study with sheikhs. Unlike many conservatives, however, they believed that Druze spirituality should be dovetailed with the milieu of modern times. For example, a controversy current at the time was whether Druze women should be allowed to drive cars. Sheikh Amin was adamant that they should not; he would never allow such a thing. The chastity of Druze women would become corrupted, due to their exposure to the degraded materialistic civilization, and their society would then become degraded (*varna-sankara*). Salim and Julia, however, argued that for the benefit of the Druze community—and for humanity at large—the Druze should not maintain a dogmatic adherence to ancient strictures, whose essential meaning could be fulfilled by proper utilization of modern amenities. Julia could visit so many more families in her social work by using a car. Wouldn’t that benefit the community? Salim was confident that the Druze could utilize modern facilities without corrupting or compromising their principles. Thus he appreciated the mood of the *sankirtana* devotees. He could see that we had renounced material life because, obviously, if we had chosen to do something else with our lives, we would be living far more comfortably. Still, we did not renounce things that would facilitate our preaching and our attempt to live a purely spiritual lifestyle. Thus Salim admired our preaching spirit and our ability to use modern conveniences, such as printing presses, cars, and fax machines, without becoming sidetracked into material life. Salim also did not agree that the Druze message should be kept secret.

Salim and Julia were a sharp, although not easily defined, contrast to Kasem and Naaeem. Whereas Kasem and Naaeem were the quintessential, simple Druze family, happy in their village life and not involved in social reform, they did not observe traditional religious practices, nor did they markedly try for spiritual goals. Although Naaeem, like most of Rama’s Druze women, wore conservative, Western-style dresses, being influenced by Rama’s Christian community, she would never consider driving a car or sending her children to a non-Druze school. Conversely, although Julia was a strict follower of her religious codes and an exemplary mother of five children, she had a career and sent some of her children to Jewish schools so they could get the best available education.

Rama’s Druze community was influenced by Western mores perhaps more than any other major Druze population in Israel. This influence came from Rama’s proportionately large Christian population. This is what accounts for Sheikh Farhoud’s reluctance to associate with the handful of other sheikhs in Rama, some of whom even watched television. While Osafia and Daliyat El Carmel have maintained a large, strictly religious contingent of sheikhs, these villages are exposed to much heavier Western influence than Rama’s pious Christian population: Israelies and tourists from all parts of the world regularly drive through and shop in these Carmel villages, especially in Daliyat. Especially on Saturdays, Daliyat’s *shuuk* (market) attracts large crowds of tourists. Osafia and Daliyat El Carmel have become major attractions on Israel’s tourist map, offering visitors an opportunity to witness authentic Druze life. Of course, most tourists only get to see a crowded Saturday *shuuk,* where they mostly see other tourists and buy blue jeans. Only time will tell to what extent Daliyat and Osafia will be affected by Western exposure.

In conversation Salim would often oscillate between berating materialistic civilization and the need for Druze to expose themselves to the outside world. *Krsna-bhaktas* in Srila Rupa Gosvami’s line understand the need for this balance—the importance of *yukta-vairagya,* or the proper utilization of everything in the Lord’s service. The impersonalist, considering the world false, rejects everything without realizing its connection with its creator, Krsna. The materialist tries to use everything for his own enjoyment, also without considering either the creator or the spiritual mission of human life. Devotees want to use Krsna’s material energy in His service. To learn how to do this, we should receive expert guidance from a perfectly realized, pure, spiritual teacher, such as Srila Prabhupada. Otherwise, we will be misled by our own material desires and become influenced by impersonalistic or materialistic philosophies.

We liked Salim, and after a few weeks of negotiation we signed a contract to help build his house. We planned to move to Osafia in June 1990, when our contract in Rama expired. When the residents of Rama heard that we were planning to move, we received many offers to stay in the village. Some families offered us sections of their homes for very little cost. But we continued with our Osafia plans, because we wanted to form a larger community of devotees in Israel.

## 5: Osafia

Situated at the foot of the North, near all the Druze and most of the Muslim and Christian villages, Osafia is perfectly located for a devotional community. About one quarter of the Israeli Druze population resides in Daliyat El Carmel and Osafia. Formerly, there were many Druze villages on the Carmel, but they were destroyed or vacated. Also, Osafia is only ten minutes from Haifa University and less than half an hour from downtown Haifa. About an hour from Tel Aviv, Osafia is also accessible to devotees and guests from that city. Whereas Rama is an ideal location for working with the Druze and Arabic communities, Osafia is more suitable for the movement’s work with the entire Israeli population.

Salim’s house is in a far corner of Osafia, near the end of a road on a mountain ledge. The view from his house is breathtaking, a panorama encompassing the lower section of Osafia, Daliyat El Carmel. To the left is the fertile Zvulun Valley, and one can see indistinctly images of many towns and villages. From March through May we visited Salim several times to ensure that progress was being made on the construction. There were, of course, delays, but we worked through them together.

When my father, Jules Wolf, visited Israel from the United States in April 1990, we took him to Osafia and our future home. He was so impressed with the view, village, and surroundings that he asked if we would have a room for him. At one point he wandered toward the end of the mountain road. Ferro Kais, a professor at Haifa University who lives in the last and most outlying house on the road, spotted my father exploring the area and insisted that he come to his home. Almost any Druze would have shown the same hospitality, but my father was fortunate to meet a learned Druze who could converse fluently in English. Ferro Kais told my father how Maha-Laksmi and I had visited his home more than a year ago and had sold him some books. They talked about their lives and the world in general, while savoring pita bread, *labneh,* and olive oil. Before parting, my father invited Mr. Kais to stay with him when he visited America.

My father was thrilled by the experience of having a stranger invite him into his home and their having established such a warm and personal friendship so quickly. He commented that even after living next to his neighbors for many years in his comfortable apartment complex in Pennsylvania, he had not developed as close a relationship with anyone. Continuing in this vein, he reflected on how modern society had neglected important values.

By the end of May, Isvara Krsna dasa moved into Osafia with his wife Radha-Madhava dasi and daughter Jahnavi. We moved from Rama the second week of June. Although some fine points of construction, such as screens and locks, had not yet been completed, the place was habitable and we set up our temple.

One advantage of the building was that Salim had agreed to allow us to design the inside ourselves. Thus it had been constructed according to our needs. The entrance, situated under Salim’s house at the bottom of a dirt driveway, opened into a hallway. On the left was a large kitchen with two refrigerators and two stoves to facilitate cooking for festivals. On the opposite side of the kitchen was a window leading to the temple/*prasadam* room, which covered more than thirty square meters. The window allowed us to pass plates and pots to the *prasadam* hall and allowed the cooks to hear classes being held in the temple room.
