Next to the temple room was an office, and next to the office was a large men’s *asrama*. The *asrama* contained a small office and a large room with two bunkbeds. Opposite the wall from the men’s *asrama* was the women’s *asrama*. The women’s *asrama* over-looked the countryside. Although Salim had friends and relatives work on the house voluntarily or at low rates, construction costs were higher than expected, so there was not enough material for the dividing wall between *asramas* to reach the ceiling. Still, it was high enough to act as a real divider. The women’s side was larger than the men’s, and included a laundry room, storage room, a large room of about sixteen square meters, and a smaller room.

Before settling in Osafia, we asked Salim about crime in the area. “There was a thief a few years back,” he said, “but he has been caught.”

We started doing *sankirtana* extensively in Osafia and Daliyat. I distributed five to ten Arabic *Gitas* per day, and at least one set per week, and Parama-guru was doing the same. We went door to door to every villager, as we had done in villages on the east side of the country while living in Rama. As devotees had visited almost every home at least once in villages such as Rama, Beit Jann, Samiah, and Yanook, they now did the same in Daliyat and Osafia.

After we had all been in Osafia for about a week, we held a grand opening festival for the new temple. We invited friends from Rama and other villages, as well as our new neighbors from Osafia. Parama-guru made two round trips to Maghar, so that Wahib Saad could come and video the event. Salim, Julia, and their children came downstairs for the fun, although Julia, as a chaste Druze woman, was shy about participating in the festivities and spent most of her time talking with the *matajis* in their *asrama*. Kamil came from Rama, as did Noef, an English teacher from Maghar. Isvara Krsna gave a lecture, which was preceded and followed by *kirtana*. After the feast, we conducted a video interview with Salim about the significance of the Hare Krsna movement coming to Osafia.

Whereas in Rama we had been located in the most bustling section of town, in Osafia we lived on the quiet outskirts. While villagers and their children would still visit us, they now had to make a special effort to do so; we were not on the way to anywhere. For us, going to the market, post office, or other store required a quick drive or a healthy walk. To walk to the opposite end of Osafia from the temple took almost an hour.

Even the conservative Vedic dress code is somewhat liberal for the Druze village. A *dhoti* with an unsewn fold in front is considered risquī. It was more acceptable to tuck the bottom tip of the front fold in at the waist, giving the *dhoti* a *sharwahl* look. Also, the Druze consider wearing no shirt, even in hot weather, objectionable. For women, covering the whole body, including the head and mid-section, is compulsory. Thus *cholis* were not practical.

Osafia is larger than Rama, with about eight thousand residents, about three-quarters of whom are Druze. The remainder of Osafia’s population is mostly Christian. There is also a small Muslim population, a few missionary Christian families from the West, and even a few Jewish families who find it a peaceful place to live. Daliyat, with more than ten thousand residents, was completely Druze until recently, when a few Muslims established their families on the outskirts of the village.

For Maha-Laksmi, Julia was stimulating association. She spoke Hebrew fluently, unlike most of Rama’s female population, and that made it easier for Maha-Laksmi to communicate. She was also philosophically inclined. Thus topics could go beyond kitchen matters. Ola, Julia’s seventeen-year-old daughter, visited us frequently and also discussed philosophy with the women. She was trying to establish her identity. Her parents encouraged her to attend college and to get a career, although of course they also wanted her to remain a faithful Druze. On the other hand, she wasn’t inclined toward academics and thought she might be satisfied to live the simple life of a traditional Druze woman.

Parama-guru began to cultivate Yosef, an Osafian Druze in his fifties, and his son. Yosef had a serious illness and was deeply inquisitive about spiritual matters. He often came to our Friday evening programs. His son was an artist and had recently completed a huge painting of the five horses.

When they saw the five horses of the same color depicted in the *Bhagavad-gita,* they were overwhelmed and driven to delve into the matter. Yosef had an inclination toward mysticism, and he found great meaning in the description of the Panca-tattva. He thought there must be a connection between Lord Caitanya and His associates and Nabi Schweib and the four other prominent prophets. Also, he related to Parama-guru that, according to Druze canon, at the end of the material creation God assumes a fearsome feature on a white horse. This corresponds with the Vedic description of the Kalki-avatara.

*Golan Excursion*

We planned a *sankirtana* trip to Ramat Ha Golan. Isvara Krsna had a friend who studied and worked as an actor in Jerusalem, who also owned a camping resort with a few cabins near the Golan. The six of us, as well as the soon-to-be-born Sita-Rama, went there for a *sankirtana* vacation. Noam and most of his clientele were part of Israel’s liberal scene. As such, they were open to hear about the philosophy and ideals of Krsna consciousness. The concept of restricting our senses for spiritual goals, however, was difficult to integrate. In any case, their facility provided an effective base for Golan *sankirtana*. Parama-guru, Isvara Krsna, and I went on *sankirtana* together, sometimes to Jewish settlements and sometimes to Druze villages.

*The Good Fence*

I had been hearing about the Good Fence for a long time. At the Lebanese border in the Jewish town of Metulla, The Good Fence served as a path of legal traffic between Israel and Lebanon. Thousands of Lebanese—Druze, Christians, and Muslims—came through the fence each day to work in Israel, on farms, and in offices and factories. I thought this would be a good place to meet Lebanese and to distribute books to them. Entrance and exit hours, 5–7:00 a.m. and 4–6:00 p.m., were, I was told, the most crowded times.

I arrived at the Good Fence at 4:30 a.m. on a Wednesday morning. From a lookout point I could see the large Lebanese Druze town of Hasbayya. We had heard about Hasbayya and had yearned to visit there with Arabic books. As workers began to come through the area, I presented the books. A crowd of Druze formed and I distributed several books into their hands. I requested them to turn to the “changing bodies” picture, then to the picture of the five horses. For me, the effect had almost become commonplace, but for these Lebanese Druze, the experience was fresh and extraordinary. Most of them were sheikhs in work clothes with attached *sharwahls,* and they all wanted the Arabic books. When I mentioned the price, they were disappointed and disbelieving. So began the bargaining. It was as if I had asked them to pay hundreds of dollars for a *Gita* and thirty dollars for a paperback *Coming Back*. In the end, I gave the books for whatever donation they could gather. As they departed for various Israeli locations, I could see crowds of Druze inside the buses clustered around the sheikhs, reading the *Bhagavad-gita* with fascination.

*Aloine*

At about 9:00 a.m., as I was leaving, a taxi driver from the village of Ajar (could be pronounced Raja or Ghadjar) approached me and purchased an Arabic *Bhagavad-gita.* He then invited me to his village. Since our first visit to the Golan, I had heard about Ajar and had met a few school teachers from there who worked in Druze villages in the Golan. The citizens of Ajar, I heard, were Muslims of the Aloine sect, and it was rumored that they believed in reincarnation. All other Golan Muslim villages had been ravaged by war, as was evident by the rubble and vacated, semi-demolished buildings on the roadsides leading through the territory. As Ajar sounded like an interesting visit, I had often inquired about it but had usually been told that I would be refused entrance. Ajar, it seemed, was beyond the border, and one needed special clearance to enter and leave it. But this taxi driver said I could enter without problem and that I could use his name if necessary.

Hopping into the van, I headed for Ajar. On the road leading up the Golan to the Druze villages was a sign indicating Ajar to the left. The road was extremely rough, with gaping potholes poised to swallow the Suzuki. After about ten miles, I came to a military blockade. “What do you want?” asked the young Israeli lieutenant.

“I have some books I want to show the villagers.”

“Let me see your ID.” He remained stern. I presented my Israeli ID and offered to show him the receipts I used. He wanted to know what kind of books I had. I started to preach *Bhagavad-gita.* He realized he was dealing with a Hare Krsna who wanted to distribute books. He took my ID and told me to enter. As I pulled out I could hear jovial soldiers singing “Hare Krsna.”

Ajar was less than a kilometer from the military check point. It looked like a typical Israeli Arab village, except for an abundance of cows roaming the paths and streets, reminding me of village India.

An Aloine in his late teens invited me to his home. The first thing I noticed was a picture of Jesus on the wall. “Pretty strange for a Muslim village,” I thought. The teen gave a small donation for an Arabic *Coming Back*. Most of the men were not home during the day, and I was greeted mostly by old men and youngsters. Almost everyone I encountered took a *Coming Back,* and two families purchased Arabic *Gitas*. Many pictures of Jesus appeared on the walls, and I finally asked someone about this. They calmly replied that worship of Jesus was part of their belief. It seemed incongruous to me. I had heard that there were about four million Aloines in Syria, including Assad, the nation’s political leader. I wondered whether they all believed as the residents of Ajar did. Concerning reincarnation, they generally skirted the issue when asked, although they were attracted to the *Coming Back* book. book.

I left Ajar in time to meet the evening shift of workers returning from Israel to Lebanon through the Good Fence. Generally, the crowd was more hurried than in the morning, and few stopped to talk or look at the books. In the evening I returned to the *moshav* campgrounds, where the group of *sankirtana* devotees had been staying for a few days, and related the events of the day to the other devotees, who had distributed in Jewish *moshavs* in the area.

Everyone at the campgrounds, including the devotees and other guests, were glued to the television next to the bar. Sadaam Hussein had invaded Kuwait, and Israel’s security was at risk.

Maha-Laksmi wanted to visit Ajar too. Although she was now in her ninth month of pregnancy, she was enthusiastic to do *sankirtana* while she was still able. The residents of the Golan appreciated her sincerity and took many books.

Maha-Laksmi: *One evening in Buqata I was in a store, talking to the manager. A sheikh entered and saw me. He began chastising the manager. ‘This woman is pregnant. Why don’t you help her? Where is she going to stay tonight? Why don’t you do something!’ In this way, everyone was very concerned and helpful.*

After more humorous pastimes with the border guards, we entered Ajar late in the morning, pulling up in front of the *moatsah makomit* \[village council building\]. After talking with the clerks and the mayor, I went door to door in the village with the same type of results as I had had on the previous day. In the late afternoon Maha-Laksmi and I both distributed books, taking separate halves of the village. As evening approached, men and their families returned to their homes. Many families eagerly accepted Arabic *Gitas.* I walked along the back side of the village, which ended at a steep cliff. At the bottom of the cliff was a narrow river. Before the river was a barbed wire fence, and on the hills on the opposite side were many military encampments, which I assumed to be Lebanese.

At about 8:00 p.m., as the sun set, I walked to the other side of the village. Maha-Laksmi sat on the porch of a home, surrounded by many Aloine, especially women. The women’s dress and decoration were unique. Although not luxurious, their long dresses and robes struck the eye with their varieties of bright colors—pink, purple, bright red, yellow—unlike the usual subdued colors of Muslim and Druze women. Also, they wore a lot of jewelry, including necklaces, earrings, and bangles. Like other Arab women, they covered their heads. Maha-Laksmi seemed to be in the middle of a festival of curious Aloine women, all seeking to find out more about her and the books.

August 23, 1990 ML (Masade) to Danavir dasa:

We (Dhira Govinda Prabhu and me) are in the Golan Heights now, right on the Syrian border in a Druze village called Masade. For the last three days we’ve been distributing Srila Prabhupada’s books in this area with nice success. Yesterday we went to an Alawin-Muslim village named Ajar on the border of Lebanon. It is Lebanon, practically speaking, so much so that the village is surrounded by barbed wire and iron bars and guarded by soldiers. We had to leave our identity cards at the entrance. The villagers, however, didn’t seem so “dangerous” to me. Actually, I thought that we had entered Vrndavana...Every person we approached took a book and gave a donation and was begging us to come in and eat something. All over the village there were cows and calves roaming freely in the roads, which were smeared with cow dung. These Aloine Muslims are a special type of people. They believe in Mohammad and the Koran *and* in Lord Jesus Christ (!) and in reincarnation. This is an amazing combination that I never met before. I don’t want to sound like a sense enjoyer, but these remote Arabic villages remind me of a big cup of ice cream with different colors and flavors. I am sure that if we could get to Lebanon or Syria, we would find so many different types of Arabs with different combinations of faith and culture that would be more than happy to purchase Prabhupada’s books.

This morning at 4:30 a.m., Dhira Govinda Prabhu went to the “Good Fence” by Metulla, which is a border with Lebanon that is open for Lebanese workers to come during the day to work in Israel and go back to their homes at night. He stood there with the books and managed to give some books to the excited and curious Druze farmers that were surrounding him. They are more poor than the Druze we know, but they love our books!

Actually, I wanted to tell you about some problem, but I forgot myself narrating the nectar of book distribution. The problem is a very big war which is about to start in the Mid-East. Iraq is threatening to attack Israel with chemical weapons, which are horrible and destructive. Everyone is panic stricken ...

*Absorbed in the Bhagavad-gita*

During the final morning of traveling *sankirtana,* I was struggling in the hot, dusty side streets of Buq’atha when I met an old sheikh’s wife at the door of her home. We couldn’t communicate well due to the language barrier, but she was attracted to the *Bhagavad-gita.* Someone came by and saw us speaking. He called out something to the woman, apparently praising the book, which she then purchased. I had just thanked her and started to leave when the old sheikh returned. He smiled at me, and I explained that I was presenting books about God. His wife told him that she had already bought one, and he appearaed doubtful about the wisdom of this decision. After encouraging them both to read the *Gita,* I departed. About half an hour later, I passed the back window of the old sheikh’s house on my way to a different part of Buq’atha. From the window I faintly heard an old man reciting in broken language: *sri-caitanya-mano-’bhistam, sthapitam yena bhu-tale, svayam rupa kada mahyam, dadati sva-padantikam, vande ‘ham sri-guro sri-yuta ”*

I couldn’t resist peeking in the window. The sheikh sat with his eyes intently gazing at Srila Prabhupada’s *Gita* introduction, and his wife sat across from him, equally transfixed on the sound vibration. A shudder ran through my body, and I moved on.

About two hours later, I was returning to the car for lunch, and as I passed the window I heard *kamais tais tair hrta-jnana, prapadyante ’nya-devata* in choppy Druze-Sanskrit. Srila Prabhupada quotes this verse in the Introduction. Stunned, I looked in the window. The sheikh and his wife were in the same position, still studying the *Bhagavad-gita.* I thought, “Even if no one ever reads a word from all the books I have ever distributed, and all my efforts for this cause are otherwise for naught, it has all been more than worthwhile just so this sheikh and his wife could so earnestly relish the *Bhagavad-gita*.” But I knew the actual situation: many people were seriously reading the books.

More than an hour later, after *prasadam,* I again walked past the old couple’s home, and their positions remained unchanged. The man had reached the first verse of the first chapter, and was reading the translation about Dhrtarastra, Sanjaya, and Kuruksetra. I stayed and listened to his recitation of translation and purport as it had been rendered into Arabic by Ravanari dasa.

For the second year in a row, we had made a *sankirtana* excursion to the Golan during mid to late summer, and for the second year in a row, the Golan residents treated the devotees to as many delicious apples as we could load in our vehicles. In 1989 we had brought back carloads of Golan apples to Tel Aviv, which we used for scores of preparations in celebrating Balarama’s Appearance Day and Sri Krsna Janmastami. Again, as we offered the Golan apples in our temple, the devotees commented that these were the best apples they ever tasted, grown as they had been in the cool mountain climate, far from the city’s pollution. Besides apples, the Druze also cultivated *chavoosheem* \[quince\] trees. *Chavoosheem* resembles lopsided green apples, but its consistency is different, harder than an apple, and used only for cooking.

*Letters*

June 18, 1990 Gaura-bhakta dasa (France) to Dhira Govinda dasa (Osafia):

because of the wonderful article you did in the “*Sankirtana* News,” I am inspired and indebted to your ecstatic service. Thank you very much for mentioning my insignificant service I did in your association. Actually your article was read by the temple president in *ista-gosthi* and all devotees came to me and appreciated the preaching I did in Israel. I had an opportunity to tell them more about it and all devotees are inspired and happy by the success. As you know I am in France since 1 month and half. I enjoyed very much my godbrothers’ association.

A very good news about Druze in France. Because I knew about book fair in Paris (Arabic one), I went to the Lebanon stand. I meet the editors. Little by little I showed them Arabic *Bhagavad-gita* and then they revealed themselves as Druze. The appointment is made to meet the director of the whole publication—who is working with Walid Jumbalat (Kamal Jumbalat’s son)—so I preached to him. I told him about Israel, about Sheikh Amin, etc., and they were excited. They took for their library two *full sets of Bhagavatam,* two *Gita* (Arabic), and other several books. He said he is going to put them in library and analyze them and then they can buy more. They can print also for us. For a very cheap price and better publication the Arabic *Coming Back*. I phoned Hari-vilasa Prabhu. I gave him the news. He is very happy. He gave me Trai dasa’s address. Maybe you can inform him since you are more experienced in this field. This is a very good sign by Krsna. I even heard that there is a Druze community in south of France, but they did not reveal to us where they are located exactly.

P. S.—I have some problems with visa (French permission) to return to Cairo. Maybe it is temporary. I am sorry that Maharaja is alone. In this situation I depend totally on Krsna’s mercy. I wait until they will give me permission to go back to Egypt.

June 26, 1990 Dhira Govinda dasa (Osafia) to many devotees around the world, including Danavir dasa, Kirtiraja dasa, Tamal Krishna Goswami, Bhakti-caru Swami, Jagadisa Goswami, Harikesa Maharaja, Jayapataka Maharaja, Satyaraja dasa, Thukura Haridasa dasa, Jayadvaita Swami, Ananda-svarupa Swami, Gaura-bhakta dasa, Hari-vilasa dasa, Madhusevita dasa, Sundararupa dasa, Dhira dasa, Prabhavisnu Swami, Subhananda dasa, Jnana Sakti dasa, Bhavananda Raya dasa (Russia), Sergei Zhyotikov, Sivarama Swami, Nanda-kisora dasa (Fiji), Dhanurdhara Swami, and Ravindra-svarupa dasa:

Most academicians will claim that the Druze are an unorthodox sect of Islam. However, if one probes into the Druze mentality, he will find that the Druze, generally speaking, do not appreciate being considered as such. Actually, the term “Druze,” like “Hindu,” is a misnomer. Eldraze was a heretic to the faith which would be named after him by those who wished to demean the sect. The Druze consider themselves to be a current manifestation of the “Mwachey Doon,” which translates roughly as “one religion,” or, “*sanatana-dharma*.” When one examines the history and culture of the Mwachey Doon, it becomes apparent that the roots of the Druze are much more situated in the Vedic tradition than in any of the Mid-Eastern religions.

First, the Druze speak of world history dating back hundreds of millions of years, with incarnations of God in the human form appearing at regular intervals. We have heard that their scriptures describe God as “Brahman” and “Hari,” who appeared 5–7,000 years ago. The most recent incarnation, according to their knowledge, is El Hackem, who appeared as a Muslim king about 1,000 years ago. Those who could recognize his Godhood and follow his Mwachey Doon teachings later became known as the Druze. (Eldrazi’s mistake for which he was rejected from the Mwachey Doon is that he preached the knowledge when it was not supposed to be preached.) Thus it is believed that the eternal knowledge of Mwachey Doon surfaces on the planet in different ways at different times, such as the current manifestation of the Druze. However, it is understood that there are presently Mwachey Doon on different parts of the planet who go by different names.

When the Druze of Israel, both as a community and as individuals, were presented with Srila Prabhupada’s books, they were confronted with all of this “Mwachey Doon” knowledge, which is supposed to be a carefully guarded secret. As far as I’ve heard, there’s no real sublime philosophical reason for the Druze’s secrecy. The main reason given is that if they reveal the knowledge, then they will be persecuted by the Muslims. Their habitation high in the mountainous areas is also explained as being the result of running away from Muslim persecution. Until very recently, and maybe also at present, textbooks for school children in Muslim countries described the Druze as a blood-thirsty people who have tails one meter long. A secondary reason for the secrecy of the Druze is that many people, including most Druze themselves, could not properly handle the knowledge. As a result, a great percentage of the Druze have only a vague understanding of their own philosophy. Thus they can recognize that the books of the Hare Krsna movement are dealing with concepts that they accept to be true, such as reincarnation. Presently, many Druze are delving into Srila Prabhupada’s books for a greater understanding of their own tradition, which is not accessible to them unless they become initiated as “sheikhs.”

One of the great heroes of the Druze in modern times, Kamal Jumbalat, made many visits to India and did extensive research into the *Vedas.* Jumbalat’s books, both in Arabic and English, extol the *Bhagavad-gita* and Krsna, although his understanding is ultimately impersonal and Gandhian in that he used the *Gita* to support his political strategies in Lebanon (he was also assassinated). In any case, his work has deeply impressed the Druze psyche about the importance of the *Vedas* to their own culture. This further explains their receptivity to the original Vedic scriptures being presented in a language they can understand.

From what I understand, only the Israeli and Lebanese Druze are able to boldly express their Druze identity. Elsewhere, such as in Syria and Jordan, they are forced to identify themselves as Muslims for fear of persecution. Especially in Israel, it is particularly advantageous for the Druze to be distinctly Druze, as they are enjoying a mutually beneficial political relationship with the Jewish government. Although there is a faction of Israeli Druze who identify themselves as Arabs, with all of that designation’s connotations, most prefer to remain distinct from the “Muslim” Arabs. In January of 1989, Salman Falach, Druze Minister of Education in Israel, purchased from the devotees about $8,000 worth of books for Druze schools and for himself personally. We now understand this as an attempt to support their position as non-Muslims, with roots stemming from India. Mr. Falach has expressed interest to do a doctoral thesis demonstrating that the *Vedas* are the actual roots of the Druze religion.

Some other noteworthy points of similarity between Druze and Vedic tradition include regulative principles of purity as well as appreciation of the principle of renunciation. An initiated sheikh, if he is actually following strictly, must restrict himself from smoking, drinking, and gambling, and is only permitted to unite with his wife for procreation (we’ve also heard that this is permitted only once in three or five years for those who are serious). Kamal Jumbalat lived the latter part of his life as a celibate “*vanaprastha,*” and was strictly vegetarian. Generally speaking, the Druze appreciate vegetarianism as a moral and spiritual principle, although most eat meat as a result of exposure to Muslim society. Our experience from living in their villages is that food preparations that are native to the Druze are vegetarian. They especially like vegetarian-filled pastries, such as *samosas*. Also, it seems that coffee drinking is a bad habit they have picked up over the generations, although it is not native to their culture. As far as renunciation, I recall a lengthy discussion I had on sankirtana with a high school principal. At one point he stated, without any hints on my part, that it is customary in their culture to renounce material engagements such as business at about the age of fifty. Another interesting point is that according to Druze astronomy, it is not possible that man has gone to the moon. Also, their use of analogies is often identical with those with which we are familiar. When they see *Bg.* 2.22, the Druze usually become delighted and amazed, as they describe transmigration in the same way. Another hero of theirs, Sultan Atrash, is the image of a saintly *ksatriya.* He was a strict sheikh that led his country in a battle for independence. Afterwards he lived a quiet life, not accepting any position of power.

In a recent meeting with Sheik Amin Tarif, possibly the greatest Druze leader in the world today, the sheikh stated that he would like the Druze to work cooperatively with the Hare Krsna movement “as one race.” This is quite a significant statement, considering the clandestine and insular nature of Druze society. Many Druze have difficulty believing that the Sheikh said “as one race.” \[Our acceptance was happening so quickly and at such high levels in Druze society that some of the Druze, upon hearing the Sheikhs statements, found them hard to believe.\] But we’re quickly being accepted as one of the Mwachey Doon. Recently, a journalist in Rama, the village where we’ve been living, printed an article about Hare Krsna in *Diwali,* the Druze newspaper in Israel. Practically he let me dictate the article while he translated the words into Arabic. The response has been fantastic. Sometimes when we knock on doors they identify us as Druze and take all the books. We now present an eight-volume Hebrew set as well as BGAII and a new edition of *Coming Back,* both in Arabic. This edition has a picture of Kamal Jumbalat, which they really love. We also inserted some quotes from Kahil Gibran, whom most Arabs greatly admire, about reincarnation.

On April 25th we attended the holiest Druze festival of the year at the site of the tomb of the prophet Schweib (the Biblical character known as Jethro). Thousands of sheiks attended from Lebanon, Israel, and the Golan. On the whole, they were friendly and receptive to the devotees, and extremely curious about Krsna consciousness. Many took books.

Many Druze are reading Srila Prabhupada’s books deeply and their lives are being clearly affected. A few weeks ago I met a woman school teacher on *sankirtana.* She was reluctant to speak due to natural chastity, but managed to express that she’s read the first five books in the set (*BG, KB, SB1, CC* *Adi-lila* in two parts), is following the regulative principles even to the point of giving up coffee (very difficult in an Arab village), and wants to learn more about the chanting. In Rama, many young men have become vegetarians. One simple construction worker named Rezek has been chanting at least eight rounds a day for more than a year and reads the books religiously. He states, “Krsna is my way. I want to go back to Krsna.” Recently on *sankirtana* I met a school manager who had received a set of books and japa beads a year earlier. He said whenever he feels some anxiety, he chants the Hare Krsna *maha-mantra* on the beads and feels immediate relief. The children in the village of Rama chant Hare Krsna as a natural part of their play. Variations of the mantra include, “Hare Krsna, Hare Rama, Krsna Rama, Krsna Allah.”

It’s common in talking to Druze to hear remembrances of past lives. Toward the end of one mantra, my wife heard one Druze proudly say, “You don’t love Krsna like I love Krsna!” He went on to relate his past life in the Himalayas.

We have recently had some friendly contact with Mr. Assad Assad. Mr. Assad is one of the top-ranking (if not *the* top) Druze in the Israeli military. He served for some time in New York at the UN as a representative for Israel. Currently he is a top advisor to the Minister of Minority Affairs. Needless to say, he has a full set of books. He recently visited us in our humble abode in Rama to ask serious philosophical questions. Mr. Assad is being sent to India by the Israeli government to meet the Dalai Lama (we suspect there are other undisclosed reasons). He plans to visit our ISKCON centers as well.

One boy in Rama has recently completed an Arabic translation of PQPA, which he did voluntarily. We are currently living in Osafia, a predominantly Druze village near Haifa, with Isvara Krsna dasa, his wife Radha-Madhava dasi, their daughter Jahnavi, and Parama-guru dasa Brahmacari. Our landlord is Mr. Salim Zedan, an English teacher who is scholarly in both Arabic and English. Salim has expressed interest to translate Srila Prabhupada’s books into Arabic. Elsewhere in Arab Israel, the Muslims and Christians are inclined to take books and give donations to the devotees out of natural piety. Recently in Tel Aviv, Guna-avatara and Devahuti Prabhus had very nice success at a big book fair. Also, Nama-hatta preaching is going strong. Many in Israeli society, as a result of many years of tireless preaching by the devotees there, are accepting the Hare Krsna devotees as authorities on the wisdom of India, and as sincere representatives of the *Bhagavad-gita.* This is despite the existence in Israel of a bewildering array of bogus yoga groups and speculative scholars. Recently, the devotees were invited to give a *BG* lecture to a group of tour guides from a big agency that tours India. Also, several university professors are requesting the devotees to address their classes

Additional notes: According to the Druze, a great sage will appear around the year 2000, and this will mark the beginning of a new era when the knowledge of Mwachey Don can be preached openly. We are hoping to continue to flood their villages with Srila Prabhupada’s books, and hopefully they will realize that Prabhupada is the great sage they’re looking for

We also had great success distributing books in the one village in Israel inhabited by Aloine Muslims. They believe in reincarnation and they were also worshiping Jesus Christ. Syria has millions of Aloines; we think there’s good potential there. Hare Krsna.

I feel there is full potential to execute all aspects of BBT Arabic work here. I really think something amazing can happen here. The Druze are expecting an incarnation of the Supreme Logic Potency (something like a *saktyavesavatara*) to appear in the present age for the purpose of uniting all the bona fide religions of the world. I’m sure that if we continue to infuse their communities with books, they will realize that Srila Prabhupada is the pure soul for whom they are awaiting.

July 18, 1990 Bhakti-caru Swami (Zurich) to Dhira Govinda dasa (Osafia):

I received your letter and article. I found your article extremely informative and inspiring. In your letter you asked me to help you in your Arabic preaching. Please let me know how you want me to help you. This year my schedule is very tight, but if necessary I will try to visit Israel for a couple of days.

You are rendering a wonderful service to Srila Prabhupada and I am sure he will bless you abundantly for rendering this service. Krsna consciousness movement is going to spread all over the world and that will be possible through the sincere service of selfless and dedicated devotees like yourself. Whenever you need my help please let me know. I will try my best.

July 18, 1990 Sivarama Swami (Bhaktivedanta Manor) to Dhira Govinda dasa (Osafia):

I received your report dated July 1st on the history and preaching of the Druze. Very interesting. I can simply encourage you to continue on in your nice preaching work. I am sure Srila Prabhupada is very pleased with your efforts and he will give you all blessings and support in this endeavor.

If I can be of any further help please let me know.

August 13, 1990 Tamal Krishna Goswami (Dallas) to Dhira Govinda dasa (Osafia):

I am just writing to thank you for your very nice, informative letter describing our preaching activities in Israel. I have read this letter with great interest and posted it for everyone to read. I will be very happy if you continue to keep me on your mailing list whenever you distribute such newsletters. I pray that the preaching in Israel continues to expand more and more and that many persons sincerely join our ranks and become full-time devotees.

September 25, 1990 Jagadisa Goswami (Vrndavana, India) to DG (Osafia):

It is very encouraging to hear your report of the preaching there, obviously Krsna has some special plan for these people who are so eager to know Him and join us in our Krsna consciousness. I pray tht Krsna gives you and the other devotees there the strength to guide these people effectively towards His lotus feet.

*Janmastami Celebration*

Guna-avatara and his wife Devahuti stayed with us in Osafia for several weeks before their trip to India for Karttika. Devahuti directed the planning and arrangements for a huge Janmastami feast consisting of more than 108 preparations. We invited many friends from different segments of the Israeli population.

On Janmastami day, I went to the Carmel forest surrounding Osafia, hiding myself in the woods to chant sixty-four rounds of *japa.* When I returned at about 5:00 p.m., the *matajis* had almost finished preparing the feast, and many guests were starting to arrive. I went to Kibbutz Shaar Ha Gahy to pick up our friends Ilana Gordon and David Carmeli, who had been associating with the movement since the time our center was in Ranaana. On the way back, we stopped in Haifa and picked up Avi Yaacobovitch, director of the vegetarian movement in Haifa and another inveterate friend of ISKCON Israel. By 6:00 p.m., many of our Osafia friends had arrived, including Salim and his son Ihab. In addition, many devotees and friends from Tel Aviv came, such as Suddha-sattva dasa, Jalangi dasi, Nitya-siddha dasa and his wife Saci-devi dasi, and Bhakta Meyer Artsi. Mati and Nurit Zeidman also attended. Nurit had been studying the devotees for about four years for her master’s thesis in Cultural Anthropology, and had become a good friend of the devotees. Recently, she and Mati have moved to Philadelphia to work on their doctorate degrees.

Marwan Wahby, a young English teacher from Daliyat, also came to the Janmastami celebration, along with a friend from his village. I had known Marwan for more than a year. He had taken a thirty-volume *Srimad-Bhagavatam* set in English and was interested in translating the *Bhagavatam* into Arabic. He was also working on a review of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* for an Israeli-Arabic literary magazine.

Nira Rousseau, a famous culinary arts journalist, also made an appearance at the Osafia Janmastami celebration. She was overwhelmed by the *matajis’* work and its tasty results, and wrote a favorable article about the huge *prasadam* feast.

Festivities began at 8:00 p.m. with *kirtanas* and *bhajanas* about Krsna’s pastimes, interspersed with dramatic readings by various devotees from *Krsna* book. At midnight, we offered *arati* and a grand feast to Lord Krsna, with everyone joining in a rousing *kirtana*. Salim was almost in tears that such an event was occurring in his house. This type of cultural event, with deep philosophical significance, is why he had been eager to bring devotees to his village. Adding to the joy was seeing so many of his fellow Osafians sharing the experience. As the *kirtana* ended, guests and devotees sat in rows and around the sides of the temple room as servers distributed the multitude of preparations. Most had never seen such a feast. Servers continued piling more onto the plates from seemingly endless pots and containers, until the guests absolutely insisted that they could take no more. Upon departing at about 2:00 a.m., Nabiah Aboorooken, a scholarly friend from Osafia, commented, “This was the most unforgettable experience of my life!”

Several devotees stayed until the next day and celebrated Srila Prabhupada’s Appearance Day. Murari Caitanya dasa joined us from Tel Aviv with his wife Iris and his son Nitai. The community of devotees, who had been serving apart in various sections of Israel, gathered and shared an intimate glorification of their movement’s founder-*acarya.*

Julia’s grandmother was greatly loved and respected in Osafia. When she passed away, everyone felt terrible grief, especially our upstairs neighbors. Isvara Krsna, myself, and our families attended the funeral in Osafia. Some Druze travelled more than three hours from the Golan, where Julia’s family originates, to attend the ceremony.

We had witnessed many Druze funerals on *sankirtana*. When an influential person or sheikh passed away, the body was carried in a box in procession through the streets, often led by hundreds of black-robed sheikhs with tall, white, pill-box shaped headdresses. In fact, one of the clearest memories of Rama life is the sol-emn Arabic voice announcing through the cool winter air just after sunrise the names of those who have died and the times and locations of the funerals. Most funerals, which seemed to occur more in the winter months, were held in nearby Beit Jann, a strongly religious village with hundreds of venerable sheikhs.

During the funeral, Salim, donning a cloth turban, delivered an eloquent eulogy in Arabic. We quickly learned that women were not permitted to attend this part of the ceremony, as our wives were instructed to leave. The Druze women prayed separately in a *khilwa.* We noticed that although the Druze buried bodies, the grave site was not ornate, as is the Western custom. We were told that after some time, another body may even be placed in almost the same spot. As the focus of their religion and funeral rites is the eternality of the soul and its transmigration, the Druze do not emphasize the dead body.

While performing *sankirtana* in Daliyat, I knocked on a door. A Druze farmer answered. Although he had never before met a devotee, he was happy to see me. He became excited and told me that he and his family chanted Hare Krsna every day. Then he sang the familiar tune. Somehow he and his family had obtained books and adopted the principles of Krsna consciousness.

During the same visit to Daliyat, I was greeted at another door by a young Druze, about sixteen years old. Athough he was attracted to the philosophy and wanted the entire set, he only had a few shekels and gave what he could for a *Bhagavad-gita* and some small books. We were absorbed in discussion, and as I was leaving, he followed me to the car.

The young man was especially attracted to the principles of austerity and the idea of sacrificing one’s material tendencies to attain higher spiritual consciousness. I opened the back of the Suzuki to put my cart away, preparing to return to Osafia. As we sat on the car and discussed, he noticed in a box a copy of *Who Are They* magazine. On the cover was a handsome devotee couple, the woman being a fashion model who had adopted Krsna consciousness. The publication was designed to show people how Krsna consciousness could accomodate everyone; it was not some eccentric monastic order for frustrated monks. I always kept a few copies in the car to show to the secular population, but never to Druze or Muslims. Upon noticing the cover, the young man became somewhat unsettled and thought I had misled him. “What’s that?” he asked.

I appreciated the nice culture of these people: this teenager felt offended to see such a thing. After a pleasant discussion about the progression of the *asramas* in the Vedic social system, I left for home.

Maha-Laksmi noticed that Ola, as well as some other youngsters in the village, was wearing a rabbit on a chain. Some had the rabbit on their clothes or belt buckles. It was the Playboy rabbit, but the villagers had thought it was a cute bunny, not aware of the insidious Western influence entering their midst. Maha-Laksmi explained to Julia the significance of the symbol, but, coming from a culture where women would not go to the beach or allow themselves to be photographed, she wasn’t able to fully comprehend.

Ihab knocked on the door and informed me that I had a phone call. I went upstairs to receive it. Farchat Berani wanted me to come to his home in Daliyat El Carmel for discussion. Farchat was a Ph.D. in history, and I knew him as one of Salman Falach’s chief ministers. He supervised and inspected the history curriculum for Israeli’s Druze villages.

I drove to his house, a three-story, Western-style structure. Farchat, a big man in late middle age, treated me graciously and offered me a bowl of fruit. He also showed me a new book of poetry he had just published, and asked if I could help market it. Then he suggested the possibility that I could teach English in the Druze school system, contributing cultural diversity to the educational program.

Farchat went on to describe some highlights from a trip he had taken to the United States a few years earlier as an Israeli representative. He had given many lectures, presenting the Zionist cause from a Druze perspective. With great emotion, he recounted a talk in Seattle wherein a Palestinian in the audience had challenged him, accusing him of representing an imperialist state. Drawing from his impressive stock of Middle Eastern history, Farchat unleashed an exposition of the spread of the Islamic culture and religion, illustrating how other than Saudi Arabia, all Muslim lands were conquered. Farchat said his opponent had left the room and the audience had cheered. The next day the newspapers had reported the episode, referring in the headlines to “The Druze Cannon.”

Shortly after our Radhastami celebration, my daughter Sita-Rama was born. For a few days we couldn’t select a name for her. We had thought of one we liked, but my mother, who had flown from Philadelphia to see her first grandchild, didn’t like it. After further consideration, we agreed on Sita. Several months earlier, we had written to Danavir dasa in America, requesting him to bestow a name upon our future child, but we had received no reply. The day after taking Sita from Rothschild Hospital in the upper Hadar and officially filing her name with the government, we received a letter from Danavir dasa that had somehow been delayed in the mail. He suggested the name Sita-Rama if a girl was born. We all agreed, including my mother, that this was the arrangement of Radharani and the Supersoul.

Maha-Laksmi: *I was very worried about the impending war. I was telling Ola that I was in great anxiety, with a baby only a few weeks old. I didn’t want her to have to experience bombs and bomb shelters, etc. Ola said, “Don’t worry. We leave this body and we go to another one.”*

It had been several months since we’d been to Rama, and we decided to take Sita to the village where Maha-Laksmi had carried her for so many months. When they had discovered that we were leaving Rama, the residents made us promise to bring the baby to see them when she was born; they had already considered her a member of their village family.

First, we visited Hussein and Sheikha, our former next-door neighbors. They were excited to see us, especially with our new baby. Sheikha held Sita on her lap and began playing with her with the ease and deftness of a grandmother who has handled dozens of children and grandchildren. Maha-Laksmi took a photograph. This was a mistake. Sheikha did not have her head covered. It would be a great embarrassment for her to be photographed without her head covered, although she was an elderly woman. Maha-Laksmi promised to tear the photograph up. Sheikha then put on her head cover and we took more photos. Hussein gave us a big bottle of olive oil as a gift before we left his home.

Then we visited Amin Kasem and his family. They were delighted to see Sita-Rama. As we left, they too donated a large bottle of freshly pressed olive oil. Most Israeli Druze villages produce olive oil, but Rama is especially famous for its olive groves and oil.

Then we walked to the top of the village and visited Sheikha, Kasem’s sister. In her house we had a philosophical discussion with some elder members of the village. As we left, they donated two more liters of olive oil. We went on like this until the floor of the van was filled with bottles of olive oil.

I also visited Kamil, who was considering a trip to India. Kamil presented me with his Arabic translation of *Perfect Questions, Perfect Answers,* work he had done voluntarily. Then I visited Rezek Zedan, who was steadily chanting eight rounds a day.

Villagers recounted that in the weeks following our departure from Rama, there had been two calamities. First, someone had robbed Rama’s Barclay’s bank, where we had had an account, and then during the next week, a Muslim from Rama had rushed into the same bank and knifed another Muslim, injuring him seriously.

Parama-guru dasa decided to produce a video about the Druze–Hare Krsna phenomenon. Wahib Saad had already taken footage of an evening program in Rama and of the opening night in Osafia. Now he was willing to lend Parama-guru his camera for a few days to do the project.

In Osafia, Parama-guru interviewed Yosef and his son in front of the son’s painting of the five horses. Then we went together to the eastern side of the Galil. First, he filmed me outside the centrally located Bank Hapoalim in Maghar, showing books to a crowd of Druze, many in traditional clothing. By Krsna’s arrangement, one man took a *Bhagavad-gita* and the whole interaction was lively and philosophical. Then Parama-guru took more shots of me on *sankirtana* in other areas of the village, especially near Maghar’s statuesque mosque.

After this, we visited a serious young *brahmacari* sheikh, who worked as an engineer. He had studied the *Bhagavad-gita* and other Krsna conscious literature. During the interview, he made many wonderful comments about the *Bhagavad-gita* and the Hare Krsna movement.

Then we went to Rama, where Sheikh Farhoud kindly granted us a spontaneous interview for the video project. Parama-guru then drove to Beit Jann, where he filmed village scenes of children playing and chanting Hare Krsna.

In the evening, we gathered with Salach and Rezek in an olive grove near Rama. The Druze *bhaktas* expressed their realizations about chanting Hare Krsna and the importance of Srila Prabhupada’s books.

Rochelle Katz, former wife of Harold Katz, owner of the Philadelphia 76ers, visited us for three days at our Osafia temple. I had known Rochelle as my Aunt Betty’s best friend of many years. Now she was seeking self-realization. She was on her way to India, where she would visit Vrndavana, the land where Krsna had appeared. Rochelle wore a *sari* during her stay in Osafia and attended the morning *Bhagavatam* classes.

One day, Maha-Laksmi and I toured the Galil with Rochelle. We visited the Nabi Schweib memorial. Rochelle felt inspired by this and said that her rabbi, Zalman Shechter, would be excited to hear that she had visited Jethro’s burial site. After visiting Rama and bathing in the Sea of Galilee, we returned to Osafia. The next day we took Rochelle to Muqraka, near Daliyat El Carmel, where St. George, who later became the patron saint of England, is said to have beheaded a terrible dragon to rescue a maiden. Both Druze and Christians consider this spot sacred.

In Shefaram, where there is substantial interaction between Druze, Muslims, and Christians, a group of Muslim teenagers, curious about my attire, called me over to a porch to speak with them. I showed them the Arabic *Bhagavad-gita,* turning to the “humble sage” illustration and explaining the inherent spiritual equality of all souls. They became rowdy and stated that they would like to see Sadaam march through Tel Aviv. Two of them told me they had just been released from jail the previous day. Somehow, to my surprise, they endeavored and succeeded in collecting enough shekels among them to buy a *Bhagavad-gita.* Then I visited a Maronite Christian church. Lebanese Christians are mostly Maronite. The church administrator took a copy of *Coming Back* in Arabic.

Sadaam Hussein was threatening to attack Israel with biological warheads and even nuclear weapons. The Israeli government began to distribute gas masks to the populace. There were always war planes flying over Osafia to northern destinations. Now the roar of the engines overhead could be heard more frequently as Israel prepared its forces for imminent battle. David Oren said, “I don’t want to see my granddaughter wearing a gas mask!” and offered to pay for plane tickets to the United States for Sita-Rama and Maha-Laksmi.

In 1989, Maha-Laksmi had had no difficulty obtaining a visa to enter the United States. This time, however, the United States embassy presented several complications, including the requirement that I appear for an interview. The reason was that Meyer Kahana had just been shot on the streets of New York by an Arab from New Jersey. The United States government didn’t want the Middle East political conflict fought on their streets. Thus they were reluctant to allow Israelies to visit the States. After a few visits to the embassy in Tel Aviv, however, we obtained the visa. One morning, when I was waiting in line to enter the embassy, there was a bomb threat and eveyone had to be cleared from the area for a few hours.

Although Israel’s capital is Jerusalem, the United States embassy is in Tel Aviv to avoid unnecessarily agitating the Arab world. Jerusalem is disputed territory, and locating an embassy there would indicate acknowledgement that Israel has uncontended rights to the city.

My mother was also enthusiastic about the idea that we visit America, and offered to help with my ticket. Israel’s precarious situation concerned Maha-Laksmi. Simultaneously, Israel’s GBC and Danavir dasa, who was in the United States, expressed that they thought it was a good idea for us to travel to America during this time. I was reluctant to leave such an enlivening program—living with devotees in a Druze village and distributing Srila Prabhupada’s books to the Arabic and Druze population—but considering all factors, we decided to visit the United States for at least three months until the immediate threat of foreign attack subsided. On November 29, 1990, Maha-Laksmi, Sita-Rama, and I flew from Ben-Gurion Airport to New York, where my father met us and drove us to Philadelphia.

## 6: Jethro, the Druze, And Vedic Origins

*A serendipitous, though intensive, case study of Israeli Druze reveals striking similarities between Vedic and Druze philosophy and culture. This article uses these congruities as a springboard for researching Druze origins. Jethro, commonly known as the father-in-law of Moses, is the greatest saint in the pantheon of Druze prophets. He is a Midianite, from a tribe descending from the sons of Keturah, who were sent by Abraham to the east. Analysis of the relationship between Moses and Jethro reveals that Moses oftentimes accepted the role of Jethro’s student. It is suggested that Moses was the disciple of Jethro in practical as well as spiritual matters. This conforms with the Midianite-Kenite hypothesis about the origin of the Hebrew religious system. The author relates this idea to the Druze connection with India, and suggests that Druze oral and written traditions be studied, alongside a reexamination of the Midianite-Kenite hypothesis, to determine the extent of Vedic influence on Judaic history.*

In 1988, I went to northern Israel, not as a student of religious history but as a book-seller. With a team of six other Vaisnavas, I visited the towns, cities, and villages of the Galil, presenting Hebrew translations of the books of A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada to the Jewish population. In the process, we stumbled upon the Druze and entered a world of fascinating interethnic experiences, the subject of this book. I first published the book in 1994. It describes many occurrences, some of which will be narrated below, suggesting a connection between Vedic and Druze culture and philosophy. This paper attempts to indicate a relationship between Vedic civilization and Judaism through Jethro, the major prophet of the Druze, and urges scholars of Judaic and Indic studies to pursue this line of inquiry.

On several occasions we met with the late Sheikh Tarif Amin, former world spiritual leader of the Druze. He expressed genuine appreciation that hundreds of Druze households were placing sets of Vedic books in their homes. Sheikh Amin stated that he wanted the Druze people to work with the Hare Krsna movement “as one race.” This is an amazing declaration, considering that the Druze are primarily known for their isolation and clandestine religious practices.

Salman Falach, Druze Minister of Education in Israel, purchased hundreds of Srila Prabhupada’s books for Druze schools and libraries. For his personal collection, Mr. Falach bought all the books we had in English and, after paging through the *Srimad-Bhagavatam,* stated, “I think after reading these books I will discover that our religion is coming from them.” All segments of Druze society, including farmers, political leaders, educators, sheikhs and businessmen, became fascinated by the Vaisnavas and the Vedic literature they presented. Within a short time, Druze were accepting this Sanskrit literature as their own scriptures. What is the explanation for this?

We spoke with many Druze sheikhs and intellectuals, and they shared deeply with us. Although Druze are considered by the academic community to be an Arabic sect of Islam (Firro, 1992), albeit an unorthodox one, most Druze consider their roots to be Indian. Their beliefs are pervaded by characteristically Vedic conceptions. For instance, their scriptures, like the Vedic *Puranas* and *Itihasas,* and unlike chronicles of Middle Eastern religions, describe history dating back hundreds of millions of years, with incarnations of God in a human form appearing at regular intervals. This is akin to the Vedic idea of the regular appearances of *avataras*. Also, transmigration of the soul is a central tenet of Druze philosophy. In fact, to describe this principle the Druze use the same analogy as Krsna uses in the *Bhagavad-gita* \[2.22\]: “As a person puts on new garments, giving up old ones, the soul similarly accepts new material bodies, giving up the old and useless ones.”

Kamal Jumbalat, the late Druze political hero and renowned spiritualist, often extolled Krsna, the *Bhagavad-gita,* the *Ramayana,* and other Vedic books and personalities in his writings (Dasa, 1994, p. 219). He also spoke of Druze going to India and taking *sannyasa* (Jumbalat, *I Speak for Lebanon,* p. 34), and Jumbalat was himself a vegetarian and considered himself, in his later years, to be living as a *vanaprastha,* in the retired order of life according to the Vedic social system.

Even on esoteric points, sheikhs would surprise us with parallels between Vedic and Druze understandings. For instance, Sheikh Farhoud, an eminent Druze religious leader in the northern Galil, commenting on the ontology of Jesus Christ, explained that the Christ on the cross was an illusory Christ. His exposition was strikingly similar in quality to the Vaisnava understanding, based on the *Kurma Purana* (cited in *Caitanya-caritamrta, Madhya-lila,* 1.117), that the Sita stolen by Ravana was Maya-Sita, or an illusory representation of the real Sita. Occasionally sheikhs expounded on Druze astronomy, and the descriptions were very similar to those of the Fifth Canto of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam*.

Actually, the term “Druze,” like the word “Hindu,” was coined by the Muslims. Almost a thousand years ago, El Drazi was a heretic to the emerging Druze faith, and Muslims, to deride this new sect, derisively referred to the group by the name of their dissident. Druze consider themselves as Muwahidoon, which translates as “the one, eternal religion” (Abu-Izzeddin, 1984; Betts, 1988; Firro, 1992), much as a practitioner of Vedic religion is performing *sanatana-dharma,* the eternal occupation of the soul, rather than any temporally or geographically based religion (Prabhupada, 1972, from Introduction). The current manifestation of the Muwahidoon, known as the Druze, originates from al Hakim Bi-Amr Allah, the sixth Fatimid Caliph, who ruled Egypt during the late 10th and early 11th centuries (Abu-Izzeddin). According to the sheikhs, there are other manifestations of the Muwahidoon.

To avoid persecution as well as to prevent unqualified persons from obtaining knowledge, Druze conceal their beliefs. They have developed a philosophy of social interaction called *taquiyya,* meaning that one should perform duties according to the national milieu while internally remembering one’s identity as a member of the Muwahidoon. Even Druze are not permitted to read their scriptures until they take vows of initiation (Dana, 1980). Yet top sheikhs were revealing confidential information to us, considering us to be Muwahidoon from India. Some Druze pundits revealed that the original language of their scriptures was Sanskrit, and indicated that incarnations such as Buddha and Krsna are described in their books.

In 1928, Richard Gottheil (Hitti, 1928, Foreword) declared, “The Druzes have been the wonder of scholars, All sorts of theories have been advanced by scholars to account for their particular tenets and customs. . .. The scholars have been unsuccessful, and the Druzes still remain the great mystery.” Modern-day research has done little to uncover the origins of the Druze, though Abu-Izzeddin (1984, p. 121) states, “Recently discovered manuscripts throw new light on influences from India,” and provides strong evidence of the Muwahidoon culture extending to India during the middle of the 11th Century. Also, the story of the disappearance of al Hakim is vague, and many scholars and Druze believe that he left Cairo and went to India to meditate during the final stage of his appearance on Earth (Abu-Izzeddin).

To find a spiritual culture with such unmistakable Vedic influence in Israel was astonishing, and this impromptu ethnographic research should be valuable for scholars investigating the connection between India and the Middle East. This author has done some examination of relevant literature and would like to share findings that may have profound implications for the major Western religions.

Jethro, Moses’ father-in-law, father-in-law, is the foremost prophet for the Druze. Their largest annual celebration is held at Jethro’s tomb, near Tiberias (Dana, 1980). Jethro, known as Nabi Schweib by the Druze, was a Midianite (Exodus 18:1), a tribe descending from Keturah, a wife of Abraham (Genesis 25:1–2). Genesis (25:6) describes that Abraham sent the sons of Keturah to the east. Rabbi Menashe Ben Israel (Glazerson, 1984) asserts that Abraham sent them to India. This is more evidence linking the Druze with India, or at least the East. If we examine the relationship between Moses and Jethro, the story becomes even more interesting.

Jethro is commonly understood as an idol-worshipping pagan, a Midianite priest who converted to Judaism by associating with Moses. If we study the role of Jethro, however, this description seems unsatisfying. In Exodus (Chapter 18), Jethro brings Zipporah and her two sons to Moses, and Moses bows down and kisses Jethro. Then Jethro praises the God of the Jews and “took a burnt-offering and sacrifices for God; and Aaron came, and all the elders of Israel, to eat bread with Moses’ father-in-law before God.” It is interesting to conjecture why the elders of Israel are eating food that was sacrificed by an idol worshipper. On the next day Jethro instructs Moses on the formation of an administrative structure for the Israeli people. Some scholars assert that he also helped set up the system of worship (Auerbach, 1975). Before giving instruction, Jethro admonishes Moses for placing himself (Moses) as direct arbitrator for all decisions, great and small. Also, he introduces his remarks by stating, “Hearken now unto my voice, I will give thee counsel, and God be with thee.” It sounds like Jethro has quite a bit of authority, granted by God Himself. According to Exodus, “Moses did all that he \[Jethro\] had said.” Albright (Beegle, 1972) claims that the twelve tribes of Israel formed as a result of Jethro’s reorganization of the people.

Before returning to Egypt, Moses asks Jethro’s permission to embark on the journey (Exodus 4:18). Furthermore, Auerbach (1975, p. 205) writes, “The first sacrifice in Israel was offered up not by Moses but by the Midianite priest Jethro. He too was apparently the one who came to fashion the ark.” Thus Jethro was the first priest of the Israeli nation, and many scholars suggest that he instructed Moses and Aaron in the practices of priesthood. Auerbach (p. 122) concludes that “a strong Midianite influence on Israel’s cult and law is undeniable ” In addition, the Ark of the Covenant is directly connected with Jethro and is considered to be of Midianite origin (Auerbach, 1985). Moreover, when Moses asks Jethro to be a guide, he is literally asking him to “be for us as eyes.” (Baskin, 1983, p. 59) Baskin, quoting the Sipre on Numbers, expresses the request of the Hebrews to Jethro as follows. “In everything that is hidden from our eyes, you shall enlighten us.” Baskin goes on to explain, “Jethro will bring to light things that have been neglected or forgotten.” (Baskin, 1983, p. 59) In summary, the idol-worshipping pagan from the East has an astonishingly easy and profound influence on the Israelite nation and Moses, the prophet of prophets.

I propose that Jethro instructed Moses, not only about administering justice but also about spiritual knowledge and practices. That is, Moses’ prime preceptor for understanding how to please and worship God was Jethro, priest of the Midianites. In Exodus, Jethro takes the initiative in offering sacrifices to YHVH (Exodus 18:12) because he was more familiar with the God of Moses than Moses himself. Interestingly, this is not a particularly original hypothesis.

Many scholars have asserted that the Israelites received their religion from the Kenites, a tribe associated with Jethro (Judges 1:16, 4:11) and a part of the Midianites. *The Jewish Encyclopedia* summarizes the theories of these scholars. “Jethro initiates Moses and Aaron into the worship of YHVH. Several modern scholars believe, in consequence of this statement, that YHVH was a Kenite deity, and that from the Kenites through the agency of Moses his worship passed to the Israelites. The Kenites, then, were a nomadic tribe, more advanced in the arts of life than Israel.” (*The Jewish Encyclopedia,* 1916, p. 467) Ben-Sasson calls this the “Midianite-Kenite” hypothesis on the origin of the name Yahweh (Malamat, 1976, p. 45).

To summarize the line of reasoning, many scholars have believed that the Hebrew nation acquired their religion from the Kenites. These scholars apparently were unaware of the similarity between Druze culture and philosophy and the *Vedas.* Nor did they seem much concerned with the connection between the Kenites and the East. In light of recent ethnological findings regarding the Druze (Dasa, 1994), and the intriguing status and role of Nabi Schweib in relation to Moses, the Kenite theory acquires a fresh cogency.

Jethro was a Kenite, a tribe from the East, and with roots perhaps stemming from India. He is the major preceptor of the Druze, whose present philosophy and lifestyle are remarkably Vedic, considering they are situated in the Middle East. The interaction between Jethro and Moses suggests that Moses, at least in some capacity, was Jethro’s disciple. This hypothesis conforms with the theory of Kenite origins of the Hebrew religion. Thus, the *Vedas* may be the source of the Judaic religious tradition.

In many Midrashim, the East, in comparison with any other direction, is hallowed as a source of light and knowledge. For example, in The Midrash Rabbah (1977, Vol. 3: Numbers, p. 90) it is stated “The east is the source from which light goes forth into the world, and there camped the standard of Judah which bred kings, scholars, and men of pious deeds. For this reason Moses and Aaron and his sons camped there The three tribes of the South, however, who were close to quarrelsome men, perished together ” Also, Jewish commentators throughout the ages have described India as a land of potency and paradise. *The Legends of the Jews* (1925, Vol. 5, p. 196) delineates stories of sages who went to India to obtain “plants of paradise,” and narrates how Solomon (Vol. 4, p. 149) secured water with special healing powers from India. And in Volume 1, page 11, of *The Legends of the Jews,* it is written, “Beyond the inhabited parts to the east is Paradise with its seven divisions, each assigned to the pious of a certain degree.” Genesis (2:8) places Eden in the East. Additionally, Keturah, whose sons went to the East, is especially characterized for fine qualities. According to the Midrashim (The Midrash Rabbah, Vol. 1, p. 543), “\[the name Keturah\] implies that she united piety and nobility she was like one who seals up a treasure ”“Keturah,” according to *The Legends of the Jews* (Vol. 5, p. 264), means “sweet like incense.”

In many fields, such as linguistics (e.g., Kak, 1987, 1988, 1989, 1990), astronomy (e.g., Thompson, 1989, 1997; Kak, 1987), archaeology (e.g., Rao, 1988, 1991, 1993; Jacobi, 1894; Hicks and Anderson, 1990), mathematics (e.g., Seiden-berg, 1962), and Indology (e.g., Rajaram and Frawley, 1995; Burrow, 1973; Jarrige and Meadow, 1980), there is compelling evidence for an original Vedic culture. This article has presented a case for an east to west flow of religious and spiritual knowledge, emanating from ancient India. This hypothesis helps to answer many questions, such as those concerning the relationship between Moses and Jethro, and the origins of the Druze. The community of religious scholars may benefit from pursuing the line of inquiry outlined herein. This would require reviving and rethinking the Midianite-Kenite theory, with reference to Torah, *midrashim, agaddah,* and scholarly analysis that has been done on this topic throughout the generations. Additionally, and perhaps most importantly, fruitful investigation would entail researching the oral and written traditions of the Druze, which may be a formidable task, considering their heritage of secrecy.

## 7: Letters

**ABBREVIATIONS:** DG—Dhira Govinda dasa; ML—Maha-Laksmi dasi; PG—Parama-guru dasa

November 5, 1988—Dhira Govinda dasa (Haifa *sankirtana* house) to Danavir dasa (United States):

The ecstasy is increasing as everyone is entering more and more into the fire of distributing Srila Prabhupada’s books. I’ve just returned from a week of traveling *sankirtana* with Bhakta Pini. It was very successful—lots of books distributed. Everyone here (Bhakta Pini, Maha-Laksmi, Santasya, and Nagapatni) is doing very nicely and is happy to be engaged in this program of book distribution. Also, we have a nice spiritual program, and everyone is doing lots of reading. Personally, presenting the books as the main paraphernalia and in a direct way, I’m experiencing that the people of Israel as a whole are advancing in Krsna consciousness, based on *sankirtana* I’ve done over the past several years. So many people are favorable, are interested in the books, and basically have a nice opinion about Hare Krsnas. Selling books and sets directly is most enlivening. I can feel myself becoming purified

November 26, 1988—DG (Haifa) to Danavir dasa (United States):

*Sankirtana*scores Nov. 13–19: DG—15.97 (*laksmi*), 108.15 (book points) (6.5 sets); Nagapatni—14.63, 105.65 (7 sets); Santasya—17.02, 95.35 (6.5 sets); Maha-Laksmi—10.27, 62.9 (4 sets); Dolayatra—2.7, 18.55 (1 set); Isvara Krsna/ Radha-Madhava—1.6, 12.8 (1 set); Guna-avatara/Devahuti—11.2 bk. points; Bhakta Pini—2.4, 3.9 bk. points; Govinda’s—2.3 bk. points.

Totals: 65.51 *laksmi*, 420.8 bk. points, including 26 full five-book sets. Bhakta Pini was ill most of the week. Dolayatra joined in for only the last two days.

So this week, November 20–26, is bigger. Here are the scores without the BBT scores, which I haven’t received yet.

December 5, 1988—DG (Haifa) to Danavir dasa (United States):

Here are the recent *sankirtana* scores:

Nov. 6–12: 250 bk. pts., 12 sets , 91 Maha big

Nov. 13–19: 420.8 pts., 26 sets, 161 Maha big

Nov. 20–26: 432.4 pts., 27 sets + 2 completed sets, 165 Maha big

Nov. 27–Dec. 3: 383.20 pts., 27 sets + 1 completed set, 147 Mahabig

The devotees here are all blissfully engaged in distributing Srila Prabhu-pada’s books. We’ve heard from different sources that there are 10,000 Arabic *Bhagavad-gitas* somewhere in Cairo. Bhakta Yigal says you may know more details. We could distribute them at a nice price in no time. Any information on this or about any Arabic literature will facilitate the book distribution here greatly Last Tuesday I returned from *sankirtana* with Bhakta Pini after the two of us had given seven sets during the day. The *matajis* gave three, making a ten-set day.

Maha-Laksmi dasi (Haifa) to Danavir dasa (United States):

Just to show that not only Druze are making spiritual advancement but also \[Jewish\] Israelis, our old neighbor from next door in Frishman, Mrs. Auerbach, she used to always complain that we make the stairs dirty and she would always shout at me. So we decided to pacify her and agreed to clean the stairs thrice a week and brought her plates of *prasadam.* Now she gives gifts all the time and only gets angry if we don’t pay her a visit when we come to Tel Aviv. Her daughter, who is a psychologist, borrowed the *Bhagavad-gita* from us, read it, her husband read it, and also her fifteen-year-old daughter read it and received an excellent mark in an exam in school from all the knowledge she had. *Sankirtana yajna ki jaya!*

P. S. I cannot help but tell you that just now Dhira Govinda Prabhu came back to the car from *sankirtana* after he has given six sets! I think this is a day’s record.

While we were waiting for him the neighbor’s son came to ask us if we have a place to stay. He kept inviting us over and over to his house and brought to the car for us hot herb tea. Sometimes they come to the car with a tray of hot milk\* ...We talked to him a little bit, and he told that his friend’s grandfather had died and now he took birth as a young boy and he is going to marry a girl who in his last birth was none other than his granddaughter. So it is easy to preach to them about our real identity as being the servants of God because they can see that all other designations, such as father, mother, wife, etc., are temporary.

P. P. S. This week our northern party has distributed forty-two sets in Hebrew and two English sets. Book distribution *ki jaya!*

“Whatever is offerable we offer, otherwise we avoid politely without offending them.

December 10, 1988—DG (Haifa) to Danavir dasa (United States):

We usually take rest on Saturday, but it was Saguna’s first day with the party and she was so enthusiastic that I thought a few of us would go out for a few hours. Saguna went with Santasya, and they gave six sets in two hours. I went out late, gave the two sets in my cart, returned to the car to find disappointed devotees with no books to distribute. Nagapatni soon returned to the van, having given three sets, only to experience the same frustration as me. Bhakta Pini hadn’t made it out of the car yet, and awoke to find only one set left. He was determined and sold the set to a curious onlooker hanging around the car. That finished the books and we had to return, meditating on tomorrow’s book distribution. I hope you’ve received my previous letters with the scores. This will be the biggest week ever, although totals have not been calculated yet. It’s just amazing. We now have seven devotees and only one car. At least one devotee has to stay back each day. Maha-Laksmi, Dolayatra, Saguna, Bhakta Pini, Nagapatni, and Santasya Prabhus are all doing wonderfully in the Haifa *sankirtana* program. I’m beginning to catch a little of their enthusiasm. So everyone’s *sadhana* is nice. We return from *sankirtana* early as you suggested. Lots of time for reading Srila Prabhupada’s books. Also, we have nice morning programs in Haifa.

December 18, 1988—DG (Haifa) to Danavir dasa (United States):

The devotees here are happy to hear that their attempts to distribute Srila Prabhupada’s books are inspiring others to do the same. In my last letter (sent along with *japa* beads and recommendation letter for Bhakta Pini), I gave the *sankirtana* scores through Dec. 3. But since then we’ve increased, by the mercy of Lord Caitanya. Dec 4–10, a new Israeli record: 171 Maha big books, including thirty (30) full sets and a total of 439 book points. Mother Santasya distributed 54 Maha big books and ten sets. But actually, out of humility, she gives half her score to her partner. During the week she was involved in the distribution of 100 Maha big books and 19 sets. Both the men and women devotees are going in full devotional dress, including *tilaka,* and preaching directly. It’s real ecstasy. But what I wanted to say was that this week, Dec 11–17, we defeated all the old records of last week. Dec 11–17: 172 Maha big books (31 sets) and 451.2 book points. In one day, Mother Nagapatni gave five sets. Also this week the BBT team of Guna-avatara and Devahuti Prabhus gave three sets and twenty-one Maha big books. So everyone is having a lot of transcendental fun with this book distribution.

DG (Haifa) to Danavir dasa (United States):

consistently giving 3–4 sets every time out. Yesterday Nagapatni and Maha-Laksmi went as a team and gave four sets. As I told you in a previous letter, we’re going in devotional dress and giving books and sets directly. Everyone is ecstatic. We’ve met many Druze who say they want to go to Lebanon and distribute Srila Prabhupada’s books if we have Arabic books.

December 23, 1988—Danavir dasa (San Diego) to DG (Haifa):

The book distribution reports are elixir for my soul, and they are so tasty that I keep longing for more, although I’m ashamed of being so greedy. Still, I can’t help it.

We could give more English sets, but we’ve sold out. Also, the *matas* (ML and Santasya) received an order for fifty Hebrew sets for Druze educational institutions from the chief minister of Druze Education. He personally donated 750 shekel for a 30-vol. English set. The fifty-set transaction is scheduled to manifest next week. We’re praying it will go through. The weather in these mountain villages is often subzero degrees C. We’ve seen snow on *sankirtana* in Israel this year. The devotees are determinedly going out in all conditions, although their Israeli bodies are not accustomed to such temperatures. Some have never experienced snow in Israel before. And the *sankirtana* involves many kilometers of walking up and down steep mountainsides. But it’s real nectar. Krsna is adventure. Also, last week Krsna allowed me to distribute six sets in one day to teachers and school principals in a village named Maghar.

January 15, 1989—Danavir dasa (San Diego) to DG (Haifa):

The *sankirtana* score of December 18–24 of 701.1 book points, 43 sets, 2 English *Bhagavatam* sets, and 100 *laksmi* points is remarkable. I thought 450 book points was superb, but you are destroying material conceptions. I don’t know if I should tell you, but even your 450 point scores would generally place in the top two spots for North America. In the second World War, there was a song/slogan, “Give me ten men who are stout-hearted men and I’ll soon give you ten thousand more!” I think the Israeli devotees are the stout-hearted devotees that will generate ten thousand more—soon. Keep up this nectarine preaching spirit.

I hope you can order more English *Bhagavatam* sets from the U.K.

I have received many letters from all the devotees in Israel and have copied them and sent them to IWR for a grand article, or maybe it’ll become a series.

I know that the austerities involved in book distribution in the mountainous areas is quite severe, including freezing weather. I thank you all thousands of times for performing this sacred task, and pray for your protection and spiritual advancement.

January 15, 1989—Danavir dasa (San Diego) to ML (Haifa):

The editor of IWR has asked for copies of all letters received from Israel regarding this phenomenal “set explosion.” (I am sending copies.) Now the Second Canto has arrived. This is very auspicious.

The story you told of the Minister of Education for the Druze population is a great example. This is exactly what Srila Prabhupada wanted: that the devotees should approach the leaders, show them our books, and try to convince them of the importance of this knowledge.
