Right now the Middle East is certainly a hotbed, and if any place has a need, it needs Srila Prabhupada’s books. Please continue on with your enthusiasm and determination.

September 26, 1990—Madhusevita dasa (Milano, Italy) to DG (Osafia):

Thank you very much for your letter dated 16/9/90. Your enthusiasm for book distribution is a great encouragement for us engaged in the service of printing Srila Prabhupada’s books.

I have a few points:

(1) Could you give us an estimate of how many Arabic books you would be able to distribute every three months?

(2) Are you going to continue the distribution of Hebrew books? If so, how many approx. every three months?

October 2, 1990—Ananda-svarupa Swami (Cairo) to DG (Osafia):

Thanks for your letters, reports, encouraging words, and above all great patience with me. I should have been in touch a lot more regularly. Please forgive me. However, with me it is not a case of out of touch out of mind. I think about Israel’s Arab preaching all the time.

I am very eager to come to Israel and tomorrow I am going to the Israeli Consulate for my visa. I sometimes listen to the news from Israel radio and I hear that now the weather has become cooler. I plan to come on October 19 and spend at least ten days. Is that all right with you? Can you meet me in Tel Aviv? I want to bring a friend with me, a prospective devotee. He is a student, son of the Indian vice-Ambassador to Egypt. Top family and good brains. His mother chants thirty-two rounds a day, his sister and father eight. They became devotees during the last six months and gave up eating meat and drinking—stock in trade in the Diplomatic Corps.

Three new titles in Arabic (*Readings in Vedic Literature, Isopanishad,* and *Consciousness, The Missing Link*) are either already printed or being printed in Hong Kong.

Your newsletter was great! I am excited by the discoveries you are making. Druze and other Arab groups have an inestimable potential. I am convinced Mahaprabhu Himself is revealing these wonderful things to us. At least, I feel responsible to help as much as I can. Please pray to Lord Caitanya and Nityananda to be merciful to me and engage in this preaching program to the full limits of my capacity.

I know Ravanari personally. He toured South India sometime in 1982 or 1983 when I was in charge. I took care of him night and day for about three weeks. I even gave him my personal Nrsimha kavaca (in a solid gold box) to protect him (as he was going back to Beirut).

P. S. If I haven’t already told you, your production of the Arabic *Coming Back* was just wonderful. I am immensely pleased with it and long to see more of the same. The quotes from Kamal Jambalat were good. Did you get any feed-back on them? Anyhow, this is something we can discuss at depth when we meet.

Danavir dasa to DG:

you will continue with this spirit more and more. You’re definitely going for Prabhupada’s most treasured mercy, namely the Arabic population. As you know, the famous quotation from Srila Prabhupada, “Anyone who is willing to preach to the Arabs, I’m wishing to take the dust from his lotus feet and place it upon my head.” And as I think everyone would agree, especially at this moment, preaching to the Arabs will be the most important work for bringing peace to this world.

October 7, 1990—DG (Osafia) to Jayadvaita Swami (San Diego):

Thank you for your letters of Sept. 8 and Sept. 19. Madhusevita Prabhu has responded promptly and has expressed willingness to support Ravanari Prabhu’s translation work in Israel. I have written to Ravanari Prabhu in Berkeley, conveying the relevant portions of Madhusevita’s letter. Now it’s up to his decision. We can offer lots of ecstatic Arabic preaching and a rent-free room in the temple, at least until Sept. ’91, in which to translate. So I don’t think the BBT subsidy would have to be very much. I’ve asked Ravanari to do the needful and come as soon as possible. I’ve also sent to Berkeley the manuscript of PQPA in Arabic, translated by a local Druze. There’s great potential here, especially for a native Arabic speaker such as Ravanari. I’m going daily on direct book distribution, which is of course nectarean. But it doesn’t leave much time for follow-up preaching, for which I feel the Druze community is ripe, as their villages are saturated with books which they’ve shown they are reading. I think Ravanari could do a lot in this field, even attracting some full-time devotees.

I noticed in the GBC resolutions that Lebanon is not listed in anyone’s zone. Who is the GBC there? The hundreds of thousands of Druze there, though poor, will certainly be eager for Srila Prabhupada’s books. Can we organize something to get them books? Gaura-bhakta Prabhu met a favorable Lebanese printer at a book fair in France who is interested to cheaply print our books. I think Harivilasa Prabhu has the details. Also, we had great success distributing books in the one village of Aloine Muslims in Israel. Syria has millions of Aloines, so it also promises to be a great field. There certainly seems to be a lot of enthusiasm for Arabic preaching.

You’ve probably heard that more than 100,000 Russians are immigrating to Israel. Among the Jewish population, the Russians have always been the ethnic group most eager for Srila Prabhupada’s books. So this Aliyah is a great opportunity.

As far as our being affected by the war threat—well, some devotees are visiting travel agencies and arranging transport to places where there is less likelihood of poison gas bombs dropping in the vicinity The Israeli government is distributing gas masks to all the citizens. The national economy is a fright. But somehow the Druze are still buying books. So all is well.

October 15, 1990—Sivarama Swami (Bhaktivedanta Manor) to DG (Osafia):

It appears that there is no GBC for Lebanon at the present. Although my “speculation,” if I can say that, is that Harivilasa Prabhu is in charge, since he is the GBC for the Mid-East

October 20, 1990—Danavir dasa (Brooksville, Florida) to ML (Osafia):

I am in due receipt of your letter dated August 23, 1990, from the Golan Heights Your description of the preaching in the Druze village Massade, which is near the border of Lebanon, is very ecstatic. It is actually a good indication that you are seeing everything as Vrndavana. Lord Caitanya Mahaprabhu also saw Vrndavana everywhere. Advaita Acarya also said this about Him: “That wherever you are, My Lord, that is Vrndavana.” The type of preaching that you and Dhira Govinda Prabhu and the others are engaged in there is certainly historic, and it is extremely valuable for bringing about a possible peace in this horrible world full of dissension. It is also inspiring that you have continued with the preaching, although your baby was imminent.

October 21, 1990—DG (Osafia) to Danavir dasa:

I’ve gotten many encouraging letters from around the globe, from such noteworthy Vaisnavas as Tamal Krishna Goswami, Sivarama Swami, and Jayadvaita Maharaja. Practically, the Italian BBT is printing 10,000 Arabic books (*Sri Isopanishad* and *Consciousness, The Missing Link*), and the BBT has agreed to subsidize Ravanari Prabhu’s translation work in Israel. We’re now waiting to hear from Ravanari for his decision. We’re expecting Ananda-svarupa Swami and his Indian bhakta to arrive in Israel tomorrow. The book distribution is going well. I feel frustrated, though, that I’m not able to arrange time to do much follow-up preaching. Especially in the Druze community, they read the books so voraciously, I’m sure follow-up preaching would yield some quick and significant results. Maybe Ananda-svarupa Swami and/or Ravanari can fill in this gap. We’ve just held a very nice Govardhana-puja festival, with the devotees from Tel Aviv (Nitya-siddha, Murari Caitanya, Suddha-sattva) and some guests attending.

October 24, 1990—DG (Osafia) to Madhusevita dasa (Italy):

Currently, I go out with *Bhagavad-gita* Arabic, *Coming Back* Arabic, and some Hebrew books on the side. My average monthly distribution is about eighty *Bhagavad-gita* Arabic, ten Maha big Hebrew, 120 *Coming Back* Arabic, and a few Hebrew small. If you send *Sri Isopanishad* and *Consciousness, The Missing Link,* at a low price, I estimate I will distribute about 350 of each in a three-month period. I am currently the only distributor here. There is a great potential in Israel for Russian book distribution, as hundreds of thousands of Russians are coming to the country. HARIBOL

October 29, 1990—DG (Osafia) to Hrdayananda Maharaja (Los Angeles):

Your advice is well taken. The Italian BBT has agreed to subsidize Ravanari Prabhu’s translation work in Israel. I have not yet succeeded in contacting Ravanari to inform him of the good news. Some other good news is that the Italian BBT has just printed 10,000 small books in Arabic. I’m planning to be in the States for Dec.–Feb., but when I return to Israel I’ll be happy to distribute these books. Generally, there seems to be quite a bit of enthusiasm from different corners for the Arabic preaching mission. I think if some overall coordination were to be arranged, some very nice results would be forthcoming. As I’ve heard that you will be the GBC for Israel, I’m eager to do whatever I’m able to assist you to spread Krsna consciousness in this part of the world.

October 29, 1990—DG (Osafia) to Ananda-svarupa Swami (Amsterdam and Cairo):

So the Italian BBT has agreed to subsidize Ravanari Prabhu’s translation work in Israel. I’m anxious to distribute the new Arabic books upon my return from the States. Also, Gaura-bhakta Prabhu is eager to come and preach to Arabs. Maybe you can do something in the way of coordinating everything during your visit to Mayapur this year. I’m going out daily on book distribution; there’s plenty of follow-up work to be done in the Arab and Druze communities here in Israel. Hare Krsna.

November 13, 1990—Hrdayananda Maharaja (Los Angeles) to DG (Osafia):

I am quite interested in your preaching program to the Druze community and I look forward to meeting with you to discuss your project. I am very eager to see the preaching develop in Israel. Will you be coming to India?

November 14, 1990—Jayadvaita Swami (San Diego) to DG (Elkins Park, PA):

So let us see what happens with Ravanari Prabhu.

Regarding Lebanon, if the GBC resolution doesn’t mention it, I assume that it’s no one’s zone.

I’ve asked Madhusevita Prabhu to take direct interest in Hebrew and Greek BBT affairs, and I think he is starting to do that. He is very competent, and I am hopeful that because he is relatively nearby he will be able to help you. Formerly, the American devotees assisted in the preaching throughout the world. Even in Europe, the Americans were the leaders. Now perhaps it is time for the European devotees to take the lead, not only in Europe but in the Middle East.

Despite so many difficulties, the preaching you have done is having effect. These difficulties are temporary, but our endeavor to serve Krsna is always a success.

November 14, 1990—Gaura-bhakta dasa (France) to DG (Osafia):

I am still in France, because I am always waiting for the visa for Egypt, which will be given by the authorization of the French government. It is a question of time now. I am very happy that Ravanari Prabhu is back to Arabic preaching. He is so valuable. Please take care. Thank you for your wonderful dedication to the mission of Srila Prabhupada—the books are the base. I am very eager to come to Israel and amid you in your ecstatic preaching. I applied visa application in Israel embassy, I showed them the papers I got from you, so they accepted to give it to me, but it will be stamped on my passport. I told them this is not very good, because I cannot travel in other Arabic countries. So I refused.

P. S. Ananda-svarupa Swami came to Europe and went back to Cairo. Two *bhaktas* are with him. He’s very enthusiastic. Abhidheya dasa is a Moroccan devotee. He left with his truck full of books to preach in his country.

I am staying in Paris temple and preaching in the Arabic areas. I meet nice people. Also Druze people. I am dreaming to go to Lebanon and preach. I think I should wait little time until political crisis stop. How is Parama-guru dasa doing? Please give my *dandavats* to all devotees of Israel. Please if you can, send me the Arabic prospectus you made in Israel with the four regulative principles (blue one). I would like to copy it and distribute in France. I am waiting the answer from Maharaja what to do. I may go to Algeria and visit few contacts.

Maybe it will be a good thing if you could visit Harivilasa in San Francisco.

November 27, 1990—Hrdayananda Maharaja (LA) to DG (Elkins Park, PA):

I am very happy to hear that the Italian BBT will help to subsidize Ravanari Prabhu’s translation work. That is very nice. I will send this letter to your address in Pennsylvania, and you may contact me at 213-659-8921.

Regarding our written communications about Arabic preaching, I think we should be careful not to boldly state that we are trying to convert them. Of course, Krsna consciousness is not at all sectarian. It is a spiritual science that will help the practitioner of any religion to perfect his own tradition. Since written communications often have a long life and are seen by many, many people besides those for whom it is intended, and since there are all kinds of people who would like to sabotage our work, we should not give the impression that we are trying to take people away from their traditions, especially in an area as sensitive as the Middle East. In any case, I appreciate your work very much and I share your enthusiasm for the opportunities there. I am always ready to help you in whatever way I can, and Danavir Prabhu has spoken highly to me of your service.

December 7, 1990—PG dasa (Cairo) to DG (Elkins Park, PA):

I’m having nice time here. Went to the Great Pyramid, chanted *japa* in the “king’s chamber” with some Egyptian boys. The same feeling on the streets like doing *sankirtana* in Muslim villages. Celebrated Bhaktisiddhanta’s Appearance with a few friends of the *yatra.* Also visited Mr. and Mrs. Reina. They’re very nice Vaisnavas taking now to KC. Mr. Reina finishes his job here on February and moving to Delhi. He says that there’s a possibility to get a five-year visa to India with limitation of entering six months at a time and six months out. Unfortunately, Maharaja had a sudden change of plans. He’s in India not to be *Coming Back.* The Egyptian *yatra* which is practically one devotee. Sriman Surya dasa (ex-*bhakta* Simon) is to wind up the place at 27th Dec. ’90.

P. S. Mr. Reina is very much impressed and appreciates the “a lot of activity” of Israel *yatra*. Hope you are well.

February 5, 1991—PG (Vrndavana) to DG (USA):

P. P. S. The center in Cairo is closed. Ananda-svarupa Swami changed direction, he’s touring South India He’ll be in Mayapur for a few days in the beginning of Feb. to meet Harivilasa (Gaura-bhakta is also here). Then he’s going to Europe. Please send a copy of the English “The Nation” article.

May 28, 1991—DG (Osafia) to Trai dasa (Italy):

I’m leaving a copy of an Arabic translation of PQPA with Guna-avatara Prabhu. The translation was done by Kamil Schadey of Rama village. His phone # is 04–881560. Also in Rama are Rezek Zedan, 04881649, who has been chanting eight rounds per day for more than one and a half years, and Salach Abas, 04986385, who was doing book distribution in his village. The blueprints for *Coming Back* are in the cabinet in the office in Osafia. In every Druze village (21) and most Muslim and Christian villages we have many favorable contacts who have the books. Please let me know if there’s anything I can do to assist you or any other devotee who is preaching in this field, or anywhere in Israel.

I hope you have a nice stay in Israel. The preaching potential is fantastic, especially with the influx of Russian immigrants. Hare Krsna.

P. S. I had sent a copy of this PQPA translation to Berkeley for Ravanari Prabhu to check, but I don’t know what happened I estimate there are about 2,700 copies of Arabic *Coming Back,* about two-thirds of which are in Osafia and the rest at Frishman Street in Tel Aviv. The printing cost of the book was about $1. We had to start from scratch, with no plates. It’s a special Arabic edition with a quote from Kahlil Gibran at the beginning and a picture of Kamal Jumbalat as one of the famous people who accept reincarnation. *Haribol!*

April 10, 1994—Bhakta David Karmeli (ISKCON Denver) to DG (Gainesville):

I’m on TSKP most of the time. Came in second here in the X-mas marathon. All’s simple and blissful ...

## 8: A Hare Krsna Devotee In the Israeli Army

*May 1986*

Riding with a busload of recruits to Tel Ha Shomer, outside Tel Aviv, I wondered how I came to be entering Basic Training in the Israeli army. About two years earlier I had entered the Bhakta Training program at the Hare Krsna temple, also outside Tel Aviv. Soon after, having been in Israel more than a year, I became an *oleh hadash,* an Israeli citizen. Technically, *oleh hadash* means “new ascendant.” When Jews from the diaspora come to Israel, the Jewish nation considers them to have spiritually ascended.

At the age of eighteen, both men and women enter the army. Men are required to serve for three years and women for two. After that, the army puts men on *melooeem* (reserve), from which they are called for duty about one month per year. As I was already twenty-five years old, they wanted me to serve one year of military service, after which I would be placed on reserve.

There are religious exemptions from the Israeli army. Yeshiva students are a prime example of those not required to do military service. I hoped being a devotee might exempt me too. At pre-recruitment interviews, therefore, I mentioned to the officers that I was a devotee of Krsna. I wanted to serve the Israeli people, but I really wanted to serve *all* living entities. I explained that I didn’t think the best service I could render involved carrying a rifle. Teaching authentic spiritual knowledge was the best use of my abilities. Unaffected by my entreaties, and not recognizing Krsna consciousness as a religion, Misrad Ha Gioose (the Recruitment Office) mailed me a directive to appear at their offices on a certain date at a specified time. I had consented, and a few hours later I was on my way to Tel Ha Shomer, a huge military and medical facility near Petah Tikva. I knew where it was—I had often visited this place as a civilian to perform *sankirtana* among the staff.

Moshe, the barber, posed the first problem. Everyone entering the army had to cut their hair short. I had just shaved my head the previous day, so this was not a problem. But Moshe wanted to cut off my *Sikha.* I explained that the *Sikha* had spiritual significance. Moshe didn’t care. He had a job to do, and was adamant about removing my *Sikha*. Simon, one of the officers, appeared to settle the dispute. After a brief discussion about the similarities and differences between *peos* and *Sikha,* Simon desisted. He didn’t want a controversy, especially if it could lead to legal action against Tsahal for religious discrimination. Although he agreed that I could keep my *Sikha,* however, Simon insisted that I remove my neck beads. He said they posed a hazard during training exercises.

Moving slowly through their procedures, the army personnel outfitted us with uniforms of an approximate fit. Already unsettled on their first day in the army, the recruits were perplexed about how to react to a Hare Krsna in their division. Officers and staff, however, appeared trained to move everyone along according to plan, without becoming alarmed by personal eccentricities. Each month, thousands of young adults passed through the recruitment station on their way to Basic Training, each with their specific expression of youthful independence. Basic Training, however, was designed to convert this unbridled tendency into submissive discipline, whereby these young men could work effectively as a team under pressured, life-threatening conditions.

“My sister’s a Hare Krsna!” exclaimed Amir. I knew all the devotees in the country, and doubted whether his sister was one of them. I assumed he was confusing Krsna consciousness with another group. When he mentioned her name, however, it turned out to be Radha-Madhava-devi dasi, a stalwart *sankirtana* devotee. She had been a member of our *yatra* for many years. Amir was favorable toward Krsna consciousness, and I was glad to have found a friend.

Group calisthenics, sleeping in crowded tents, timed running with a small unit of new soldiers, tough-sounding officers, and seemingly endless bureaucracy characterized our few days at Tel Ha Shomer. Finally, we were apportioned to training camps in different parts of Israel, and we piled into buses according to our assignment.

Slowly, the bus navigated the rough, dusty roads through the West Bank. After stopping briefly in the town of Hebron, we arrived at Bahd Arbah, after several hours of travel.

Tired recruits alighted from the bus, their fear and insecurity masked by arrogance and defiance. Officers barked us into straight lines. As we bungled our orders, the commands grew harsher and louder. When we were finally in line, a commander ordered us to stand straight. A young, husky sergeant stood opposite us. Glowering, his outstretched hand flashed metallic in the glare of the sun. Looking closer, I saw he was clasping a full cartridge of bullets. In a rage, he shouted names at us that I hadn’t learned at the *ulpan*. Then came the ultimate command: that we forget high school. This was the army. Forget everything else. Of course, a few of us, myself included, were older and already well-distanced from high school, but he was addressing the vast majority of recruits, all of whom fell between the ages of eighteen and twenty. Then someone smirked, or blinked, or did *something* that irked the sergeant, who took the opportunity to single that man out for a severe rebuke.

The officers divided us into units, and then each unit split into three platoons, each containing about twenty men. My platoon consisted mostly of Israeli Jews in their late teens, but also an older Christian Arab from Nazareth and two *oleem hadasheem* in their mid-twenties. We were part of Machzor Mai, the recruitment group in May. Somehow, May was renowned as the month when the highest number of difficult characters entered Basic Training. Our platoon was no exception. One young man was attempting his fourth Basic Training, as the officers had refused to promote him after his first three attempts due to serious discipline problems. Although the army is generally a difficult place for all soldiers, most consider Basic Training the toughest portion of military life, and no one desires to repeat the experience. Several convicted criminals and juvenile delinquents also graced our barracks.

Adi was an educated, American-Israeli with dual citizenship and a sincere Zionism. We immediately established a rapport and chose to occupy adjacent bunks. Adi’s name reminded me of the *Adi-lila* section of the *Caitanya-caritamrta.* At least something in Bahd Arbah, I thought, will help me remember Krsna.

Adi knew Hebrew better than I did, so offered to help me with communication. For the commanders, there was no thought of special consideration for some-one with weak Hebrew. This was the Israeli army. If you didn’t know Hebrew, you had better learn it. Although I had a basic knowledge of the language from Hebrew Sunday school, an *ulpan* in Arad, and mostly from practical experience on *sankirtana,* the sharp commands and quick street jargon often left me staring blankly.

Richard was a South African *oleh.* He was, for me, a pleasant combination of complacent mellowness and cynicism. As the training progressed, he irritated some, who thought he used his age and fabricated injuries to avoid doing his share. He was completely accepting of me, however, finding my devotion to Krsna a welcome respite from the torment of the Training.

One would not expect to find John, who was stationed in the adjacent platoon, in the Israeli army. He was a tall, blond American from a Christian background. He had come to Israel to play professional basketball. From what I had heard, Israel imported many of its best basketball players from the United States, many of them black. Somehow or other, the government made these players citizens, so they had to do a short stint in the army. Besides his athletic prowess, John was also an intellectual. He was considering settling in Israel permanently, as he had a Sephardic Moroccan girlfriend he was planning to marry.

During our first evening at Bahd Arbah, a commander entered and we stood at attention next to our bunks. Walking slowly down the middle aisle, he stopped before me and spouted a Hebrew command. I couldn’t understand what he had said and my face registered ignorance. Adi whispered that the commander wanted me to be the leader of the platoon. Realizing that my Hebrew was weak, however, the commander chose a different soldier.

Line-up was at 5:00 a.m. We met our commanders, whom we were instructed to address as “Ha Mifaked” or “Ha Mifakedet,” depending on gender. Four first-line commanders monitored our platoon. There was Yitzik, a secular Jew from Acko; Noah, a young, devout Jew from the religious stronghold of Bene Barak, near Petah Tikva; Osnat, a young, energetic woman from a Sephardic *moshav* between Jerusalem and Tel Aviv. Rivka, a mild-mannered, Ashkenazie kibbutznik, was our fourth first-line commander. Above them was Yosi, the commander who had entered our barracks the previous evening. Yosi’s commander was Yaacov, who looked like he could have played the lead role in the movie *Exodus*. Chaim, a middle-aged religious *oleh* from the United States, was the commander of the entire unit. When he heard that I was a vegetarian, he assured me that my needs would be provided for, and he seemed pleased that I had a degree in psychology and experience in counseling.

From the start, several trainees tested their authorities. Even the smallest infraction of regulation, however, was met with severe chastisement. As I was about five years older than all the first-line commanders, they treated me more respectfully than the teenagers. Also, they saw that I wasn’t defiant, just a little slow due to the language barrier. They also saw that I was out of shape and not mechanically inclined (I found it difficult to disassemble and rebuild weapons).

We had shooting practice every day, and the American M-16 was the first rifle we received. We were strictly ordered to carry our guns everywhere.

Awaking for the second morning line-up, I found my M-16 missing. At line-up, a few others had the same embarrassing problem. “Wolf, where’s your weapon?”

“Well ” An officer had managed to take it from under my bed while I was asleep. I then learned to keep it directly under the mattress. They told me to make sure it never happened again. Still, that evening, returning from the lavatory, Chaim confronted me. “Dahveed, where’s your weapon?” Obviously, I had forgotten to take it with me. Apparently feeling pity, Mifaked Chaim shook his head and, in a serious tone, murmured that this was really a bad habit.

Our division had many discipline problems, and everyone would suffer as a result. One boy, Shlomo, was a real menace for the officers. Sometimes when he became angry he would point his weapon at people. They took away his rifle and eventually put him in jail. Due to these and other antics, our platoon became accustomed to running extra laps until late at night. Although six hours of sleep was scheduled into the training program, our platoon rarely got more than three or four.

Despite this, I was determined to maintain at least sixteen rounds of *japa* each day. I always carried my *japa* beads and a *Teachings of Lord Caitanya* in one of my holster pockets. By the third day, my *japa* chanting had become an object of everyone’s attention in the barracks. Half-joking and half-serious, the trainees wanted to know what I was doing. I explained the difference between spirit and matter, and the purifying effect of spiritual sound. I happened to have a few extra sets of beads, and guided a few of them in chanting their first round of *japa*.

“Have you seen the movie *Hair*?” I asked them while relaxing in our bunks after the day’s activities. Almost all knew the popular Hare Krsna tune from the movie. I pulled out a small pair of *karatalas,* clanged them together, and began chanting. About fifteen voices joined the eternal chant, although they sung the words of the mantra in a variety of sequences. Who expected to be having such fun in the army? After three mantras I stood up and began swaying. A few soldiers danced wildly in the middle of the barracks. We had established what would become our nightly ritual.

As the first week ended, the entire unit was buzzing about Krsna consciousness, but our platoon especially was adopting its practices. Some of the most difficult discipline problems became stimulated by the philosophy of Krsna consciousness. I had some books, such as Hebrew editions of *Chant and Be Happy, Sri Isopanishad,* and *Perfect Questions, Perfect Answers,* and I distributed them to the soldiers. During long runs, and practically whenever there was an opportune moment, the soldiers would request me to lead a *kirtana*. When I did, they would dance and chant uproariously. Not only did they enjoy it, but they saw that when they sang Hare Krsna, the officers became disconcerted.

One day, Osnat took me out of afternoon line-up. “Wolf, you must stop influencing these boys with Hare Krsna.” She was stern. I was not actively preaching, I explained. Soldiers would approach me for books and ask questions, and I would oblige. Osnat was adamant, though. She insisted that even if I was asked, I should not distribute literature or lead *kirtanas.* She said that because I was older than the others, they were being unduly affected by Hare Krsna. Although her ultimatum was difficult to accept, I nodded my head and enunciated the obligatory “*Cain,* Ha Mifakedet \[Yes, Commander\].” Turning to walk away, I suddenly heard “Daveed?” Osnat, no longer the grim commander, had transformed into a curious nineteen year old. “Do you have one of those little white books \[*Chant and Be Happy*\]?” I did, and I gave her a copy. After a quick glance of thanks, Osnat resumed her aspect as Ha Mifakedet.

After almost two grueling weeks, we looked forward to a day off on Shabbat. Actually, the schedule allowed for almost two free days—from Friday noon until early Sunday morning. Completely fatigued, the trainees eagerly awaited the opportunity to rest for a day and a half. Even more than their girlfriends’ association or their mothers’ cooking, they longed for a long, uninterrupted sleep. As the Raanana temple held a feast program on Saturday evenings, I invited everyone to attend, although I doubted if they would give up their sleep to attend a Hare Krsna program.

Before they released us for our day off, however, the officers inspected us. They wanted us to look our best when we were outside the camp so that we could properly represent the army. One older officer from a different unit spotted an imperfection in my morning shave, as well as some dust on my boots. He said that I couldn’t be dismissed until I had rectified the flaws. As I departed to find my razor, the officer continued speaking in a friendly, conversational mood. He had seen the devotees chanting on Dizengoff Street in Tel Aviv and had once bought a book on the street. Impressed by the depth of the philosophy, he wanted to know more. Thus he offered to drive me to the bus station.

The passengers didn’t know how to react. There I was, sitting on the bus, army boots shining, full military uniform, an M-16 with a full cartridge in my holster, and a shaved head and *Sikha,* chanting *japa*. After two long bus rides and a short trip from Cfar Saba, I walked twenty minutes to Krsna’s Raanana villa. My uniform and weapon caused great merriment among the devotees.

After more than a day of rest and some *prasadam,* I went downstairs for the feast program. Just then, several members of my platoon, including Adi and Yoram, an eighteen-year-old from another platoon (Ploogah Bet) who now chanted two rounds a day, arrived with some of their friends from home. They enjoyed a lecture by Danavir dasa and the *prasadam* feast.

To arrive in Jerusalem by 7:30 a.m. to catch the army bus to Bahd Arbah, I had to be in Cfar Saba by 5:30 a.m. Loaded with as much *prasadam* as I could carry, I rode to Jerusalem. The devotees prepared breads, *sabjis,* cakes, and other sweets. In Jerusalem, while waiting to be picked up, I shared the *prasadam* with other soldiers. John especially enjoyed the feast as we talked on the steps near the bus station.

Our rigorous training continued, as did our spiritual program of *japa* and *kirtana*. Once, during a long run, the soldiers spontaneously erupted into a joyous *kirtana*. They still didn’t have the names in the correct order, and they hadn’t really gotten the knack of responsive chanting, but for me the running became blissful. Yitzik turned around and warned me to stop the singing. I caught up with him. Shrugging my shoulders, I indicated that I wasn’t the cause of the *kirtana*. He understood that there was nothing he could do.

Boris was an eighteen-year-old Russian immigrant in our platoon. He had a tall, fragile build, but he was well-read and philosophically inclined. Many in Ploogah Bet derided Boris. Boris often approached me almost in tears for solace. We would discuss Tolstoy, Dostoevsky, and, of course, *Bhagavad-gita*. From Raan-ana I had brought copies of *Bhagavad-gita* and *Krsna* in Russian. Fascinated, Boris immediately began to read the books. He liked the Vedic philosophy, if only because it exercised his intellectual muscles during the Basic Training. When we held *kirtana* in the barracks, Boris was reluctant to chant and dance. Although he appreciated that *bhakti-yoga* may have some power, he couldn’t accept that the wild dancing of these ruffians, who didn’t even understand the meaning behind their activity, would produce eternal, spiritual benefit.

One evening after *kirtana,* before lights out, a few of the rougher recruits began to behave with me in an inordinately familiar way. Adi rebuked them. “He’s not a toy!” he scolded. They desisted and apologized.

Adi and Richard also developed an interest in the philosophy of Krsna consciousness. One evening the three of us discussed various points from the *Origins* magazine. Their curiosity piqued, they wanted to know more about *bhakti-yoga* and Vaisnava philosophy.

I didn’t know what *masah* meant—wasn’t the word for “truck” something like *masah?*—but we were told we would be embarking on a *masah.* With fully loaded backpacks, helmets, rifles, holsters loaded with cartridges, and canteens, we began to run. We kept running and running. The officers increased the pace. For us, *masah* meant a long, torturous journey. This was the toughest exercise we had experienced. Up hills, through brush, across rocky terrain and swampy marsh, the platoon struggled forward. One soldier twisted his ankle. Another collapsed from fatigue. We put them on stretchers that we carried above our heads while maintaining the pace. We took shifts on stretcher duty. Once, while running downhill on a narrow trail, I was lifting a corner of the stretcher when I tripped and fell into the bushes. Somehow I got up and continued. They wouldn’t tell us how much further we had to run. This made the journey mentally difficult. I didn’t think I could continue, and I slowed to a trot. Soldiers and officers urged me to keep pace. They could see I was trying. Rifka came back to where I was and put out her hand, offering to pull me along for some distance. I gasped, “I can’t, I’m a *brahmacari*.” She didn’t know what a *brahmacari* was, but she was satisfied when I sped up by my own endeavor. After the *masah,* Yaacov called us a few names but said that he had expected worse from a bunch like us.

Often, at Bahd Arbah and especially on field trips, officers would rush in and wake us during the night. We would have seven minutes to be fully prepared for training exercises, such as running and night drills.

“Soldier Wolf, your parents are at the sentry gate,” came the announcement through the loudspeaker. Then a messenger personally came to deliver the same information. My parents were in suburban Philadelphia. I knew they weren’t at the sentry gate. Nonetheless, I went there to see what was happening. About fifty meters outside the guard post was a familiar vehicle. Inside sat Guna-avatara dasa and his wife Jalangi dasi. Somehow they had been identified as my parents. Happy to see them, I climbed into the car and relished a sumptuous *prasadam* feast. Guna-avatara and I offered *prasadam* to the guards, who accepted it gratefully. Two soldiers from my platoon were on the shift, and they requested a *kirtana*. We all stood in a circle just inside the front gate, and Guna-avatara led *kirtana*. This was a new frontier, as the *kirtana* involved soldiers from other units. Everyone started dancing, spinning, and moving in a group circle as the *kirtana* became livelier. High-ranking officers were alerted about the spontaneous Hare Krsna festival at the base’s front gate. Alarmed, they descended on the scene. They had heard about the Hare Krsna follower in their camp, but now things were getting out of control!

Guna-avatara and I slipped away to the Ploogah Bet barracks. There, we distributed the remainder of the *prasadam* and continued with the *kirtana* fun, now among soldiers who had experience with such devotional practices. Several soldiers related to Guna-avatara that I had the “easiest” training. They appreciated that the practice of Krsna consciousness enabled one to function under difficult circumstances without undue disturbance.

Ploogah Bet had a few practicing religious Jews. They never presented opposition, however, to the development of Krsna consciousness in the platoon. I’d had several discussions with them about the essential similarity between Krsna consciousness and Judaism. The goal of both, we agreed, was to develop our love for God. Whatever process helps us do that should be accepted without bias based on the temporary body or place of birth. *Yeda* means “knowledge” in Hebrew, and *veda* means “knowledge” in Sanskrit. Ultimately, we study the *Vedas* to cultivate the highest knowledge—knowledge of Krsna, God, and how we can please Him. The religious soldiers accepted that there are many names for God, and that all are purifying when chanted sincerely. The *kirtana* was now so attractive that some religious soldiers joined in the dancing. This made everyone even more enraptured with Krsna’s holy names.

After a few days of Basic Training, when we had learned to load and shoot the M-16s, we were each assigned a few hours of guard duty every day. Everyone, myself excepted, considered the predawn shift the least desirable as it forced them to interrupt their already short sleep. As a devotee, however, I appreciated the opportunity to practice *sadhana* during the *brahma-muhurta.* Everyone was amazed that I volunteered for that shift.

Once, I was on guard with a quiet soldier named Baruch. After chanting rounds until 4:00 a.m., I took out my *karatalas* and chanted the “Gurvastakam” prayers. I was standing high up in the lookout tower, and Baruch was startled to see that I was playing *karatalas* and singing in the lookout tower. After *guru-puja* our replacements arrived. Baruch enthusiastically related to them my activities during the auspicious morning hours.

After we had learned to handle the M-16, we learned the Galil, designed by Israelis for fighting in the Galilee. Then we received the Uzi, famous for its close-range effectiveness. With each gun, we learned how to build, clean, and disassemble it. Every day and sometimes at night, we did target practice. Somehow, I was relatively successful at hitting the target. At one point the officers considered placing me on the unit’s shooting team for an interunit competition.

Our schedule also included many classes on topics such as first-aid, basic battle strategy, and Israeli and Arabic helicopters and airplanes. Due to a combination of unfamiliarity with Hebrew terms and a lack of sleep, however, I often found myself waking up near the end of the class. I do remember one lecture in particular, though. It was given at a memorial on the sandy outskirts of Bahd Arbah, after we had hiked a long distance. Yaacov narrated how a soldier had accidentally shot and killed an officer at that spot, a result of foolishly playing with a loaded rifle. This was a dramatic and convincing way to motivate the recruits to scrupulously follow all the regulations we had been taught while dealing with weapons. The timing was fitting, as our next exercise was learning to throw hand grenades.

Noah, the young religious officer from Bene Barak, sometimes became disturbed about my adherence to Krsna consciousness. Sometimes he called me “Krsna” when he became upset. Thus for his eternal benefit, he had the chance to chant God’s most intimate name.

My second day off was on a weekday. Although I was exhausted, I went with Danavir dasa and Brahmarata dasa, who was visiting Israel from Holland, to perform *bhajanas* in Dizengoff Square. I was dressed in army uniform.

*Japa* chanting became a familiar sight in Bahd Arbah. Taking advantage of a shady area in a sandy lot in front of the synagogue, I would often chant there, exchanging friendly greetings with the *daveners* as they entered for their morning prayers.

At first, the high staff at the base was confident that after a few days of tough Basic Training, I would give up my peculiar practices. Discarding that idea, they now hoped that at least the other recruits would not become influenced by Krsna consciousness, as this would cause the recruits’ parents great consternation. After all, they were not expecting Tsahal to be a training ground for new *Krsna-bhaktas.*

This reminded me of a similar experience I had had while studying at Yeshiva Esh Ha Torah a few years previously. Entering the yeshiva, I was already chanting sixteen rounds a day and following the principles of Krsna consciousness. Certain that my quirky habits would disappear with exposure to Talmudic studies, the rabbis welcomed me to the Introductory Study program. After about a week, however, when my Vaisnava convictions had only been strengthened by extensive discussions and classes with learned Jewish scholars, their excitement for my yeshiva involvement waned. After three weeks, when several younger yeshiva *broochas* had become inquisitive about Krsna consciousness, the Esh Ha Torah administration and I agreed that it was time to discontinue my term at the school.

Each unit planned a big party on the final evening of Basic Training. In Ploogah Bet, most of the soldiers requested me to lead a momentous *kirtana*. I agreed, on the condition that everyone, even those who were usually timid, chanted and danced with full energy. Even the officers, showing a different face to the recruits, joined the party. This, along with enough intoxicating beverages to stifle the inhibitions, and a general euphoria arising from the awareness that Basic Training was over created a carefree, jovial atmosphere. Such an ambiance made for a long and ecstatic *kirtana* that became the cynosure for the soldiers and officers of all three platoons.

After resuming my *sankirtana* service for two days in Raanana, Tsahal called me to report to Tel Ha Shomer. I showed up in full devotional attire and waited on a bench for several hours. Finally, a soldier escorted me to meet a high-ranking officer. After a few minutes of conversation, the officer asked what I thought about being sent to a tank division in the North. I responded that I didn’t think I was qualified. Then I sat on a bench for a few more hours before being told to go home and report to the base three days later.

Three days later, the events progressed similarly. This time I spoke with an even higher-ranking officer. Bahd Arbah only had to deal with the Hare Krsna soldier for one month. My next assigned base would have me for almost one year. It seemed no one was enthusiastic for the challenge. After about two weeks of occasionally reporting, then waiting for a brief interview, Tsahal issued my release papers.

*This work was completed on August 29, 1994, on Sri Krsna Janmastami. Many names have been changed throughout to protect everyone.*

## KID: Appendices

## Historical Sketch of Israel Yatra

**Historical Sketch of Israel Yatra**

In 1971, encouraged by Srila Prabhupada, Dinesh Candra dasa and Krsna-devi dasi became the first ISKCON devotees to preach Krsna consciousness in Israel. Soon after their arrival in the country, Kulasekhara dasa Brahmacari joined them. From Srila Prabhupada’s letters and conversations during that period, it is apparent that although the devotees were experiencing great hardships in their attempts to preach, he considered this region an important place to establish a temple. Yaacov Shames, a young man in Jerusalem who was interested in Krsna consciousness, corresponded with Srila Prabhupada, who had planned to visit Israel before his visit to Moscow.

71-2-19—Boston Bombay 16th February, 1971

My Dear Dinesh Candra,

Please accept my blessings.…

So far as your establishing one center in Israel, I have already written Krsna Devi about this. I have all blessings for you both in this regard, so you may go there immediately.

71-2-22—Boston Bombay 16th February, 1971

My dear Krsna Devi,

Please accept my blessings. I am so glad to have received your letter dated 23rd January, 1971 and have noted the contents carefully. So far as opening a center in Israel, I have all approval. Immediately you can open correspondence with one boy from Israel who has written me twice to come there and open a center. He is very eager to help. His name and address are as follows: Yacov Shames; c/o Dr. Ritchie; 79 Rehov Bait Vegan; Jerusalem, Israel. So make arrangements with the government and go there immediately. I have all blessings for this enterprise. Try and preach Krsna Consciousness in this part of the world. So you go there husband and wife with child and start a center. If you are successful in your effort, then I may stay with you a few days there before going to London.

Your husband’s letter along with yours is very encouraging and I have answered it separately. I’m so glad to know that both of you are enhancing your Krishna Consciousness by mutual cooperation. May God bless you. You are all helping me by abiding the orders of my Spiritual Master, His Divine Grace Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati Goswami Maharaja and He’ll be pleased upon you who are His grand spiritual children. So this program to open a branch in Israel has got all my approval.

Yes, you can set up a Guru-Gouranga altar and if you can offer full artik ceremony, that is nice. Pancha Tatwa are most merciful.

Hoping this will meet you in good health.

Your ever well-wisher,

A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

71-4-22—Boston Bombay 13th April, 1971

My Dear Dinesh Chandra and Krsna Devi,

Please accept my blessings. Last time I heard from you, you wanted to go to Jerusalem to start one center there and I agreed. Almost everything was settled up. What happened next? I thought that you had already gone. Presently I am making plans for going to Australia, Malaysia, and Russia also and I was planning to go to Jerusalem as well on my way to Russia. So let me know immediately in this connection what your plans are.

Just today I received another letter from Yacov Shames in Jerusalem (c/o Dr. Ritchie: 79 Bait Vegan; Jerusalem, Isreal) and he is very much anxious to have me visit there, so I have consented that on my way to Russia I will go there for some time. I asked that you correspond with this boy in my previous letter but he makes no mention of it. What happened? Please let me know as soon as possible.

Hoping this will meet you in good health.

Your ever well-wisher,

A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

ACBS/adb

71-5-13—Brooklyn Calcutta 18th May, 1971

My Dear Dinesh Chandra,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated 24th April, 1971 and have noted the contents carefully. Also I have received one letter from Yacov Shames and he acknowledges that he has been corresponding with you in regards to opening one branch there in Jerusalem.

It is very much encouraging to note that you have made final arrangements for going to Jerusalem on 2nd June, 1971, accompanied by one brahmachary. You all have my full blessings and may Krishna help you all in this noble venture. Just as soon as you arrive there, you can let me know

71-7-33 Los Angeles 13th July, 1971

My Dear Dinesh Chandra,

Please accept my blessings and offer the same to your wife Krishna Devi, daughter Vishnu Arata and Kulashekar Prabhu. I am in due receipt of your very encouraging letter dated 30th June, 1971 and have noted the contents carefully. Your endeavor is very much appreciated. Thank you very much. You have my all good wishes. Success or no success, it doesn’t matter; you have come to Israel with your family and that is my pleasure. All blessings upon you.

So for the time being, you can stay in your apartment, have discourses and hold kirtan and whoever voluntarily may join, no one can object. Whoever comes, give him nice prasadam. For the time being this can be done. There is no need of outside engagements if they are not possible to get. If you can, that’s all right. But inside activities should go on. So live peacefully and whatever service you can render is all right.

You should preach on the position of God. You can describe how God Consciousness can be developed. We are not presenting another God. We shall talk on God, his nature, His exalted position, his omnipresence and how we can realize him. For example in Bhagavad Gita we can realize, as it is stated there, that God is in everything extraordinary. We can realize God in the taste of water. When we are thirsty and drink water, the nice taste which quenches the thirst, that is God. So gradually you can introduce and disclose that according to the Vedic faith God is called Krishna, just as he is called Jehova or Allah. Indirectly you can say that God has no name but because we are accustomed to understanding everything by giving some nomenclature, so God has names which are ascertained by His activities. For example God is creating, so if I call God ‘the master of the world’ (Jagadisha) then what is the wrong there? And who can deny that God created the world?

We are not concerned with the common man because they will think that we are criticizing. So speak on general features of how we understand god. God is called great in every religion. Our appreciation of greatness are six in number. These are stated in the Krishna Book Introduction. So you have to introduce the matter of God scientifically, and that will be appreciated by any reasonable man. If we say that your God is like this and our God is like that, then there will be great disturbance. Simply speak on general topics, that God is like this. Then let them read our literature and ask questions. In this way try to push on our missionary activities.…

So far my coming to Israel, I will be glad to come but I do not know yet if it is possible. But when my program is fixed up I will let you know, probably by the first week in August.

71-11-30 Delhi 20th November, 1971

My dear Krishna devi,

Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter of November 7, 1971, and I am encouraged to note that you are working very nicely in Krishna consciousness, despite being there in a foreign land with so many obstacles. This kind of service is especially appreciated by Krishna, that some devotees should take risk for His preaching work. And my Guru Maharaj repeatedly requested his disciples to go out into far-off lands for spreading the message of Lord Caitanya. Thank you very much for this.…

Sankirtan means preaching. So if you are regularly holding classes, street Sankirtan may be postponed until the proper time. Meanwhile it is very encouraging that Dinesh Prabhu may be starting to teach classes in Vedic literature at Tel Aviv University. Just try to give this Krishna philosophy to the intelligent class of men, and distribute our books as many as possible, and your all efforts will come out successful. Know it for certain that by this your sincere working for Krishna, very soon you will go back to Home, back to Godhead.

I have notified the GBC that they should supply you with more men as they are required by you, so when that time comes you may apply to Shyamsundar, who is travelling with me, or to Karandhar or Rupanuga in the US and they will do the needful. needful.

**Prabhupada:** You went to Israel but again came back. Why? What was the difficulty?

**Kulasekhara:** Well, there’s no activity there Prabhupada. I got attacked on the street. I got very sick and I was attacked on the street when I was on *sankirtana*. *sankirtana*.

Havi dasa: *I was very interested in spiritual topics. I would read a lot of philosophy. Then I read* Siddhartha, *by Herman Hesse, and I knew there was something spiritually important in India. We were attending Tel Aviv University, me and a friend. I was studying music. Then one day an Argentinian boy was passing through from India. He heard there were some Spanish speakers, so he stayed with us. I was a little suspicious of his hippie appearance and mentality, but our talks were enthralling for me. Then I asked, “Do you do yoga?” He said, “Yes, I do* bhakti-yoga*.” Then the next day he left. Later that day I was reading a popular Israeli magazine, and there was an interview with Dinesh Candra and Krsna dasi. They talked about* bhakti-yoga*. I thought, “This is it.” Their address was in the paper, so I went to visit them. On my way I felt like my heart was coming out of my body. I got to the door and Dinesh opened and said “Hare Krsna.”*

I would visit often. Sometimes I was the only one there. I was averse to some of the ideas, especially about worshiping pictures on an altar. As an Israeli, this is about the worst thing, so I would be challenging. But the more he was talking, the more I was captivated. The philosophy was irresistibly convincing.

*The first book I read was* Krsna Consciousness, the Topmost Yoga System*. It was perfect. I agreed with everything in the book. Then I would learn English by reading the* Bhagavad-gita *and translating it into Spanish. Also, I would listen to Prabhupada chanting* bhajanas *and* kirtanas *on headphones. The sound completely fascinated and captivated my mind. I bought copies of the original three volumes of the* Srimad-Bhagavatam*. My association with the devotees inspired my first musical composition, “Dulce Hogar” (“Sweet Home”), about reviving our God consciousness.*

*At first, Dinesh and Krsna dasi had a house in a* moshav *near Herzliyya. Then they moved to an apartment in Raanana. I remember when I would get down from the bus in Raanana, I would run to their house in happiness. I had a smile on my face I couldn’t remove if I wanted to. I would count the steps until arriving. Extreme happiness.*

*Although I was born in Israel, I spent most of my childhood in Venezuela. Now, after learning about Krsna consciousness, I went back to Venezuela. There were no devotees based there, but devotees would pass through the country. Then Citsukh-ananda started a center. Then he went somewhere else. I tried to carry on the preach-ing. I remember discussing* Bhagavad-gita *in the park with a very intelligent young man, who was later initiated as Virabahu dasa. We were initiated together by Srila Prabhupada in Los Angeles, with a few other Venezuelan devotees.*

Shortly after Srila Prabhupada’s disappearance, Locanananda dasa and Guna-avatara dasa made the next concerted effort to establish preaching in Israel. Dina-dayal dasa was in the country at the time of their arrival, preaching and distributing *prasadam* from a van, but he left Israel shortly after the other devotees arrived. Guna-avatara is an Israeli-born devotee who received first initiation from Srila Prabhupada. During this period, the devotees established a fixed residence in the Tel Aviv area, where guests could come and experience Krsna consciousness. Activities included distribution of English books on the street and in shopping areas. Also, during this time, Devahuti dasi and Jalangi dasi came to Krsna consciousness, as did Murari Caitanya dasa, the Israeli table tennis champion.

By 1979, the devotees had rented a nice villa in an affluent suburb of Tel Aviv called Herzlia Pitooach, and were guided by Atmananda dasa with his wife, Salendriya dasi. Locanananda dasa had left Israel by this time. Devotees printed a Hebrew translation of *Easy Journey to Other Planets* during this period. Also, the general public opinion of the Hare Krsna movement became negative, largely as a result of various non-Krsna conscious activities performed by some “fringies” who frequented the temple.

In 1980, Ayodhyapati dasa came to preach in Israel. He situated the devotees in a country *asrama* about fifteen miles outside Haifa, and established a *prasadam* restaurant in an upper-class section of Haifa. Many Israeli devotees joined during this period, including Radha-Madhava dasi, Isvara Krsna dasa, Maha-Laksmi dasi, Bhaktamrta dasi, and Vaisnavi dasi. The devotees worked in the restaurant and collected *laksmi* through *sankirtana* with stickers, flowers, cassettes, and books. All through these years the devotees withstood great persecution from various segments of Israeli society, most notably the religious Jews. Once, the devotees attempted to do *harinama* in Jerusalem and were beaten by the religious Jews until the police arrived and intervened.

From the time that Locanananda and Guna-avatara came to Israel, the preaching efforts were overseen by Bhagavan dasa. He would visit occasionally, as would Sundararupa dasa. Through 1982, devotees would often spend considerable lengths of time in European countries collecting for the Israeli preaching. Nitya-siddha dasa, an Israeli who joined the movement in Holland, along with Murari Caitanya dasa and Devahuti dasi, spent most of this time doing *sankirtana* in countries such as France.

When Ayodhyapati was refused entrance into Israel in 1982, Danavir dasa accepted the service of leading the Israeli *yatra*. Vacating the restaurant and country *asrama* in the Haifa area, the devotees spent time based in various hotels. Our *sankirtana* at that time mostly consisted of selling paintings. Also, more of Prabhupada’s books were being translated into Hebrew.

During the autumn of 1983, the devotees moved to a villa in Raanana, a suburb of Tel Aviv. This became the base for intense BBT work and a strong *sankirtana* party. The *yatra* was directly managed by Nitya-siddha, who had returned from Europe. In addition, Santasya dasi had joined the devotees, as had Saci-devi dasi, a senior Israeli *mataji* who had been serving in the English *yatra* for more than five years. There were also two more *brahmacaris:* Sivananda dasa, a native Israeli, and Nitya-lila dasa, a Mexican-born Jew who had been with the Israeli devotees at different times since the late ’70s. During this time, the devotees were beginning to attract individuals from the higher classes of Israeli society to support the movement. Most notably, Bhakta Yigal, a successful Israeli lawyer, adopted Krsna consciousness and assisted the devotees in legal matters, of which there were an abundance.

In 1985, the *yatra* printed an unabridged edition of *Bhagavad-gita As It Is* in Hebrew, with a deluxe, Grand Classics cover. At the end of 1985, the first Vedic marriage and initiation ceremonies were held in Israel. Those who took first initiation were Suddha-sattva dasa, an Israeli kibbutznik in his forties who had contacted ISKCON during his travels in India, and Dhira Govinda dasa, a graduate of Pennsylvania State University who had joined the movement in Israel. The *yajna* also established the marriage of Isvara Krsna dasa and Radha-Madhava dasi, and more than 150 friends and relatives attended.

During this period, the devotees were sending a regular newsletter to a congregation of more than 500 people, most of whom had been met by the devotees out on *sankirtana*.

In 1986, the Israeli devotees absorbed themselves in collecting *laksmi* to produce and print Srila Prabhupada’s books in Hebrew while simultaneously distributing the Hebrew books they had already printed. By the end of 1986, *Bhagavad-gita,* the First Canto of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam, Krsna* book, and *Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Adi-lila,* Part One, had appeared in Hebrew in the Grand Classics style. In addition, we printed small books such as *Easy Journey to Other Planets, Perfect Questions, Perfect Answers* (this was printed in 1982 with Harivilasa Prabhu’s financial assistance), *Sri Isopanishad, Coming Back, Chant and Be Happy,* and *The Science of Self-Realization* (abridged). Devahuti dasi had been heading the translation team.

The *yatra* hired many workers to facilitate the book production and to take care of temple maintenance. We wanted to free up as many devotees as possible to go out on *sankirtana*. One worker, a young woman hired to clean the temple, became a devotee herself. Later, she was named Nagapatni dasi. Also at that time, Nurit Zaidman, an anthropology student, began an in-depth study of the Israeli *yatra* for her thesis topic.

Our main income at this time came from the sale of hand-made candles, produced by Suddha-sattva dasa. In addition, the devotees began to operate a preaching center in Tel Aviv in 1986.

At the start of 1987, the devotees opened new centers in Haifa (North Israel) and Beersheba (South Israel). Isvara Krsna dasa and Radha-Madhava dasi started a temple in Haifa, and Guna-avatara dasa opened a temple in Beersheba with the help of Nitya-lila dasa, whose original paintings helped support the center. Meanwhile, Dhira Govinda dasa and Maha-Laksmi dasi operated the Tel Aviv center in conjunction with Nitya-siddha dasa and his wife, Saci-devi dasi, and the other devotees based in the Raanana center.

In Tel Aviv the center held a guest program six evenings a week with *prasa-dam* distribution, *kirtana,* and a *Bhagavad-gita* class. Many *bhaktas* and *bhaktins* joined at this time, two of whom—Dolayatra dasi and Saguna dasi—became fixed in the principles of Krsna consciousness. Also, the center became a place where many friends of Krsna in the Tel Aviv area would frequently visit. In Haifa, despite severe opposition to preaching activities, Parama-guru dasa joined the devotees as a result of their contact book distribution *sankirtana*.

In Beersheba, the opposition to preaching activities by the religious Jews was overwhelming. As a result, in the summer of 1987 the Hebrew BBT located its activities in the Beersheba apartment and did not attempt to run a public center.

The devotees left the Raanana villa in the late summer of 1987 and moved to the city centers. The autumn of 1987 marked the beginning of some positive media coverage of our activities. Specifically, several major newspapers favorably reported how the devotees had publicly demonstrated against Soviet oppression of the Hare Krsnas in Russia. Over the next six months, several similarly positive articles appeared on events such as the Vedic wedding ceremony of Dhira Govinda and Maha-Laksmi (performed by Danavir dasa during a day off from Israeli army Basic Training), the birth of Jahnavi to Isvara Krsna and Radha-Madhava, and Danavir dasa’s adventures in the Israeli army. (In order to preach in Israel, Danavir and Dhira Govinda had both become Israeli citizens and were recruited into the army.) Also, a five-page article, with both color and black-and-white photos, of the Tel Aviv preaching center appeared in Israel’s largest newspaper. Bhakta Adrian, a New York jeweler and an ISKCON life member of several years, provided much of the inspiration and organization behind this wave of favorable public relations.

By the summer of 1988, the Tel Aviv center was attracting more than one hundred guests a week and doing a daily *bhajana* program on Dizengoff Street. As it was apparent that the best location for a public center was Tel Aviv, the devotees concentrated their preaching efforts in that area.

The BBT devotees (Guna-avatara dasa, his wife, Devahuti dasi, and Saguna dasi) took an apartment near the Tel Aviv city temple, and Isvara Krsna dasa and Radha-Madhava dasi came to Tel Aviv to coordinate the preaching. Meanwhile, Dhira Govinda and Maha-Laksmi went to Haifa with a group of stalwart *sankirtana* devotees (Parama-guru dasa, Santasya dasi, Nagapatni dasi, and Dolayatra dasi) to concentrate on book distribution. By the end of 1988, the devotees had a deluxe set of six books to distribute, as *Adi-lila* Part Two and the Second Canto of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* had been printed in Hebrew. Also, the abridged edition of the *Srila Prabhupada-lilamrta* had become available.

Until this time, the devotees performed *sankirtana* almost exclusively in Jewish cities and villages, with occasional visits to Nazareth, a city consisting mainly of Christian Arabs. We thought it neither fruitful nor safe to perform *sankirtana* in other Arab towns. However, the devotees in the Haifa party began to venture into Arab villages and met astounding success in their book distribution.

First, devotees entered Druze villages. The Druze are a religious sect based mainly in Syria, Lebanon, and Northern Israel. In Israel, about eighty thousand Druze occupy about twenty villages. The devotees discovered that the Druze are irresistibly attracted to Srila Prabhupada’s books; they would eagerly purchase full sets. Within a short time, Druze book distribution completely supported the Israeli *yatra*.

The Druze hold reincarnation as one of their primary philosophical tenets. There are many remnants of Vedic culture still apparent in their lifestyle and culture. Also, a political-spiritual leader of theirs, Kamal Jumbalat, had studied the Vedic scriptures extensively and had even written books about Krsna. He had been wanting to translate *Bhagavad-gita* into Arabic before he was assassinated. Thus a large percentage of the intellectual Druze—teachers, village mayors, lawyers, doctors, etc.—have taken sets of books.

The peak of this Druze book distribution came in January of 1989, when Salman Falach, the Minister of Education for the Druze and some other minority groups in Israel, and one of Santasya dasi’s *sankirtana* contacts, purchased fifty full sets for Druze educational institutions for more than $8,000. Mr. Falach also purchased, for himself, all of Srila Prabhupada’s books in English, and expressed his opinion, shared by many Druze, that the roots of his religion were in the Vedic scriptures. Druze businessmen began to offer to distribute Srila Prabhupada’s books in Lebanon if we could supply them books in Arabic. To summarize, all segments of the Druze community in Israel—religious, cultural, political—became impressed by Krsna consciousness.

As of February 1989, devotees began to visit Muslim villages, also meeting great success in their book distribution. Although it is generally found that Arab villages are politically agitated, the devotees, wearing devotional dress, have not had trouble approaching the intellectuals of the Muslim community. One Muslim village arranged for the devotees to give a program for fifty of its most respected members, all of whom enjoyed and appreciated the program. As it is known that Arabic countries are extremely difficult places to present Krsna consciousness, we hoped that the success in Northern Israel would serve as a catalyst to introduce Krsna consciousness in other Arabic areas.

At the time of this writing, the book distribution is going strong, and Israel is in fifth place in terms of books distributed per capita \[this was written in May 1989\]. The devotees are now endeavoring to secure books in Arabic, as there is clearly a huge market for Srila Prabhupada’s books in that language. At present, the Hebrew BBT Trustee is His Holiness Jayadvaita Swami. For several years the devotees have considered establishing a Hare Krsna kibbutz, and we hope this project will soon manifest. The Hare Krsna movement has many friends, both among the Jewish and Arab sectors.

Recently, the devotees had a successful meeting with Sheikh Tarif Amin, the topmost spiritual leader of the Druze, and he expressed great appreciation for the activities of the Hare Krsna movement in propagating the universal message of love of God. Also, the devotees recently mounted a “Hare Krsna—Purity is the Force” public relations campaign.

In summary, the *sankirtana* preaching spirit is strong in the country. Regarding the dangerous political situation in Israel, the devotees are preaching to all factions that only through God consciousness can all peoples come out of the bodily concept of life and live peacefully. Of course, because of the extremely sensitive atmosphere, each move to expand Krsna consciousness must be intelligently considered, or the reaction could be severe.

As of this May 1989 writing, the current services of full-time temple and BBT devotees are as follows:

Haifa *sankirtana* house—Dhira Govinda dasa, Parama-guru dasa, Maha-Laksmi dasi, Santasya dasi, Nagapatni dasi, and Dolayatra dasi.

Tel Aviv Preaching Center—Isvara Krsna dasa, Radha-Madhava dasi, and Suddha-sattva dasa.

BBT house—Guna-avatara dasa, Nitya-lila dasa, Devahuti dasi, Saguna dasi, and Bhaktin Alisa. Bhakta Yigal takes care of legal affairs for the *yatra* while maintaining a private legal practice.

Written in October 1990

In September 1989, the Israel *yatra* printed 2,000 copies of *Bhagavad-gita* in Arabic. It had first been printed in 1972. Also, toward the end of 1989, we printed the Third Canto of the *Srimad-Bhagavatam* in Hebrew. In November of 1989, Dhira Govinda and Maha-Laksmi again met with Sheikh Tarif Amin. Sheikh Amin stated that he would like the Druze people to work together with the Hare Krsna movement “as one race.”

The center at Frishman Street continued its evening programs until February of 1990, when most of the devotees traveled to India. At that time, Isvara Krsna and Radha-Madhava moved to Haifa, and Suddha-sattva maintained the ground-floor Frishman Street apartment. He continues until the present to hold Saturday evening feast programs in Tel Aviv.

Dhira Govinda and Maha-Laksmi moved to the Arab village of Rama in late 1989, maintaining themselves by book distribution—mainly by distributing the Arabic *Gita.* In February of 1990, they were joined by Parama-guru dasa. The devotees in Rama collected *laksmi* to print *Coming Back* in Arabic, and that book appeared in April 1990. Also in April, Gaura-bhakta dasa visited Israel from Cairo, and participated in the Arab preaching, including the successful book distribution effort at the celebration for Nabi (Prophet) Schweib, the greatest Druze prophet.

In June of 1990, Isvara Krsna dasa, Dhira Govinda dasa, their families, and Parama-guru dasa moved to the Arab village Osafia, outside Haifa. More than fifty Jewish, Druze, and devotee guests attended the Janmastami festival in the new Osafia temple. The festival, as well as the general phenomenon of the Hare Krsna devotees living in Osafia, received much positive media coverage. Recently, Isvara Krsna dasa has developed the field of cultural *nama-hatta* preaching to respectable audiences in the Jewish community. Also, Guna-avatara and his wife are cultivating friends of ISKCON in the Tel Aviv area.

Written in February 1993

Suddha-sattva dasa holds Saturday evening programs in Tel Aviv. Guna-avatara and Devahuti are based in Osafia, doing BBT work. Isvara Krsna and Radha-Madhava are living in a vegetarian *moshav* in the north of Israel. They do *prasadam* catering and give lectures at kibbutzim. Isvara Krsna is also in the process of organizing a *pada-yatra* around the Sea of Galilee.

## Sheikhs Visit Holy Dhamas

From studying Srila Prabhupada’s books for more than a decade and associating with devotees, the Druze spiritual leaders increasingly developed interest in Krsna consciousness and the similarities between Vaisnavism and the beliefs and cultures of the Muwahidoon. This culminated in more than a dozen Druze sheikhs visiting Vaisnava holy places in India, including Mayapur and Vrnda-vana, during March and April of 2001. Below are some reports from devotees who hosted and associated with the Druze leaders in India.

The sheikhs arrived in Mayapur yesterday, and were taken to Srila Prabhu-pada’s *samadhi*. His Holiness Jayapataka Swami was there, along with about five students from the *gurukula* carrying garlands and chanting prayers to welcome the delegation. Together, everyone entered the *samadhi* building, and Jayapataka Maharaja showed the sheikhs the various mosaics on the ceiling and explained the events in Prabhupada’s life that they represented. After that, we were all taken to our rooms in the Conch building. Lunch was served in the GBC *prasadam* area around 2:30. The Druze were served very nicely by *brahmacaris* and were all pleased.

This morning we went down to the temple room immediately after *mangala-arati* and *tulasi-puja* and held a ceremony to welcome and honor the sheikhs. It was wonderful! The Druze delegation came in singing songs about Krsna and describing how they had come to be with His wonderful devotees.

Then they went out and stood on a raised platform and stood in line to be greeted by His Holiness Jayapataka Swami. Maharaja spoke for a few minutes, explaining to the devotees who the Druze were, where they had come from, and why they were there at all. Then Sheikh Hussein Aburukun spoke to the devotees. He said that the Druze and the devotees are like one race, like brothers, and that he is happy to be here among so many wonderful people. He said he hoped in the future that our two groups may continue our cooperation between the “brothers Druze and the brothers Krsna.” Every time he made a point and his words were translated into English and Hindi, the assembled devotees would cheer enthusiastically. Then other sheikhs spoke, sharing Sheikh Aburukun’s mood and presenting the same hope for cooperation.

We could not have asked or wished for a better ceremony or reception. The Druze were enthused and ecstastic. Today we’ll visit the *gurukula,* attend an initiation ceremony, watch an *abhisekha* of Lord Caitanya, partake of a wonderful feast with his Holiness Jayapataka Swami and all of Prabhupada’s disciples, and visit the Ganga.

I am quite tired now between the travel and trying to be sure that the Druze’s visit is perfect, but I plan to keep a journal of their visit and let you know how it goes. We will be going to Jagannatha Puri on March 11th, where we are scheduled to meet the King of Puri at 7 p.m. on that day.

Your servant,

Vijnana dasa Vanacari

After breakfast on the 9th, the Druze went to visit the *gurukula,* where they met with the teachers. In the afternoon, they finished the tour of the Samadhi by visiting the upstairs museum depicting Srila Prabhupada’s life, then going to the top to see the view. In the evening, they were offered *prasadam* with Prabhu-pada’s disciples on the roof of the Lotus building. It was an ecstatic experience to see them happily honoring a fifty-preparation feast. By the end, they were in total bliss and laughing and smiling happily. After the feast, unlimited pictures were taken with various *sannyasis* and senior devotees. They were all totally blissful.

On the 10th, the Druze had breakfast at the restaurant. After that, they met for almost two hours with Jayapataka Swami in his room. They asked a lot of questions and wanted to continue talking, but it was then time for lunch. Maharaja will be traveling to Puri tomorrow on the same flight as us, and they are going to meet him again there.

Lunch was served on the first floor of the Lotus building. Just after reciting the “*Sarira*” prayer, Bhakti-vikasa Swami made a short speech, again welcoming them. This sparked off speeches from the Druze, glorifying the devotees, and more speeches from the devotees glorifying the Druze. This is a usual practice among the Druze when they are at ceremonial functions. After *prasadam* they all seemed totally satisfied.

*Prasadam* finished about 2:30 p.m., and at 4:00 we went down to the Ganga where a boat was waiting to take us to Swarup Ganj and Bhaktivinoda Thukura’s house. Mahavisnu Swami (the younger) accompanied us. Upon arriving, we paid respects to Srila Bhaktivinoda Thukura and Gaurakisora dasa Babaji, then went upstairs to the balcony. There, Mahavisnu Swami gave a short talk on Bhaktivinoda Thukura’s life, and his vision of a grand temple with people from all over the world coming to chant. The Druze then asked questions.

Maharaja also told the story of the cobbler and the *brahmana,* and the Druze appreciated the story. At the end, Maharaja began a *kirtana,* and the Druze participated by clapping hands and chanting.

We continued the *kirtana* all the way back to the boat. We returned to the temple around 6:15 p.m. We had scheduled a tour of the temple guided by Tusti-mohana Krsna Prabhu at 7:30.

By then, I had such a severe headache that I was unable to continue with the tour and had to go and rest in a dark room. What follows is Gopakumara’s report. He also took twelve pictures.

The Druze went to the temple with Tusti-mohana Prabhu. While Tusti-mohana acted as their guide, Mahavisnu Swami remained in the background, enthusing the crowd with *kirtana.* Tusti-mohana Prabhu ordered two *puja* plates and gave them to Jananivasa Prabhu. The Druze were then asked to approach Radha-Madhava’s altar to offer Them incense. They offered the incense, making sure that they offered to each of the eight *gopis* and to Radha-Madhava. The crowd was going wild behind them, crying out, “Hare Krsna!”

Then they were shown to Panca-tattva’s altar and were offered Panca-tattva’s garlands. When they took *darsana* of Lord Nrsimhadeva, they again offered incense. Many pictures were taken. At the end, we went to Srila Prabhu-pada and offered flowers at his lotus feet. Each of them then again offered a stick of incense while listening to the loud *kirtana* glorifying Srila Prabhupada.

When the Druze stepped out of the temple room, three *gurukulis* greeted them and thanked them in Arabic (they had learned how to thank them for this occasion). Then each Druze was given *maha-prasadam.* They were so excited that they asked Tusti-mohana Prabhu to come with them to their room and continue his all-embracing preaching. They are still talking with him downstairs as I write. But he is trying to tell them that he has many other things to do.

About Gopakumara and Tusti-mohana, who are both Druze: We had doubts that the sheikhs would accept them, because they have stepped outside the traditional Druze religion.
